CHAPTER INTRODUCTION 1. THE PROCUREMENT PROBLEM. Government procurement during peacetime involves legal problems of considerable importance. The advent of war brings with it an intensification of those problems as well as difficulties peculiar to wartime procurement. Materiel is required promptly and in large quantities. Requirements may shift or change overnight and new requirements must be met. This, however, is only one facet of the problem of war procurement. Others involve the financing of huge war contracts, the control of war profits, the conversion and reconversion of war plants and the maintenance of uniform and fair treatment of contractors dealing with the Government. 

2. PURPOSE AND SCOPE OF HANDBOOK. 

8.

Legal concepts of government contracting. This text is designed to present a discussion of the legal principles involved in Government procurement and related fields, with emphasis on the Department of the Army. The first portion of the text considers the legal concepts underlying Government contracting, including the Government's right to contract, the legal similarities and differences between Government and private contracting, and the necessity for statutory authorization and appropriations as a basis for the execution of contracts by executive agencies of the Government. b. 

Procurement methods. 

Peacetime procurement methods are summarized, including the system of contracting based on advertising for bids and the statutory formalities required to be observed. These peacetime methods have been greatly modified to secure the speed and flexibility necessary to wartime procurement. Consideration is accordingly given to the effect of these modifications, and to the principal legislative enactments, orders and regulations which govern contracting under the wartime system. C.

Performance of government contracts. The principles relating to performance of Government contracts, while generally similar to those applicable to private contracts, are different in some respects by reason of special applicable legal principles and special contract provisions which vary the rights and duties of the parties. In addition, the problem of implied contracts constitutes almost a separate branch of Government contract law. Perhaps the greatest difference between Government and private contracts is in the remedies available to the contract or for enforcement of its contract rights. A discussion of these subjects is also included.

d. Wartime contracting. A portion of the text is devoted to problems peculiar to wartime contracting. Consideration is given to contract terminations, including the settlement of termination claims, the financing of contractors and subcontractors, and the disposal of property on hand at the time termination takes place. Other subjects considered include Government-owned facilities, price adjustment by agreement, repricing by administrative order, and renegotiation.

CHAPTER BASIC LEGAL CONCEPTS OF GOVERNMENT CONTRACTS 3. POWER OF GOVERNMENT TO ENTER INTO CONTRACTS. The Government has inherent power to enter into contracts. This power is not based upon any specific constitutional or statutory grant, but exists as an incident of sovereignty.1 

4. SIMILARITIES TO PRIVATE CONTRACTING. Legal principles governing contracts with the Government are substantially the same as those governing contracts between private persons. The essential elements of a valid contract are the same in both Government and private contracting. There must be a valid acceptance,2 mutuality,3 consideration, 4 certainty of terms,5 and competent parties. 

a. General. The principal 5. DIFFERENCES FROM PRIVATE CONTRACTING. 

distinguishing features of Government contracting stem from statutory requirements, distinguishing the dual personality of the Government as contractor and as sovereign,7 and the limited character of procedures for enforcement of Government contractual liability. 

1. Thus, in the absence of specific statutory authority, the Government may take a bond as security for performance (United States v. Tingey, 5 Pet. (30 U.S.) 114 (1831)), or a mortgage on private property to secure a debt (Van Brocklin v. Tennessee, 117 U.S. 151, 154 (1886)). 2. 

38 Op. Atty. Gen. 555 (1937); Haldane v. United States, 69 Fed. 819 

3. United States v. Purcell Envelope Company, 249 U.S. 313 (1919). 

4. Willard, Sutherland and Company v. United States, 262 U.S. 489 (1923).

It has been held, however, that consideration in Government contracting must involve a benefit running to the Government and not merely, as in private contracting, a detriment to the contractor. Vulcanite Portland 

Cement Co. v. United States, 74 Ct. Cl. 692 (1932); "Notes on Jurisdiction of the Secretary of War" (1920), p. 5. 5. 

United States v. Ellicott, 223 U.S. 524 (1912).

7. See Ch. 19, "Breach by Government." 3

b. Immunity from suit.

The Government is immune from suit, st, except to the extent that it has voluntarily consented to be sued. 

This immunity extends to proceedings in rem against property owned by the Government.9

C. xtends

Illustrative Case: 

Certain land in Alabama, on which state taxes were outstanding and unpaid, was conveyed to the Government. 

Subsequently the land was sold by the state at tax sales, and the Government sued to have the tax liens and the sales declared invalid. Held: The tax sales were invalid. The tax liens, however, continued in effect even though unenforceable as long as the Government held title to the property.10

Consent to be sued. The Government has consented to be sued generally in actions based "upon any contract, express or implied, with the Government of the United States, or for damages liquidated or unliquidated, in cases not sounding in tort."11 Such actions may be brought in the United States Court of Claims without regard to the amount 8. 9.

United States v. Clarke, 8 Pet. (33 U.S.) 436 (1834). This immunity may be extended by Congress to a corporate instrumentality of the 

Government. Maricopa County v. Valley National Bank, 318 U.S. 357 (1943). 

For discussion, see Ch. 20. "Remedies." "The same exemption from judicial process extends to the property of the United States, and for the same reasons." The Siren, 7 Wall. (74 

U.S.) 152, 154 (1868). See also Minnesota v. United States, 305 U.S. 382 (1939), in which a state unsuccessfully sought to condemn a right of way over lands held by the Government in trust for Indians, and Stitzell Weller 

Distillery v. Wallace, 30 F. Supp. 1010 (D. C., 1940), denying the right to proceed against a fund in the United States Treasury in which the 

Government had a proprietary interest. 

10. **** we perceive no reason why the United States, albeit protected with respect to proceedings against it without its consent, should stand, so far as the existence of liens is concerned, in any different position from that of other purchasers of lands in Alabama who take conveyances on and after the specified tax date." United States v.

involved, and in the District Courts of the United States where the amount involved does not exceed $10,000.12 d. Law applicable. In determining questions arising under 

Government contracts, courts are not bound by the law of any particular state as in private contract cases. In so far as a federal common law can be said to have developed in the field of contracts, it will be applied in cases involving Government contracts where the question to be determined is not governed by the Federal Constitution or a federal statute.13 the 

6. NATURE OF AGENCY IN GOVERNMENT CONTRACTING. a. General. Authority of an officer or agent to contract in behalf of the Government must stem from the Constitution or from a federal statute. The contracting representativ of the Government is known ordinarily as a contracting officer. He is a special agent possessing only such authority as is given him either expressly or by necessary implication. 15 It therefore becomes important to ascertain from the Constitution or applicable statute the extent of the agent's authority. The risk of lack of authority falls on the contractor. 12. 13. 

36 Stat. 1093 (1911), 28 U.S.C. 41 (20). See ch. 20, "Remedies", for further discussion of this subject. 

Kemp v. United States, 38 F. Supp. 568, 571 (D. Md., 1941): "In matters governed by the Federal Constitution, or by Acts of Congress, the law to be applied is not the law of the State, and while no one specific Act of Congress is directly involved in the present case in the sense that its interpretation is involved, the contract is based upon the authority given to the War Department by statute." 

See United States v. Allegheny County, 322 U.S. 174 (1944). This subject is further discussed in Ch. 20, "Remedies." The general rules of contract law remain substantially unchanged by this situation. 

For example, the doctrine of anticipatory breach of contract is recognized and applied in the federal courts. Roehm v. Horst, 178 U.S. 1 (1899). 

14. "We have no officer in this Government, from the President down to the most subordinate agent, who does not hold office under the law, with prescribed duties and limited authority." The Floyd Acceptances, 7 Wall. (74 U.S.) 666, 676 (1868). See Whiteside v. United States, 93 U.S. 247, 257 (1876). For authority of contracting officers under appropriation acts, see Ch. 3, "Government Appropriations, Relation of GAO to Government Contracts." 

15. Federal Trade Commission v. Raladam Co., 283 U.S. 643, 649 (1931).

For discussion of special agency, see Mechem on Agency (2d Ed. 1914), sec. 61. པ་ 5

Illustrative Cases: 

(1) The Government held a recorded chattel mortgage on personal property to secure payment of a loan. The Government county agent gave the owner permission to sell the property upon condition that the Government receive part of the proceeds in payment on account of the debt. A representative of the owner sold the property and converted the proceeds of the sale. The 

Government brought replevin to recover the property. Held:

Judgment for the Government. The authority of the Government agent did not extend to releasing the mortgage under these facts. 

Whoever deals with a Goverment agent does so at his own risk as to the agent's authority.16 (2) A collector of internal revenue, acting within the scope of his authority, accepted a surety bond from a taxpayer to accompany a claim for abatement of income tax. Thereafter, the collector consented to termination of liability on the bond and surrendered it before its condition was fully satisfied. The Government nevertheless sued on the bond. Held: Recovery allowed. The collector had no power to release the bond. "Subordinate officers of the United States are without that power save only as it has been conferred upon them by Act of Congress, or is to be implied from other powers so granted. "17 b• Implied contracts. Further, one who lacks authority to make an express contract on behalf of the Government may not by his acts bind the Government on an implied contract. It is immaterial that the claimant conferred a benefit if the Government representative had no authority to receive the benefit in behalf of the Government.18

C. Actual and implied authority. The rule in peacetime contracting is that the person purporting to act for the Government_must have actual authority. Actual authority may be express or implied.19

In Government contracting, the concept of implied authority is applied more strictly than in the law of private contracts. 20 Authority may be implied from some duty imposed upon the agent or from express authority given him. 

Authority may be conferred upon the agent expressly in part and by implication in part. 

16. United States v. Christensen, 50 F. Supp. 30 (E.D. Ill., 1943). 

17. Royal Indemnity Co. v. United States, 313 U.S. 289, 294 (1941). 18. 

See Ch. 6, "Implied Contracts". 19. 20.

The limitations on express and implied authority are discussed in 

Mechem on Agency (2d Ed., 1914), secs. 707 and 708. 

See Chase v. United States, 155 U.S. 489 (1894).

Illustrative Case: 

Congress passed an act appropriating funds for the purchase of 65,000 volumes of the Congressional Globe and 70,000 printing plates. The act directed that the volumes and plates be placed in the custody of the Public Printer. The Public Printer, having no storage space available, stored the property with claimants. 

Claimants sued to recover storage charges. The Government contended that the Public Printer had no authority to bind the Government to pay such charges. Held: Recovery allowed. "The act * * * made it his duty to provide for the care and preserva tion of the property. The authority of the Public Printer *** sprung from a necessity created by the act of Congress* * *"21 

d. Lack of actual authority. Where an agent lacks actual authority, the Government is not bound by his acts on any theory of estoppel22or apparent authority.

Illustrative Case: 

The Secretary of the Treasury had authority to appoint special agents to receive and collect abandoned or captured property in the Confederate States. An assistant special agent contracted with the claimant to obtain possession of cotton in those states for the Federal Government. The claimant obtained evidence necessary to establish the right of the Government to the cotton, and spent his own money for transporting it. He sought reimbursement for the money spent. Held: No recovery. An assistant special agent had no authority to make such a contract. The Government was not bound by its representative's apparent authority.23 e. Apparent authority. Application of the rule that apparent authority does not bind the Government may lead to undesirable results in wartime because of the necessity for speed in procurement. Accordingly, 21. Rives v. United States, 28 ct. cl. 249, 251 (1893). 

22. "The Government is never estopped by the acts of its agents in enter ing into an arrangement which 'the law does not sanction or permit'." 

United States v. Gudewicz, 45 F. Supp. 787, 789 (E.D.N.Y., 1942). 

23. Whiteside v. United States, 93 U.S. 247, 257 (1876): "Although a private agent, acting in violation of specific instructions, yet within the scope of his general authority, may bind his principal, the rule as to the effect of the like act of a public agent is otherwise, for the reason that it is better that an individual should occasionally suffer from the mistakes of public officers or agents, than to adopt a rule which, through improper combinations or collusion, might be turned to the detriment and injury of the public." 7

8. AGENCY CONTRACTS SUBJECT TO APPROVAL. Where a contract is entered into subject to approval by higher authority, the giving of such approval is a condition precedent to the existence of a valid contract. In the event the contractor proceeds to perform before action by higher authority has been taken, and approval is later denied, the contractor may not recover even on the basis of implied contract. 24.

Illustrative Case: 

The Government leased from the contractor a wharf and various pieces of machinery. The contract was signed on behalf of the 

Government by an assistant quartermaster at Key West, and was approved by his commanding officer. The officers at Key West had no authority to make such an arrangement without the approval of the Quartermaster General. The Government used the property for four years, but the Quartermaster General never approved the arrangement. Held: The contractor's claim for rent must be disallowed. 28

Where a contract requires approval of higher authority as a condition to its validity, the act of the contracting officer in executing the contract subject to such approval is within the scope of his authority. Approval, therefore, differs from ratification, since in ratification the original act itself is assumed to have been unauthorized. However, the legal consequences of approval and ratification are, in general, substantially the same. Indeed, reference is often made to approval and ratification interchangeably.29′

CHAPTER GOVERNMENT APPROPRIATIONS, RELATION OF GENERAL ACCOUNTING OFFICE TO GOVERNMENT CONTRACTS 

11.

NECESSITY FOR APPROPRIATIONS. The Federal Constitution provides that 

"No money shall be drawn from the Treasury, but in Consequence of Appropriations made by Law * * *"l This provision "means simply that no money can be paid out of the Treasury unless it has been appropriated by an act of Congress. n2 

The limitation is absolute, and prohibits the payment even of judicially established Goverment debts unless Congress provides an appropriation from which such payment may be made.

Illustrative Cases: 

a. The Government sued the contractor for an alleged overpayment under a contract. The contractor denied liability and pleaded underpayment in a counterclaim. Judgment was entered for the contractor in the amount of $188,496. The contractor's executrix sought a writ of mandamus to compel the Secretary of the 

Treasury to pay the judgment. Held: An affirmative judgment can not properly be entered against the Government under a counterclaim, but even if it could, the Secretary of the Treasury was not authorized to pay, and a court could not order him to pay, without an appropriation therefor.3 b. An Army officer was mustered out of the Army in 1871. 

In 1879, the President reinstated him, pursuant to the authority contained in a special statute, effective 1871. The officer sued to recover pay for the period between 1871 and 1879. 

The 

Government urged as a defense the lack of any appropriation to pay the officer for that period. Held: Judgment for the plaintiff.

The constitutional prohibition against withdrawal of money from the Treasury, except under authority of a Congressional appropriation, is not a limitation on the power of a court to render a judgment against the Government where the court has jurisdiction of the suit. It merely limits the power of executive officers to pay the judgment.4 

1. U.S. Const., art. 1, sec. 9, cl. 7. 

2. Cincinnati Soap Co. v. United States, 301 U.S. 308, 321 (1937). 3. 

Reeside v. Walker, 11 How. (52 U.S.) 271 (1850). 

4. Collins v. United States, 15 Ct. Cl. 22 (1879). 11

12. METHOD OF MAKING APPROPRIATIONS. a. Function of Bureau of the 

Budget. The Bureau of the Budget and the General Accounting Office were established in 1921.5 The Bureau of the Budget has the duty of preparing budgets for the President for submission to Congress as a basis for the making of appropriations. It is headed by a Director and an Assistant Director, appointed by the President.7 It operates under rules and regulations prescribed by the President and is an agency of the executive branch of the Government. b. Preparation of annual budget. Heads of executive departments and establishments of the Government transmit to the Bureau of the Budget during the Fall of each year estimates of the funds which will be required by their respective agencies during the next fiscal year, which begins 1 July. 

The Bureau of the Budget analyzes these estimates, holds informal hearings and recommends to the President the amount which it believes should be allotted to each agency. The President, with the assistance of the Bureau, prepares the final budget, which is sent to Congress with a Presidential message shortly after the opening of the regular session in January. budget as transmitted to Congress is, in effect, a Presidential estimate of expenditures to be made by the agencies listed therein, 8 and a request that the necessary funds be appropriated. The C. Action of Congress. Appropriation bills may originate in either the Senate or the House of Representatives.9 By custom, however, such bills ordinarily originate in the House in the form of a bill for the carrying out of existing legislation.

After the budget is received by Congress, it is referred in the House to the Committee on Appropriations. This committee is divided into a number of subcommittees to each of which, with certain exceptions,10 is assigned for detailed study the proposed expenditures of one or more 

5. 42 Stat. 20 (1921); 31 U.S.C. 1 et seq. (The Budget and Accounting Act.) 

6.

It was transferred from the Treasury Department to the Executive Office of the President by Joint Resolution of 7 June 1939, approving the 

President's Reorganization Plan No. 1. See 4 Fed. Reg. 2727. 

7. 42 Stat. 22 (1921); 31 U.S.C. 16. 8.

Some agencies, principally Government corporations engaged in incomeproducing activities, operate on revolving funds rather than appropriations, and are not listed in the budget. 

9. Revenue bills, however, must originate in the House of Representatives. 

U.S. Const., art. I, sec. 7, cl. 1. 

10. One subcommittee is concerned with recommending an appropriation for the 

Legislative Establishment, another with considering requests for deficiency appropriations. 12

specified agencies. After preliminary study and hearings, each subcommittee prepares and submits to the full Committee on Appropriations a bill appropriating to the agencies assigned to its jurisdiction such funds as it believes proper. Upon approval by the full committee, the bill is reported to the House.

After passage by the House, with or without amendments, the bill is transmitted to the Senate. There it is referred to the Senate Committee on Appropriations, which is also divided into subcommittees for purposes of hearings and detailed study. After additional hearings and consideration by the appropriate subcommittee and the full committee, the bill is reported to the Senate, with any amendments proposed by the committee. 

Subject to the normal legislative procedures for resolving disagreements between the Senate and the House, the bill is eventually passed and forwarded to the President for signature. The President obtains the views of the Bureau of the Budget and the agencies concerned before acting on the bill.11 

13. EFFECT OF APPROPRIATION ON GOVERNMENT CONTRACTS TYPES OF 

APPROPRIATIONS. The existence and nature of an appropriation statute may determine, not only the power of executive officers to withdraw money from the Treasury, but also the power of such officers to enter into contracts obligating the Government. Ordinarily, authority for a Government contract must be found in one or more of three types of statutes: (1) a statute authorizing the contract expressly or by implication; (2) an appropriation act appropriating a specific sum for the particular work or services to which the contract relates; or (3) an appropriation act appropriating a lump sum for various designated purposes, one of which is work or services of the type to which the contract relates. 

14. CONTRACTS AUTHORIZED BY LAW. a. When the statute authorizes the work and does not limit the cost. Where a contract is made under authority of a statute expressly or impliedly authorizing the contract, whether or not the statute contains an appropriation therefor, the contract is often termed "a contract authorized by law." This term does not refer to contracts impliedly authorized solely by virtue of the existence of an appropriation. If the statute authorizes the work without limiting the cost, the contracting officer may validly agree to any amount, even if Congress thereafter appropriates for the project an amount less than the contract price. Although an appropriation of an amount less than the contract price does not limit the authority of the contracting officer, it does limit the amount available for payment under the contract. While the contractor is entitled in such circumstances to recover judgment against the Government for the difference between the contract price and the amount actually paid pursuant to the appropriation, payment of the judgment would require an additional appropriation, dependent on the will of Congress. 

II. 

For a detailed discussion of budgetary and appropriation procedure, see 

Selko, The Federal Financial System (1940), ch. 4-10. 

13

Illustrative Case: 

The Postmaster General was given statutory authority to enter into contracts for the carrying of the mail for terms not in excess of four years. For the fiscal year 1914, Congress appropriated the sum of $7,105,000 for star-route mail service and for temporary service to the newly established post offices. This appropriation was insufficient to cover the cost of such service. Held: Since the Postmaster 

General is authorized by law to enter into contracts for the carrying of the mail, he may enter into a contract for star-route service during the fiscal year 1914 even though the contract price exceeds the amount of the appropriation.12 b. When the statute authorizes work and limits the cost. A contractor may not recover any amount in excess of that stipulated where the statute specifically authorizes the work but places a definite limitation on the cost to be incurred.

Illustrative Case: 

A statute provided for construction of a Government mint in San Francisco at a cost of not to exceed $300,000. A contract was made within the statutory limit. The contracting officer ordered extra work, which was done, bringing the aggregate cost to $306,609. 

Held: Contractor can recover only $300,000. The contractor could not bind the Government for any amount in excess of the statutory limit, and the contractor was bound to take notice of the statutory provisions.13

C. When the statute appropriates a stated amount without specific limitation. A statute may provide for particular work and appropriate a stated amount, without specifically limiting the cost of the project to the amount appropriated. In such a case, the amount of the appropriation is not a limitation, and a contract made pursuant to the statute is valid even if in excess of the money provided.

Congress may, of course, suspend or repeal a general authorization prior to its exercise, "by an amendment to an appropriation bill or otherwise." United States v. 

Dickerson, 310 U.S. 554, 555 (1940). An appropriation act will not be construed, however, as amending or repealing a substantive law unless such construction is necessary to carry out the clear legislative intention. See National Labor Relations Board v. Thompson Products,

Пlustrative Case: 

Congress authorized a commission to conduct experiments with steam boilers and appropriated $6,000 for the project. Claimant was employed at $300 per month. Held: Recovery allowed for total salary due, even though in excess of the amount of the appropriation. The contract was authorized by law and could continue until the work was performed.14 

15. CONTRACTS COVERED BY SPECIFIC APPROPRIATIONS. Where the sole authority for the contract is a statute appropriating a given sum for specific work,15 the rule is similar to that applicable where a general law authorizes the contract but the amount to be spent is limited. 

Illustrative Case: 

A statute appropriated $7,000 "for the purpose of constructing a macadamized road from the city of Vicksburg, Mississippi, to the national cemetery near that city." The contract provided for the payment of $7,000. Claimant sued for an additional amount for extras. Held: 

The contractor could recover only $7,000. "Where an alleged liability rests wholly upon the authority of an appropriation, they must stand and fall together, so that when the latter is exhausted the former is at an end, to be revived, if at all, only by subsequent legislation by Congress."16

In the illustrative case cited, the original contract did not exceed the appropriation, but the excess was caused by extras ordered during the progress of the work. Some question exists as to the status of a contract originally made in excess of the amount appropriated.17

16. CONTRACTS PURSUANT TO GENERAL APPROPRIATIONS. a. Duty to know condition of appropriation account. A general appropriation providing for a number of different projects places a burden upon the contracting officer not to spend in excess of the specified amount. However, persons contracting with the Government to perform only part of the work authorized under general appropriations are not bound to know the condition of the appropriation account at the Treasury or on the contract book of the department.18 

Illustrative Case: 

Acting under a general appropriation act, the Government made various improvements in Yellowstone National Park. Among the contracts let was one for construction of a bridge and culvert. The contractor was ordered later to suspend work because the appropriation had been exhausted. The contractor sued for damages for breach by the Government in suspending the work. Held: Recovery allowed. The contractor was not bound to know the condition of the appropriation account when it entered into the contract.19

Prior to the passage of either statute, where Congress had appropriated inadequate amounts for construction of drydocks by the Navy, the Attorney General held that authority to contract did not exist under an inadequate appropriation, but added: "The amount appropriated may be expended in purchasing the necessary sites, in purchasing and collecting the materials, with a view to the completion of the works under the future direction of Congress." 4 Op. Atty. Gen. 600 (1847). In a subsequent opinion, still prior to the first criminal statute, the Attorney General stated that a contract in excess of an appropriation is "utterly void, and without more validity than a piece of blank paper"; but he added that the contractor "can claim no more than the sum appropriated." 9 Op. b. Wartime rule. The use of general appropriations is desirable in war procurement since it reduces the risks of contractors and thereby assists in enabling them to execute contracts and proceed with production with a minimum of delay. War procurement is conducted almost exclusively pursuant to general appropriation acts. C. 

Availability of funds appropriated annually. Most appropriation acts are annual and provide funds for one fiscal year only. An annual appropriation is available only to pay obligations incurred during the fiscal year for which such appropriation was made. 20 Work covered by an annual appropriation can not in some cases be completed within the fiscal year. Where such work involves construction of public buildings, rivers and harbors, or lighthouses, the appropriation remains available for payment until completion of the work. 21 In other cases, however, where at the end of the fiscal year funds have been obligated but not paid out, it is provided by statute that such payment may be made not later than two years after the fiscal year has ended. Unexpended balances, in such cases, remaining on the books of the Treasury for two fiscal years must be covered into the general fund of the Treasury at the end of the second year. 22 Accordingly, an annual appropriation, except where for public buildings or other exempted categories, is available only to pay obligations incurred during the fiscal year for which it was made, and payment therefrom must be made during either that fiscal year or the next two fiscal years thereafter.23

Пlustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract for the cultivation and protection of rubber-bearing plants at a specified price per acre, to be paid for upon completion of the contractor's services. These services were to be performed during the crop year, which covered a portion of two fiscal years. Sums were available from either year's appropriation.

The Comptroller General held that payment should not be split between the appropriations. "The general rule is that the fiscal year appropriation current at the time the contract is made is chargeable with payments under the contract, although performance thereunder may extend into the ensuing fiscal year. "24 d. Effect of annual appropriations on leases. Government leases under an annual appropriation act may not extend beyond the fiscal year for which the appropriation is made. An appropriation by such an act of a sum for the rental of premises impliedly authorizes a lease for that fiscal year, but no longer. Not infrequently, leases have been entered into pursuant to such an annual appropriation act for a period longer than a year, with a proviso that if an appropriation is not made for any of the years covered by the lease, than the lease shall terminate automatically as of the last day of any fiscal year for which an appropriation was last available. In spite of the agreement in such case, the lease is not continued automatically for another year upon the making of a new appropriation. Unless the Government performs some affirmative act in addition to the appropriation indicating its desire for continuance of the lease beyond the current fiscal year, the lease will terminate as of the close of such year.

Illustrative Cases: 

(1) Pursuant to an annual appropriation act, the Government occupied premises under a five-year lease. Before an appropriation was passed for the second year, it notified the lessor that it would relinquish the premises at the end of that fiscal year. The lessor promptly denied the right of the Government to terminate the lease.

17. APPORTIONMÉNT, ALLOCATION AND ALLOTMENT OF FUNDS IN THE DEPARTMENT OF THE ARMY. a. The Anti-Deficiency Law. All general appropriations must be so apportioned within the departments by monthly or other allotments as to prevent deficiencies caused by too rapid. expenditures in the early part of the fiscal year. 27 Basically this requires budgetary control over expenditures. This is done in the 

Department of the Army by a system of apportionments, allocations and allotments of appropriated funds. b. Apportionment procedure. After a Department of the Army appropriation statute has been enacted, the Treasury Department prepares an appropriation warrant which is countersigned in the General Accounting Office. The effect of the appropriation warrant is to place the appropriated funds to the credit of the Department of the Army on the books of the 

Treasury Department and General Accounting Office. The appropriated funds are thus made available for obligation and expenditure. After approval of the appropriation warrant, the Budget Officer of the Department of the Army prepares a request for apportionment of funds, which is submitted through the Secretary of Defense to the Bureau of the Budget for approval.28 

25. Leiter v. United States, 271 U.S. 204 (1926). 

26. Freedman's Savings & Trust Co. v. United States, 16 Ct. Cl. 19 (1880). 

Cf. Goodyear Tire & Rubber Co. v. United States, 276 U.S. 287 (1928), in which the Government advised the lessor in advance that it would not pay rent for the coming year beyond the period during which it actually occupied the premises. Congress made a new annual appropriation, and the Government occupied the premises about six months into the new fiscal year. The Goverment was held liable for rent only for the period of actual occupancy, such occupancy not amounting to an affirmance of the lease under the circumstances. 

27. 34 Stat. 48, 49 (1906), 31 U.S.C. 665, known as the Anti-Deficiency Law. 28. 

E. O. No. 8512, 13 Aug 1940. 19

The scheduled rates of obligation must be approved by the Secretary of 

Defense.29 The apportionment, as approved by the Secretary of Defense and the Bureau of the Budget, forms the basis for the financial program of the Department of the Army.

C. Allocation and obligation of funds. The Budget Officer of the 

Department of the Army, upon receipt of the approved apportionment, designates a portion of the appropriated funds as a general reserve to meet contingencies. 

The balance of the appropriation is apportioned over the four quarters of the fiscal year. The Budget Officer then allocates the funds available during each quarter among the several technical services and other units within the Department. From their respective allocations, the chiefs of the technical services and the heads of other units issue allotments to their procurement districts or other purchasing units. The procurement districts may then enter into contracts or otherwise obligate funds up to the amount of their respective individual allotments.30 

Funds not obligated or spent during the quarter to which they have been apportioned are available for expenditure during subsequent quarters of the same fiscal year. a. 

18. CONTROL OVER DISBURSEMENTS - THE GENERAL ACCOUNTING OFFICE. 

Origin. Prior to enactment of the Budget and Accounting Act, 1921, 

Government expenditures were audited by accounting officers in the Treasury

Department. The Budget and Accounting Act created the General Accounting 

Office and transferred to it the functions previously exercised by the 

Treasury accounting officers.31 b. Appointment and functions of the Comptroller General. The 

Comptroller General is the head of the General Accounting Office and is assisted by the Assistant Comptroller General. The President appoints both, by and with the advice and consent of the Senate, for terms of fifteen years.32 They are removable only by joint resolution of Congress 

29. Section 404, Pub. Law. 216, 81st Cong. 30.

Contracting officers must submit all proposed contracts to the fiscal officer for verification as to sufficiency of funds in the allotment to cover the cost of the contract. WD PR 310.1. 

31. 42 Stat. 23, 24 (1921); 31 U.S.C. 41,44. 

32. 42 Stat. 23 (1921); 31 U.S.C. 42,43. 20

-- and not by the President.33 The Comptroller General has taken the position that he and the General Accounting Office are agents of Congress, responsible to and subject to the sole control of that body rather than the executive branch,34 and charged with the primary duty of insuring that appropriations by Congress are expended in strict accordance with its directions.35 The 

Supreme Court of the United States in a fairly recent decision, referring to the independence of the General Accounting Office of the Executive Establishment, used this language which may be viewed as obiter dictum "The chief change effected by the Budget and Accounting Act was that it transferred powers lodged with officers of the Treasury Department to the Comptroller General and made his office independent of the Executive Branch of the Government."36 The "Reorganization Act of 1949, "37 apparently confirming the view expressed by the Comptroller General and by the Supreme Court that that official is not a part of the Executive Establishment, Title I, Section 7 of that Act, under "definition of 'agency'" states - "such term does not include the Comptroller General of the United States or the General Accounting Office, which are a part of the Legislative Branch of the Government."

C. Functions of General Accounting Office. In accordance with this conception of its functions, the General Accounting Office is primarily interested in whether expenditures have been made in accordance with all applicable federal statutes, as those statutes are interpreted by the Comptroller General. For this purpose, the General Accounting Office exercises control over the making of expenditures by executive agencies of the Government. d. Accounting procedures. The exercise of such control by the 

General Accounting Office rests on the "accountable warrant" procedure, and the resulting accountability to the General Accounting Office of disbursing officers and statutory certifying officers appointed for the various executive agencies of the Government.38 In the Department of the Army, for example, individual disbursing officers require funds with which to pay vouchers for payments due contractors and for other purposes. To obtain these funds, they request advances of money from the Chief of Finance. 39 The Chief of Finance forwards to the Treasury Department requisitions for the sums requested. The Treasury Department draws an accountable warrant, which must be countersigned in the General Accounting Office. On the basis of the warrant, the Treasury Department places a portion or all of the funds appropriated to the Department to the credit of the disbursing officer, in accordance with the requisition. The disbursing officer then becomes personally accountable to the General Accounting Office for such funds,40 except as his responsibility for the proper expenditure thereof may have been shifted to a certifying officer under the act of 29 December 1941 (55 Stat. 875; 31 U.S.C. 82b-82c, as amended by the act of 28 April 1942, 56 Stat. 244; 31 U.S.C. 82f), cited in footnote 42.

When contractors become entitled to payment, vouchers for payment of the amounts due are presented to the disbursing officer. The latter, if he believes the payment to be authorized and proper, draws a check on the Treasurer of the United States and sends it to the contractor. 

The disbursing officer submits the vouchers and supporting papers to the 

Army Finance Center, St. Louis, Mo., which processes them and submits them for audit to field representatives of the General Accounting Office stationed in St. Louis.

If the General Accounting Office finds that the payment was proper, it simply files the documents involved. If it is in doubt, it normally takes an "exception" to the payment (sometimes referred to as "suspending" the item) pending receipt of further information. If it believes the payment to have been improper, it "disallows" the payment. In such case, 38. See Selko, The Federal Financial System (1940), pp. 467-479. 

39. Par. 3d, AR 35-5, 6 Jun 1930. 

40. See Selko, op.cit. 461. 22

steps may be taken to recover the amount of the payment from the contractor, either by refundment or by deducting it from other sums which may be due it, or by the institution of suit.41 If collection can not be made from the contractor, the amount of the payment may be charged back against the disbursing officer's account, 42thus placing on him responsibility for making good the amount due under his bond, or by collection from a statutory certifying officer under the act cited in footnote 42 below, if applicable. 

Congress may, by legislation, relieve him and his sureties of liability. 

The Comptroller General has authority to relieve a statutory certifying officer under the act cited in footnote 42 below, and of course the Congress may also relieve a certifying officer of his statutory liability. 

It is evident that under this procedure, disbursing officers are inclined to make only such payments as accord with prior rulings and 41. If the money is deducted from other sums due it, the contractor may challenge the validity of the deduction by bringing suit to recover the amount involved. Where no other sums are due the particular contractor, a suit against it can be instituted only by the Department of 

Justice. If the Attorney General disagrees with the Comptroller:

General's conclusion that the payment was improper, he may refuse to bring the suit. See Mansfield, The Comptroller General (1939), pp. 107-109.

States v. Heller, 1 F. Supp. 1 (D. Md. 1932). See also par. 5, AR 

35-180, 23 Dec. 1946. Such order would not, of course, be a defense available to the payee in a suit against him. Heidt v. United States, 56 F. 2d 559 (C.C.A. 5th 1932).

In most Government departments, there is also a burden upon the officers or employees within the executive department who certify vouchers. "The officer or employee certifying a voucher shall (1) be held responsible for the existence and correctness of the facts recited in the certificate or otherwise stated on the voucher or its supporting papers and for the legality of the proposed payment under the appropriation or fund involved; * * * and (3) be held accountable for and required to make good to the United States the amount of an illegal, improper, or incorrect payment resulting from any false, inaccurate, or misleading certificate made by him, as well as for any payment prohibited by law or which did not represent a legal obligation under the appropriation or fund involved: Provided, that the Comptroller General may, in his discretion, relieve such certifying officer or employee of liability * * *." 55 Stat. 875 (1941), 31

U.S.C. 82c. The foregoing provisions are applicable to the Department of the Army only with regard to certification of vouchers for payment of salaries and expenses within the District of Columbia. 55 Stat. 876 (1941), 31 U.S.C. 82e. As to the responsibility of Department of the 

Army certifying officers in cases not covered by the statute, see par. 4, AR 25-180, 23 Dec 1946. 23

decisions of the Comptroller General.43 Such rulings and decisions, which deal with hundreds of federal statutes, thus control, to a great extent, expenditures by the executive branch. e.

Conflicts of opinion as to propriety of expenditure of funds. 

Frequently conflicts arise between the Comptroller General and the various executive departments concerning the interpretation of statutes, and the resulting propriety or impropriety of payments which have been made or which the executive department desires to make. The head of any executive department is authorized by statute to "require the opinion of the Attorney General on any questions of law arising in the administration of his department. 144

The Comptroller General, however, does not accept opinions of the Attorney General as binding upon him.45 He likewise does not consider himself bound by rulings of the courts contrary to his own views, except for rulings of the Supreme Court of the United States.46 result, complicated situations sometimes arise. f.

CHAPTER L STATUTORY RESTRICTIONS ON GOVERNMENT CONTRACTING 19. PURCHASE OF LANDS. 

Section 3736, Revised Statutes: 

"Lands in general; restrictions on purchase.-- No land shall be purchased on account of the United States, except under a law authorizing such purchase." 

This statute denies an executive department the power to purchase land on account of the United States unless the intention of Congress that such power be exercised is expressly granted or necessarily implied.1 

Illustrative Cases: 

(1) An appropriation "for erecting a mound or sea wall along the peninsula which separates Lake Erie from Buffalo" creek was held to justify the purchase of the land necessary for construction. "The power to build a sea wall implies the power to do whatever is necessary to that end. "2 (2) An appropriation act for the prosecution of "projects involving construction and operation of migratory labor camps" held impliedly authorizes the purchase of land necessary for the sites of such camps.. 3

This section does not prohibit the acquisition directly by the United States of legal title to land when it is taken by way of security for a debt. This it may do as an incident to its right of sovereignty.4 

This statute prohibits exchanges of land in the absence of statutory authority.5 

1. Op. Atty. Gen. 3 Jun 1941. 

2. Burns v. United States, 160 Fed. 631 (1908). 

3. Op. Atty. Gen. 3 June 1941. See. 19 Op. Atty. Gen. 79 (1887) for the view that under this Act the authority to purchase land must be conferred by the express language of the statute and that no authority may be implied. 

4. Van Brocklin v. Tennessee, 117 U.S. 151 (1886); see Chapter 2. 

5. 36 Op. Atty. Gen. 183 (1927). 27

The word "purchase" (in this statute) is used in its non-technical sense, that is, land acquired for a consideration. Hence, its prohibition does not extend to land donated to the United States where the donation does not involve the expenditure of public money. 20.

SUPPLY CONTRACTS LIMITED TO YEAR.-R.S. 3752. 

"It shall not be lawful for any of the executive departments to make contracts for stationery or other supplies for a longer term than one year from the time the contract is made."

Illustrative Case: 

The Procurement Division, Treasury Department, agreed to purchase as much gear oil as it required (no definite quantity was required to be purchased) for a period of 15 months. The Comptroller General stated that the award of such a contract would clearly be in violation of R.S. 3735.7

This section is subject to R.S. 3679, which prohibits the executive departments from spending in any one fiscal year any sum in excess of the appropriation for that year. 8 

21. ACCEPTANCE OF VOLUNTARY SERVICES PROHIBITED.--R.S. 3679. 

" *** Nor shall any department or any officer of the

Government accept voluntary service for the Government or employ personal service in excess of that authorized by law, except in cases of sudden emergency involving the loss of human life or the destruction of property." 

This statute was designed to prevent deficiencies in appropriations from occurring as a result of claims for unauthorized services or extra services. Its prohibitions extended to two situations: (1) acceptance of "voluntary service", that is, where there is no prior contract, service intruded as a "volunteer"; (2) employment of personal services in excess of that authorized by law. The evil at which Congress was aiming was not appointment or employment for authorized services without compensation, but the acceptance of unauthorized services not intended or agreed to be gratuitous and therefore likely to afford a basis for a future claim on Congress.9 

6. 28 Op. Atty. Gen. 413 (1910); 19 Comp. Dec. 1 (1912). 

7. MS. Comp. Gen. A-60589, 12 Jul 1935. 

8. 12 Comp. Dec. 553 (1906). See Chapter 3.
