CHAPTER METHODS OF PROCUREMENT SECTION I. FORMAL ADVERTISING 22. INTRODUCTION. The "Armed Services Procurement Act of 1947″1 

united the Military Establishment under one general procurement law. 

This law became effective 19 May 1948, and set forth the general principle that all purchases and contracts for supplies and services shall be made by advertising. The passage of this law returned the Military Establishment to the long-established policy of procurement by advertising, which had been established in 1861 by R.S. 3709.2 The purpose of these Acts in requiring procurement by advertising was to prevent fraud or favoritism, afford equality of opportunity to all prospective Government contractors and secure for the Government the advantage of bona fide competition. 

R.S. 3709 provided: "All purchases and contracts for supplies or services in any of the departments of the Government, except for personal services, shall be made by advertising a sufficient time previously for proposals respecting the same, when the public exigencies do not require the immediate delivery of the articles or performance of the service. When immediate delivery or performance is required by the public exigency, the articles or service required may be procured by open purchase or contract at the places and in the manner in which such articles are usually bought and sold or such service engaged between individuals."

The above cited provisions of R.S. 3709 were found to be too restrictive in certain types of procurements and it was necessary for Congress to pass subsequent legislation which permitted negotiation in certain other types of procurement. 

In the Armed Services Procurement Act of 1947, Congress, although setting forth the broad general principle that procurement would be by advertising, has embodied in one act the policy and the numerous exceptions thereto which have been found over a period of years to be required in order to facilitate procurement and meet the needs of the Government. These exceptions to the Act will be discussed in detail in Section II of this chapter. 

I. Public Law 413, 80th Congress; 41 U.S.C. 151. 2. 

41 U.S.C. 5. 29 29

In carrying out the policy of procurement by advertising, the Armed Services Procurement Act provides that an award made as a result of advertising shall be made "to that responsible bidder whose bid, conforming to the Invitation for Bids, will be most advantageous to the Government, price and other factors considered". This provision is much broader than the like provision in R.S. 3709, which provides that the award would be made to the "lowest responsible bidder". Hence, Congress in the new procurement act has given a broader right of initiative on the part of procuring activities in determining to whom the award shall be made than that which was granted under R.S. 3709.

In considering the various phases of procurement by advertising, it might be well to discuss them briefly in chronological order. 

23. INVITATION FOR BIDS. The new procurement act provides that advertisement for bids shall be a sufficient time previous to the purchase or contract, and Specifications and Invitations for Bids shall permit such full and free competition as is consistent with the procurement of the types of supplies and services necessary to meet the requirements of the agency concerned. The Act does not prescribe the procedure to be followed nor the forms to be used in advertising for bids. Hence, it may be assumed that the establishment of such procedures and forms will be left to the procuring activity as it was under R.S. 3709. The peacetime procedure generally followed by the Department of the Army is to invite bids on the basis of specifications. The specification is a statement of the need of the Government and should be such as to permit bidders to compete on a common basis and not unduly restrict competition. Bids may be solicited in a number of ways and the method selected should be the one that will assure free and full competition. Some of the methods for soliciting bids are (a) mailing or delivering to prospective bidders, (b) displaying in public places, (c) publishing in trade journals, and (d) publishing in newspapers; or a combination of the above. The method or methods selected will depend on the nature of the procurement and the availability of known sources of supply. For example, procurement involving the purchase of large quantities of machinery, engines, and like major items, would require more extensive advertising than would the purchase of a small number of inexpensive items. In general, the methods of advertising selected should be such as to assure that reasonable publicity is given to the requirements of the Government in order to assure competition and hence obtain the best price under the circumstances. 

The method of soliciting bids is, in general, left to the discretion of the contracting officer with the exception that written authority must be obtained from the Secretary of the Army, or his duly authorized representative, if the method is to be by publishing in newspapers. The solicitation must be made in sufficient time to enable prospective bidders to prepare and submit their bids prior to the time set for opening. 30

24. BIDS. The Attorney General has held "that the authority to invite proposals implies an authority to prescribe reasonable terms and conditions".3 Hence, the responsibility is upon the bidder to fill out his bid in such a manner that it will conform to the terms and conditions of the Invitation for Bids and any instructions accompanying the appropriate bid form. However, any minor informality or irregularity in the bid may be waived by the Government, provided such a waiver does not give an undue advantage to the particular bidder. Bids must be submitted in sufficient time to reach the designated office prior to the time set for opening. Any bid received through the mail at the designated office after the time set for opening cannot be considered, unless the failure to arrive on time was due solely to a delay in the mails, for which the bidder was not responsible. Telegraphic bids will not be considered, unless authorized by the Invitation. However, bids may be modified by telegraphic notice provided such notice is received prior to the time set for the opening of the bids. Bids may be withdrawn at any time prior to the time set for the opening of the bids and no modification of a bid will be considered if it arrives after the time set for opening, unless the modification is in the interest of the Government and not prejudicial to other bidders. 

25. OPENING OF BIDS. Section 3(b) of the Armed Services Procurement 

Act provides that all bids shall be publicly opened at the time and place stated in the Invitation. This general statutory requirement has been implemented by regulations which require that all bids received prior to the time of opening will be kept secure and unopened until the time of opening, whereupon they shall be publicly opened. Hence, all bidders should be given the opportunity to be personally present at the time of opening. 

The regulations further provide that the names of the bidders and the prices bid shall be recorded and be available for public inspection. 

Any bid which does not conform to the essential requirements of the 

Invitation for Bids should be rejected, although such a bid may be considered when it would be clearly in the interest of the Government and not prejudicial to other bidders. Furthermore, all bids may be rejected by the contracting officer when rejection is in the interest of the Government, or when he finds that the bids are not reasonable, or were not independently arrived at in open competition, or are collusive, or were submitted in bad faith. As has been pointed out before, minor informalities or irregularities in a bid may be waived by the contracting officer when it is in the interest of the Government. Such errors do not substantially affect the terms of the bid or vary the terms of the Invitation. However, mistakes in bids require certain positive acts on the part of the contracting officer prior to any correction thereof. 

3. 13 Op. Atty. Gen. 510 (1871). 31

The general rule is that a unilateral mistake, that is, a mistake on the part of the bidder, is not grounds for permitting a withdrawal or correction of the bid. However, under certain circumstances, a mistake in bid may be corrected where it is in the interest of the Government and not prejudicial to other bidders. For example, any clerical mistake obvious or apparent on the face of the bid may be corrected by the contracting officer prior to award, provided there has first been obtained from the bidder, in response to a verification of the bid, a statement or explanation of the apparent clerical mistake. In the event of a suspected or alleged mistake in bid other than an obvious clerical mistake, the contracting officer shall obtain from the bidder prior to award, a verification of the bid or evidence in support of the mistake. Whereupon, the entire file on the matter should be processed to higher authority for decision as to whether or not the mistake can be rectified. However, the Armed Services 

Procurement Regulation provides that: "(a) if the bidder fails or refuses to furnish evidence in support of the mistake, the Contracting Officer shall consider the bid in the form submitted; or (b) if time does not permit processing in accordance with customary procedures, and if there is no room for doubt as to the price or other terms intended in the bid in which a mistake occurred, the Contracting Officer (i) in the case of a mistake in the lowest bid which as clearly intended would not be the lowest bid, may disregard such bid, or (ii) in the case of a mistake in the lowest bid which as clearly intended would still be the lowest bid, shall make the award on the basis of such low bid as originally submitted, but subject to correction if authorized by the General Accounting Office, of (iii) in the case of a mistake in any bid other than the lowest bid, shall consider such bid on the basis of its price or other terms as clearly intended." 

26. AWARD. Section 3(b) of the Armed Services Procurement Act provides that "Award shall be made with reasonable promptness by written notice to that responsible bidder whose bid, conforming to the Invitation for Bids, will be most advantageous to the Government, price and other factors considered."

Such an award may be effected by mailing or otherwise delivering to the bidder a properly executed award. What is a reasonable time for making the award will depend on the circumstances of the case, taking into consideration type of supplies or services being procured, place of delivery, etc. In most instances, the Invitation for Bid will state a definite period of time for making the award and an acceptance of the bid on the part of the Government after the expiration of that period is not acceptance within a reasonable time. The term "responsible bidder" has been defined by the courts and by the Comptroller General, 4. 17 Comp. Gen. 546 (1938). 

32

to include more than just a pecuniary ability. For example, judgment, skill and the capacity and integrity of the bidder have been included within the expression. Furthermore, the Armed Services Procurement Regulation requires that a bidder be a manufacturer or regular dealer in the supplies bid upon. 

The purpose of this restriction is to assure that the Government is dealing with a contractor who has a regularly established business for the manufacture of the items desired, or carries, or has the facilities to stock, the items desired. 

SECTION II. 

NEGOTIATION 

27.

INTRODUCTION. With the advent of World War II, it was necessary that Congress pass legislation to facilitate the procurement of the requirements of warfare. For this purpose, Congress enacted the act of 

18 December 1941, known as the "First War Powers Act of 1941".1 This empowered the President to authorize any Government agency exercising functions in connection with the war effort to enter into contracts, or modifications of contracts, without regard to previous laws relating to contracts, whenever such action would facilitate the prosecution of the war. Under the authority of this law, the President issued Executive Order 9001, which authorized the War Department to dispense with advertising and competitive bidding. This authority did not preclude obtaining informal bids or quotations when time permitted. With the return to the peacetime policy of procurement through advertising under the Armed Services Procurement Act of 1947,2 Congress also provided certain exceptions to that policy which were felt necessary and desirable in the interest of Government procurement. 

28. AUTHORITIES. There are seventeen exceptions listed in the 

Act which permit negotiation in the following circumstances: (a) When it is determined to be necessary in the public interest during the period of a national emergency declared by the President or by the Congress. This authority was designed to permit the immediate change from procurement by advertising to procurement by negotiation in the event the President or Congress declares a national emergency and hence obviate the necessity for Congressional action in the field of wartime procurement and avoid the delay incident thereto. (b) When the public exigency will not admit of the delay incident to advertising. This provision is similar to one in the act of 2 March 

19013 amending R.S. 3709, which added another exception to the peacetime policy of procurement by advertising set forth therein. The public exigency contemplated under this provision is one in which the delay in 

I. 55 Stat. 839; 50 U.S.C. 611. 

2. 41 U.S.c. 151. 

3. 31 Stat. 905; 10 U.S.C. 1201. 33

the procurement would seriously injure the Government. Such an exigency must be a sudden, unforeseeable occurrence or condition, requiring immediate action. Any occurrence or condition that could properly be anticipated does not constitute an exigency and may not be converted into an emergency condition merely by a belated decision to act thereon.4 

It is contemplated that this exception will be resorted to in the event of disasters such as floods, fires, etc., requiring immediate action in order to protect the welfare of the country. (c) When the aggregate amount does not exceed $1,000. This authority is similar to that authorized by Congress, as an exception to R.S. 3709 in small purchases, in amounts less than $500. Congress appreciated the purchasing power of the modern dollar in increasing under the new Act the amount involved in small purchases to $1,000. The National Military 

Establishment has implemented this exception by agreeing that it will be used in preference to any other of the exceptions when the amount of money involved in the procurement does not exceed $1,000. (d) For personal or professional services. R.S. 3709 specifically exempts personal services from the requirement that supplies or services shall be made by advertising. However, an amendment to R.S. 3709 in 1946,6 limited this exception to the performance of personal services by an individual contractor in person and the service must be of a technical nature or performed under Government supervision and paid for on a time basis. Although this exception is broader than that contained in R.S. 3709, in that it includes professional services as well as personal services, it is felt that the restriction imposed by the 1946 amendment - namely, "When the services are required to be performed by the contractor in person", would not prevent a firm from performing the professional service. In addition, the Armed Services Procurement Regulation has imposed an additional condition that the authority shall be used only when the procurement of the services is authorized by law and is effected in accordance with the requirements of any such law. (e) For any service to be rendered by any university, college or educational institution. No similar exception existed under R.S. 3709 as amended and it was included in the Armed Services Procurement Act of 1947 at the request of the Services, who realized its advantage in negotiating contracts for the training of personnel in educational institutions and for research work. The Armed Services Procurement regulation illustrates certain circumstances under which this authority may be used: 

4. 14 Comp. Gen. 875 (1935). 

5. 34 Stat. 258; 10 U.S.c. 1205. 

6. 60 Stat. 809; 41 U.S.c. 5. 34

(1) Educational or vocational training services to be rendered by any university, college, or other educational institution in connection with the training and education of personnel, and for necessary material, services, and supplies furnished by any such institution in connection therewith; (2) Experimental, developmental, or research work (including services, tests, and reports necessary or incidental the reto) to be conducted by any university, college, or other educational institution, and reports furnished in connection therewith; (3) Analyses, studies, or reports (statistical or otherwise) to be conducted by any university, college, or other educational institution. (f) When services or supplies are to be procured and used outside the limits of the United States and its possessions. No similar exception existed under R.S. 3709 in the Department of the Army. However, such an exception did exist in the Department of the Navy and has been reasserted in the new procurement act and extended to the Air Force and the Army. It has been interpreted by the Armed Services that the expression "United States and its possessions" would necessarily include territories, and hence the authority under this Act will be used only when the supplies or services are actually purchased and used outside the limits of the United States, its territories and possessions (irrespective of the actual place of negotiation or execution of the contract). (g) For medicines er medical supplies. A similar exception was expressly granted by Congress to R.S. 3709 with respect to the purchase of medicines and medical supplies by the Medical Department of the Army. This exception, set forth in the Armed Services Procurement Act of 1947, is identical to that exemption from R.S. 3709 and extends the exception to the Armed Services. The Armed Services Procurement 

Regulation provides that the authority shall be used only when the following two requirements have been satisfied: (1) such supplies are peculiar to the field of medicine, and include technical equipment such as surgical instruments, surgical and orthopedic appliances, X-ray supplies and equipment, and the like, but do not include prosthetic equipment; and (2) whenever the probable cost of medicines or medical supplies purchased by negotiation under this paragraph will exceed $10,000, suitable advance publicity of the proposed purchase shall be given, with the form and extent of such 

7. 34 Stat. 258; 10 U.S.C. 1205. 35

publicity (which shall be given for a period of at least 15 days wherever practicable) to be determined in accordance with procedures prescribed by each respective Department. This ex- (h) For supplies purchased for authorized resale. ception, which permits the purchase of articles with brand names or of a proprietary nature, has the effect of combining two exceptions to R.S. 3709, namely, the act of 12 February 18950 which granted the exception in the case of subsistence stores, and the act of 2 March 19019 which granted this exception to the Army where competition is impracticable. As in the case of negotiation without advertising for medicines or medical supplies, the Armed Services Procurement Regula tion has imposed similar conditions upon such purchases as follows: 

This authority shall be used only for purchases for resale, where appropriated funds are involved, and ordinarily only for purchases of articles with brand names or of a proprietary nature as required by patrons of the selling activity. 

In any event, however, when the probable cost of such supplies will exceed $10,000, suitable advance publicity of the proposed purchase will be given, with the form and extent of such publicity (which shall be given for a period of at least 

15 days wherever practicable) to be determined in accordance with procedures prescribed by each respective Department. (i) For perishable subsistence supplies. No previous exception existed to R.S. 3709 in the Army, although the exception "Competition 

Impracticable" could possibly be used for the purchase of subsistence supplies on the theory that to advertise for items, whose use and value deteriorate rapidly and whose cost fluctuates considerably within a short period of time, would be unduly costly and wasteful to the Government. This exception will permit the Services to purchase by negotiation items difficult to describe by means of specifications and whose value or usefulness to the Government depends on their immediate condition. (j) For supplies or services for which it is impracticable to secure competition. This exception, which existed under R.S. 3709, as amended for the Department of the Army as pointed out above, is very broad and its use should be subject to close supervision by the procuring agency. In view of this, the Armed Services Procurement 

Regulation has set forth illustrative circumstances with respect to which the authority may be used, and has placed limitation upon the use of this authority as follows: 

8. 28 Stat. 658; 10 U.S.C. 1197. 

9. 31 Stat. 905; 10 U.S.C. 1201. 36

(1) when the supplies or services can be obtained from only one person or firm (sole source of supply); (2) when competition is precluded because of the existence of patent rights, copyrights, secret processes, control of basic raw material, or similar circumstances; (3) when bids have been solicited pursuant to the requirements of Section II, and no responsive bid (a responsive bid is any bid which conforms to the essential requirements of the solicitation of bids) has been received from a responsible bidder; (4) when bids have been solicited pursuant to the requirements of Section II, and the responsive bid or bids do not cover the quantitative requirements of the solicitation of bids, in which case negotiation is permitted for the remaining requirements of the solicitation of bids; (5) when the contemplated procurement is for electric power or energy, gas (natural or manufactured), water, or other utility services; (6) when the contemplated procurement is for training film, motion picture productions, or manuscripts; (7) when the contemplated procurement is for technical, non-personal services in connection with the assembly, installation, or servicing (or the instruction of personnel therein) of equipment of a highly technical or specialized nature; (8) when the contemplated procurement is for studies or surveys covering industrial mobilization planning; (9) when the contemplated procurement involves maintenance, repair, alterations or inspection, in connection with any one of which types of services the exact nature or amount of the work to be done is not known; (10) when the contemplated procurement is for stevedoring, terminal, warehousing, or switching services, and when either the rates are established by law or regulation or the rates are so numerous or complex that it is impracticable to set them forth in the specifications of a formal solicitation of bids; (11) when the contemplated procurement is for commercial ocean or air transportation, including time charters, space charters and voyage charters over trade routes not covered by common carriers (as to which, negotiation is authorized under the provisions of paragraph 3-217 and Section 321 of Part III of the Interstate 

Commerce Act of 18 September 1940, 49 U.S.C. 65), and including services for the operation of Government-owned vessels or aircraft; 37

(12) when the contract is for services related to the procurement of perishable subsistence such as protective storage, icing, processing, packaging, handling and transportation whenever it is impracticable to advertise for such services a sufficient in advance of the delivery of the perishable subsistence; (13) when it is impossible to draft, for a solicitation of bids, adequate specifications or any other adequately detailed description of the required supplies or services. 

The authority of this paragraph shall not be used when negotiation is authorized by the provisions of paragraphs 3-211, 3-212, 3-213, 3-214, 3-215, or 3-216. Every contract that is negotiated under the authority of this paragraph 3-210 shall be accompanied by a signed statement of the Contracting Officer justifying its use, and a copy of such statement shall be sent to the General Accounting Office with a copy of the contract negotiated and executed hereunder. (k) If the agency head determines that the purchase or contract is for experimental, developmental or research work, or for the manufacture or furnishing of supplies for experimentation, development, research or test. As a result of the tremendous advances made in the field of science and technology during the period prior to World War II, and especially during World War II and thereafter, it was felt by Congress that such work was important to the Military Establishment and should be encouraged for the general welfare of the country. It was also appreciated that from the very nature of the work in this field, it would be impracticable to solicit bids. In many types of research and development work, the number of interested contractors is very limited. In addition, the very nature of the work, the special skills, research equipment and facilities are of the utmost importance and hence the contract must be entered into with that contractor possessing these requirements. Furthermore, in advertised contracts the cost must be determinable, but in many research and development contracts it is impossible beforehand to determine or estimate the cost. Therefore, cost reimbursement type contracts would be the most advantageous to the Government and such a type contract can only be entered into through negotiation. The Armed Services 

Procurement Regulation sets forth the following illustrative examples of the proper application of this authority: (1) contracts relating to theoretical analysis, exploratory studies, and experimentation in any field of science or technology; (2) developmental contracts calling for the practical application of investigative findings and theories of a scientific or technical nature; 38

(3) the purchase of such quantities and kinds of equipment, supplies, parts, accessories, or patent rights thereto, and drawings or designs thereof, as are necessary for experimentation, development, research, or test; (4) services, tests, and reports necessary or incidental to experimental, developmental, or research work.

This general authority to negotiate for experimental, developmental or research work was specifically implemented by Congress in the Act as follows: 

"Provided, That beginning six months after the effective date of this Act and at the end of each six-month period thereafter, there shall be furnished to the Congress a report setting forth the name of each contractor with whom a contract has been entered into pursuant to this subsection (11) since the date of the last such report, the amount of the contract, and, with due consideration given to the national security, a description of the work required to be performed thereunder."

This emphasized the importance that Congress placed upon this work and its desire to be kept informed, as far as possible, of all matters relative thereto. It might be well to point out at this time that an administrative limitation on the authority to negotiate at first appears in this authority and in the remaining six authorities to be discussed hereunder. 

As you will note above, it is required that the agency head must detemine that the purchase or contract is for experimental, developmental or research work. This is further amplified under the Act, which provides that the power of the agency head to make the determination or decisions specified in Paragraphs (1), (m), (n), (o), and (p) below shall not be delegable and the power to make the determination specified in this Paragraph (k) shall be delegable only to a chief officer responsible for procurement and only with respect to contracts which will not require the expenditure of more than $25,000. This limitation is not imposed upon the authority to negotiate contained in the first ten exceptions, a to j, set forth above. Hence, they are delegable and under the Armed Services Procurement 

Regulation have been delegated to the contracting officer with certain requirements imposed upon him. (1). For supplies or services as to which the agency head determines that the character, ingredients, or components thereof are such that the purchase or contract should not be publicly disclosed. This provision expands a previous exception that existed under R.S. 3709 as amended by the act of 13 July 193910 with respect to aircraft and appurtenances. The desirability of this exception is quite obvious 

10. 53 Stat. 1000; 10 U.S.C. 313. 39

and was appropriately inserted in the act in order to give to the Armed Services the discretion to determine what procurement matters should be guarded to protect the Government interests. The Armed Services Procurement Regulation has implemented this by providing that this authority will be used only for such purchases or contracts as are classified confidential or higher. (m) For technical equipment requiring standardization and interchangeability of parts. This exception did not exist under R.S. 3709, but Congress felt that it was important, from both a financial point of view and a military point of view, that the armed services be permitted to purchase additional units and replacement items of technical equipment and spare parts which have been standardized. This authority would be appropriate when it is desired by the armed services to limit the quantity of spare parts that must be carried in stock, or to make possible by such standardization the availability of parts that may be rapidly interchanged among equipment during combat or other emergency. 

For example, even though technical equipment may be available from more than one supplier, test and experience may have shown that varying performance characteristics would prevent interchangeability of parts, hence it might be extremely important, during a war or other emergency, to have available for interchanging, parts from one supplier that have been tested and proven to give the highest performance. The armed services have set certain limitations upon the use of this authority as follows: 

(1) it shall not be used for initial procurements of equipment and spare parts which will ultimately be standardized, (2) nor for the purpose of selecting arbitrarily the equipment of certain suppliers, (3) nor shall it be used unless and until the Secretary of a department has determined (i) the supplies constitute technical equipment or component parts thereof, (ii) negotiation is necessary in order to assure standardization of the equipment and interchangeability of parts, and (iii) such standardization and interchangeability is necessary in the public interest. (n) Technical or specialized supplies requiring substantial initial investment or extended period of preparation for manufacture. 

No similar exception existed under R.S. 3709. It is the result of the vast strides made in the past years in technical and scientific equipment, and particularly from a consideration of the time, material and work that contractors have devoted to the development of such equipment. This authority may be used when the Secretary determines that advertising and competitive bidding may require a duplication of investment or preparation already made, or will unduly delay procurement of such supplies. The types of technical or specialized supplies contemplated under this exception are aircraft, tanks, radar, guided missiles, rockets and similar types of equipment or major components 40

thereof, and any supplies of a technical or specialized nature which may be necessary for the use or operation of any such equipment. Such pro- curement generally involves: (1) high starting costs which already have been paid for by the Government or by the supplier; (2) preliminary engineering and development work that would not be useful to or usable by any other supplier; (3) elaborate special tooling already acquired; (4) substantial time and effort already expended in developing a prototype or an initial production model; and (5) important design changes which will continue to be developed by the supplier.

Such procurement will generally be made with the supplier who has developed the equipment and thereby either assures to the Government the benefit of the techniques, tooling and equipment already acquired by that supplier, or avoid undue delay that would arise if the equipment was purchased from a new supplier who had to acquire such techniques, tooling and equipment. (o) Negotiation after advertising. No exception similar to this existed under R.S. 3709 but its value is realized for the protection it will afford the Government where solicitation for bids is made and the responses thereto, or other means, show collusion on the part of the bidders, or the bids are excessive. The authority of the contracting officer to reject all bids when such rejection is in the interest of the Government is well established. However, this exception, which authorizes negotiation after advertising when it is determined that the bid prices are not reasonable or have not been independently arrived at in open competition, has been interpreted by the Comptroller General and the Armed Services to permit the rejection of some of the bids and the consideration of others. In other words, the statutory language "rejection of all bids" has been interpreted to mean "the rejection of all unreasonable bids." The act itself places certain limitations upon the application of this exception by providing that no negotiated purchase or contract may be entered into under this paragraph after the rejection of all bids received, unless: (1) notification of intention to negotiate and reasonable opportunity to negotiate shall have been given by the agency head to each responsible bidder, (2) the negotiated price is lower than the lowest rejected bid price of a responsible bidder, as determined by the agency head, and (3) such negotiated price is the lowest negotiated price offered by any responsible supplier. 41

Every contracting officer has the primary duty of protecting the Government's interests and this exception will permit him to reject unreasonable prices and protect the Government against frauds. In addition, the Armed Services Procurement Regulation places a duty upon each department of the Military Establishment to submit any evidence of bids not independently arrived at to the Department of Justice for possible criminal prosecution under Antitrust laws. (p) Purchases made in the interest of national defense or industrial mobilization. No similar exception existed under R.S. 3709, although the 

Department of the Army had been authorized, with the approval of the President, to place a contract for munitions with certain manufacturers for the purpose of familiarizing them with the manufacturing of munitions. 

From the wealth of experience gained from the mobilization in World War II, Congress appreciated the value to the Government of keeping plants, facilities or manufacturers available for furnishing supplies or services in the event of a national emergency and thus save valuable time in obtaining services and supplies essential during such an emergency. the authority should be used to provide incentives to manufacturers to maintain and keep alive engineering and designing staffs and manufacturing facilities for mass production. The following are illustrative of circumstances with respect to which this authority may be used: (1) procurement by negotiation is necessary to keep vital facilities or suppliers in business; or to make them available in the event of a national emergency; Hence (2) procurement by negotiation with selected suppliers is necessary in order to train them in the furnishing of critical supplies to prevent the loss of their ability and employee skills, or to maintain active engineering, research, or development work; (3) procurement by negotiation is necessary to maintain properly balanced sources of supply for meeting the requirements of procurement programs in the interest of industrial mobilization. 

A similar provision is contained in this exception as was found in exception 2(c)(11). Congress calls for reports every six months, requiring the Department to furnish information that will keep Congress informed as far as possible of all procurement relating to the exception contained herein. 

(q) Otherwise authorized by law. Section 10 of the Act speci fically repeals certain acts relating to procurement by the Armed Services. 

In addition, the Act provides that certain acts shall not apply to the procurement of supplies or services by the Armed Services. One of these is R.S. 3709 amended. It further provides that parts of certain acts are repealed. It then sets forth the common provision that all other laws and parts of laws, to the extent that they are inconsistent with 42

this Act, are hereby repealed. Hence procurement by negotiation is permitted if authorized under an existing law not inconsistent with the 

Act. 

We may summarize this brief discussion of procurement by negotiation by reasserting the importance of the Armed Services Procurement Act to peacetime procurement. It is the Act under which the Armed Services have authority to procure services and supplies under the conditions set forth therein. Several other acts pertaining to certain types of procurement are still in effect. There are very few of these and a discussion of them is not believed appropriate in this text. 

SECTION III COORDINATED PROCUREMENT 

29. a. General. Although coordinated procurement existed to a small degree under R.S. 3709 as amended, it was not until the passage of the National Security Act of 19471 and the Armed Services Procurement 

Act of 19472 that coordinated procurement became an important consideration in the procurement of services and supplies by the Armed Forces. 

In order to facilitate procurement of services and supplies by each department for the other departments, and the joint procurement of services and supplies required by such departments, the head of each department may make such assignments and delegations of procurement responsibilities within his department as he may deem necessary or desirable, and the heads of the departments may make such assignments and delegations of procurement responsibility from one department to another and may create such joint and combined offices to exercise such procurement responsibility as may be deemed desirable or necessary. 

There are three different types of coordinated procurement: (a) "single department procurement", whereby one department purchases certain supplies or services for another department, (b) "joint procurement", whereby a jointly staffed and financed procuring activity purchases certain supplies or services for the three departments, and (c) "collaboration procurement", whereby procuring activities of more than one department occupy offices in the same area but make separate contracts, the objective being to center in one place geographically all procurement of similar supplies or services. A brief consideration of these three types of coordinated procurement will illustrate the advantages to be derived from this type of procurement. 

1. Pub. Law 253, 80th Cong. 2. 

Pub. Law 413, 80th Cong. 43

b. Single department procurement. This is established either by agreement between the departments concerned, or assignment of procurement responsibility by the Munitions Board, which in general is charged with coordinating the procurement activities of the Military Establishment. 

The purchasing department, that is, the department making the purchase for another department, will generally enter into separate contracts for the supplies or services being procured. However, when economy or more efficient procurement would result, the purchasing department may combine in a single contract the requirements of all the departments, with the quantities of each item for each department being shown separately and being clearly identified as to the proper appropriation or fund to be charged. The procurement shall be in accordance with the provisions of the Armed Services Procurement Regulation and the procedures prescribed by the purchasing department. Of course, the specifications or a description of the items desired would be furnished by the department which initiated the request, together with such details as time, place and method of delivery; shipping and packing instructions; payment instructions; and certification of funds by a fiscal office. This type of procurement has a number of advantages. For one, it will result in a larger single purchase, in that it will be one purchase rather than three purchases which might be made by the three departments of the Military Establishment if all were buying the same item, thus probably obtaining a better price due to the single purchase of a large quantity. In addition, it may result in more efficient procurement in that one department concerns itself with the procurement of a particular item and familiarizes itself with the entire needs of the Military Establishment and hence becomes a specialist in that particular item, better acquainted with the market and the sources of supply than would exist if the three departments were separately concerning themselves with those matters. Furthermore, administrative economy is effected by a reduction in the personnel necessary to handle the single procurement.

C. Joint procurement. As pointed out above, this is procurement by an agency staffed and financed by the three departments. By means of this type of procurement, the agency so established is enabled to consolidate the requirements of the three departments, supervise the administration and performance of contracts, consolidate transportation requirements and coordinate joint storage by the three departments. 

As in "single department procurement", economy and more efficient procurement results in such coordination by the administration and supervision of such procurement by a smaller staff, the full utilization of storage spaces and the consolidation of transportation requirements. Examples of joint procurement agencies are the Armed Services Medical Procurement Agency, which procurs medicines and medical, surgical, hospital, dental and veterinary supplies for the three Services and the Armed Services Petroleum Purchasing Agency which performs similar functions with respect to the Petroleum needs of the three Services. 44

d. 

Collaboration procurement. The third type of coordinated procurement is, in effect, separate procurement, but because of the location of the procuring offices in the same area, the departments are able to maintain close relations with each other and collaborate in their procurement. This has the advantage of keeping all three departments well informed of each others procurement of similar supplies or services and enables them to readily exchange information with respect thereto, concerning sources of supply and current prices. 45

CHAPTER IMPLIED CONTRACTS SECTION I. NATURE OF IMPLIED CONTRACTS 

30. General. In addition to express contracts, the type usually entered into by the Government and contractors in which the contracting parties expressly state the terms of their bargain, contract law also recognizes two classes of implied contracts: (1) contracts implied in fact, and (2) contracts implied in law. 

Contracts implied in fact result from the mutual assent of the parties evidenced by their conduct or action taken rather than by a writing or agreement otherwise expressed in words. These obligations are founded upon the fundamental principle that no one ought unjustly to enrich himself at the expense of another.1 

There is no effectual difference between express agreements and agreements implied in fact, the only distinction being the method of proof in establishing them. In each there must be a meeting of the minds.2 

Certain obligations which arise neither from tort nor from contract, but which are imposed or created by law without the assent of the party bound are termed contracts implied in law or quasi contracts.3 

2. 3. 

2 Harv. L. Rev. 64; Ann. Cases 1913A 471; Clark on Contracts (4th Ed., 1931) 693.

Bigby v. United States, 188 U.S. 400 (1903); Coleman v. United States, 

"A quasi contract arises where the law imposes a duty upon a person, not because of any express or implied promise on his part to perform it, but even in spite of any intention he might have to the contrary. 

A quasi contract, which is a fictional contract, is not to be confused with a contract implied in fact, which is an actual contract, and which arises where the parties agree upon the obligations to be incurred, but their intention, instead of being expressed in words, is inferred from their acts in the light of the surrounding circumstances." Cameron v. Eynon, 332 Pa. 529, 532 (1939), quoted with approval in American La 

France F. E. Co. v. Borough of Shenandoah, 115 F. 2d 866, 867 (C.C.A. 

3d 1940). See 1 Williston on Contracts (Rev. Ed. 1936), sec. 3. It has been stated that quasi contracts are not within the constitutional prohibition against enactment by the states of laws impairing the obligation of contracts. Crane v. Hahlo, 258 U.S. 142 (1922). 

40

These obligations are not true contractual obligations, for agreement is lacking, but they are clothed with the semblance of contract for the purpose of remedy.4

Suits founded upon contract with the United States, if the contract is not an express one, must be implied in fact and not implied in law. 

The Government has consented to be sued on "any contract, express or implied. 116 This consent, however, has been construed by the courts to extend only to suits or contracts implied in fact but not to actions based on contracts implied in law.7 

Illustrative Cases: 

a.

Plaintiff sued another private party in a United States District Court and was required by court order to deposit a given sum in the registry of the court pending the outcome of the suit. 

The money was deposited by the clerk of the court in a national bank designated as a court depository. It remained there about two years, at the end of which time it was ordered returned to plaintiff. In accordance with a regulation promulgated by the 

Secretary of the Treasury, the bank had paid the interest on the deposit to the Treasurer of the United States. Plaintiff sued the 

Goverment to recover the interest accumulations, claiming that

True, 53 N.H. 627 (1873); Montgomery v. Waterworks Co., 77 Ala. 248 (1884); Schaeffer v. Miller, 41 Mont. 417, 109 P. 970, 137 Am. St. 

Rep. 746 (1910). The term "constructive contract" is also used to describe such obligations created by law. Hertzog v. Hertzog, 29 Pa. 465 (1857), Throckmorton, Cas. Contracts (2d Ed.) 5. 5.

An implied contract, under the Tucker Act, may be sued upon such as where the Government received money illegally into the Treasury. Chief 

Justice Whaley stated in Kirkendall v. United States, 90 Ct. Cl. 606, 

614 (1940): "The United States is required to be honest with its citizens just as much as its citizens are required to exercise common honesty with their Government." See also, McGuire, Implied Contracts with the United States, 57 Am. L. Rev. 57 (1923). 47

8. the regulation and the payment of the interest to the Government were improper. Held: Recovery denied. Even though payment of the interest to the Goverment may have been improper, the Government had never agreed to repay it to plaintiff. "An implied contract, in order to give the Court of Claims or a district court under the Tucker Act jurisdiction to give judgment against the Government, must be one implied in fact and not one based merely on equitable considerations and implied in law."8 b. The Government, under authority of an annual appropriation, entered into a lease, as lessee, for the period from October 1921 to 30 June 1926. The lease contained a provision that it should terminate at the end of any fiscal year if an appropriation were not made for the following fiscal year. An appropriation was made for the fiscal year beginning 1 July 1923, but the Government notified the lessor that it would surrender the premises on 30 June 1923. 

The Government held over beyond 30 June 1923, but notified the lessor that it would pay rent only for the period of actual occupancy. 

The lessor contended that the Government was liable for rent for the entire fiscal year ending 30 June 1924. The lessor's contention was based on the existence of an appropriation and on a state law making hold-over tenants liable for a year's rent. Held: The 

Government was liable only for rent for the period of actual occupancy. The appropriation did not of itself bind the Government for another year. State law as to hold-over tenancies was inapplicable to the case. In addition, this was a claim for recovery on a contract implied in law and therefore could not be sustained.9

C. The Government was sued for waste committed by it during occupancy of certain premises under a lease. The lease contained no express covenant against waste. Held: The Government is liable for damage resulting from the acts of its employees, except for that caused by reasonable use of the premises. A contract not to commit waste is implied in fact between the parties.10

SECTION II. REQUISITES TO RECOVERY ON CONTRACTS IMPLIED IN FACT. 

31. General. Contracts which are implied in fact embrace obligations arising out of mutual agreement and intent to promise which are tacit rather than expressed. To recover from the Government on such a contract it is necessary to prove that: (1) the Government received a benefit, (2) authority existed to make an express contract involving the subject matter of the contract sought to be implied, and (3) the benefit was received for the Goverment by an authorized officer. 

Notwithstanding provisions of law requiring certain governmental contracts to be in writing, such as those with Army, Navy, or Interior, yet if a parol contract is wholly or partially performed, recovery can be had upon quantum meruit. Where written approval is necessary for extra work and the contractor performs without securing such approval, he cannot recover upon quantum meruit while the original contract remains in force.12 Where work performed by the contractor is not provided for by contract but is ordered to be done and no compensation is agreed upon, recovery can be had by the contractor upon the basis of quantum meruit.13

In an action based upon quantum meruit for work performed over and above that required by express contract, authority of the Government officer to act must be shown before implication to pay can arise. Where a contract is not signed by both parties as required by Section 3744 of the Revised Statutes, 15 and the contractor has supplied and the Government has accepted and used the materials, the contractor is limited in his recovery upon quantum valebat.16 

In the absence of acceptance by the Government of something of value for which it can be held liable as upon quantum meruit, there must be, in order to constitute a contract, a writing or writings signed by the parties.17 If an action is based upon express contract there is no basis for recovery upon quantum meruit.18 It is clear that there can be no recovery in quantum meruit as upon an implied contract for extra

services rendered, or expenses incurred, or performance when such. services or expenses were primarily for the benefit of the contractor, or were not ordered or required by the contract.

Illustrative Cases: 

19 

a. During the First World War, the Tidewater Coal Exchange was organized to expedite the transfer of coal from cars to ships at certain ports. The Fuel Administrator, a Government official, directed that all coal for trans-shipment at ports involved should be consigned to the Exchange. All coal was pooled at such ports and each shipper credited with an amount of coal equal to its shipments, with the right to withdraw from the pool the amount so credited. 

The plaintiff shipped coal to the Exchange on several occasions between November 1917, and December 1918, but could not withdraw it until 5 December 1918 because its shipments had not been properly credited by the Exchange. The price of coal declined during the period that plaintiff was erroneously prevented from withdrawing coal. Plaintiff sought to recover the resulting loss on implied contract, alleging that the Exchange was in effect an agency of the Government. Held: Recovery denied. There was nothing to show that the Government intended to appropriate for its own use the coal withheld or that it received any benefit from the withholding. 

Accordingly, no contract to reimburse plaintiff could be implied. b. Under a limited appropriation, the Government entered into a contract obligating the contractor to perform certain dredging and excavation. In reliance upon erroneous estimates of a Government inspector, the contractor performed more work than the appropriation would pay for at the specified unit rate. When the mistake was discovered, the contractor was ordered to cease work.

The contractor sued to recover for all work performed at the agreed unit rate. Held: The contractor may recover only the amount of the appropriation. **** since no official of the

Government could have rendered it liable for this work by an express contract, none can by his acts or omissions create a valid contract implied in fact. "21

C. 

Plaintiff, an expert in ship construction, performed certain services for the Government. There was no express agreement concerning his compensation, but both he and the Government agents under whose direction he rendered the services contemplated that he would be paid. These agents had authority to contract for services of the type involved. Held: Plaintiff is entitled to recover on an implied contract to pay the reasonable value of his services.22

The question of recovery under implied contracts frequently arises in connection with two types of situations: (1) where supplies or services have been furnished under defective express contracts, and (2) where private property has been appropriated by the Government for a public use. 

32. RECOVERY IN IMPLIED CONTRACT WHERE EXPRESS CONTRACT IS DEFECTIVE. 

The contractor may recover on an implied contract to pay reasonable value for supplies or services received by the Government under an authorized express contract which fails to comply with prescribed statutory formalities, such as advertising or reduction in writing.23 

A contractor may not recover even for supplies delivered or services rendered if the express contract was unauthorized and was never approved or ratified. 24

Burchiel v. United States,4 Ct. Cl. 549 (1868). Recovery may be had only to the extent the contract is executed. In Emery v. United 

States, 13 F. 2d 658, 660 (D. Conn. 1926), the court stated: "It is true that, on an executory contract required to be in writing by section 

3744, and which does not comply with its requirements, no recovery may be had against the Government for its failure to perform. *** Where, however, services are rendered or material furnished under an oral contract, and the services or articles are accepted by the Government and inure to the benefit of the Government, recovery may be had upon a quantum meruit." 

See Filor v. United States, 9 Wall. (76 U.S.) 45 (1869); Pan-American 

Petroleum Co. v. United States, 9 F.2d 761 (C.C.A. 9th 1926), aff'd., 273 U.S. 453 (1927). Receipt and acceptance by an authorized officer of the Government with knowledge of the facts may constitute approval or ratification. 

51

Illustrative Cases: 

a. The Government, without first advertising for bids as required by law, entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to develop, manufacture, and deliver certain airplanes for a price of $744,311.46. The contractor delivered the planes in accordance with the contract. Held: Plaintiff is not entitled to the contract price of the planes, but may recover on an implied contract to pay their reasonable value (which the court found was $665,029.72).25 b. The Government entered into a written contract under which it agreed to pay plaintiff $5,000 for an experimental four-inch naval gun. By a supplemental agreement, which was not written and signed in the manner prescribed by statute, an additional $969.64. Plaintiff may recover the reasonable value the extra work, in addition to the original contract price.

The 33. RECOVERY UNDER CONTRACT SETTLEMENT ACT. a. General. 

Contract Settlement Act27 deals primarily with the termination of war contracts by the Government at its option or for its convenience. 

Section 17 of that Act, however, is not limited to termination claims, but applies to war contracts generally. Section 17 provides for relief to war contractors20 furnishing or arranging to furnish war materials (1) where no formal contract exists, and (2) where an existing contract is defective. b.

Relief where no contract exists. The act provides for relief to war contractors furnishing or arranging to furnish to the Government or to other war contractors materials, services or facilities related to the prosecution of the war, without a formal29 contract, in reliance 25. 26. 

Douglas Aircraft Co., Inc. v. United States, 95 Ct. Cl. 140 (1941).

Emery v. United States, 13 F. 2d 658 (D. Conn. 1926). 

27. 58 Stat. 358 (1944); 41 U.S.C. 101 et seq. 

28. See sec. 3(c) of the Act for definition of "war contractor". 

29. The word "formal" is not restricted to the sense in which it is used in WD PR 302.2. It appears to include all properly authorized and executed agreements, whether technically denominated formal or informal contracts. 52

in good faith upon (1) the apparent authority of an officer or agent of a contracting agency, or (2) oral instructions or any other request to proceed from a contracting agency. .30 The contracting agency is authorized and directed to pay the contractor fair compensation in either of these situations.

This portion of the Act differs materially from the provisions of the Dent Act31 adopted for relief of war contractors after World War I. The Dent Act authorized the granting of relief by administrative officers and the courts to contractors furnishing war materiel, making expenditures, or incurring obligations under authorized contracts not executed in the manner prescribed by law. A contractor could recover under that Act only if the person ordering or contracting for the materiel had actual authority to do so.32 

Under Section 17 of the Contract Settlement Act, apparent authority of a Government agent is, in a proper case, sufficient basis for bringing the situation within the provision directing the contracting 33 agency to pay fair compensation. The Act provides for compensation to war contractors for "arranging" to furnish materials, services or facilities as well as those who actually furnish them, but only to the extent that the contractor has performed or incurred expenses for performance. This provision prevents, as did the Dent Act specifically, 34 for granting or recovery of anticipated profits on work not done. 

C. Relief where an existing contract is defective. The Act further provides that where a formal or technical defect or omission in a prime contract, or in any grant of authority to an officer or agent of a contracting agency might invalidate a contract or commitment, the contracting agency shall (1) not take advantage thereof, (2) shall amend, confirm or ratify such contract or commitment without consideration in order to cure the defect, and (3) shall make a "fair settlement" of any obligation thereby created or incurred. It is specifically provided that such

53

fair settlement shall be made whether the obligation incurred is express, implied in fact or in law, or in the nature of an implied or quasi contract.35

A contractor could not recover under a defective contract prior to passage of the Contract Settlement Act, but had to rely on principles of implied contract,36 under which it could recover only for reasonable value of its performance. Lack of authority to contract, however, prevented recovery even on implied contract and the contracting agency was under no duty to ratify.37 Contracting agencies are required under the Act to cure technical or formal defects in authority as well as defects in the contract itself, to confirm or ratify the contract without consideration, and to make fair settlements of obligations thereby created.

The reference in the Act to obligations implied in law or in the nature of quasi contracts does not subject the Government generally to suits based on such obligations. It is apparently intended only to prevent the denial of compensation (in a situation otherwise coming within the purview of this provision of the Act) solely on the ground that the obligation is technically one arising under principles of quasi contract. 

d. Measure of compensation. 

The Act sets forth two measures of compensation under Section 17. The contracting agency is directed to: 

(1) pay "fair compensation" in a proper case where no contract exists; and (2) make a "fair settlement" of any obligations created by the curing of defects and the ratification of defective contracts.39 "Fair compensation" and "fair settlement" are not defined in Section 

17. Certain standards of "fair compensation" are established in another section of the Act in connection with the settlement of termination claims.40 While these standards are not expressly made applicable to 

35. Sec. 17(b). 

36. See, however, discussion of First War Powers Act, 1941 and E.O. No. 

9001 in Ch. 20, "Remedies." 

37. See discussion, supra, this chapter. 

38. Sec. 17(a). 

39. Sec. 17(b). 

40. Sec. 6. In general the standard is cost plus a reasonable profit. 54

claims under Section 17, it would appear that, where appropriate, they may serve as a guide in interpreting the words used. This con- struction would not, however, exclude payment of fair market value or agreed value for completed items, since in termination claims the measure of fair compensation is directed to uncompleted rather than completed work. It would appear proper to pay for completed items on the basis of fair market value, as was done under a similar provision of the Dent Act.41

The term "fair settlement" is used to measure compensation under defective contracts required to be corrected and confirmed by the contracting agency. A defective formal contract will normally stipulate the price to be paid for performance thereunder. A fair settlement, following ratification, presumably would involve payment of the contract price for completed units. If no price is specified in the contract, a fair settlement of the obligation to pay for completed units would probably require payment of reasonable value, taking into consideration the contractor's costs and a reasonable profit. In the event of termination, a fair settlement for work begun but not completed would be determined in accordance with the principles laid down in other sections of the Act with respect to settlement of termination claims. e. It is the general rule, however, that when Congress specifically empowers an executive officer to determine the merits of a claim or other matter, his decision is not subject to review by the Comptroller General in the absence of fraud, bad faith or the like. That of Claims or a United States District Court.44 A contractor is not required to exhaust its remedies by administrative appeal before filing suit. In the event an appeal is taken or a suit filed, it will be governed by the provisions of Section 13 of the Act.45 section provides that where the contractor and the contracting agency cannot reach an agreement, the contracting agency will make a determination of the amount to be paid and serve a copy of its findings on the contractor. The findings of the contracting agency, while not binding on the Appeal Board or the courts, are prima facie correct. Contractors therefore have the burden of proving that the findings of the contracting agency do not provide "fair compensation" or a "fair settlement" as the case may be. 

34. SUPPLIES AND SERVICES FURNISHED BY VOLUNTEER. The Government is not liable for services or supplies furnished on a voluntary basis.

Illustrative Case: 

A railroad voluntarily constructed temporary barracks for use of troops guarding its own and Government property. 

The railroad company had not requested or intimated that it expected payment when it made the expenditure. Held: No liability on implied contract. The railroad company was a mere volunteer.46

The Contract Settlement Act does not change this rule. Under its terms, however, a request by an officer or employee having apparent authority, if relied on in good faith, would take the supplier out of the volunteer category.

SECTION III. APPROPRIATION OF PROPERTY BY THE GOVERNMENT FOR A PUBLIC USE. 

47 35. GENERAL. The Government may not take private property for public use without just compensation. It will be presumed that public officers comply with this requirement in the absence of clear evidence to the contrary. Hence, where the Government, without instituting condemnation proceedings, takes for the public use private property to which it asserts. no title, the owner may recover its fair value on the theory that the taking raises an implied promise to pay for the property. This rule is applicable whether the property taken is land, chattels, services or intangibles.

Пlustrative Cases: 

a. 

Congress appropriated money for the construction of a dam and aqueduct to convey water from the Potomac River for use in the District of Columbia. In constructing the dam, the 

Government permanently appropriated land belonging to plaintiff. 

Held: Plaintiff is entitled to recover its fair value. "The law will imply a promise to make the required compensation, where property, to which the Government asserts no title, is taken, pursuant to an act of Congress, as private property to be applied for public uses. "48 b. Quartermaster officers requisitioned the use of plaintiff's steamers, together with the services of their masters and crews, for the transportation of Government freight on the Mississippi. 

The steamers remained under plaintiff's control, and he paid the expenses of their operation, including the wages of masters and crews. Held: Plaintiff may recover on an implied contract to pay for the services performed.49

C. Plaintiff had contracts with X under which X agreed to construct for plaintiff two cargo ships. Before the ships were completed, the Government, in August, 1917, requisitioned the uncompleted vessels and appropriated plaintiff's rights under the contracts. Held: Plaintiff may recover the value of the contracts.50 

47. U. S. Const., mend. V; 4 Comp. Gen. 378 (1924). 

48. United States v. Great Falls Mfg. Co., 112 U.S. 645, 656-657 (1884); see also United States v. Buffalo Pitts Co., 234 U.S. 228 (1914). 

49. United States v. Russell, 13 Wall. (80 U.S.) 623 (1871). 

50. Consorzio Veneziana Di Armento e Navigazione v. United States, 64 

57

The courts have been liberal in permitting recoveries on implied promises to pay for property taken. "If the acts amounted to a taking, without the assertion of an adverse right, a contract would be implied whether it was thought of or not."51 On the other hand, a taking by a Government officer which is not authorized, either expressly or by implication, gives no right to recovery against the Government. The right to recover for an authorized taking by the 

Government is subject to certain limitations. 

36. EXERCISE OF SUPERIOR GOVERNMENTAL POWER. a. General. Some private property rights are qualified in that they can be exercised and enjoyed only to the extent the Government expressly or tacitly consents. 

If such rights are restricted or destroyed by an act of the Government in the exercise of a dominant governmental power, no constitutional duty of compensation arises, and no agreement to pay will be implied. 51.

Portsmouth Co. v. United States, 260 U.S. 327, 330 (1922). The tendency to apply the rule liberally is illustrated by United States v. Bethlehem Steel Co., 258 U.S. 321 (1922). The Government had con tracted to purchase 8, 10 and 12 inch guns equipped with Model 1888 M2 breech mechanisms. After the contract was entered into, a patent for an improved mechanism was issued to one Liebert, and assigned to plaintiff. Thereafter, a patent for another improved mechanism was issued to an Ordnance Bureau employee. The latter mechanism was adopted by the Ordnance Bureau as Model 1895, and plaintiff was instructed to use it in place of the 1888 M2 mechanism. Plaintiff claimed that the new device infringed the Liebert patent and sought compensation on an implied contract to pay for its use. It did not appear that the Government had considered the question of possible infringement when it adopted Model 1895. When plaintiff's claim was made, Government officials advised plaintiff that they did not feel competent to decide the legal question of infringement. Held: 

Recovery allowed. The Liebert patent was in fact infringed. Although the Government did not originally intend an infringement, the conduct of its agents was consistent with an intention to pay for any property rights which might in fact have been taken. See also

Russian Volunteer Fleet v. United States, 282 U.S. 481 (1931). 

52. United States v. North American Co., 253 U.S. 330 (1920). See Hooe v. 

United States, 218 U.S. 322, 336 (1910): "The taking of private property by an officer of the United States for public use, without being authorized, expressly or by necessary implication, to do so by some act of Congress, is not the act of the Government. 58

Illustrative Case: 

Plaintiff, a resident of Puerto Rico, had purchased from the Spanish government in perpetuity the office of procurador (solicitor) of the courts of the first instance of San Juan. In 1898, by the Treaty of Paris, Spain ceded 

Puerto Rico to the United States. The treaty provided that the cession should in no way impair private property rights. 

In 1900, the American military governor of Puerto Rico abolished the office of procurador. Plaintiff sought to recover on an implied contract to pay the value of the office. Held: Recovery denied. Plaintiff's property right in the office was qualified by the superior right of the new sovereign to abolish without compensation a system of office-holding foreign to its governmental institutions.53 b. Riparian rights. Another illustration of the same principle is to be found in the much litigated problem of the respective rights of the Government and riparian proprietors in the bed and waters of navigable streams. Any rights possessed by the riparian proprietor below high watermark are qualified by and inferior to the Government's right, under the commerce power, to improve the stream in the interest of navigation.54 Destruction or impairment of private rights below high watermark as the result of improvement of the stream for navigation will not create an implied promise to pay the value of the rights so destroyed.

Illustrative Cases: 

(1) The Government erected a series of dams in the upper 

Mississippi River to provide a navigable channel between Minneapolis and the mouth of the Missouri River. Due to a resulting rise in the river level, sections of the right-of-way and tracks of a railroad company were inundated, and the company was forced to relocate these sections of its line. Portions of the flooded right-of-way were above high watermark, and other portions were below it. Held: The Government is under a duty to make compensation only for those portions of the line above high watermark. .55 

53. Sanchez v. United States, 216 U.S. 167 (1910). See as to exercise of superior powers under international law Juragua Iron Co. v. United 

States, 212 U.S. 297 (1909). See also Field v. Geigengack, 73 F. 

2d 945 (App. D.C. 1934). 

54. United States v. Chandler Dunbar Water Power Co., 229 U.S. 53 (1913); 

Lewis Blue Point Oyster Bay Co. v. Briggs, 229 U.S. 82 (1913). Recent cases have extended the doctrine to include improvement in the interests of flood control and general watershed development as well as navigation. Appalachian Electric Power Co. v. United States, 311 U.S. 377 (1940); Oklahoma v. Atkinson Co., 313 U.S. 508 (1941). 

55. United States v. Chicago, Milwaukee St. Paul R. R. Co., 312 U.S. 592. 59

(2) The plaintiff owned land riparian to the St. Mary's

River in Michigan. The Government constructed a pier in the river directly in front of his property, completely cutting off his access to the river, and refused his request for permission to land freight on the pier for transportation across the intervening strip. Held: No recovery. Since plaintiff's riparian right of access to the river is inferior to the Government's right to develop it for navigation, exercise of the latter right involves no promise to pay for the destruction of the former.56 (3) The plaintiff, under authority of a Pennsylvania charter, constructed a bridge across a navigable river in that State. The Government had offered no objection at the time the bridge was constructed. Some years later the Secretary of War, on the ground that the bridge constituted an obstruction to navigation, directed that claimant remove the structure or alter it at considerable expense. The plaintiff claimed that such an order was a taking of the property, implying a duty to make compensation. Held: Under the commerce power, the Government has paramount authority to protect the navigability of navigable streams. It may therefore require the removal or alteration of obstructions to navigation without giving rise to any duty on its part to make compensation.57 

37. CONSEQUENTIAL IMPAIRMENT OF PROPERTY RIGHTS. An injury is said to be "consequential" when it results from the exercise of governmental powers which do not encroach directly on private property, though the consequence of the exercise of such powers may impair or destroy the value of such property. Such an injury, even if it results in the total destruction of the property, does not give rise to an implied contract to pay its value.

Illustrative Cases: 

a. The plaintiff had a contract with the Allegheny 

Steel Company entitling plaintiff to purchase quantities of steel plate at a price well under the market. Before the plaintiff had requested delivery of any plate under the contract or any plate had been allocated to it, the Government requisitioned the Allegheny Steel Company's entire production of steel plate for war production. The plaintiff sought to recover on an implied promise to pay the value of its contract. Held:

Recovery denied. The Government's requisition did not result in a direct appropriation of glaintiff's contract, but merely made performance impossible. b. The plaintiff had a contract with a shipbuilding company for the construction of certain vessels. When the vessels were partially completed, the Government requisitioned them for war purposes, at the same time requiring the shipbuilder to complete and deliver the vessels to it. The 

Government offered to reimburse plaintiff for payments already made by plaintiff to the shipbuilder, but the plaintiff sought to recover the value of the contract. Held: Recovery allowed. The Government's action amounted to a direct appropriation of plaintiff's contract for its own benefit, and not a mere indirect interference with its performance. 59

TEMPORARY INVASION OF PROPERTY: Governmental activity re sulting in a temporary invasion of private property in the nature of a trespass, without any intention to appropriate it to the Government's use, is not a taking which will create an implied promise on the part of the Government to make compensation.

Illustrative Cases: 

a. The Government constructed a series of dikes and revetments in the Missouri River. During 1937 and 1938, these improvements raised the natural level of the river, causing it to overflow plaintiff's land. It appeared that on completion of other structures planned or under construction plaintiff's lands would not be subject to any greater overflow than it was before the 

57. (cont.) structure over to the United States at the conclusion of the license period at less than its fair value. United States v. Appalachian 

Electric Power Co., 311 U.S. 377 (1940). 

58. Omnia Commercial Co. v. United States, 261 U.S. 502 (1923). 

59. Brooks-Scanlon Corp. v. United States, 265 U.S. 106 (1924). See also Taylor v. Brown, 137 F. 2d 654 (Emerg. Ct. of App., 1943), cert. den., 320 U.S. 787 (1943), in which enforced reduction of rents under the Emergency Price Control Act of 1942, pursuant to the 61

program was begun. Held: Recovery denied. Since the overflows were mere temporary invasions, there was no taking of plaintiff property and hence no implied promise to make compensation. b. As a result of the erection of a Government dam, plaintiff's land became permanently subject to recurring overflows at certain seasons of the year. Held: Recovery allowed. Since a permanent servitude was imposed upon the property, there was a partial taking, and the Government was liable on an implied contract to pay the fair value of a flowage easement." 39.

UNFORESEEABLE DAMAGE. An implied in fact contract can arise only where there has been an assent, based on conduct, to its terms. 

Where damage is not foreseeable at the time the acts producing it are committed, there is no basis for implying a promise to compensate for the damage. 59. 60.

Illustrative Cases: 

a.

Plaintiff owned certain lakes which it had used for years in the manufacture of soda. For a period of 20 years, the level of the lake waters had never varied more than two feet. 

Seepage and percolation from a Government project on a nearby waterway caused a 19-foot rise in the water level of the lakes, made further recovery of minerals impossible, flooded plaintiff's lands and buildings, and destroyed the value of plaintiff's property. Held: Seepage and percolation of ground waters not being foreseeable, no promise by the Government to pay the value of plaintiff's property can be implied. (cont.) 62 war power of the Government, was held to give no basis for recovery on the ground of taking of property. 

Goodman v. United States, 113 F. 2d 914 (C.C.A. 8th 1940). Where, however, the Government takes temporary possession of land with the expectation of later securing a lease, it is liable on an implied contract to pay the reasonable value of its occupancy if no lease is in fact executed.
