X used the dragline and equipment in performing construction work for the Government. X defaulted on both his purchase contract with plaintiff and his construction contract with the Government. The Government undertook to complete the job, taking over X's equipment at the site for the purpose under a provision in the contract giving it that right. 

It used the dragline in the belief that it was X's property. 

Upon receipt of notification from plaintiff that it was the true owner, the Government ceased using plaintiff's equipment and delivered it to plaintiff. Plaintiff sought to recover the rental value of the dragline for the period of use. 

Recovery denied. There could be no implication of a promise to pay for use of the dragline since the Government was not aware that anyone other than X had an interest in it.63

40. TAKING UNDER DENIAL OF LIABILITY. The courts are free, in the silence of the Government at the time of taking, to imply a promise to pay for the property taken. Where the Government, when it appropriates property, expressly negatives such an intent, no promise can be implied. The Government's action may be wrongful, but no recovery is 64 permitted under the Tucker Act.

Illustrative Case: 

Plaintiff leased a steam shovel to X for use in performing a contract with the Government. On conclusion of the contract, the Government took possession of the steam shovel, claiming that it had the right to do so under the terms of its contract with X and of the lease between X and the plaintiff. Plaintiff sought to recover the value of the shovel. Held: Recovery denied. 

Regardless of whether the Government was correct in its contention, the fact that it denied liability on taking the shovel negatived any implication of a promise to pay its value.65 

The denial of liability or the assertion of right or title must be made at or before the time of taking. pay for property appropriated is created, will not defeat the right of recovery." 66 63.

SECTION IV. MEASURE OF RECOVERY. 

GENERAL. The measure of recovery in a suit on implied contract for supplies and services furnished is the reasonable value of such supplies and services.68 In the absence of other evidence, the price stipulated in the defective express contract will be considered to represent the reasonable value.69 In any event, no sum greater than the amount so stipulated can be recovered. 70

Where property is taken for a public use, the damages to which the owner is entitled in a suit under the Tucker Act "will be the same as that which he might have been awarded had the (Government) instituted * * * condemnation proceedings" 71 The measure of damages in condemnation, and therefore under the Tucker Act, is normally the market value of the property on the date of taking.72 67. 

Hill v. United States, 149 U.S. 593 (1893); Schillinger v. United 

States, 155 U.S. 163 (1894; Ball Engineering Co. v. White and Co., 250 U.S. 46 (1919); Klebe v. United States, 263 U.S. 188 (1923). 

See, however, Stovall v. United States, 57 Ct. Cl. 226 (1891) for a contrary theory. See also Yearsley v. Ross Construction Co., 309 U.S. 18 (1940), where the court indicated that the remedy under the Tucker Act is as broad as the owner's rights under the Constitution. 

68. Emery v. United States, 13 F. (2d) 658 (D. Conn., 1926). See footnote 

25, supra. 69. 70. 

Saloman v. United States, 19 Wall. (86 U.S.) 17 (1873). 

St. Louis Hay and Grain Co. v. United States, 191 U.S. 159 (1903). 

71. Hurley v. Kinkaid, 285 U.S. 95, 104 (1932). 72. 

United States v. Miller, 317 U.S. 369 (1943); United States ex rel. 

Tennessee Valley Authority v. Powelson, 319 U.S. 266 (1943); Seaboard 

Airline Ry. Co. v. United States, 261 U.S. 299 (1923); L. Vogelstein and Co. v. United States, 262 U.S. 337 (1923); United States v. New 

River Collieries Co., 262 U.S. 341 (1923); Greshon Bros. v. United 

States, 284 Fed. 849 (C.C.A. 5th 1922). Cf. United States v. General

Motors Corp., 323 U.S. 273 (1945). 

64

Doubt and confusion existed regarding the question as to whether or not interest is recoverable as a part of "just compensation" when suit has been brought upon the basis of an implied contract resulting from the taking by the Government of privately owned property until the decision of the Supreme Court in Jacobs v. United States, 290 U.S. 

13 (1933). The District Court in the Jacobs case awarded interest as a part of "just compensation", 73 but on appeal, after consolidation, the decision was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals, 74 which stated interest could not be allowed as a part of "just compensation" in an implied taking of real estate under the Tucker Act. 

The Jacobs cases, on the question of interest alone, came before the Supreme Court, which reversed the Circuit Court and awarded interest to the claimants. 75 

73. Jacobs v. United States, not reported, (D. Ct. N.D. Ala. 1930). 

United States v. Jacobs, 45 F. 2d 34 (C.C.A. 4th 1930); 63 F. 

2d 326 (C.C.A. 5th 1933 

740 75. Jacobs v. United States, supra: "The suits were based on the right to recover just compensation for property taken by the United States for public use in the exercise of its power of eminent domain. That right was guaranteed by the Constitution. The fact that condemnation proceedings were not instituted and that the right was asserted in suits by the owner did not change the essential nature of the claim. The form of the remedy did not qualify the right. It rested upon the Fifth Amendment. Statutory recognition was not necessary. A promise to pay was not necessary. 

Such a promise was implied because of the duty to pay imposed by the Amendment. The suits were thus founded upon the Constitution of the United States. 28 U.S.C. 41 (20)." See 96 ALR 1. 65

CHAPTER TYPES OF CONTRACTS SECTION I. METHODS OF COMPENSATING THE CONTRACTOR 42. FIXED PRICE CONTRACTS. The fixed price type of contract generally provides for a firm price or prices for the supplies or service which are being procured. This type of contract is used when costs can be estimated with reasonable accuracy and a fair price determined. The advertising-bidaward method of procurement is always on a fixed price basis. Also, in the case of negotiated contracts it is the policy of the Army to negotiate contracts on a fixed price basis whenever possible. Under this type of contract the contractor pays all costs and the difference between the contract price and the contractor's costs represents the contractor's profit. In order to prevent an excessive profit on the part of the contractor or to prevent a loss to the contractor it is extremely important that the contractor's costs be estimated as accurately as possible. Inasmuch as his remuneration is based on the price fixed there is an incentive for him to keep his costs at a minimum.

In those situations where exists the possibility of an unforeseen contingency which may considerably increase or decrease the costs of performance, the fixed price contract should contain a provision for increasing or decreasing the contract price accordingly. If this provision is not present in the contract the contractor might attempt to include, in the contract price, allowances for possible contingencies which increase the cost of performance. This practice is not recognized by the Government inasmuch as an increase in the fixed price without an assurance to the Government that if the contingency does not occur the price will be lowered, would always operate to the disadvantage of the Government. Hence, the importance of a provision to lower the price in the event of the happening of a contingency which reduces the cost of performing the contract. 

43. INCENTIVE-TYPE CONTRACTS. This type of contract provides for a tentative base price or target price (called the contract price) and a maximum price or maximum fee, with price or fee redetermination after completion of the contract. The purpose of this is to establish a final price or fee based on the contractor's actual costs plus a sliding scale of profit or fee which varies inversely with the cost but which in no event shall permit the final price or fee to exceed the maximum price or fee stated in the contract. In other words, two prices are arrived at. The first, or lower, is what is estimated to be a fair and reasonable cost of performing the contract. The second, or higher, is the maximum amount which the contract price cannot exceed. In other words, we arrive at two possible contract prices-the estimated and the maximum. If the final cost of performing the contract is equal to the estimated cost, the contractor receives the predetermined profit on the estimated cost. If the final cost is equal to the 66

maximum price, the contractor would receive no profit inasmuch as any profit on such cost would exceed the previously agreed maximum price. If the final cost is less than the estimated cost the contractor, under previously negotiated percentages, would share in the cost saving. If the final cost is greater than the estimated cost, but less than the maximum, the contractor's profit is reduced based on the percentages. Thus, the contractor has an incentive to keep costs low and thereby increase his profit. The use of the incentive type contract is usually limited to instances in which the procurement of supplies or services involves a reasonably long production line, in which the contractor has an acceptable accounting system and adequate manufacturing experience, and in which a reasonably close contract price can be negotiated. This type of contract is not resorted to often, since obviously a contractor, by cutting costs to the minimum, even to the point of doing inferior work, may realize profits which might well be out of proportion to any saving to the Government. It should be resorted to only where it is fairly certain that it will be a less costly method than any other type of contract or where it is impracticable to secure the supplies or services of the kind or quality required without the use of such type of contract. 

44. COST CONTRACTS. The cost (or cost-sharing) type of contract provides for payment to the contractor of allowable costs incurred in the performance of the contract to the extent prescribed in the contract. 

This type of contract establishes an estimate of the total cost for purposes of (a) obligating current funds and (b) establishing a ceiling beyond which the contractor cannot go (except at his own expense) without prior approval of the Contracting Officer. Although this type of contract provides for no fee or profit to the contractor it is recognized that certain benefits other than immediate monetary profits may accrue. An example may be found in contracts for research and development work which have possible future commercial values to the contractor. This type of contract is seldom used by the Army and has the same limitations in its use that are set forth above for the incentive-type contract. 

45. COST-PLUS-A-FIXED-FEE CONTRACTS. The cost-plus-a-fixed-fee type of contract is similar to the cost contract in that the contract provides for payment to the contractor of all allowable costs as defined in the contract and establishes an estimate of the total cost. It differs from the cost contract in that it also provides for payment of a fixed-fee based on the estimated cost of the contract. This fixedfee does not vary with actual cost (except in the case of an incentive type contract) but only with a change in the estimated cost as a result of a change in the scope of work under the contract. The advantage of this type of a contract is evident in cases where it is impossible to estimate accurately costs in order to arrive at a fixed price, but where 67

an estimate can be arrived at upon which to base an appropriate profit for the contractor. The disadvantage of this type of contract is in the lack of incentive for the contractor to keep his costs to a minimum. 

Inasmuch as the Government has agreed to pay all allowable costs as set forth in the contract, the contractor is less likely to effect economies in his operations. Furthermore, the contractor is subject to rigid controls by the Government in his subcontracting and other expenditures and is also subject to inspections which may prove burdensome. The limitations on the use of this type of contract are the same as those for the incentive and cost type contracts.

A type of contract that might be included in the cost-plus-a-fixedfee type is the cost-plus-a-percentage-of-cost contract. This type was used by the Army in the past but is now specifically prohibited by statute. 

The contractor was paid a profit on the basis of a percentage of his cost, in other words, the higher the cost the greater the profit to the contractor. Under a cost-plus-a-percentage-of-cost contract the contractor was able to a degree, to control his profit by increasing his costs. 

46. TIME AND MATERIALS CONTRACTS. The time and materials type of contract provides for the purchase of supplies or services on the basis of (a) direct labor hours at specified hourly rates (which rates include direct labor, overhead, and profit) and (b) material at cost. This type of contract shall not be used if any other type of contract is equally advantageous to the Government. Representative situations in which this type of contract might be used are contracts for engineering and design services in connection with contracts for the production of supplies, contracts for repair, maintenance, or overhaul, and contracts for the production of supplies in special cases of emergency. This type of contract was used during the war period when it was necessary to get work started before accurate estimates of the cost could be determined and before the full scope of the work was realized. of contract is infrequently used during peacetime, and then, only when the Government owns the facilities in which the work is to be performed. 

SECTION II. FORM 

This type 

47. INTRODUCTION. Inasmuch as procurement contract forms are now in the process of revision and standardization this section is concerned only with the general use of the various contract forms. Contracts for the Department of the Army are divided into two general groups--formal and informal. A formal contract is one which is required to be reduced to writing and signed by the contracting parties. An informal contract is one which consists of an oral quotation or written offer signed by the contractor and an acceptance signed by the Contracting Officer. 

Every purchase transaction by a Contracting Officer except those where payment is made coincidentally with receipt of supplies will be evidenced by a written contract, formal or informal. 68

48. LETTER OF INTENT. Another method of initiating procurement is by a Letter of Intent. This type was used during the war period, especially in the initial stages, when it was desired that a contractor commence work immediately. It authorizes the contractor to commence work, incur costs and make commitments pending negotiation and execution of the final definitive contract. It should contain a tentative price or an agreed specific amount and should also contain such other basic terms as can be a greed on at the time. It obligates the Government either to make a final definitive contract within a specified time or to reimburse the contractor for costs incurred under the Letter of Intent. The Letter of Intent is infrequently used now and under the Armed Services Procurement Regulation it shall be used only when one of the following conditions exists: (a) When it is essential to give the contractor a commitment in order to permit him to work immediately; or (b) When the nature of the work involved prevents the preparation of definitive requirements of specifications thereby making it impossible to negotiate a final contract at that time. 49.

PURCHASE ORDERS. Another procurement instrument that is frequently used by the Department of the Army is known as the Purchase Order. This device facilitates procurement in that it obviates the necessity of obtaining the signatures of both parties to the transaction and will permit a contractor to deliver the material as soon as he receives the order. The Purchase Order may be used in a number of ways. 

For instance, if the contractor re wives written quotations and then issues the Purchase Order based on those quotations the transaction would be regarded as an informal contract consisting of two instruments. 

This would also be true if there were no written quotations but the Purchase Order was followed by a written acceptance on the part of the contractor. Under these circumstances departmental procedures permit the use of the Purchase Order in transactions up to $100,000 and when delivery or performance time does not exceed 60 days. However, the monetary limitation on the use of the Purchase Order form may vary in certain procurements. The problem in such a transaction is in determining when a binding agreement comes into existence. Under one theory the contractor, by his oral quotation, has made the Government an offer and the Government accepts this offer and a binding agreement comes into existence by issuing the Purchase Order. On the other hand, there is the theory that the Purchase Order is an offer to the contractor which he accepts by performance. That is by delivering the articles called for in the order. In practice the use of the Purchase Order has given rise to little difficulty as to when the agreement came into existence, The reason for this is probably that the delivery order has been used mostly for off-the-shelf type of article with an early delivery. The Purchase Order should not be used in complicated procurement involving supplies made to specifications. 69

50. FORMAL CONTRACTS. As pointed out above, a formal contract is one reduced to writing and signed by the contracting parties. This type of contract is one that must be used in complicated procurement involving the expenditure of large sums of money. It should be used when in the opinion of the Government it is desired to have the signature of both parties to the agreement in order to remove any doubt as to when the agreement came into existence or any of the terms therein. Formal contracts may be used for any purchase transaction regardless of amount and must be used for all purchase transactions the contract price of which is $100,000 or more. 

51. OPEN-END CONTRACTS. An open-end contract is a specialized type of contract used to facilitate procurement under certain circumstances. 

Under this type of contract the contractor agrees to furnish the Government such supplies or services as the Government may call upon him to deliver. The contract should contain as many of the appropriate or necessary provisions as it is possible to include at the time of entering into the contract. It is sometimes referred to as a Requirements contract. 

That is, under the contract the Government may issue orders upon the contractor to furnish such supplies or services as are required by the Government. In other words, the contractor makes a continuing offer to the Government which the Government accepts by means of a delivery or Purchase Order. It has its usefulness in the procurement of certain types of subsistence supplies, utilities services and in student training. 

For example, under an Open-end contract a military organization might order its milk supplies, throughout the year, such requirement being difficult to predict, in that the amount required will vary with the strength of the organization on each particular day. Similarly, in the case of the procurement of certain utilities such as gas, electricity etc. it is impossible to determine the requirements at the start of the year and hence an Open-end contract for such utilities as are needed is most practicable. Under certain types of Open-end contracts the contractor may require the Government to order at least a previously determined minimum amount of supplies or services with the right to order whatever the Government requires above the minimum amount. type of contract, although not used to a great extent, has its value in the field of Army procurement. This 70

CHAPTER 8 LAWS, EXECUTIVE ORDERS, ETC. RELATING TO LABOR 

GENERAL. Government procurement is affected by certain labor law and regulation requirements and by policies and procedures as set forth in Presidential Executive Orders and Armed Services Procurement 

Regulation. Some of these laws, regulations, policies, and procedures are applicable to all Government contracts involving the employment of labor while others apply either to supply or construction contracts. a. 

53. LAWS AND EXECUTIVE ORDERS APPLICABLE TO ALL GOVERNMENT CONTRACTS. 

Convict Labor Law. This lawl provides as follows: 

Whoever, being an officer, employee, or agent of the United States or any department or agency thereof, contracts with any person or corporation, or permits any warden, agent, or official of any penal or correctional institution, to hire out the labor of any prisoners confined for violation of any laws of the United States, shall be fined not more than $1,000 or imprisoned not more than three years, or both." 

(1) Implementation of the Act. Executive Order No. 325A dated 

18 May 1905 implements and extends the policy set forth in the aforesaid law by a requirement that a contract article be included in all contracts involving the employment of labor. It provides the following: "Whereas by an Act of Congress which received executive approval on February 23, 1887, all officers or agents of the United States were as a matter of public policy forbidden under appropriate penalties, to hire or contract out the labor of any criminals who might thereafter be confined in any prison, jail, or other place of incarceration for the violation of any laws of the Government of the United States of America. 

"It is Hereby Ordered, That all contracts which shall hereafter be entered into by officers or agents of the United States involving the employment of labor in the States composing the Union, or the Territories of the United States contiguous thereto, shall, unless otherwise provided by law, contain a stipulation forbidding, in the performance of such contracts, the employment of persons undergoing sentences of imprisonment at hard labor which have been imposed by courts of the several States, Territories or 

Municipalities having criminal jurisdiction." 

1. 24 Stat. 411; 18 U.S.C. 436. 71

(2) Convict labor clause.

The clause adopted in Armed Services Pro curement Regulation as satisfying the requirements of the Executive Order is as follows:2 

"In connection with the performance of this contract, the Contractor agrees not to employ any person undergoing sentence of imprisonment at hard labor." (3) Exceptions. The prohibition contained in the aforesaid statute and Executive Order does not apply to purchases of items manufactured, or services rendered, by Federal Prisons Industries, Inc.3 and does not prohibit the employment by contractors or subcontractors of persons serving sentences on parole or probation, or to convicts who have served their terms. According to the interpretation placed on the convict labor provisions in Armed Services Procurement Regulation, the prohibition is also inapplicable to contracts for purchases of supplies or services from any 

State or Federal prison.

(1) Interpretations of the Order. The Committee on Fair Employment

"My Dear Mr. Attorney General: "You have brought to my attention the Comptroller General's opinion holding that Executive Order 9346 is directive only and not mandatory in requiring insertion in all Government contracts of a provision obligating the contractor not to discriminate against any employee or applicant for employment on account of race, creed, color, or national origin; and requiring the contractor to include similar contractual provisions in all subcontracts. "There is no need for me to reiterate the fundamental principles underlying the promulgation of the Executive Order, namely, that the prosecution of the war demands that we utilize fully all available manpower, and that the discrimination by war industries against persons for any of the reasons named in the Order is detrimental to the prosecution of the war and is opposed to our national democratic purposes. "I realize the hesitancy of the Comptroller General to withhold payments on Government contracts in which these provisions have not been included where there is doubt as to whether the Order is mandatory. The Order should be so construed by all Government contracting Agencies.

C. Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938. This Act8 provides for the establishment of minimum wage and maximum hour standards, creates a Wage and Hour Division in the Department of Labor for purposes of interpretation and enforcement (including investigations and inspections of Government contractors), and prohibits opressive child labor. No contract provision in Government contracts is required and the Comptroller General has ruled that such article is not permitted. It is the policy of the 

Department of the Army not to advise contractors on fixed-price contracts concerning the Army's view toward the applicability or nonapplicability of this law, it being the view of the Department that this question is one exclusively for court determination upon the facts in each particular On the other hand, the Army does assist its cost-plus-fixed-fee contractors in determining possible applicability for the reason that Government costs might be increased by litigative costs and extra damages if the contractor erroneously considers himself to be exempt from the provisions of said law9 with respect to some of his employees.10 case. 

54. THOSE APPLICABLE TO SUPPLY CONTRACTS. The Walsh-Healey Public 

Contracts Actll provides that all contracts entered into by any Department for the manufacture or furnishing of supplies in any amount which exceeds or may exceed $10,000 shall incorporate the representations and stipulations required by said Act pertaining to such matters as minimum wages, maximum hours, child labor, convict labor, safe and sanitary working conditions, and the contractor's status as a manufacturer or regular dealer. The provisions of the Act are also applicable to contracts for the construction, alteration (including repair), furnishing, or equipping of any naval vessels. Applicability. The Secretary of Labor has rendered opinion that contracts which are originally $10,000 or less, but are subsequently modified to increase the price to an amount in excess of $10,000, are subject to the Walsh-Healey Act, and that contracts in an amount in excess of $10,000, which are subsequently modified to a figure of $10,000 or less, are not subject to said Act with respect to work performed after such modification if modification is effected by mutual agreement. b. 55. CONSTRUCTION CONTRACTS. a. Eight-Hour Law. The Eight-Hour Law actually consists of two basic statutes, one adopted in 189216 and the other in 1912.17 Both statutes, as amended, remain in effect. The earlier 

Act applies criminal sanctions to violators. It provides that laborers and mechanics shall not be employed by the Government or by any contractor or subcontractor upon a public work more than eight hours in any one calendar day. Intentional violations of the Act are punishable by fine, imprisonment, or both. 

(1) Contract Clause required. The 1912 Act provides that all Govern ment contracts which may require or involve the employment of laborers or mechanics must contain a provision that no such laborer or mechanic shall be required or permitted to work more than eight hours in any one calendar day. The contract is required to stipulate that a penalty of five dollars per day shall be paid for each violation. Certain exemptions are contained in the Act. 

(2) Removal of absolute prohibition. By a general statute, enacted in 1940,18 Congress removed the absolute prohibition of the law and authorized overtime work provided that compensation is paid, at not less than one and one-half times the basic rate, for work in excess of eight hours per day. (3) Criminal and civil liability. Since the 1892 Act and the 1912 

Act, as amended, are both in effect, a contractor may be held liable for both criminal and civil violations, 19 if the contract falls within the scope of both statutes. Criminal intent is an essential element of any violation of the 1892 Act, while a violation of the 1912 Act may consist merely of an unintentional breach of the provision contained in the contract.20 (4) Applicability. Both laws apply only to laborers and mechanics. 

The terms "laborers" and "mechanics" have been broadly construed. 21 Skilled and unskilled workmen are included. 22 A laborer is defined as "one who performs manual labor or labors at a toilsome occupation requiring physical

The 1892 Act applies only to those laborers and mechanics employed upon a "public work", which has been construed to mean that the "objects of labor referred to have some kind of permanent existence and structural unity, and are severally capable of being regarded as complete wholes."24 The term has also been defined as including "all fixed works contracted_for public use, as railways, docks, canals, waterworks, roads, etc. "25 This Act does not apply to supply contracts. "23 (6) Construction of 1912 Act. The 1912 Act applies to laborers and mechanics doing any part of the work contemplated by the contract. 

The contract must be one to which the Government is a party or one made for on its behalf. Except as otherwise specifically provided, both construction and supply contracts are within the provisions of this Act to the extent that laborers or mechanics are required for work "contemplated by the contract." The manufacture of castings, under contract for the manufacture of projectiles, would not be included unless they were manufactured solely for that contract, since otherwise their manufacture would not be work "contemplated by the contract", according to an opinion of The Attorney General.26 (7) Exemptions. Certain exemptions are contained in the 1912 Act, namely: (a) contracts for transportation by land or water; (b) contracts for transmission of intelligence; (c) contracts for the purchase of supplies, whether manufactured to specifications or not, or for such materials as may usually be bought in the open market (except armor and armor plate), whether made to specifications or not; (d) the construction or repair of levees or revetments necessary for protection against floods or overflows on the navigable waters of the United States. These exemptions are all subject to the proviso that every class of work which has been or may be performed by the Government shall, when done by contract, be performed in accordance with the 1912 Act. This proviso is interpreted to apply only to work which, up to the time of making the contract, has been performed by the Government ordinarily, rather than occasionally or only to a limited extent.27

(8) Supply contracts. The exemption giving rise to the most problems has been that referring to contracts for supplies and materials. The provisions of the statute exempting "the purchase of supplies by the Government" is practically synonymous with the provision exempting contracts "for such materials or articles as may usually be bought on open market."28 Supply contracts which require or involve the employment of laborers or mechanics on work contemplated by the contract, are subject to the 1912 Act unless (a) the supplies contracted for are such as may be bought in the open market; and (b) the work required is such as was not ordinarily performed by the Government. 

The fact that supplies exempted from the provisions of the Act are to be manufactured according to specifications or require additional machining or tooling does not bring them within the scope of the Act.29 

Where an article or material belongs to a class "which may ordinarily be bought in the open market", it is exempt regardless of the extent to which it may be individualized and distinguished by the requirements of the particular specifications under which the contract is executed. 

A contract for supplies, otherwise exempt from the Act, requiring installation, is subject to the Act only if the installation work is substantial and not a mere incident to the contract.30 (9) Emergencies. Contractors and subcontractors are covered by the Act.31 Violations of the Act may be excused by the President if caused by emergency or other extraordinary event, but mere hardship or inconvenience is insufficient.32 The contractor is not bound by the provisions of the 1912 Act, unless the provisions of the Act are incorporated into the contract, as directed by the statute.33

"EIGHT-HOUR LAW OF 1912: This contract, to the extent that it is of a character specified in the Eight-Hour Law of 1912 as amended (40 U. S. 

Code 324-326) and is not covered by the Walsh-Healey Public Contracts Act (41 U.S. Code 35-45), is subject to the following provisions and exceptions of said Eight-Hour Law of 1912 as amended, and to all other provisions and exceptions of said law:

No laborer or mechanic doing any part of the work contemplated by this contract, in the employ of the Contractor or any subcontractor contracting for any part of the said work, shall be required or permitted to work more than eight hours in any one calendar day upon such work, except upon the condition that compensation is paid to such laborer or mechanic in accordance with the provisions of this clause. 

The wages of every such laborer and mechanic employed by the Contractor or any subcontractor engaged in the performance of this contract shall be computed on a basic day rate of eight hours per day; and work in excess of eight hours per day is permitted only upon the condition that every such laborer and mechanic shall be compensated for all hours worked in excess of eight hours per day at not less than one and one-half times the basic rate of pay. For each violation of the requirements of this clause a penalty of five dollars shall be imposed upon the Contractor for each such laborer or mechanic for every calendar day in which such employee is required or permitted to labor more than eight hours upon said work without receiving compensation computed in accordance with this clause; and all penalties thus imposed shall be withheld for the use and benefit of the Government.

Davis-Bacon Act: The Davis-Bacon Act36 applies to all Govern ment contracts in excess of $2,000 entered into for the construction, alteration or repair (including painting and decorating) of public buildings or public works. It provides that such contracts shall contain a provision to the effect that no laborer or mechanic employed directly upon the site of the work contemplated by the contract shall receive less than the prevailing wages as determined by the Secretary of Labor. 34.

C. "Anti-Kickback" Act. The Copeland ("Anti-Kickback") Act46 provides in part that "whoever, by force, intimidation, or threat of procuring dismissal from employment, or by any other manner whatsoever induces any person employed in construction, prosecution, completion or repair of any public building, public work, or building or work financed in whole or in part by loans or grants from the United States, to give up any part of the compensation to which he is entitled under his contract of employment, shall be fined not more than $5,000 or imprisoned not more than five years, or both." The 

Secretary of Labor is charged by the Act to make reasonable regulations implementing the Act including a provision that each contractor and subcontractor shall furnish weekly a sworn affidavit with respect to the wages paid each employee during the preceding week. 

(1) Applicability. The requirements of the Copeland Act cover con tracts for the same kind of work as the Davis-Bacon Act, supra, except that it is also applicable to contracts under $2,000, and does not cover the types of work exempted from coverage under the Davis-Bacon Act. (2) Contract article. In accordance with the Act and regulations of the Secretary of Labor the following contract article is provided by Armed 

Services Procurement Regulation for inclusion in applicable contracts:4

CHAPTER OTHER LAWS RELATING TO GOVERNMENT CONTRACTS 56. ASSIGNMENT OF CONTRACTS. a. General. By statute, no contract or order can be transferred by the party to whom such a contract is given to any other party and in the event of any transfer or assignment, the 1 contract shall become voidable at the option of the United States. The purpose of this statute was the protection of the Government. 

It was passed in order that the Government might not be harassed by multiplying the number of persons with whom it had to deal and might always know with whom it was dealing and, of course, also to insure to the Government the benefit of the performance by the contractor with whom it entered into contractual relations.2 Any attempt at assignment of Government contracts makes the contract voidable at the option of the Government.3 However, if the assignee performs the contract, he will be entitled to recover under the original agreement if the Government expressly validates the assignment after it has been made.4 If the Government does not validate the assignment, but accepts and retains the benefits of the assignee's performance, the assignee may recover upon an implied contract to pay the reasonable value of the services rendered.> b. Assignment of Rights Article. Every contract which calls for the payment of $1,000 or more, except top secret, secret, confidential and re- stricted contracts, must contain the standard Assignment of Rights Article. 

This Assignment of Rights article is based on the Assignment of Claims Act of 1940.7 This article provides that the contractor may assign its claim for all sums due or to become due under the contract to a bank, trust company or other financing institution, including any Federal lending agency; that the assignee may further assign a claim to other bank, trust company, etc.; that the claim shall not be subject to set-off against the assignee

of any indebtedness of the assignor to the United States arising independently of the contract. The provision regarding set-off may be deleted in any case where special circumstances make it advisable in the best interest of the Government. ass

Assignments by operation of law,9 transfers which are incident to the sale of a business, a corporate merger, amalgamation and similar situations, 10 subcontracting, and transfers of reversionary interests in leased property12 are not forbidden by either of the two statutes. 

The purpose of the Assignment of Claims Act and the Standard Assignment of Rights article is to assist Government contractors financially, enabling them to obtain money advances to finance their operations while the contract is in the process of performance. The Assignment of Claims 

Act has not repealed either the statute forbidding assignment of government contracts or the statute forbidding the general assignment of claims against the Government. 57. OFFICIALS NOT TO BENEFIT. Section 3741, Revised Statutes, 14 

requires every contract and agreement made or entered into, or accepted by or on behalf of the United States to contain an express condition that no Member of or Delegate to Congress shall be admitted to any share or part of such contract or agreement, or to any benefit to arise thereunder. There is accordingly inserted into each contract, a clause designed to comply with this statutory requirement. This law does not prohibit contracts with corporations, which may have such officials as stockholders.15 It does prohibit a contract with a partnership of which a member of Congress is a member;16 and also the employment of members of 

Congress for professional services. 17

From time to time in recent years, Congress, in enacting legislation has made exceptions to this basic requirement. These exceptions are not here pertinent.

Congress has seen fit to further implement this prohibition by the enactment of criminal statutes providing for the punishment of the officer of the United States making such contracts, as well as the member of Congress. The latest codification of these statutes and the exceptions thereto may be found in Sections 431-433 of the Criminal Code, enacted 25 

June 1948.18 13.

A "financing institution" within the meaning of the Act and the Article has been defined as "an individual, partnership, or corporation dealing in money as distinguished from other commodities as a primary function of its business activity." 22 Comp. Gen. 44, 46 (1942). Thus a partnership engaged in the business of extending credit on the collateral of accounts receivable is a financing institution. 20 Comp. Gen. 415 (1941). re. FOREIGN PURCHASES. a. General. The Buy American Act,19 

quires that each department or independent establishment of the Government purchase only (1) such unmanufactured articles, materials and supplies as have been mined or produced in the United States, and (2) only such manufactured articles, materials and supplies as have been manufactured in the United States substantially all from articles, materials or supplies mined, produced or manufactured, as the case may be, in the 

United States. 

b. Exceptions. There are four principal exceptions to the requirements of this Act: (1) The head of the department or independent establishment concerned may determine it to be inconsistent with the public interest to buy the American product, or (2) He may determine that the cost of the American product is unreasonable, or (3) The items bought need not be American if they are to be used outside the United States, or (4) Foreign items may be bought if the American item is not produced in sufficient and reasonably available commercial quantity and of a satisfactory quality.

Co Application of Act to Manufactured Products. In the case of manufactured products, the Buy American Act applies only to the end product itself and to the components directly furnished for that end product, but does not apply to supplies that are used in the manufacture of any such components; for example, in the procurement of clothing, the law would apply to the clothing itself and to the cloth used in the manufacture of such clothing but would not apply to the yarn used in the manufacture of the cloth. It will be noted that the Act does not require that manufactured supplies be entirely manufactured from supplies mined, produced or manufactured in the United States. The Act merely requires that such supplies be manufactured "substantially all" from supplies mined, produced or manufactured in the United States. Under the current procurement regulations 20 supplies are to be considered manufactured "substan tially all" from American sources whenever the cost of the foreign supplies used in the manufacture constitutes 25% or less of the cost of all supplies used in such manufacture. The term "United States" when used in the Buy 

American Act includes the United States and "any place subject to the jurisdiction thereof #21 

19. Section 1-5, Title III, Act of 3 March, 1933 (47 Stat. 1520–1521; 

41 U.S.C. 10 a-c). 

20. ASPR 6-103.2. 

21. 47 Stat. 1520 as amended; 41 U.S.c. 10c. 87

d. Procurement Regulations; Unreasonable Costs. Although the procurement regulations change from time to time, it is interesting to note that the current procurement regulations 22 have determined by joint agreement of the Secretaries of the three Departments (Army, Navy and Air Force) that the cost is unreasonable where the lowest net cost of the U. S. supplies exceeds the lowest net cost of foreign supplies, including any tariff or duty, by 25% or more (100% in the case of foreign supplies costing $100 or less). Another requirement of the current procurement regulations makes it mandatory that the contracting officer submit the matter to the Secretary of the department concerned where the difference is more than $5,000 even though less than 25%. e.

Failure of Contractor to Comply with Act. The Buy American Act further provides that in cases where the contractor has failed to comply with the provisions of the Act as it applies to construction contracts, the Secretary of the department concerned shall make public his findings, including the name of the contractor, and no other contract for construction, alteration or repair of any public building or public work in the United States or elsewhere shall be awarded to such contractor, subcontractor, materialmen or suppliers with which such contractor is associated or affiliated, within a period of three years after such finding is made public. 

59. APPROPRIATION LIMITATION ON FOREIGN PURCHASES. The annual appropriation acts should be examined, since it has been customary to prohibit the use of Army funds for procurement of any article of food or clothing not grown or produced in the United States except (1) to the extent that the Secretary of the Army, determines that any such article cannot be procured in the United States of satisfactory quality and in sufficient quantity and at United States market prices and without unduly increasing future United States market prices as and when needed (such determinations are promulgated from time to time by the Secretary in the procurement regulations); and (2) procurements by vessels in foreign waters and emergency procurement or procurements of highly perishable foods by establishments located outside the continental United States, Hawaii and Alaska for personnel attached thereto.24

b. Penalty. The penalty for breach or violation of this warranty gives to the Government the right to annul the contract without liability, or in its discretion to deduct from the contract price the full amount of such commission, percentage or brokerage fee. The difficulty encountered in coping with the so-called #influence brokers" arises from the necessity of distinguishing a "bona fide" broker or agent or employee. This warranty provision has been interpreted by the courts on occasion and the tests to be applied were laid down as follows: (1) Is the particular agent or broker a "bona fide" one, and (2) Is the particular agent or agency "maintained" by the contractor as a matter of habit or custom for the purpose of securing business.27 It has been ruled by the Comptroller General that licensed real estate agents or brokers who had listings on available space which the Government desired to lease, and who would receive commissions from the owner for negotiating the lease, were properly within the exception to the warranty.28 A breach of the covenant against contingent fees, in addition to the other penalties indicated, might also impose criminal liability upon the contractor by virtue of the provisions of 18 U.S.C. 

1001, which makes knowing and willful falsifications or false, fictitious and fraudulent statements or representations made to any department or agency of the United States punishable by a fine of not more than $10,000 or imprisonment for not more than five years, or both. As yet, there is no legislation imposing any civil or criminal responsiblity upon so-called 25.

61. ADVANCE PAYMENTS. a. General. During World War II, author ization was given by Executive Order No. 9001 to make advance payments to contractors up to the full amounts of their contracts. An earlier and more limited authorization was obtained in the act of 2 July 1940.29 The authorization of advance payments on Government contracts has now become a permanent part of the Government's contract law.30 b. Statutory Limitations. Although the Department of the Army is authorized to make advance payments "in any amount not exceeding the contract price upon such terms as the parties shall agree ***", the statute limits the exercise of this authority to those cases in which it is found: (1) that the advance payments are made upon adequate security, (2) the Secretary of the Department concerned shall find that advance payments are in the public interest or in the interest of national defense, and (3) that the advance payments are necessary and appropriate in order to procure the required supplies or services under the contract.

C. Armed Service Policy. The current policy of the Armed Services tends to restrict still further the use of advance payments by forbidding them unless all of the following requirements are satisfieds- (1) no other contractor is able to furnish the desired supplies or services upon terms satisfactory to the department, without provision for advance payments; (2) except for non-profit research and development contracts with educational institutions, no other means of adequate financing is available to the contractor; and (3) the amount of the authorization is predicated upon the use of the contractor's own working capital to the fullest extent possible. 

d. Security Provisions; Liens in Favor of Government. The statu tory authority for making advance payments further provides -"The terms governing advance payments may include as security provision for, and upon inclusion of such provision there shall thereby be created, a lien in favor of the Government, paramount to all other liens, upon the supplies contracted for, upon the credit balance in any special account in which such payments may be deposited and upon such of the material and other property acquired for performance of the contract as the parties shall

30. See par. 5(a), Pub. Law 413, 80th Cong., relating to contracts of the Armed Services and Section 305(a), Pub. Law 152, 81st Cong., relating to procurement by other Government agencies. 

31. ASPR 3-500 through 505 incl. 90

agree." 

The current procurement regulations further augment this section by stating that advance payments should be limited to the contractor's financial needs and withdrawals from the special bank accounts provided therefor should be closely supervised. e.

Advance Payments Applicable to Termination and Settlement of 

Contracts. Another type of advance payment is established by 41 U.S. Code 

108(e), which provides for the making of advance payments upon such terms as the contracting agency may deem necessary or appropriate, as a part of the termination negotiations and settlement of contracts which are terminated for the convenience of the Government 

62. NEUTRALITY ACT OF 1939. The Neutrality Act of 193933 forbids the purchase by the Government of arms, ammunition or implements of war, which are listed in the Presidential Proclamation No. 2549 of 9 April 1942, from any manufacturer, exporter, importer or dealer who has failed to register with the Secretary of State as required by the Act. Thus in contracts for the purchase of any of the items listed in the Presidential Proclamation, a clause should be inserted in the contract stating that the contractor either (a) represents that it is properly registered under said Act and agrees to furnish satisfactory evidence thereof, or (b) represents that it is not subject to said Act and agrees to furnish satisfactory evidence thereof. 

63. THE RENEGOTIATION ACT OF 1948. Introduction. Excessive profit-taking has occurred in wars throughout history but no really effective efforts to cope with exorbitant profits were undertaken prior to World War II, when for the first time definitive legislation was enacted to prevent the evil. The new concept was termed "Renegotiation". 

Renegotiation consists of statutory repricing of a contractor's government business on an overall, fiscal year basis, after an examination of the contractor's performance and the financial results thereof. Excessive profits, if any, are covered into the Treasury pursuant to agreement or unilateral order. b.

Determination of Amount of Excess Profits; Factors to be Con sidered. In arriving at the determination of the amount of excessive profits the Renegotiation Act of 19434 required the following factors to be considered: 

32. 58 Stat. 655; 61 Stat. 951; E. 0. No. 9809, 12 Dec 1946. 

33. 54 Stat. 10; 22 U.S.C. 452(g). 

34. 56 Stat. 245 as amended; 50 U.S.C. App. 1191. 91

1. efficiency of contractor, with particular regard to attainment of quantity and quality production, reduction of costs and econamy in the use of materials, facilities and manpower; 

2. reasonableness of costs and profits, with particular regard to volume of production, normal pre-war earnings, and comparison of war and peacetime production; 

30 amount and source of public and private capital employed and net worth; 

4. extent of risk assumed, including risk incident to reasonable pricing policies; 

5. nature and extent of contribution to the war effort including inventive and development contribution and cooperation with the Government and other contractors in supplying technical assistance; 

6. character of business, including complexity of manufacturing technique, character and extent of subcontracting, and rate of turnover; 

7. such other factors the consideration of which the public interest and fair and equitable dealing may require, which factors shall be published in the regulations of the Board from time to time as adopted. C.

The World Expiration; Renegotiation Act of 1943 and Amendments. 

War II Renegotiation Acts expired by their terms on 31 December 1945. 

64. THE RENEGOTIATION ACT OF 1948. With the advent of the increased procurement for National Defense incident to the "Cold War", Congress enacted the Renegotiation Act of 1948The coverage of the 1948 act as originally enacted is indicated in the following excerpt from the law. 

"Sec. 3 (Supplemental National Defense Appropriation Act, 

1948) (a) all contracts in excess of $1,000 entered into under the authority of this Act, obligating funds appropriated hereby, obligating funds consolidated by this Act with funds appropriated hereby, or entered into through contract authorizations herein granted, and all subcontracts thereunder in excess of $1,000 shall contain the following article: 

35. Pub. Law 547, 80th Cong. 92

-- 
'Renegotiation Article This contract is subject to the Renegotiation Act of 1948 and the contractor hereby agrees to insert a like article in all contracts or purchase orders to make or furnish any article or to perform all or any part of the work required for the performance of this contract." 

Other provisions of the Act authorized the Secretary of Defense to conduct renegotiations, and to eliminate excessive profits, either by mutual agreement with the contractor, or by order which could be appealed first to the Tax Court and then further in the manner provided by law for appeals from Tax Court decisions.36 The Secretary of Defense was directed by the Act to promulgate and publish in the Federal Register regulations interpreting and applying the Act, and prescribing standards and procedures for determining and eliminating excessive profits, using, so far as he deems practicable, the principles and procedures of the World War II Renegotiation Acts, having regard for the difference between current economic conditions and those prevailing during the period 1942 to 

The Secretary of Defense has issued directives under which there has been created the Military Renegotiation Policy and Review Board and also an Armed Services Renegotiation Board. Power has been delegated by the Secretary of Defense to the Policy and Review Board to promulgate regulations, assign contractors for renegotiation purposes, review determinations, audit contractors' books, and to exempt from renegotiation contracts or subcontracts by general classes or types. The Armed Services 

Renegotiation Board is comprised of three Divisions, Army, Navy and Air 

Force. To the Chairmen of the respective Divisions, the Secretary of 

Defense has delegated power to conduct renegotiation with assigned contractors, make reviewable determinations and to audit contractors' books.

The power to exempt individual contracts or subcontracts has been delegated to the respective Secretaries of the Army, Navy and Air Force. 

Military Renegotiation Regulations have been duly promulgated and are now available from the Superintendent of Documents. 

The Renegotiation Act of 1948 has been twice extended. By enactment approved 25 June 1948,37 the Secretary of Defense was authorized to make subject to the Act any Army, Navy or Air Force contracts, obligating fiscal 1949 funds, for the procurement of ships, aircraft, aircraft parts or construction of facilities or installations outside continental United States. By appropriate directive, contracts for Navy or Air Force aircraft or aircraft parts have been subjected to renegotiation under the above authorization, 36. Subchapter B of Chapter 5 of the Internal Revenue Code. 

37. Section 401, Second Deficiency Appropriation Act, 1948 (Pub. Law 785, 

80th Cong.). 93

The recently enacted National Military Establishment Appropriation 

Act38 contains a provision subjecting all Army, Navy and Air Force negotiated contracts entered into during fiscal 1950, and subcontracts thereunder, to renegotiation, if the contract or subcontract is in excess of $1,000. The last above mentioned Appropriation Act suspended the application of the Vinson-Trammell Act39 (discussed in paragraph 65 hereof) to any contract or subcontract which is subject to the Renegotiation Act of 1948.

There are mandatory exemptions under the 1948 Act which are the counterpart of the mandatory exemptions under the World War II Acts. 

These mandatory exemptions cover in a general way, contracts with governments and governmental agencies, contracts for unprocessed products of mines, oil or gas wells, or timber, contracts for agricultural commodities in their raw or natural state, and contracts with tax exempt contractors. 

Subcontracts under exempt contracts are also exempt. 

Even though a contractor has contracts subject to renegotiation, the 1948 Act relieves the contractor from submitting its defense business to renegotiation, if the aggregate of the contractor's renegotiable receipts or accruals during the contractor's fiscal year is less than $100,000. 

65. VINSON-TRAMMELL ACT. The Vinson-Trammell Act40 was the first legislation enacted in peacetime which was designed to limit profits arising from the sale of munitions to the Government. This Act originally imposed profit limitations on vessel contracts of the Navy and was extended by amendment to cover also aircraft contracts. The profit limitation im posed by the Vinson-Trammell Act allows a maximum of 10% profit on the contract price of Naval vessels and subcontractor s41 thereunder, completed by an individual contractor in a given year, and allows a maximum of 12% profit with respect to aircraft contracts and subcontracts.

The Vinson-Trammel Act was suspended as to any contract or subcontract entered into during a taxable year during which the excess profits tax was applicable. Contracts for vessels and aircraft entered into after 

31 December 1945 are generally subject to the provisions of the Act.

CHAPTER 10 CHANGES AND MODIFICATIONS 

66. GENERAL. Alteration may be made to the terms of a contract either in accordance with the terms of the contract or without said terms where found to be desirable or necessary during the life of the contract. Those made within the terms of the contract are made pursuant to clauses contained in the contract, such as; the changes clause, the extras clause, or variation in quantity clause. Those without said terms are known as modifications. 

Amendments or changes 

67. MODIFICATION. a. Authority to modify. 

in the terms of a contract which are not authorized by the contract itself, but are found to be desirable or necessary during the life of the contract, are made by supplemental agreements. Such agreements must be assented to by both contracting parties and contain all the essential elements of a valid contract. The Government has the same power to modify its written agreements as has a private individual.“ 

The head of an executive department of the Government or his duly authorized representative may enter into a supplemental agreement with a contractor modifying the terms of a contract. However, a number of conditions on the use of supplemental agreements modifying 

Government contracts must be observed. b. Government to benefit. No Government agent has authority to waive a vested contractual right of the Government. Therefore, a supplemental agreement is valid only,,if based upon a new consideration or benefit moving to the Government. A contract may not be modified after having been fully performed, since in such case there can be no new consideration to the Government. 4 1. 2. 3. 4.

An oral agreement by a Government agent to extend the time for performance has, however, been held binding. Salmon v. United States, 19 Wall (86 U.S.) 17 (1873); Maryland Steel Co. v. United States, 235 U.S. 451 (1915). 

2 Comp. Dec. 182 (1895); 21 Op. Atty. Gen. 207 (1895); Kling Bros. & 

Co. v. United States, 89 Ct. Cl. 329 (1939). 

20 Comp. Gen. 703 (1941); American Sales Co. v. United States, 32 F (2d) 141 (C.C.A. 5th 1929); Bausch and Lomb Optical Co. v. United 

States, 78 Ct. Cl. 584 (1934); Yale and Towne Manufacturing Co. v. 

United States, 67 Ct. Cl. 618 (1929); 20 Comp. Gen. 448 (1941); 

22 Comp. Gen. 915 (1943).

Yale and Towne Manufacturing Co. v. United States, 67 Ct. Cl. 618 

(1929); 25 Comp. Dec. 764 (1919). 95

C. Limitation on extent of modification. An existing contract may not, by supplemental agreement, be transformed into a wholly new or different contract. A supplemental agreement may not be used to defeat the statutory requirement of advertising and competitive bidding. An exception is made where the new work was not contemplated when the original contract was made, and is so inseparably a part of the work already done that only the old contractor can satisfactorily perform it.5 It appears that the exception would apply also if the contract were of a type obtained by negotiation pursuant to law rather than by advertising and competitive bidding. 

d. Administrative functions 

A supplemental agreement may not be used to perform non-administrative functions. The interpretation of a contract and the correction of mutual mistakes after the contract has been executed are not within the province of a contracting agency, and supplemental agreements may not be used to accomplish these objectives.6 

68. EFFECT ON SURETY'S LIABILITY. The consent of the surety guaranteeing performance of a contract is not necessary for changes authorized in the contract. However, such consent should be obtained for supplemental contracts modifying the original contract. A paid surety is generally not released from his obligation by reason of an alteration in the terms of a contract made without his consent unless such alteration is actually prejudicial. 

69. CHANGES, EXTRAS AND VARIATION IN QUANTITY CLAUSES. a. Changes 

Clause. The changes clauses provide that the contracting officer may at any time by written order make changes in drawings, designs and specifications within the scope of the contract, and in supply contracts provision is made for changes in methods of shipment and packing, and changes in place of delivery. The standard changes clauses permit changes within the general scope of the contract. They do not authorize the Government to remold the contract at will.7 If such changes increase or decrease the cost or the time required for the performance of the contract, an equitable adjustment shall be made. Any claim for adjustment must be asserted within a limited number of days from the date the change is ordered (30 days for fixed-price supply contracts)8 as stated in the clause. If the parties fail to agree upon the adjustment, an appeal may be taken under the "Disputes" clause. If 5.

C. Variation in Quantity Clause. Fixed-price supply contracts must contain a clause which provides that no variation in the quantity of any item called for by this contract will be accepted unless such variation has been caused by conditions of loading, shipping, or packing, or allowances in manufacturing processes, and then only to the extent, if any, specified elsewhere in this contract.11 Some Government contracts have included a clause allowing, unless otherwise specified, 10% variation in the quantities specified. The clause authorized such variation if caused by loading, shipping, packing, or allowances in manufacturing processes.

In absence of such a provision an order for extras would be required. If an additional quantity is furnished under the contract as a result of conditions other than those specified above and if any amount in excess of 10% caused by the specified conditions is furnished an extra order would still be needed.

CHAPTER 11 TAXES SECTION I. NATURE, PURPOSE, AND LIMITATIONS 70. PURPOSE AND SCOPE OF CHAPTER. The purpose of this chapter is to direct the attention of Department of the Army personnel to the incidence of Federal, state and local taxes on Department of the Army contract and procurement activities. This is done with a view to insuring that direct and indirect expenditures for Federal, state and local taxes are made in accordance with legal requirements of the various taxing jurisdictions in order to limit Army expenditures to the lowest amount compatible with effective operation.

In the conduct of contract and procurement activities, the Department of the Army is concerned constantly with problems involving the application of Federal, state and local taxes to the purchases of supplies, material, and equipment.1 The Judge Advocate General seeks to minimize, where legally possible, inroads of tax assessments upon Army appropriations. This requires the preparation of legal opinions, legislation and reports to the Congress on tax matters, and conferences relating to the application of Federal, state and local taxes to the Army, its agencies, personnel, activities and contractors. It necessitates the preparation of tax regulations and procedures for the Department of the Army, of action required with respect to tax litigation affecting the Army, and coordination of tax activities with the Department of Justice, the Treasury Department, and the Departments of the Air Force and the Navy. In order to give all persons engaged in Army contract and procurement activities efficient guides and information to enable them to effect tax savings, the Department of the Army and other agencies have prepared numerous publications as tax guides for 1. 

In that connection negotiations are conducted with Federal, state and local tax authorities and with tax administrators of manufacturing, industrial and business organizations. 98

all interested Army personnel. Commercial publications and services are also available in many offices.3 No compilation, however, is complete in detail, by virtue of the frequent changes in tax laws and regulations which affect tax law administration. Knowledge of the nature of taxes and their constitutional features is an essential prerequisite to the legal officer, who is often called upon to interpret the law for the field services and to present tax briefs to administrators and officials on behalf of the Department of the Army. In that connection, this chapter affords sufficient background to the incidence of Federal, state and local taxes on Department of the Army contract and procurement activities.

In analyzing the varied taxes which may confront Army procurement and construction personnel, consideration of tax exemptions of general applicability to all taxpayers is usually subordinated to a review of special immunities and exemptions peculiarly applicable to transactions involving the United States for the apparent reason that the Department of the Army is concerned essentially with cases where the payment of the tax ultimately falls upon the Government. 

The legal officer and all others engaged in procurement and construction activities often are in the position where they cannot readily answer questions by reference to the many tax guides and regulations which are their tools in trade. In that event the matter is usually presented through proper channels with appropriate comments to The Judge Advocate General for advice. This procedure is particularly important where (a) the applicability of the tax is not specifically discussed in the tax guides and regulations; or (b) tax authorities have adopted or may adopt a position at variance with the position taken by the Department of the Army; or (c) steps are contemplated to protect the interests of the Army in connection with payment of taxes; or (d) from a review of all tax guides and regulations it appears that there exists a possibility of obtaining refunds of taxes already paid, the economic burden of which has been borne by the United States. 

In order to insure uniformity of action on behalf of the Army in tax matters, direct negotiation by Army or contractor personnel with tax officials for the purpose of obtaining exemption or refund or to determine the applicability of any tax is not undertaken except upon ex press authority of The Judge Advocate General.6 The following discussion in Section I will describe generally the nature and purpose of taxation, the principal limitations upon state taxation, express or implied, in the Federal Constitution, including (a) interstate and foreign commerce, (b) import and export duties, 5. 71.

DEFINITION. Taxes may be defined as the burdens, charges or impositions put or set upon persons or property for public uses. The 

Supreme Court of the United States has said: "A tax is a pecuniary burden laid on individuals or property for the purpose of supporting the government."10 It has also stated: "A tax is an enforced contribution to provide for the support of the government. בנז Taxation has also been defined as "The act of laying a tax or imposing these burdens or charges upon persons or property within the state. It is the process or means by which the taxing power is exercised. "12 Taxation proper involves the process of not only imposing the tax but also that of collecting it. The process terminates with the payment of the proceeds into the funds for which the tax is levied and all further steps in the use of the funds come within the field of public finance. 

72. NATURE AND CHARACTER. Taxation in its true sense is but the compensation paid by the individual for protection to his person or his property. Theoretically, where there is no benefit, there is no power to tax, but benefit is generally presumed from a general tax for a public purpose. The essential characteristics of taxes are that they are (a) enforced contributions (b) exacted by a sovereign power (c) under the taxing power (d) for the purpose of raising revenue to be used for public purposes.

As taxes are enforced contributions and do not rest on a 

Chapter I, The Scope of the Federal Taxing Power; State Tax Immunity as Compared to That of the United States, a study made by the Department of Justice, 1938; Taxation of Government Fondholders and 

Employees. The Immunity Rule and the Sixteer.th Amendment.

contractual basis, the courts usually hold that taxes are not debts, unless expressly so provided by s tatute.14 Likewise, unless the taxing statute so declares, it is usually held that (1) the claim for taxes does not create the relationship of debtor and creditor, 15 (2) the taxes are not assignable as ordinary debts,16 (3) the taxpayers and the sovereign power may not subject taxes to set-off17 and (4) overdue taxes do not bear interest.18 Special assessments, fines, forfeitures, penalties, the taking of property by eminent domain and other contributions, charges, or burdens imposed for particular purposes or under particular powers or functions of the Government may be distinguished from taxes. 

Special assessments are imposed on the theory that a special private benefit accrues therefrom and unlike taxes, their validity depends on the apparent relationship between the assessment and the benefit.19 Fines, forfeitures and penalties are imposed as punishments because of the commission of an unlawful act or the failure to perform a required act. 

They are not imposed under the taxing power and are not for the purpose 20 of raising revenue for public purposes. Eminent domain is in the nature of a forced sale of property to the sovereign power. It affects only the individual concerned rather than all persons or property in the same class, and since property taken by eminent domain is not taken as the owner's contribution to the public burden, just compensation must be made therefor.21 Fees paid to public officers for services rendered and charges imposed under the police power of the state for the purpose

73. VALIDITY OF PURPOSE. 

pose of raising money which is to be expended for the public use.

If & tax is levied for other than a public use, it amounts to the taking of property without due process of law, and on this ground every taxirg statute is subject to judicial review. 28 Although taxes may not be levied for private purposes, the fact that private interests are incidentally benefited will not invalidate a tax. In practice, the courts resolve all presumptions in favor of the validity of taxing statutes. 

As all debts incurred by the state and all contractual obligations entered into must ultimately be met by taxation, it follows that unless debts or contractual obligations are incurred for public purposes, they are not valid. 

74. "HAT CONSTITUTES PUBLIC USE. To constitute a public use, the proceeds of the tex must be used for the support of the Government or 36 for some of the recognized objects of Government.- In determining what constitutes public use, the courts are governed mainly by custom and usage but previous decisions are not always authoritative since custom and usage change with the years. One of the necessary elements of a public use is that the benefit bestowed must be upon those who will enjoy it as members of the public and not as individuals.21 Public morey may validly be expended for the erection of public buildings, schools and public institutions. Fine distinctions have sometimes been drawn by the courts in deciding what constitutes a public use. For example, it has been held that public funds may be used to support universities but public money cannot validly be appropriated to provide for scholarships for the support of students in public schools.32 Some states have constitutional provisions prohibiting the expenditure of public funds for religious purposes. While a state cannot use the funds raised by taxation to engage in a private business merely for profit, it is well established that the state may use such funds for the purpose of furnishing certain necessities, such as water, gas or light.33 Power to engage in these enterprises is generally upheld wherever the enterprise is of such 

28. Robbins v. Kadyk, 312 I11. 290, 143 N.E. Ɛ63 (1924). 29. 

31. 32. 33. 

Stanley v. Jeffries, 86 Mont. 114, 284 Pec. 134 (1929). 

Beach v. Bradstreet, 85 Conn. 344, 82 Atl. 1030 (1912).

In re Opinion of Justices, 231 Mass. 603, 122 N.E. 763 (1919). 

State v. Switzler, 143 Mo. 287, 45 S.". 245 (1898).
