N.Y. 257, 46 N.W. 499 (1897), upholding the building of a subway with public funds. 104

a character that it cannot be undertaken by individuals without the aid of a franchise from the state. The promotion of agriculture is also recognized as being of public interest; it has even been held that in the case of a drought, a state may raise money by taxation to provide seed grain to indigent farmers. It is fundamental that the tax must not only be levied for a public use but that it must also be levied for the use of the public of the district. It is clear that one taxing district, whether it be state, county or municipality, cannot be taxed for the benefit of another district. 

A tax which benefits one district will not be declared invalid, however, because it incidentally results in a benefit to the people of another district. Thus, a municipal corporation may be authorized to expend its funds for a bridge even if such bridge is not wholly within the territorial limits of the municipality. While a municipality cannot be compelled to expend municipal funds for county purposes, this does not mean that a municipality cannot appropriate funds for the establishment of a state institution within its limits, since such an institution will be of especial benefit to the people of the municipality. 

75. CLASSIFICATION OF TAXES. Taxes may be classified according to subject, basis, purpose and other differences. The most common methods of classification are (a) as property, excise or poll taxes, (b) as direct or indirect taxes and (c) as specific or ad valorem taxes. Property taxes are those assessed upon all the property or a certain class of property within a given jurisdiction. Generally, such taxes are also classified as direct taxes and as ad valorem taxes. 

Excise taxes include every form of charge imposed by public authority for the purpose of raising revenue upon the performance of an act, the enjoyment of a privilege or the engaging in an occupation. They are not imposed on persons or property as such. Excise taxes are generally classified as indirect taxes. Such taxes are sometimes ad valorem and sometimes specific in nature. Poll or capitation taxes are: fixed taxes upon each person or upon all persons of a certain class, resident within a territory. Such taxes are generally classified as direct taxes and are invariably specific. Direct taxes are assessed upon 34. 35. 36. 37. 

State v. Nelson, 1 N.D. 88, 45 N.W. 33 (1890). 

Amos v. Matthews, 99 Fla. 1, 126 So. 308 (1930). 

State v. Williams, 68 Conn. 151, 35 Atl. 24 (1896). 

Sinclair v. City of Lincoln et al., 101 Neb. 163, 162 N.W. 488 (1917). 105

38 the property, person, business, income, etc., of those who are to pay them.

Indirect taxes are paid by one person but are in effect borne by another person, the ultimate consumer. In addition to the usage of "direct" and "indirect" in connection with property, excise and poll taxes above, the terms have perhaps a more authoritative usage under the Federal Constitution, providing that "no capitation, or other direct tax shall be laid unless in proportion to the census Although the construction of the terms in each instance is helpful, the two usages must not be confused. Specific taxes are taxes of a fixed amount by the head, weight or number, requiring no assessment other than a listing of subjects to be taxed. Ad valorem taxes are taxes of a fixed proportion of the value of the property. 

Poll taxes are invariably specific; property taxes are usually ad valorem, although they may be specific in certain instances; excise taxes are commonly specific, although the line of demarcation becomes extremely thin when an excise tax is measured by the actual value of the property. 

76. POWER TO TAX. The power to tax is an inherent attribute of sovereignty and is possessed by the state without being expressly conferred by the people. As the power to tax flows from the power to make laws, the taxing power is vested in the law-making body of the state. 

The executive branch of the Government administers the laws; the judicial branch construes them; but under the separation of powers theory of Government found in the United States, neither the executive nor the judiciary can exercise the taxing power. The only limitations on the taxing power, outside of those limitations arising from the nature of taxes, are those contained in the Federal and state constitu tions. The U. S. Supreme Court in Haavik v. Alaska Fackers' Association39 

said: "Unless restrained by constitutional provision, the sovereign has power to tax all persons and property actually within its jurisdiction and enjoying the benefit and protection of its laws." Federal restrictions placed on state taxing power relate to (a) interstate and foreign commerce, (b) obligation of contracts, (c) import or export duties, (d) tonnage duties and interstate compacts, (e) full faith and credit to acts, records and judicial proceedings of other states, (f) privileges and immunities accorded to citizens of other states, (g) due process and equal protection of the laws, (h) transportation of intoxicating liquors and (i) Federal instrumentalities and obligations.40 41 Restrictions imposed by state constitutions vary among the states. 

Some of the most common state constitutional restrictions pertain to 38. 39. 40. 41.

U. S. Const., Art. II, sec. 3, Cl. 2. 

263 .S. 510 (1924).

For a full discussion of these restrictions, see the Federal Constitutional Restrictions division, par. 100, State Tax Guide (Commerce 

Clearing House).

For constitutional provisions to be found in each state, see the "By 

States" division, par. 1-000, State Tax Guide, supra. 106

(a) equality and uniformity in taxation, (b) taxation according to value, (c) limitations on rate or amount, (d) double taxation, (e) taxation for public purposes only and (f) delegation of taxing power. In order to conform to a constitutional provision requiring equality and uniformity in taxation, a tax must fall impartially on all persons and property in the same class. The courts usually hold, however, that substantial uniformity is sufficient to meet this requirement and have liberally construed the statutes to achieve this end. It is sometimes stated that uniformity provisions and the Fourteenth Amendment to the Federal Constitution are coextensive. 43

In general, state constitutional provisions providing for equality and uniformity have been held applicable to property taxes only, 44 but in a few states, income taxes at graduated rates have been held invalid as violating such provisions.45 Taxation according to value means that all property which is taxed shall be taxed according to value and not specifically. Unless the Constitution requires that property be assessed at its full value, the taxation of property at less than its full value is not prohibited if such rule is applied without discrimination. In general, constitutional provisions requiring taxation according to value are held applicable to property taxes only. 47 In the absence of constitutional restriction, the amount of taxes that may be validly imposed is limited only by the needs of the Government. A few states, however, have adopted constitutional provisions limiting the rate or amount of tax that may be levied in any tax year. In such states, provision is generally made for the imposition of taxes in excess of such limitation if specifically authorized by the people. Constitutional prohibitions against double taxation are frequent. Such prohibition means that a taxing authority may not twice tax property for the same purpose during the same taxing period. In the absence of such a constitutional restriction, either express or implied, property may be subject to double taxation; 48 however, equality and uniformity provisions should be borne in mind in this connection. 42. 43. 44. 45. 46. 47. 48. 46

Constitutional provisions requiring that taxes may be imposed for public purposes only are found in some states. The power to tax belongs exclusively to the legislative branch of the Government, but constitutional provisions allowing the delegation of the taxing power are frequent. In the absence of such a provision, the power to tax cannot be delegated. The intention to relinquish the power of taxation, however, is never presumed. 

77. FEDERAL CONSTITUTIONAL RESTRICTIONS IN GENERAL. As the 

Federal Constitution and the amendments thereto constitute the supreme law of the land, 51 superior to either state constitutions or statutes, no consideration of the latter is adequate without an examination of the principal provisions, express or implied, of the Federal Constitution affecting state taxation. In scope and character, these Federal constitutional provisions are never granting or enabling provisions, but restrictive, and within these limitations the state taxing power is as broad as that of sovereignty. As said in Shaffer v. Carter:52 "In our system of government the States have general dominion, and, saving as restricted by particular provisions of the Federal Constitution, complete dominion over all persons, property, and business transactions within their borders; they assume and perform the duty of preserving and protecting all such persons, property, and business, and in consequence, have the power normally pertaining to governments to resort to all reasonable forms of taxation in order to defray the governmental expenses. Certainly they are not restricted to property taxation, nor to any particular form of excises." The Eleventh Amendment prohibiting suits in the Federal courts against a state by citizens

Federal courts will not decide questions of constitutionality, such as the division of the state and Federal taxing power, until the application of the taxing statute has been determined by the proper state courts. As tax problems often arise in connection with the 

District of Columbia, the unique nature of its corporate existence will be discussed. The District of Columbia is a corporate agent, through which the United States administers certain executive functions over the locality which includes the national capitol. The District of Columbia is a separate political community in a certain sense, and in that sense may be called a State whose sovereign power is lodged in the Government of the United States, although not strictly a State 56 within the meaning of that term as used in the Constitution. The 

District of Columbia is a municipal corporation, with the right to sue and be sued, although its organization is peculiar, there being no general organic law covering all of the ordinary powers usually conferred in the creation of a municipal corporation, e.g., no formal charter.57 The District of Columbia, unlike the Territories, is a permanent part of the United States the very heart of the Union, over which the Congress has permanent and exclusive power of legislation-- the combined powers of National and State governments where legislation is possible.58

The Congress legislates for the District with the same power as the legislative assemblies of the States, and in doing so it acts as the legislature of the Union. The failure of the Congress to exercise its power in no way impairs the grant, but it must be exercised subject to the limitations contained in the Constitution.59 The District of Columbia is a municipal corporation, and as such it may be invested with legislative authority; but it can exercise only those powers which are expressly conferred by statute, which must be of a strictly municipal nature. 53. 54. 55. 56. 57. 58. 59. 60.

78. INTERSTATE AND FOREIGN COLLERCE. The Congress has the power 

"To regulate commerce with foreign nations, and among the several states, and with the Indian tribes. "61 The commerce clause is closely related to the imports and exports clause, in so far as foreign commerce is involved. Although dual in form, the question may be treated as a single one, since it is obvious that an impost upon exports and a regulation of foreign commerce may be regarded as interchangeable terms. The imposition of a percentage upon each dollar of the gross transactions in foreign commerce seems to be, by its necessary effect, a tax upon such commerce, and therefore a regulation of it; and for the same reason, to be in effect an impost or duty upon exports.62 The commerce clause in some respects is separable into its interstate and foreign phases, but this is due principally to the overlapping of the foreign commerce and imports and exports clauses and the special rules applicable to the latter, which are more fully brought out in connection with the latter. 

Considering the commerce clause alone, the interstate and foreign phases differ slightly and will consequently be discussed together. In both cases the power of the Congress is plenary and exclusive. As said in 

Board of Trustees of University of Illinois v. United States: .63 "The words of the Constitution 'comprehend every species of commercial intercourse between the United States and foreign nations. No sort of trade can be carried on between this country and any other, to which this power does not extend. 164 It is an essential attribute of the power that it is exclusive and plenary. As an exclusive power, its exercise may not be limited, qualified, or impeded to any extent by state action.65 

The power is buttressed by the express provision of the Constitution denying to the States authority to lay imposts or duties on imports or exports without the consent of the Congress. Article I, § 10, cl. 2." 

Applied to taxation, these principles have been construed to mean that any taxation which imposes a substantial burden upon interstate commerce constitutes an attempted regulation thereof by the state and consequently is invalid. In Fostal Telegraph Cable Co. v. Adams,66 this was aptly stated as follows: "It is settled that where, by way of duties laid on the transportation of the subjects of interstate commerce, or on the receipts derived therefrom, or on the occupation or business of carrying it on, a tax is levied by a State on interstate commerce, such taxation

While the imposition of like taxes upon the internal commerce of a state may be entitled to considerable weight in determining whether there has been an attempt to discriminate against interstate commerce, such imposition is not important where the tax in question results in a substantial burden upon interstate commerce. In the Case of the 

State Freight Tax, 

67 it was said: "The State may tax its internal commerce, but if an act to tax interstate or foreign commerce is unconstitutional, it is not cured by including in its provisions subjects within the domain of the State." Notwithstanding the very broad scope of the constitutional provision outlined above, certain taxation affecting interstate commerce is nevertheless permitted, as stated in 

The Delaware Railroad Tax:68 "The tax imposed by the act in question affects commerce among the states and impedes the transit of persons and property from one state to another just in the same way, and in no other, that taxation of any kind necessarily increases the expenses attendant upon the use or possession of the thing taxed. That taxation produces this result of itself constitutes no objection to its constitutionality. As was very justly observed by this court in a recent 69 case, 'Every tax upon personal property, or upon occupations, business, or franchises, affects more or less the subjects, and the operations of commerce. Yet it is not everything that affects commerce that amounts to a regulation of it, within the meaning of the Constitution. '" The application of these principles to the facts of the cases as they arise has resulted in many very nice distinctions by the courts. This problem of interpretation is brought out in Galveston, Harrisburg, Etc., Railway Co. v. Texas: "It being once admitted, as of course it must be, that not every law that affects commerce among the States is a regulation of it in a constitutional sense, nice distinctions are to be expected. Regulation and commerce among the States both are practical rather than technical conceptions, and, naturally, their limits must be fixed by practical lines.""By whatever name the exaction may be called, if it amounts to no more than the ordinary tax upon property or a just equivalent therefor, ascertained by reference thereto, it is not open to attack as inconsistent with the Constitution. 171 The question is whether this is such a tax. It appears sufficiently, perhaps, from what has been said, that we are to look for a practical rather than a logical or philosophical distinction. Yet the distinction is not without sense. legislature is trying simply to value property, it is less likely to attempt to or effect injurious regulation than when it is aiming directly at the receipts from interstate commerce. A practical line can be drawn by taking the whole scheme of taxation into account. 

That must be done by this court as best it can. Neither the state courts nor the legislatures, by giving the tax a particular name or by the use of some form of words, can take away our duty to consider its nature and effect. If it bears upon commerce among the States so directly as to amount to a regulation in a relatively immediate way, it will not be saved by name or form. "72 One of the fundamental factors involved, assuming the right to impose certain taxes, such as property taxes, is the right of a state to exclude persons from doing local business, or to impose conditions upon the doing of local business. 

Clearly a state may impose no restrictions upon the carrying on of interstate business. "We are aware of no decision by this court holding that a State may, by any device or in any way, whether by a license tax, or in the form of a 'fee,' or otherwise, burden the interstate business of a corporation of another state, although the state may tax the corporation's property regularly or permanently located within its limits, where the ascertainment of the amount assessed is made 'dependent in fact on the value of its property situated within the State. 173 

On the contrary, it is to be deduced from the adjudged cases that a corporation of one State, authorized by its charter to engage in lawful commerce among the States, may not be prevented by another State from coming into its limits for all the legitimate purposes of such commerce. 

It may go into the State without obtaining a license from it for the purposes of its interstate business, and without liability to taxation there, on account of such business. "It is true that in many cases, the general rule has been laid down that a State may, if it chooses to do so, exclude foreign corporations from its limits, or impose such terms and conditions on their doing business in the State as in its judgment may be consistent with the interests of the people. But those were cases in which the particular foreign corporation before the court was engaged in ordinary business and not directly or regularly 72. 73.

in interstate or foreign commerce."74 Neither may a state impose any conditions upon the doing of local business which result in the burdening of the interstate business of any person. "The mere fact that a corporation' is engaged in interstate commerce does not exempt its property from state taxation. It is the commerce itself which must not be burdened by state exactions which interfere with the exclusive Federal authority over it. A resort to the receipts of property or capital employed in part at least in interstate commerce, when such receipts or capital are not taxes as such but are taken as a mere measure of a tax of lawful authority within the State, has been sustained. "76 "The right of a State to exclude a foreign corporation from its borders, so long as no principle of the Federal Constitution is violated in such exclusion; has been repeatedly recognized in the decisions of this court, and the right to prescribe conditions upon which a corporation of that character may continue to do business in the State, unless some contract right in favor of the corporation prevents or some constitutional right is denied in the exclusion of such corporation, is but the correlative of the power to exclude.77

For example, a State may not say to a foreign corporation, you may do business within our borders if you permit your property to be taken without due process of law, or you may transact business in interstate commerce subject to the regulatory power of the State. To allow a State to exercise such authority would permit it to deprive of fundamental rights those entitled to the protection of the Constitution in every part of the Union. 113

In determining what taxes may lawfully be imposed, much depends in each case upon the type of tax involved. As an illustration, the widest latitude is given with respect to taxation of property used in interstate commerce. "Although the transportation of the subjects of interstate commerce or the receipts received therefrom, or the occupation or business of carrying it on, cannot be directly subjected to state taxation, yet property belonging to corporations or companies engaged in such commerce maybe; and whatever the particular form of the exaction, if it is essentially only property taxation, it will not be considered as falling within the inhibition of the Constitution. Corporations and companies engaged in interstate commerce should bear their proper proportion of the burdens of the governments under whose protection they conduct their operations, and taxation on property, collectible by the ordinary means, does not affect interstate commerce otherwise than incidentally, as all business is affected by the necessity of contributing to the support of government. 1180 "The interstate commerce clause of the Constitution does not give immunity to movable property from local taxation which is not discriminative, unless it is in actual continuous transit in interstate commerce. When it is shipped by a common carrier from one State to another, in the course of such an uninterrupted journey it is clearly immune. The doubt arises when there are interruptions in the journey and when the property in its transportation is under the complete control of the owner during the passage. If the interruptions are only to promote the safe or convenient transit, then the continuity of the interstate trip is not broken. "81 A wide latitude is likewise given the states in taxation of net income derived from interstate commerce. Net income taxes are distinguished from gross receipts or gross income taxes for this purpose in United States Glue Co. v. 

Town of Oak Creek: 82 "The difference in effect between a tax measured by gross receipts and one measured by net income, recognized by our decisions, is manifest and substantial, and it affords a convenient and workable basis of distinction between a direct and immediate burden upon the business affected and a charge that is only indirect and incidental. A tax upon gross receipts affects each transaction in proportion to its magnitude and irrespective of whether it is profitable or otherwise. Conceivably it may be sufficient to make the difference between profit and loss, or to so diminish the profit as to impede or discourage the conduct of the commerce. A tax upon the net 

80. Adams Express Co. v. Ohio, 165 U.S. 194 (1897). 81.

Champlain Realty Co. v. Brattleboro, 260 U.S.366 (1922). Compare 

Bacon v. Illinois, 227 U.S. 504 (1912). 

82. 247 U.S. 321 (1918). 114

profits has not the same deterrent effect, since it does not arise at all unless a gain is shown over and above expenses and losses, and the tax cannot be heavy unless the profits are large. Such a tax, when imposed upon net incomes from whatever source arising, is but a method of distributing the cost of government, like a tax upon property, or upon franchises treated as property; and if there be no discrimination against interstate commerce, either in the admeasurement of the tax or in the means adopted for enforcing it, it constitutes one of the ordinary and general burdens of government, from which persons and corporations otherwise subject to the jurisdiction of the States are not exempted by the Federal Constitution because they happen to be engaged in commerce among the States." Still different considerations apply in cases where the states are extending some facility for which compensation may reasonably be exacted. "While a State may not lay a tax on the privilege of engaging in interstate commerce, it may impose even upon motor vehicles engaged exclusively in interstate commerce a charge, as compensation for the use of the public highways, which is a fair contribution to the cost of constructing and maintaining them and of regulating the traffic thereon.84 As such a charge is a direct burden on interstate commerce, the tax cannot be sustained unless it appears affirmatively, in some way, that it is levied only as compensation for use of the highways or to defray the expense of regulating motor traffic. This may be indicated by the nature of the imposition, such as a mileage tax directly proportioned to the use, 85 or by the express allocation of the proceeds of the tax to highway purposes, as in Clark v. Poor,supra, or otherwise... But the mere fact that the tax falls upon one who uses the highway is not enough to give it presumptive validity. 1186 83

FULL FAITH AND CREDIT. "Full Faith and Credit shall be given in each State to the public Acts, Records, and judicial Proceedings of every other State. And the Congress may by general Laws prescribe the Manner in which such Acts, Records and Proceedings shall be proved, and the Effect thereof."101 With respect to taxation, the prime question for many years has been as to the enforcement of the claims of one state for taxes in the courts of another state and the effect which decisions of one state upon tax matters within its jurisdiction must be given in other states. In the case of Milwaukee County v. M. E. White Co.,102 the effect which must be given judgments for taxes is fully discussed. 

The faith and credit required to be given to judgments does not depend on the Constitution alone. Article 4, section 1, not only commands that full faith and credit shall be given in each state to the public acts, records, and judicial proceedings of every other state but it adds that the Congress may by general laws prescribe the manner in which such acts, records and proceedings shall be proved, and the effect thereof. And the Congress has exercised this power, by act of 

26 May 1790,103 which provides the manner of proof of judgments of one state in the courts of another, and specifically directs that judgments "shall have such faith and credit given to them in every court within the United States as they have by law or usage in the courts of the State from which they are taken." Such exception as there may be to this allinclusive command is one which is implied from the nature of our dual system of Government, and recognizes that consistently with the fullfaith and credit clause there may be limits to the extent to which the policy of one state, in many respects sovereign, may be subordinated to the policy of another. That there are exceptions has often been pointed out. Without attempting to say what their limits may be, it has been assumed for present purposes that the command of the Constitution and of the statute is not all-embracing, and direct inquiry to the question whether a state to which a judgment for taxes is taken may have a policy against its enforcement meriting recognition as a permissible limitation upon the full-faith and credit clause. Of that question the court is the final arbiter. It is said that in answering it the court should examine the record which supports the judgment and refuse to give 100. 

Peck & Co. v. Lowe, 247 U.S. 165 (1918); Feople ex rel. Stafford v. Travis, 231 N.Y. 339, 132 N.E. 109 (1921). 

132 N.E. 109 (1921). See also McGoldrick v. Gulf Oil Corp., 309 U.S. 414 (1940); Richfield Oil Corporation v. State Board of Equalization, 329 U.S. 69 (1946). 

101. 

102. 296 U.S. 268 (1935).

U. S. Const., Art. IV, sec. 1. 

103. 

R. S. 905; 28 U.S.C. 687. 117

credit to the judgment, if the cause of action upon which it is founded is one which it would not enforce, and appellee urges that a suit for taxes imposed by state statute will not be entertained outside the taxing state. It has often been said, and in a few cases held, that statutes imposing taxes are not entitled to full faith and credit. Other obligations to pay money arising under the statutes of one state must be given recognition in courts of another. 81. 

PRIVILEGES AND IMMUNITIES. "The citizens of each state shall be entitled to all privileges and immunities of citizens in the several states. "104 The privileges and immunities clause, which is to be distinguished from the equal protection clause, is well out lined in Travis v. Yale : Tovme lifg. Co., as follows: 105 "The purpose of the provision came under consideration in Paul v. Virginia,106 where the court, speaking by Mr. Justice Field, said: 'It was undoubtedly the object of the clause in question to place the citizens of each state upon the same footing with citizens of other states, so far as the advantages resulting from citizenship in those states are concerned. It relieves them from the disabilities of alienage in other states; it inhibits discriminating legislation against them by other states; it gives the right of free ingress into other states, and egress from them; it insures to them in other states the same freedom possessed by the citizens of those states in the acquisition and enjoyment of property and in the pursuit of happiness; and it secures to them in other states the equal protection of their laws. It has been justly said that no provision in the Constitution has tended so strongly to constitute the citizens of the United States one people as this."" 

And in Ward v. Maryland,107 holding a discriminatory state tax upon nonresident traders to be void, the court, by lir. Justice Clifford, said: "Beyond doubt those words privileges and immunities are words of very comprehensive meaning, but it will be sufficient to say that the clause plainly and unmistakably secures and protects the right of a citizen of one state to pass into any other state of the Union for the purpose of engaging in lawful commerce, trade, or business without molestation; to acquire personal property; to take and hold real estate; to maintain actions in the courts of the state; and to be exempt from any higher taxes or excises than are imposed by the state upon its own citizens." "Of course the terms 'resident' and 'citizen' are not synonymous, and in some cases the distinction is important; but a general taxing scheme such as the one under consideration, if it discriminates against all non-residents, has the necessary 108 

104. U. S. Const., Art. IV, sec. 2, cl. 1.

effect of including in the discrimination those who are citizens of other states; and if there be no reasonable ground for the diversity of treatment, it abridges the privileges and immunities to which such citizens are entitled." The limits to the scope of the provision are equally well set forth in Shaffer v. Carter:1 

109 "That a State, consistently with the Federal Constitution, may not prohibit the citizens of other States from carrying on legitimate business within its borders like its own citizens, of course is granted; but it does not follow that the business of nonresidents may not be required to make a ratable contribution in taxes for the support of the government. On the contrary, the very fact that a citizen of one State has the right to hold property or carry on an occupation or business in another is a very reasonable ground for subjecting such non-resident, although not personally, yet to the extent of his property held, or his occupation or business carried on the rein, to a duty to pay taxes not more onerous in effect than those imposed under like circumstances upon citizens of the latter State. Section 2 of Art. IV of the Constitution entitles him to the privileges and immunities of a citizen, but no more; not to an entire immunity from taxation, nor to any preferential treatment as compared with resident citizens. It protects him against discriminatory taxation, but gives him no right to be favored by discrimination or exemption."110 The Departments of the Army and Air Force have expressed the following view with respect to the state income liability of military personnel temporarily residing in Maryland: 111 "1. State, Territory, Possession, or political subdivision of any of the foregoing, or in the District of Columbia, while and solely by reason of being so absent.112 This will not prevent such a person from acquiring a new residence or domicile if, with the intention of acquiring or establishing a new residence or domicile, he takes affirmative steps to accomplish that purpose. "2. A person who is not a resident or domiciliary of a taxing jurisdiction under the rule set forth in paragraph 1 is not subject to taxation, by such jurisdiction, on his income or gross income, except such income or gross income as is derived from sources within such jurisdiction. 82. FEDERAL INSTRUMENTALI TIES AND OBLIGATIONS. Although nowhere expressed in the Constitution, the principle was established at an early datel14 that neither the state nor Federal Government could tax the property, obligations, instrumentalities, .officers, and the like, necessary to the conduct of the governmental functions of the other. As the exemption is based on the Federal or co-ordinate nature of the respective sovereignties, the principle applies similarly in the cases of the state or Federal Government with respect to each other. "That the power to tax involves the power to destroy; that the power to destroy may defeat and render useless the power to create; that there is a plain repugnance, in conferring on one government a power to control the constitutional measures of another, which other, with respect to those very measures, is declared to be supreme over that which exerts the control, are propositions not to be denied. "115

It was stated in Graves et al. v. People ex rel. O'Keefe, 116 wherein it was held that there was no implied constitutional immunity from income taxation of employees of the state or Federal Governments or their instrumentalities: "As that federal] government derives its authority wholly from powers delegated to it by the Constitution, its every action within its constitutional power is governmental action, and since Congress is made the sole judge of what powers within the constitutional grant are to be exercised, all activities of government constitutionally authorized by Congress must stand on a 117 parity with respect to their constitutional immunity from taxation.

And when the national government lawfully acts through a corporation which it owns and controls, those activities are governmental functions entitled to whatever tax immunity attaches to those functions when carried on by the Government itself through its departments."118 

The theory of the tax immunity of either government, state or national, and its instrumentalities, from taxation by the other, has been rested upon an implied limitation on the taxing power of each, such as to forestall undue interference, through the exercise of that power,with the governmental activities of the other. That the two types of immunity may not, in all respects, stand on a parity has been recognized from the beginning,119 and possible differences in applications, deriving from differences in the source, nature and extent of the immunity were pointed out and discussed. "120 "Those differences have been thought to be traceable to the fact that the federal government is one of delegated powers in the exercise of which Congress is Supreme; so that every agency which Congress can constitutionally create is a governmental agency. And since the power to create the agency includes the implied power to do whatever is needful. or appropriate, if not expressly prohibited, to protect the agency, there has been attributed to Congress some scope, the limits of which it is not now necessary to define, for granting or withholding immunity of federal agencies from state taxation.121 In the four cases in which this Court has held that the salary of an officer or employee of one government or its instrumentality was immune from taxation by the other, it was assumed, without

discussion, that the immunity of a government or its instrumentality extends to the salaries of its officers and employees. This assumption, made with respect to the salary of a governmental officer in Dobbins v. Commissioners of Erie County122 and in Collector v. Day, 123 was later extended to confer immunity on income derived by a lessee from lands leased to him by the government in the performance of a governmental function,124 although the claim of a like exemption from tax on the income of a contractor engaged in carrying out a government project was rejected both in the case of a contractor with a state,125 and of a contractor with the national government.126 Under the doctrine enunciated by the Supreme Court of the United States in the case of labama v. ning & Boozer,127 an independent contractor is not an agent of the Government and does not share its immunity from state taxation. 

A state tax on the contractor was declared valid, even though the burden of it will be passed on to the Government under the terms of the contract. 

The court said: "But however extensively the Government may have reserved the right to restrict or control the action of the contractors in other respects, neither the reservation nor the exercise of that power gave to the contractors the status of agents of the Government to enter into contracts or to pledge its credit." The ultimate repudiation in felvering v. Mountain froducers Corp.,128 of the doctrine that a tax on the income of a lessee derived from a lease of government-owned or controlled lands is a forbidden interference with the activities of the government concerned led to the re-examination by this Court, in the Gerhardt case, of the theory underlying the asserted immunity from taxation by one government of salaries of employees of the other and in that case the Court held that the salaries of employees of the New York Port Authority, a state instrumentality created by New York and New Jersey, were not immune from federal income tax, even though the Authority be regarded as not subject to federal taxation. *** All the reasons for refusing to imply a constitutional prohibition of federal income taxation of salaries of state employees, staled at length in the Gerhardt case, are of equal force when immunity is claimed from state income tax on salaries paid by the national government or its agencies. In this respect we perceive no basis for a difference in result whether the taxed income be salary or some other form of compensation, or whether the taxpayer be an employee or an officer of either a state or the national government, or of its instrumentalities. 

In no case is there basis for the assumption that any such tangible or

It is aimed at the protection of the operations of government,133 and the immunity does not extend 'to anything lying outside or beyond governmental functions and their exercise. '134 where the immunity exists, it is absolute, resting upon an 'entire absence of power, '135 but it does not exist 'where no direct burden is laid upon the governmental instrumentality, and there is only a remote, if any, influence upon the exercise of the functions of government. power of one government to reach this permissible object of taxation may not be curtailed because of the indirect effect which the tax may have upon the other." "Upon a like principle other forms of excise tax have been upheld, although the statutory measure of the tax included securities constitutionally immune from any form of direct taxation. state inheritance or legacy tax is valid, although measured by the value of United States bonds which are transmitted.139 By parity of reasoning an inheritance tax may be levied by a state on a bequest to the United States,140 and by the United States on a bequest to a municipality.141 

Similarly, state laws, taxing to stockholders, at full value, shares in national banks, are upheld, although the banks own tax exempt United States bonds.142 It is said that there is no logical distinction between a tax laid on a proper object of taxation, measured by a subject matter which is immune, and a tax of like amount imposed directly on the latter; but it may be said with greater force that there is a logical and practical distinction between a tax laid directly upon all of any class of government instrumentalities, which the Constitution impliedly forbids, and a tax such as the present which can in no case have any incidence, unless the taxpayer enjoys a privilege which is a proper object of taxation, and which would not be open to question if its amount were arrived at by any other nondiscriminatory method. This 

Court, in drawing the line which defines the limits of the powers and immunities of state and national governments, is not intent upon a mechanical application of the rule that government instrumentalities are immune from taxation, regardless of the consequences to the operations of government. The necessity for marking those boundaries grows out of our Constitutional system, under which the federal and the state governments exercise their authority over one people within the territorial limits of the same state. The purpose is the preservation to each government, within its own sphere, of the freedom to carry on those affairs committed to it by the Constitution, without undue inter ference by the other. ***143 The power of the Federal Government over foreign commerce, however, takes precedence over the immunity of 139. 140. 141. 142. 143.

the states from Federal taxation, even in the case of institutions or opcrations otherwise clearly protected. "The principle invoked by the petitioner, of the inmunity of state instrumentalities from federal taxation, has its inherent limitations.144 It is a principle implied from the necessity of maintaining our dual system of govern-ment.145 Springing from that necessity it does not extend beyond it. 

Protecting the functions of government in its proper province, the implication ceases when the boundary of that province is reached. 

The fact that the state in the performance of state functions may use imported articles does not mean that the importation is a function of the state government independent of federal power. The control of importation does not rest with the state but with the Congress. In international relations and with respect to foreign intercourse and trade the people of the United States act through a single government with unified and adequate national power. There is thus no violation of the principle which petitioner invokes, for there is no encroachment on the power of the state as none exists with respect to the subject over which the federal power has been exerted. To permit the states and their instrumentalities to import commodities for their own use, regardless of the requirements imposed by the Congress, would undermine, if not destroy, the single control which it was one of the dominant purposes of the Constitution to create. It is for the Congress to decide to what extent, if at all, the states and their instrumentalities shall be relieved of the payment of duties on imported articles."146 There is, of course, no direct constitutional restriction upon the taxation of a state's own property, obligations, officers, and the like, if the statutes so provide, except the obligation of contracts provision. It is equally well settled that a state may tax the obligations, property, official salaries, and similar items, of sister states, if the statutes so provide. Neither the full faith and credit clause nor any other provision, intervenes.

SECTION II. TAX PROBLEMS IN THE DEPARTMENT OF THE ARMY 83. EARLY PROBLEMS. On 1 July 1940 the principal tax questions handled in the Office of The Judge Advocate General arose in connection with attempts to tax transactions and property on Federal areas, post exchanges, Civilian Conservation Corps camp exchanges, officers' messes and clubs, and military personnel. The Army's activities were confined

There were few state tax problems, except those arising out of sales of gasoline by post exchanges and similar agencies,147 the application of state unemployment compensation (social security) taxes to post exchanges, clubs, messes, and similar activities,148 and the application of state income taxes to the pay of military personnel who were not resident within exclusively Federal areas.149 Federal tax problems were relatively minor in nature. 

84. STATE AND LOCAL TAX PROBLEMS. With the initiation and ex pansion of the defense and war program, the adoption of the cost-plusa-fixed-fee form of contract in July 1940, the passage of the Buck Act150 permitting the states to levy sales, use and income taxes within Federal areas, and the activities of the Federal Government, notably the Treasury Department and the Department of Justice, and of a number of states in seeking to restrict the application of the principles of reciprocal intergovernmental immunity, new problems developed concerning the application of state and local sales, use, gasoline, and gross receipts taxes to purchases by the War Department and by its cost-plus-a-fixed-fee contractors.151

contractors other than their fixed fees. Negotiations and correspondence with various states resulted in a majority of them agreeing with the Department's position. Some, however, refused to agree. 

Litigation ensued in Florida,155 which was decided in favor of the state in December 1940. The Government took no part in that case. 

Another case156 arose in Mississippi which was not decided until after the Supreme Court of the United States had passed upon the status of cost-plus contractors, and that case ultimately turned on a question of state law. In the spring of 1941 the question of state taxation of the defense program became so acute that it was taken up with the President in at least two cabinet meetings. After consideration of the views of the War Department, the Treasury Department and the Department of Justice, it was determined that test cases should be instituted to determine the status of cost-plus contractors. The 

Department of Justice took the position that neither the United States nor its cost-plus contractors were immune from vendor-type taxes, i.e., those taxes technically imposed upon the vendor rather than upon the vendee, even though as a matter of practice the vendor shifts the economic burden to the vendee. The Department of Justice took the view that cost-plus contractors, as agents of the Government, shared its immunity from vendee-type taxes. This is the legal incidence theory of immunity, as distinguished from the burden theory. Arrangements were made with the Alabama authorities to litigate the applicability of the Alabama sales and use taxes, both being vendee-type taxes, to purchases by cost-plus contractors. Thus began the cases of Alabama v. King & Boozer157 and Curry v. Dunn Construction Co., et al., 158 which were decided by the Supreme Court of the United 

States on 10 November 1941, holding that cost-plus contractors, under the particular contracts involved, were not agents of the United States and had no constitutional immunity from taxation. Another case in which the Government participated at the same time and which reached the same result was Standard Oil Co. v. Fontenot. 159

The War Department sought to do both. On 18 November 1941, a bill161 was introduced to exempt war

In 

Uctober 1943 the war Department was requested to express its views concerning the reintroduction of legislation similar to that discussed above. The Judge Advocate General assisted in the preparation of a letter for the signature of the Under Secretary stating that the

Department did not favor the introduction of such legislation in view of (a) the general nature of the arrangements which had been made with state taxing authorities; (b) the completion of a major part of the cost-plus construction program, and (c) the expected opposition to legislation of this character. 

The Judge Advocate General was active in connection with the above legislation. At the same time negotiations were being carried on with the various states, looking toward statutory or administrative exemption of cost-plus contractors from sales, use and gross receipts taxes. When it became evident that the Congress was not going to enact immunizing legislation this activity was intensified. Approximately twenty-three states and the cities of ivew York and New Orleans imposed such taxes. As a result of negotiation to 31 December 1945, twelve states agreed to waive sales and use taxes accruing prior to 

10 November 1941; ten states and two cities agreed that their sales and use taxes would not apply to purchases by cost-plus construction 

162. H.R. 6617, 77th Cong., 2d Sess. 128

contractors; and sixteen states and two cities agreed that their sales and use taxes would not apply to purchases by cost-plus manufacturing contractors. Alabama agreed that its sales and use taxes would not apply to cost-plus manufacturing contractors after 19 July 1944, provided that no refunds were sought for prior periods. 

The states of Indiana, Washington, and West Virginia, the latter two of which also impose sales taxes, could not be persuaded to exempt from their gross receipts taxes reimbursements made to costplus contractors. Indiana did concede an exemption as to the reimbursements for cost of materials. Generally speaking, the states made no concessions to lump-sum contractors, though kansas as a matter of administrative policy did exempt them from sales and use taxes until 18 August 1942. Colorado exempted them from sales, use and service taxes with respect to contracts awarded prior to 1 February 1942. Indiana continued its policy of exempting their gross income from tax on the ground that gross income from sales to the United States was exempt. The states which waived taxes accruing against cost-plus contractors prior to 10 November 1941 were persuaded to do so on the basis that the king & Boozer and Curry cases should not be applied retroactively. The states and cities which agreed that their sales and use taxes did not apply to purchases by cost-plus contractors were persuaded to do so on the ground that, under the terms of cost-plus contracts, purchases by such contractors were purchases for resale to the United States. Purchases for resale generally were not subject to sales and use taxes under state and local laws, and resales to the United States were also exempt, either constitutionally or by terms of the taxing statutes. Though prior to 

10 November 1941 a number of states exempted sales of gasoline to costplus contractors from their gasoline taxes, on the ground that such contractors were agents of the United States, only two made any concessions after that date. That situation was met generally by having the United States purchase gasoline tax-free and furnish it to such contractors.

The results of negotiations with various states and cities were made available to procurement officers in the form of directives,163 prepared pursuant to directions from the Office of the Under Secretary of War. These directives were generally worked out in detail in cooperation with the interested tax commission or revenue department and were approved by the state or local authorities prior to issuance. 

Any other procedure would only have provoked disagreements and litigation. 

In some instances the directives were adopted and published as regulations by the interested state authorities. In some instances, such as the 

City of New York, which issued extensive regulations, and Oklahoma, which adopted a statute authorizing waiver of sales and use taxes, no directives were issued. 

163. See paragraph 831, Army Procurement Regulations (APR). For current regulations see sec. XI, ASPR, Federal, State, and Local Taxes. 129

87. GASOLINE PURCHASED BY COST-PLUS-A-FIXED-FEE CONTRACTORS. 

fter 10 November 1941 the various states, all of which impose gasoline taxes, generally made no concessions to cost-plus contractors with respect to gasoline taxes, although Missouri and Linnesota did grant some exemption to cost-plus contractors. In view of the tremendous quantities of gasoline consumed and to be consumed by cost-plus contractors in the construction and procurement programs, and in view of the fact that gasoline taxes were very high in relation to the cost of the product, this presented a serious problem. This problem was net in most instances by Government procurement of gasoline free of state taxes, such gasoline then being furnished to costplus contractors. Two states, Texas and Ohio, sought to break this system of tax-free procurement. Ohio took the position that under the Hayden-Cartwright act, supra, the Government was required to remit to the state the tax on gasoline furnished to cost-plus contractors. 

The War Department disagreed and refused to pay. Chio was then in the position of making a demand which it could not enforce by suit or otherwise. It then made a similar demand upon Defense Plant 

Corporation, which could be sued, and the case, in which the War Department cooperated with Defense Plant Corporation, was decided by the Ohio Board of fax appeals in favor of Defense Flant Corporation.164 

Texas took a different approach and in 1942 filed suit against a costplus contractor for a use tax on the gasoline furnished by the Government. The Texas authorities were persuaded to dismiss the suit. A similar demand was again made by the Texas authorities early in 1945 but no further action was taken. 

88. PURCHASE OF GASOLINE BY THE WAR DEPARTMENT. In addition to gasoline consumed in the construction and procurement programs, great quantities were purchased by the war Department for use in its vehicles and planes. At the time of the King & Boozer and Curry decisions the only state seeking to impose its gasoline tax upon direct purchases by the United States was North Dakota. All the other states provided either statutory or administrative exemptions. After those decisions it became evident that the states could constitutionally impose their taxes with respect to gasoline sold to the United States, provided the legal incidence of the tax was upon the dealer or distributor rather than upon the United States. Consequently, where a state statute imposed the tax upon the dealer or distributor it was necessary only to repeal the exemption granted to the United States, or, in the absence of such an exemption, to reverse an administrative ruling. Where the statute imposed the tax upon the consumer it was necessary only to rewrite the law so as to impose the tax technically upon the dealer or distributor. It was anticipated that this would happen, but fortunately all but a very few legislatures were not due to convene again until 1943. In 1942, however, Utah reversed its position administratively 164. 

Defense Plant Corp. v. vatt, Ohio B.T.a., No. 8258, 13 Feb 1945; no appeal was filed.

130

and sought to tax gasoline sold to the United States. To stem the anticipated wave of such activity by discouraging it, it was directed that gasoline should not be purchased from Utah distributors or dealers unless it was impractical to purchase elsewhere. Such action deprives the dealers in a particular state of business and yields little revenue to the state. It is possible in this manner to avoid gasoline taxes because all state gasoline tax laws exempt from tax gasoline exported from the state and because no state can constitutionally impose a tax upon the United States with respect to gasoline owned by the United States and brought into the state for governmental use. It may not always be practicable to follow this procedure because of emergency needs for gasoline, shortage of transportation facilities, or high transportation costs, particularly where refineries are located within the state involved. 

Tennessee repealed, its exemption, but most of the gasoline used by the Army was procured outside the state. Wyoming reversed its position but was persuaded that such action would be unprofitable, so an exemption was granted to the United States on bulk purchases. 

Maryland reversed its position but then made administrative concessions by regulation165 resulting in the exemption of practically all gasoline sold to the United States except filling station deliveries. The 

Maryland legislature later granted an exemption. Florida reversed its position, but the nature of its tax, i.e., whether a vendor or vendee tax, was so doubtful as to require litigation, which the state won, 166 but before the case was decided the Governor had been persuaded to recommend and the legislature had passed legislation exempting bulk sales to the United States until 30 June 1945. Through the efforts of the Judge Advocate General's Office the legislature in 1945 extended the exemption to 30 June 1947. Mississippi and Louisiana started to repeal their exemptions, but Louisiana was persuaded to exempt all bulk sales to the United States plus all gasoline for aviation purposes and for use in Government vessels, while Mississippi was persuaded to exempt all gasoline for use of the Armed Forces. 

89. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION AND PROPERTY TAXES. Uther state and local taxes which had to be considered in connection with the procurement program were unemployment compensation (social security) taxes as applied to war contractors and property taxes. Unemployment compensation taxes were of concern to the War Department as they constituted reimbursable items of cost under cost-plus contracts. the spring of 1943 a movement was begun in a number of state legislatures to increase unemployment compensation taxes on war contractors. 

165. See SPJGT 1944/3729, 11 Apr 1944. 166. 

United States v. Lee, 13 o. 2d 919 (1943). In 131

The proposals followed the same general pattern, i.e., one of increasing the rate of tax payable by those employers whose payrolls had increased more than a certain percentage over their payrolls for some selected prior period. Such proposed taxes were considered as discriminatory taxes in disguise, aimed at war contractors with the hope that the United States would bear the additional cost. Fursuant to instructions from the Uffice of the Under Secretary of War representatives of The Judge Advocate General examined the proposed legislation and appeared before appropriate legislative committees in Missouri, Ohio, Illinois, and Alabama, to state the Department's opposition.167

In addition to the usual features mentioned above, the Ohio bill sought to provide for the segregation of an employer's cost-plus operations from his other operations, thus making the discrimination patent. This part of the chio bill was defeated, but the other features passed. The bills were also passed by the 

Missouri, Illinois, Alabama and a number of other legislatures, including Wisconsin, where the only action taken was to request the Governor not to approve the bill. A similar bill was defeated in committee in the Texas legislature, and the one proposed in Indiana never received consideration. Thereafter, the War Department changed its policy, and The Judge Advocate General was instructed not to oppose such bills unless they were patently discriminatory.168 

90. PROPERTY TAXES. The principal property tax problems which arose involved attempts to tax Government-owned realty, Governmentowned improvements on privately owned land, and the interest, if any, of contractors in Government-owned plants, known as possessory interest assessments; the liability of Defense Plant Corporation for real property taxes, and the liability of contractors for personal property (intangible) taxes on advance payment accounts and on amounts due to them from the Government on open account. 

New York, Ohio, Massachusetts, and Illinois sought to tax

Government-owned real property, but were successfully resisted, by. litigation where necessary. Pennsylvania made the most serious threat, attempting to impose real property taxes upon a private contractor with respect to Government-owned machinery and equipment in the contractor's plant. The Government intervened in the case at the insistence of the War Department, and a favorable decision was obtained in the Supreme Court of the United States.169 An adverse decision would have been disastrous from a financial and administrative point of view. The Government's brief in the Supreme Court indicated that an adverse decision would directly affect two billion 

167. See SPJGT 1943/6179, 27 Apr 1943 and 6179a, 5 Aug 1943. 

168. See SPJGT 1943/10339, 10 Jul 1943, SPJGT 1943/9435, 15 Jun 1943, and SPJGT 1943/6179a, 5 Aug 1943. 169. 

United States v. Allegheny County, 322 U.S. 174 (1944). 132

dollars worth of property similarly situated and owned by the War and Navy Departments and the Maritime Commission alone and might affect an additional five and one-half billion dollars worth of property held by those agencies. It must be remembered that real property taxes are usually administered by counties, with varying rates and procedures and that questions of valuation would be present in each case. This decision caused the other states and local subdivisions to desist in large measure in their attempts to tax Government-owned realty or improvements.

California was insistent after 1943 that contractors operating Government-owned plants had a taxable possessory interest in such plants. The Judge Advocate General was successful in preventing the assertion of such taxes against Army contractors. 

The Defense Plant Corporation built plants and then leased them to war contractors, usually cost-plus contractors. Under the terms of the leases the contractors were usually obligated to pay any real property taxes, and the War Department was called upon to reimburse the contractor for the taxes so paid. Under section 10 of the act of 22 January 1932, as amended,170 the Congress consented to the taxation of the real property of the Reconstruction Finance Corporation and its subsidiaries. It had not consented to the taxation of the personal property of those corporations. Some state constitutions and statutes expressly exempted from taxation property of the United States and its instrumentalities. Liany legislatures during their 1945 sessions amended their statutes to take advantage of the consent to taxation. However, the Defense Plant Corporation adopted a policy of paying taxes on its real property without regard to such exemptions. In following this policy it sought to obtain low valuations and to exclude from taxation machinery and equipment installed in its plants on the basis that they were personal property. 

This policy was of concern to the War Department since it, through its cost-plus contractors, was bearing the burden of these taxes. The 

Judge Advocate General took the position that real property taxes were not payable where there was a state constitutional or statutory exemption of property of the United States. The matter was submitted to the Under Secretary of War who decided not to challenge the Defense

Plant Corporation policy. It developed, however, that the Comptroller 

General began to question reimbursements made to cost-plus contractors covering payment of such taxes. Also several states, California and 

Pennsylvania, in particular, were not content to tax merely the plants; they sought to include the machinery and equipment as part of the real estate. As a result of these developments, Defense Plant Corporation litigated both questions, i.e., the immunity question and the personal property question. 

170. 47 Stat. 9, 55 Stat. 248; 15 U.S.C. 610. 133

91. ADVANCE OF FUNDS TO CONTRACTORS. In connection with the war program, it became necessary to advance billions of dollars to war contractors. Such advance payments were usually put in restricted bank accounts and the Government retained extensive control over them. 

Several states, California and Michigan, in particular, sought to tax such bank accounts to contractors. By negotiation The Judge Advocate 

General convinced Michigan that it should not apply its intangible tax, on the basis that even if the accounts did constitute property of the contractor, the contractor had an offsetting debt to the Government. California was originally persuaded not to levy its solvent credits tax with respect to such accounts on the basis that, because of their restricted nature and the control retained by the Government, they did not constitute solvent credits. Those same states sought to tax contractors with respect to amounts owed to them by the United States under their contracts. Until the decision by the Supreme Court of the United States in Smith v. Davis,171 holding that there was no constitutional objection to the imposition of a property tax upon an open account indebtedness from the United States to a lump-sum contractor, The Judge Advocate General was successful in preventing the imposition of such taxes. 

92. SURPLUS PROPERTY SALES TAXES. In some respects the emphasis with respect to state tax problems by 1945 began to swing from the application of such taxes to procurement to their application to the disposition of surplus property and of termination inventory. A number of states imposed vendee-type sales taxes and use taxes, for which purchasers of surplus property and termination inventory may be liable. 

Since it was administratively impossible to collect such taxes from purchasers, the states sought to have the Federal Government aid them in the collection of such taxes at the time when the sales were made. 

This problem was the subject of discussions among representatives of the war and Navy Departments, Treasury, Commerce and Agriculture Depart ments, the Bureau of the Budget, Surplus Property Board (Surplus Property Administration); Reconstruction Finance Corporation and its subsidiaries, war Food Administration, Maritime Commission, and other agencies. Practical considerations entered here, though representatives of the various agencies were of the opinion that the United States could not be legally compelled to collect such taxes. These considerations were principally: (a) the states by and large had been cooperative in respect of the application of their sales and use taxes to cost-plus contractors, and (b) the states were cooperating in the withholding of Federal income taxes from the pay of their employees. 

93. REIMBURSEMENT FOR STATE TAXES. In addition to the problems discussed above, The Judge Advocate General was concerned with the construction of the tax provisions of cost-plus contracts as to 

171. 323 U.S. 111 (1944). 134

dollars worth of property similarly situated and owned by the ar and Navy Departments and the Maritime Commission alone and might affect an additional five and one-half billion dollars worth of property held by those agencies. It must be remembered that real property taxes are usually administered by counties, with varying rates and procedures and that questions of valuation would be present in each case. This decision caused the other states and local subdivisions to desist in large measure in their attempts to tax Government-owned realty or improvements.

California was insistent after 1943 that contractors operating Government-owned plants had a taxable possessory interest in such plants. The Judge Advocate General was successful in preventing the assertion of such taxes against Army contractors. 

The Defense Plant Corporation built plants and then leased them to war contractors, usually cost-plus contractors. Under the terms of the leases the contractors were usually obligated to pay any real property taxes, and the War Department was called upon to reimburse the contractor for the taxes so paid. Under section 10 of the act of 22 January 1932, as amended,170 the Congress consented to the taxation of the real property of the Reconstruction Finance Corporation and its subsidiaries. It had not consented to the taxation of the personal property of those corporations. Some state constitutions and statutes expressly exempted from taxation property of the United States and its instrumentalities. Many legislatures during their 1945 sessions amended their statutes to take advantage of the consent to taxation. However, the Defense Plant Corporation adopted a policy of paying taxes on its real property without regard to such exemptions. In following this policy it sought to obtain low valuations and to exclude from taxation machinery and equipment installed in its plants on the basis that they were personal property. 

This policy was of concern to the War Department since it, through its cost-plus contractors, was bearing the burden of these taxes. The 

Judge Advocate General took the position that real property taxes were not payable where there was a state constitutional or statutory exemption of property of the United States. The matter was submitted to the Under Secretary of War who decided not to challenge the Defense

Plant Corporation policy. It developed, however, that the Comptroller 

General began to question reimbursements made to cost-plus contractors covering payment of such taxes. Also several states, California and 

Pennsylvania, in particular, were not content to tax merely the plants; they sought to include the machinery and equipment as part of the real estate. As a result of these developments, Defense Plant Corporation litigated both questions, i.e., the immunity question and the personal property question. 

170. 47 Stat. 9, 55 Stat. 248; 15 U.S.C. 610. 133

91. ADVANCE OF FUNDS TO CONTRACTORS. In connection with the war program, it became necessary to advance billions of dollars to war contractors. Such advance payments were usually put in restricted bank accounts and the Government retained extensive control over them. 

Several states, California and Michigan, in particular, sought to tax such bank accounts to contractors. By negotiation The Judge Advocate 

General convinced Michigan that it should not apply its intangible tax, on the basis that even if the accounts did constitute property of the contractor, the contractor had an offsetting debt to the Government. California was originally persuaded not to levy its solvent credits tax with respect to such accounts on the basis that, because of their restricted nature and the control retained by the Government, they did not constitute solvent credits. Those same states sought to tax contractors with respect to amounts owed to them by the United States under their contracts. Until the decision by the Supreme Court of the United States in Smith v. Davis,171 holding that there was no constitutional objection to the imposition of a property tax upon an open account indebtedness from the United States to a lump-sum contractor, The Judge Advocate General was successful in preventing the imposition of such taxes.

In some respects the emphasis with respect to state tax problems by 1945 began to swing from the application of such taxes to procurement to their application to the disposition of surplus property and of termination inventory. A number of states imposed vendee-type sales taxes and use taxes, for which purchasers of surplus property and termination inventory may be liable.
