93. REIMBURSEMENT FOR STATE TAXES. In addition to the problems discussed above, The Judge Advocate General was concerned with the construction of the tax provisions of cost-plus contracts as to 

171. 323 U.S. 111 (1944). 134

reimbursement for certain state taxes. Taxes which clearly fell upon procurement caused little difficulty, but franchise taxes and similar taxes which constituted items of overhead cost presented difficult problems, which were taken under consideration. The difficulties were enhanced by the variety of such taxes and by the failure of the contracts, which were by no means uniform in their provisions, to cover the matter clearly. During 1945 the war Department adopted the policy of reimbursing franchise taxes.172 

94. PROCUREMENT IN FEDERAL EXCISE TAXES. Federal excise taxes imposed on the manufacture or sale of articles or services (manufacturers' excise taxes, retailers' excise taxes, and taxes on communications and the transportation of property and persons) necessarily affected determination of appropriate contract prices. As exemptions from such taxes under Treasury regulations and decisions of the Comptroller General depended upon whether the contract price included or excluded the amount of such taxes as were applicable, Army policies were required concerning the extent to which purchases were to be made tax free. 

Shortly after the adoption of the cost-plus type of contract in July 1940, the Bureau of Internal Revenue agreed to treat purchases by such contractors substantially as if they were direct purchases by the United States. Until the repeal of the Federal excise tax exemptions the Bureau maintained that position and, consequently, it was possible for procurement to be made under cost-plus contracts without paying Federal excise taxes.

The application of Federal excise taxes to lump-sum contractors was considerably more complicated than the application of such taxes to cost-plus contractors. On 27 January 1942 Treasury Decision 

5114 was promulgated by the Treasury Department. That decision sought to clarify and settle previous doubts concerning the exemptions available to lump-sum contractors and represented a liberalization of the governmental exemption so far as its availability to lump-sum contractors and subcontractors was concerned. In general, it permitted the channeling of the governmental tax exemption through all tiers of purchases involved in the production of the ultimate article sold to the Government. At first, full advantage was taken of that decision for the purpose of excluding Federal excise from prices paid by the Government, thus conserving Army appropriations. Experience, however, showed that taking full advantage of Treasury Decision 5114 tended to increase the administrative work of the Government and of the contractors, generally, with no net saving to the Government, as taxes included in prices paid by the Army would ultimately find their way into the Treasury as revenue receipts. Consequently, on 4 January 1943, a joint statement of policy was issued by the then Under Secretary of War and the Under Secretary of the Navy.173 That policy, effective 1 March 1943, 172. See SPJGT 1944/7559, 15 Jun 1944. 

173. See par. 897, Army Procurement Regulations. For current regulations see sec. XI, ASPR, Federal, State, and Local Taxes. 135

provided that direct purchases by the Government could continue to be made on a basis either including or excluding Federal excise taxes.

It recognized that, in general, such purchases would be made on a tax-exclusive basis. Cost-plus prime contractors and subcontractors were likewise permitted to purchase articles on a basis including or excluding such taxes. As a matter of general policy, however, lump-sum contractors and subcontractors were not permitted to use the governmental exemption to purchase supplies and materials on a tax-exclusive basis. The new policy recognized that exceptions might be necessary and authorized such exceptions. In general, the authorized exceptions fell within the following categories: (a) The technical services were authorized to permit lump-sum contractors to take advantage of available exceptions in purchasing radio receiving sets and other articles which might be deemed to be radio receiving sets, such as electronic equipment. This exception was deemed necessary due to doubt in many cases whether or not particular instruments sold to the Government were radio receiving sets, which doubt made tax-inclusive pricing an expensive guessing game. Numerous complications and possible windfalls to contractors were avoided by allowing this exception. (b) 

The technical services in some cases permitted lump-sun contractors to use the governmental exemption in purchasing tires and tubes. tax on these items would be required to be paid in absence of use of that exemption since there was no exemption from this particular tax on the basis of purchase for further manufacture. Some contractors, such as truck manufacturers, purchasing tires and tubes, insisted on treating the full purchase price thereof, as increased by the tax, as a cost on which their profit was to be computed. Extending the governmental exemption to such purchases reduced contractors' costs for tires and tubes, with a resulting reduction in the gross amount of profit received by them, thus resulting in a net saving to the Government over and above the tax involved. 

The 

Upon the adoption of this new policy, it was necessary to revise the tax article for use in lump-sum contracts entered into on and after 1 March 1943. The revised article provided that, unless otherwise indicated, there were excluded from the contract price only Federal taxes directly applicable to the completed supplies or work covered by the contract, including component parts of which the contractor was the manufacturer, producer or importer, and as to which exemption from tax was available. Under this revised tax article a lump-sum contractor could not issue tax exemption certificates to his suppliers based upon the governmental exemption, unless an express exception to the provision of the tax article was made in the contract, which exception had to be authorized either by the chief of the technical service involved or by the then Headquarters, Army 

Service Forces. 

The Revenue Act of 1943 required a major change, effective 1 June 1944, in the policies concerning the application of Federal 136

In excise taxes to War Department purchases. On the theory that administrative convenience and savings in manpower would result, that act, in general, removed the exemption from Federal excise taxes previously available to the Government. While that act was pending before the 

Congress, the War Department made strenuous efforts to retain some exemptions. Since, however, the recommendation for such legislation had been sent to the Committee on Ways and Means of the House of Representatives over the signature of the President, the Department could do no more than seek amendments in instances where removal of exemptions would not accomplish the intended purpose or would create burdensome administrative difficulties. The Treasury Department sponsored this radical change in tax policy without consulting either the War or Navy Department, both of which were vitally concerned. particular, successful efforts were made to retain the exemption from tax on radio receiving sets, which exemption was deemed desirable because of the difficulty in determining whether or not certain articles were radio receiving sets. Efforts to retain exemption from the taxes on pistols, revolvers, firearms, shells and cartridges were also successful. Also, had the removal of the exemptions applied to existing contracts, a tremendous problem either of repricing or of processing vouchers for additional payments would have been encountered. That problem was avoided by continuing the exemptions in effect prior to the Revenue Act of 1943 as to all contracts entered into prior to 1 June 1944, as well as to all agreements and change orders supplemental thereto and bearing the same Government contract number. 

This furnished an arbitrary but administratively feasible test. In addition the War Department was successful in securing the inclusion in the act of a provision174 permitting the Secretary of the Treasury to authorize exemptions from manufacturers', retailers', communications and transportation taxes with respect to articles or services purchased for the use of the United States if he should determine that imposition of the tax would cause substantial burden or expense which could be avoided by granting tax exemption. Subsequently, and at the insistence of the War and Navy Departments, the Secretary of the Treasury authorized the following exemptions under that section: (a) transportation under Government bills of lading or transportation requests was exempted from the transportation taxes (this was done on the theory that these exemptions were self-executing and the administrative procedures required to pay these taxes would be substantially greater than those involved under the exemption); (b) communications services furnished to and paid for by the United States were exempted (same theory as the transportation taxes); (c) exemption was also authorized from the tax on tires and tubes in case such items were sold to any person for use as component parts in the manufacture of an article to be sold to the Government. The reasons for this exemption were identical with those leading to an exception to the joint Army-Navy policy of 1943 in the case of the tax on tires and inner tubes. All of the concessions 

174. Section 307(c). 

137

obtained were to terminate after the war, but the Revenue Act of 1945 made permanent the discretionary authority of the Secretary of the Treasury so that he now has authority to continue the exemptions if deemed necessary.

War Department policies previously in force were continued with respect to the claiming of such exemptions as remained available under the Revenue Act of 1943 on the basis of purchase for the use of the United States. Adoption of further policies, however, was required with regard to the claiming of miscellaneous exemptions available to private as well as governmental purchasers. In general, the theory behind the Revenue Act of 1943 was adopted by the War Department, namely, that complicated-and-time-and-personnel-consuming procedures should not be followed solely to avoid the transfer of funds from the pocket of one department, such as the War Department, to that of another department, such as the Treasury Department. The War Department, therefore, adopted the policy that miscellaneous exemptions, such as the exemptions based on export, should not be claimed,175 though that policy had no application to Army Exchange Service purchases with nonappropriated funds. At the insistence of the then Army Air Forces, however, claiming of the exemption under section 3451, Internal Revenue Code, was permitted as to a small group of items which could be used only on or in connection with aircraft.176

The standard form tax article for use in lump-sum contracts entered into on and after 1 June 1944 was revised in the light of the Revenue Act of 1943. Inasmuch as, in the main, purchases would be subject to Federal excise taxes, the revised clause provided that, except as otherwise specified, all applicable Federal taxes were included in the purchase price.177

Another problem which presented itself was the appropriate treatment of Federal excise taxes in connection with the conversion of cost-plus contracts to lump-sum contracts. In order that exemptions from Federal excise taxes taken by cost-plus contractors might not be jeopardized through the conversion to a lump-sum basis, it was necessary that the prices fixed in the converted contract take into account the fact that certain items on hand at the time of conversion had been procured exclusive of Federal taxes. Efforts were made to see that converted contracts were so worded as to take care of this problem.

95. STATUS OF POST EXCHANGES, COMMISSARIES, OFFICERS' CLUBS AND MESSES AND SIMILAR ORGANIZATIONS. The following activities of the Army, 

Air Force and Navy are considered to be wholly owned Government instrumentalities, integral parts of the Military Establishment, and entitled to all privileges and immunities of Federal instrumentalities:178 post exchanges, commissaries, officers' clubs, noncommissioned officers' clubs and enlisted men's clubs, all under the jurisdiction of the Department of the army; ships' service stores, ships' stores, Marine 

Corps post exchanges, commissioned officers' messes, warrant officers' messes, chief petty officers' messes, enlisted men's clubs, all under the jurisdiction of the Department of the Navy; and post exchanges, commissaries, officers' clubs, noncommissioned officers' clubs, enlisted men's clubs, all under the jurisdiction of the Department of the Air Force. 

96. FEDERAL RETAILERS' EXCISE TAXES. In 1941 the Congress imposed retailers' excise taxes upon the sale at retail of certain items of jewelry, toilet articles and furs. Luggage was later included. A number of these articles are sold by post exchanges and commissaries. 

The Treasury Department took the position that exchanges and commissaries should pay this tax on their sales of such articles, but the War Department did not agree and, after unsuccessful negotiation with the Treasury Department, submitted the question to the attorney General for an opinion. after a long period of negotiation in the course of which the Treasury Department sought unsuccessfully to obtain legislation on the subject, the quest for an opinion of the attorney General was withdrawn upon the agreement of the Bureau of Internal Revenue that such taxes would not be collected from exchanges and commissaries.

97. SOCIAL SECURITY TAXES. Under the Federal social security tax laws in effect prior to 1 January 1940, instrumentalities of the United States were exempt from social security taxes. Consequently, post exchanges, officers' clubs and messes and similar organizations were held not liable for such taxes. Effective 1 January 1940, however, the social security tax laws were changed so as to exempt from social security taxes only those instrumentelities which were wholly owned by the United States, and the Bureau of Internal Revenue held that post exchanges, messes and clubs and similar organizations were subject to such taxes. The War Department accepted the Bureau's ruling with respect to officers' clubs and messes but disagreed with respect to post exchanges. This controversy persisted until September 

1942 when the Bureau of Internal Revenue finally ruled that post exchanges were not subject to social security taxes, relying upon the decision in the case of Stendard Oil Company v. Johnson, supra.

In view of the vast expansion of the Army during end after 1940 and the consequent increase in the number of commissioned end noncommissioned officers' clubs and messes and the financial and administrative burdens imposed upon such organizations by the social security tax laws, it was decided to request the Bureau of Internal Revenue to reconsider its ruling holding such organization subject to such taxes. The then Secretary of War corresponded with the Commissioner of Internal Revenue and with the Secretary of the Treasury concerning this matter, but the Bureau refused to change its position. The Judge Advocate General, therefore, was prepared to submit the matter to the Attorney General (the same procedure which was followed with respect to the application of retailers' excise taxes to post exchanges) at the time when AR 210-50, 1 June 1944, was promulgated. In that regulation such organizations were for the first time officially characterized as organizations organized in the individual and personal capacities of the members, and the effect of that language in the regulation was to make it impossible to obtain any concessions from the Bureau of Internal Revenue or any assistance from the Department of Justice. The Judge Advocate 

General then prepared a staff study 180 on the subject, recommending to the

War Department that it determine, as a matter of policy, whether officers' and noncommissioned officers' messes and clubs should be so organized and operated as clearly to be wholly owned Federal instrumentalities, and, as such, exempt from all Federal and state taxes, including social security taxes. 

179. Part 2, sec. XI, ASPR, 19 May 1948, "Exemptions from Federal 

Excise Taxes." 180. 

SPJGT 1944/4007, 18 Apr 1944. 140

The Department determined that such organizations should be so organized as to be wholly owned Federal instrumentalities and directed the preparation of regulations to accomplish that purpose. Changes in AR 210-50 were recommended, all of which were incorporated in the revision of 

AR 210-50, 13 December 1945, and later superseded by AR 210-50, AFR 176-1, 8 March 1949. The tax problem was settled and clubs and messes were held exempt from Federal social security taxes. 181

CHAPTER 12 MISCELLANEOUS CONTRACT PROVISIONS 98. SCOPE OF CONTRACT ARTICLE. This article provides the neces sary spaces for the insertion of a brief general description of the supplies or services to be furnished, statement of the consideration, enumeration of accompanying papers relating to the transaction, and the delivery period. In short it sets forth the contractor's undertaking (subject, of course, to other clauses, terms and conditions of the contract). It is very important that these things be definitely and fully stated, as indefiniteness, incompleteness, or ambiguities in any of them causes unnecessary disputes and delays, and may result in pecuniary loss to either party or both. The form of the clause will necessarily vary with the nature of the supplies or services being procured; consequently no standard form of contractual provisions can be prescribed.1

The "Scope of Contract" clause also provides, as a rule, that the rights and obligations of the parties shall be governed by the General Provisions, the Schedule and the General Specifications for the Inspection of Material (which is incorporated in the contract by reference). It is a cardinal rule in the construction of contract provisions that the intention of the parties is to be inquired into and effected, if such intention is lawful and is possible of ascertainment.2 

99. PAYMENTS. a. General. The basic undertaking of the Govern ment is to pay the contractor for performing a contract.

In the case 1. 2. 

It should be noted that fixed-price supply contracts often contain a clause entitled "Variations in Quantity", which provides that no variation in the quantity called for will be accepted as compliance with the contract unless the variation has been caused by conditions of loading, shipping, packing, or allowances in manufacturing processes, and then only to the extent expressly provided in the contract.

A contract concerning Government functions must be strictly construed, and such functions can not be held to have been stipulated away by doubtful or ambiguous provisions. Rodgers Park 

Water Co. v. Fergus, 180 U.5. 624 (1900). Contracts are to be construed liberally in favor of the public when the subject matter concerns the interest of the public. Joy v. St. Louis, 138 U.S. 1 (1890). Contracts containing provisions obligating (or rather endeavoring to obligate) the Government to pay unlimited or indefinite amounts are not authorized. 5 Comp. Gen. 450 (1925). 142

of fixed-price supply contracts, this undertaking is to pay stipulated prices for supplies delivered or services performed; whereas in cost-type contracts, the Government's obligation is to pay the contractor the "allowable costs" incurred by it in the performance of the contract and usually, in addition thereto, a fixed fee for such performance. These undertakings are substantially different and require separate consideration. 

b. Fixed-price supply contracts. (1) General. The 

"Payments" clause used in fixed-price supply contracts provides that, upon the submission of properly certified invoices or vouchers, the contractor shall be paid the prices stipulated in the contract for supplies delivered and accepted, or services rendered, less such deductions as are specified. In order to permit payment for partial deliveries and at the same time prevent an influx of invoices for small sums, the aforesaid clause also provides that payment will be made on partial deliveries accepted by the Government whenever the amount due on such deliveries so warrants.

(2) Progress payments. However, contractors may be unwilling or unable to undertake the furnishing of supplies involving extended periods of manufacture or considerable initial investment unless a portion of the contract price is paid in advance of delivery. 

Such a need could be satisfied by a discretionary use of "progress payments" payments based upon the progress of performance of the contract and not exceeding the value of work already done. Progress payments are limited to a stated percentage (seldom in excess of 75%) of the total contract price, and are paid either on the basis of a percentage of completion of the entire contract or on the basis of invoices for direct costs of labor and materials. In any case, provision must be made in the contract for adequate security to protect the interest of the Government on account of such progress payments. 

Unless the Government acquires title to all work and materials on account of which progress payments are made, the contract must provide for a lien in favor of the Government on all such work and materials, which lien is paramount to all other liens. Progress payments are liquidated by means of crediting them against, or deducting them from, payments due to the contractor upon delivery and acceptance of the supplies or work contracted for. (3) Advance payments. Payments made in anticipation of work to be done on a contract are considered to be "advance payments." Such payments are authorized by Section 5 of the Armed Services 

Procurement Act3, but can be provided for in a contract only upon a 

3. Pub. Law 413, 80th Cong. 143

Secretarial determination that the advance payment provision is in the public interest or in the interest of the national defense, and that it is necessary and appropriate in order to procure the required supplies and services. In view of these limitations, and the additional ones imposed by the Armed Services Procurement Regulation, the advance payment method of financing is seldom used. (4) Withholding of payment in certain cases; construction contracts release. there fixed-price supply contracts not only involve the furnishing of designated supplies but also call for instruction books, plans, drawings, specifications, designs, patent rights, test after delivery, or other similar requirements, specified amounts are usually withheld from payments otherwise due the contractor until all the aforesaid requirements are met. The release of these sums is considered to be final payment inasmuch as no special final payment clause is usually provided in such contracts. 

However, in the case of construction contracts, and the like, a separate final payment clause is included, usually conditioning such payment upon the release of the Government, its agents and employees from any and all liabilities arising under the contract. 

C. Reimbursement type contracts. (1) General. In cost reimbursement type contracts the problem of "Payments" is largely a problem of "Allowable Costs." Allowable costs are those costs incurred by the contractor in the performance of the contract and accepted by the Department as properly chargeable to the contract in accordance with designated cost and accounting principles. Such principles, which are set forth in the contract or incorporated therein by reference, are now contained in the Armed Services Procurement Regulation, with separate consideration being given to costs under supply and research contracts with commercial organizations, costs under research contracts with educational institutions, and costs under construction contracts. (2) Payment of allowable costs. Payments on account of allowable costs are made upon the submission by the contractor of invoices supported by a statement of costs claimed to be allowable. Such invoices are usually required to be submitted monthly, must be in such form and detail as the Department may require, and must be certified by an official of the contractor who supervises accounting with respect to the contract. Inasmuch as all such costs are audited by the Department prior to payment (often a lengthy process), provisional payment is made by the Government in advance of such audit, with appropriate adjustments thereafter. (3) Payment of fixed fee; installment payments. The fixed fee (if any) under a cost-type contract is a percentage of the originally estimated cost. It is normally due and payable upon the 144

satisfactory completion of the entire work. However, where performance may extend over a substantial period, installment payments are made on account of the fixed fee, based either on a percentage of completion of the work or on actual costs incurred. However, at least ten percent of the total fixed fee is generally withheld until all work has been completed. 

(4) Release to Government provision. All cost reimbursement type contracts also provide that the contractor shall, as a condition precedent to final payment of allowable costs under the contract, execute a release (with or without certain excepted claims). In such cases further provision is made for withholding, pending execution of such release, a certain percentage of the total estimated cost, which sum is to be paid to the contractor at the time of final payment. These provisions as to release and reserve are also set forth in the "Payments" clause of cost-type contracts. 

100. ADDITIONAL BOND SECURITY. a. General. There is no gen eral law requiring bidders to furnish security to insure the execution of contract and bond for performance, nor requiring bidders to whom awards have been made to furnish security for performance. This matter is, for the most part, within the discretion of the administrative officers of the Government. However, there are certain laws requiring the furnishing of security or authorizing such requirement; and there are certain laws which prohibit the requiring of such security.4 b. Administrative officers may require; when. The administrative officers of the Government have full authority, except when qualified by laws, to require security to insure the execution of contract papers, and to insure the performance of contracts when undertaken, as they may see fit. These are known as bid and performance bonds. Bid bonds are required in connection with a bid covering work, materials, supplies or services for the Government and obligates the bidder not to withdraw his bid. The failure of a bidder to submit such bond or other approved form of security with his bid generally will result in his bid not being given consideration. 

101. PERFORMANCE BONDS. Performance bonds are required in connection with contracts covering work, materials, supplies, or services and the obligations under these bonds are fulfilled when the contractor delivers the articles, materials, or equipment covered by the contract or contracts in connection with which the bond is furnished, and they have been accepted by the Government. If the contractor defaults in the performance of the contract the Government may put the bond in suit immediately and 4. The Secretary of the Army may require the furnishing of bid and performance bonds in all contracts, except as otherwise provided by law, for the furnishing of supplies or services or the performance of work under the Department of the Army (20 Stat. 36, as amended; 

5 U.S.C. 218). 145

recover from the contractor or his sureties for any loss it may have sustained by reason of such default, this amount is determined generally by the excess sum the Government has to pay to effect completion of the requirement together with the damages, liquidated or unliquidated, that it may have sustained by delay in completion of the project.

In addition to original requirements for bid or performance bonds contracts may provide that if any surety upon any bond furnished in connection with the contract becomes unacceptable to the Government, or if any such surety fails to furnish reports as to his financial condition from time to time as requested by the Government, the Contractor shall promptly furnish such additional security as may be required from time to time to protect the interest of the Government and of persons supplying labor or materials in the prosecution of the work contemplated by the contract. 

102. FORFEITURE OF SECURITY. Should the bidder withdraw his bid before the time fixed therein, or should he fail to execute the contract papers, and furnish approved performance security, if required, within the time specified, he automatically forfeits his security, if it is other than a bond; and if the security is a bond, it may be put to suit immediately on default.5 

103. MILITARY SECURITY. 

a.

General. For reasons of military security, all contracts that are classified "Restricted," "Confidential," "Secret" or "Top Secret" are required. to contain certain provisions with respect to: (1) employment of aliens, (2) disclosure of information, 

the reporting of espionage, sabotage and subversive activities, subcontract provisions with respect to the foregoing.

These security provisions are combined into a single contract clause entitled "Military Security Requirements", which reads as follows:

"(b) The contractor (i) shall be responsible for safeguarding all classified matter and shall not supply or disclose classified matter to any unauthorized person, (ii) shall not make or permit to be made any reproductions of classified matter except as may be essential to performance of the contract and shall submit to the Contracting Officer, at such times as the Contracting Officer may direct, an accounting of all reproductions of classified matter, (iii) shall not incorporate in any other project any special features of design or construction which will disclose classified matter, except with the prior written authorization of the Contracting Officer.

Though the limitation on the employment of aliens appears to be inconsistent with the prohibition against discrimination in employment required by the President to be included in all Government contracts it is clearly reconciled by the joint statement of policy promulgated on 11 July 1942, by the Secretary of War, Secretary of Navy, attorney

General, and Chairman of the United States Maritime Commission. Part of this joint statement is as follows: "Even on aeronautical and classified contracts, if a qualified applicant whose services the contractor needs is an alien whose loyalty to the United States the contractor has no reason to doubt, the contractor is obligated to cooperate with the applicant in applying for consent to his employment If a contractor refuses employment to a qualified and authorized alien worker, he should be prepared to present specific and sufficient reasons to avoid a charge of discrimination."

C. Disclosure of information. The disclosure of information provision of the clause relates to national defense and is classified for reasons of military security; also, that the contractor shall be responsible for safeguarding all classified matters within' its control. 

It further provides that information relating to the contract or the work thereunder shall not be disclosed to any unauthorized person; and that, except with prior specific authorization, the contractor shall not reproduce any of the plans, drawings, or specifications, and shall not incorporate into any other project any special features of the contract design or construction. It should be noted that the contract clause permitting "Assignment of Claims" provides that in no event shall copies of the contract or of any plans, 'specifications or other similar documents relating to work under the contract, if marked "Top Secret," "Secret," "Confidential" or "Restricted," be furnished to any assignee thereunder without the prior written authorization of the Contracting Officer. 

7. 44 Stat. 784, as amended; 10 U.S.C. 310. 148

-- (1) Unauthorized disclosure of classified information "Espionage act.” Even apart from contractual obligation, unauthorized disclosure of classified information might subject a contractor to criminal penalties under the "Espionage Act."8 The Espionage Act specifies many separate and distinct crimes involving different acts requiring various degrees of intent. However, all such crimes have one element in common an act, to be unlawful, must be related to or connected with the national defense. As so used, the term "national defense" has been held to be a generic concept of broad connotations referring to military and naval equipment and the related activity of national preparedness.9 

d. Reports concerning subversive activities. The provision of the clause requiring reports of espionage, sabotage or subversive activities requires the contractor to submit immediately a complete confidential report of information concerning existing or threatened espionage, sabotage, or subversive activity at any plant, factory or site at which work under the contract is being performed or at which material acquired, fabricated, or manufactured in connection with such performance is stored. This provision also requires the contractor, whenever directed by the Department, to submit such information as it may have concerning any of its employees engaged in work at any plant, factory, or site at which work under the contract is being performed. 

It also requires the contractor, whenever directed by the Department, to exclude from places where the contract work is being performed any person or persons whom the Secretary or his authorized representative, in the interests of security, may designate. 

e. Subcontract provision. The subcontract provision of the clause provides that the contractor shall insert provisions similar to the foregoing in all subcontracts or purchase orders placed under the contract, unless the Contracting officer shall consent to the omission thereof. It expressly excepts, however, subcontracts and purchase orders for standard or commercial items procured under specifications which will not disclose any classified information. 

104. DEFINITIONS. As used in Government contracts, the following terms have the following meanings: a. The term "Secretary" means the Secretary, the Under Secretary, or any Assistant Secretary of the Department; and the term "his duly authorized representative" means any person or persons or board (other than the Contracting Officer) authorized to act for the Secretary.

The term "Contracting Officer" means the person executing the contract on behalf of the Government, and any other officer or b. 8.

149

civilian employee who is a properly designated Contracting Officer; and the term includes, except as otherwise provided in the contract, the authorized representative of a Contracting Officer acting within the limits of his authority.

C. Except as otherwise provided in the contract, the term "subcontracts" includes purchase orders under the contract. 

105. ALTERATIONS. As the majority of Government contracts are executed on printed standard forms and circumstances may require variations in certain aspects or wording therein there has been added a clause that is known as the Alteration Clause. The Clause itself simply states that "The following alterations have been made in the provisions of this contract:"

The alterations should be definitely and fully stated, as indefiniteness, incompleteness, or ambiguities in any of the alterations may cause unnecessary disputes and delays. The clauses themselves will necessarily vary with the nature and intentions of the alterations. 

106. NOTICE OF SHIPMENTS. This article provides that at the time of delivery of any shipment of supplies to a carrier for transportation, the Contractor shall give prepaid notice of shipment to the consignee establishment, and to such other person or installations designated by the Contracting Officer, in accordance with instructions of the Contracting Officer. If such instructions have not been received by the Contractor at least 24 hours prior to such delivery to a carrier, the Contractor shall request instructions from the Contracting Officer concerning the notice of shipment to be given. 150

CHAPTER 13 PRICE REVISION CLAUSES 

107. PURPOSE. Price revision clauses are approved for use in 

Army fixed-price contracts procured by negotiation as indicated in subparagraphs a through f of paragraph 109 of this text where necessary to obtain reasonable prices when contingency charges otherwise would be included in a contract price due to such factors as prolonged delivery s chedules, unstable market conditions for material or labor, or uncertainty as to cost of performance. By this type of clause included in a fixed-price contract, the Government assumes the risk of certain contingencies which a contractor would otherwise assume and would include in his contract price. The contract price ultimately is redetermined only to the extent that such contingencies actually occur.1 

108. HISTORY. Early in 1942, it became evident that huge amounts of Army purchases, even in volumes existing before Pearl Harbor, were creating problems which tended to foster excessive prices.2 The impact of huge volume alone made traditional pricing practices inadequate. 

Without benefit of past experience to draw upon, overpricing was bound to be the product not only of bad guesses, but also of the operation of the so-called "learning curve" and the influence of cumulative volume upon absorption of overhead charges. Contractors had no way of knowing the degree by which labor would increase its productivity in the course of performing under the contract, and because productivity increased sharply in many cases as new tasks were learned, the result, if not the practice, of overpricing became evident. Rapid changes in volume of production disrupted normal relationships governing distribution of overhead expenses and since the trend was upward, charges for overhead previously priced tended to be too high, were unexpended and resulted in greater profits than were originally anticipated. 

On 19 November 1942 the Purchases Division, Services of 

Supply of the War Department, formulated the Department's initial pricing policy predicated on the principle that prices and cost of war equipment should be kept at the lowest possible levels as a means of encouraging efficiency of production and conserving manpower and materials. It adopted the proposition that under wartime conditions some sort of administrative control was necessary in order to keep prices close enough to costs to exert a pressure on costs; that purchase control rather than the use of formulas and 1. ASPR 3-403. 

2. History of Purchases Div, Hq ASF; ASF Manual 101 Aug 1943, Pricing in War Contracts. 

151

The regulations was the best and simplest method of price control. policy encouraged the use of well negotiated fixed-price contracts, but at the same time acknowledged that under war conditions certain unpredictable risks were present which impelled the use of contingency charges and that closer prices could be obtained if the Government assumed some of these risks. It recommended that in cases where contingency charges were not present in prices, these risks should be transferred to the Government by means, among others, of special contractual clauses. By the first quarter of 1943 the important recommendations of the policy had become well established war Department policies and definite standard escalation and price revision clauses were included in Revision of the war Department Frocurement Regulations, issued on 26 March 1943. A discussion of these clauses is not included herein except to point out that the clauses hereinafter described as being in present use bear an important resemblance to those drafted and used at that time. Although no precise measurement of the accomplishments of the aforementioned pricing programs is available, it is accepted that substantial savings resulted therefrom. 

109. FRICE REVISION ARTICLES IN FACETIME. Present day price re vision clauses are designed to obtain at the lowest possible cost items and services needed by the Government. Although during the war these clauses were used as a means of encouraging efficiency of production and conserving manpower and materials and cost to the Government factor was given secondary consideration, present utilization of these clauses is designed to provide within available limited appropriations the greatest amount of defense material for the money available. Certain basic clauses have been approved from time to time to accomplish this objective. These clauses are frequently modified and other clauses are added to meet particular situations and changing conditions. The clauses hereinafter described are illustrative of the type of clauses used and may be changed from time to time. The following clauses presently are approved: a.

I' Form 13This article authorizes upward or downward price revision to be negotiated at fixed periods ranging from 3 to 4 months in length with prospective effect only. Both parties are bound by price for the first period. The periods should conform with operation of a cost accounting system which the contractor is required to maintain as a condition for price revision under the article and may be measured either in time or production or delivery of items. Periods need not be equal in length. The article requires that the contractor 

3. JAAFPR, 1 Nov 1947, par 4-303. 

152

furnish prior to price revision (1) a new estimate and breakdown of unit cost and proposed prices of items to be delivered in succeeding period under the contract, (2) explanation of differences in new estimate and estimate upon which previous price was established, (3) relevant accounting data upon which estimates are based, (4) cost experience on completed work, and (5) other relevant data. Provision for Government audit of the contractor's books and records is included. The article further provides that failure to negotiate a price thereunder within 30 days from the date contractor has submitted required data will constitute a dispute under the "Disputes 

Article" of the contract. Additionally, provision is made for termination of the contract at the option of the Government. Thus the Government may terminate the contract and seek the item elsewhere if it does not wish to pay what it considers to be an unreasonable price for an item. If it questions the basis for the higher price and still wants the contractor to furnish the item, the matter may be reviewed by the Armed Services Board of Contract Appeals. Form I 

may be used only where there is an absence of competition and the price is negotiated on the understanding that Form I will be used in the contract. The contract price must contain substantially no contingency charges and must be based upon estimates arrived at after employment of proper estimating methods. It should not be used unless the contractor's accounting system is sufficiently reliable and accurate for proper operation of the article. b.

Form II-A3 

Upward or downward price revision upon demand of either party is authorized by this article. The revised price has prospective effect only and the parties are bound by the initial price until such time as either party demands revision. The article will limit the frequency of price revision demands and will set forth the period before which no demand may be made. It requires submission of the same data required by Form I above and also provides for termination of the contract at the option of the Government and settlement of disagreements on price revision negotiations under the "Disputes Article" of the contract. Form II-A may be used where the nature of the product, the relative intricacies of manufacture, the proposed methods of production, the experience of the contractor and his labor force, the prior use of the particular plant, the ability of the contractor to make a reasonable accurate estimate, and other surrounding conditions are such that the Government and the contractor would normally be bound by the initial price. This article, therefore, is designed for inclusion in a contract with a contractor who has had experience in making the same or similar items called for in the contract. Its purpose is to keep the price of the item down by protecting the 

3. JAAFPR, 1 Nov 1947, par 4-303. 

153

contractor against unforeseen contingencies and affording the Government the benefits of any factors which may reduce the cost of production of the item. The initial price should be based on projections extending over the entire life of the contract but should not give effect to possible increases in costs.

Form II-B3 This article provides for upward and downward negotiated price revision, with the first period fixed and subject to retroactive revision, and prospective revision thereafter upon demand at specified intervals. Obviously article II-B should be used where the contractor is unable accurately to estimate the initial price and conditions are such that neither the Government nor the contractor should be bound by the price negotiated for the initial period. from the retroactivity of the price established on the first price revision and the provision for a time when the first price revision shall take place the article is substantially the same as article II-A. ·4 Aside d. Form III - Downward price revision negotiated upon contracting officer's demand is provided for in this article. This price revision is to be made after completion or termination of the contract. 

This article may be included in contracts in which the contract amount is $100,000 or less and the items to be manufactured are developmental or experimental in character. The initial price would of necessity have to bear a reasonable relationship to the expected final price under the contract to warrant use of this article and the contractors cost accounting system should be adequate to show costs under the contract.

The article provides for submission by the contractor of cost data within 60 days after completion or termination of the contract and the negotiation of a price which will represent fair and reasonable compensation for the performance of the contract. It provides for use of the "Dispute Article" of contract in event of disagreement in negotiating the price. 

e. Form IV5 

Under this article a contract price is agreed upon which will tend to compel the contractor to do as efficient a job as possible, and at the same time a maximum price is fixed as a ceiling of any upward revision under the article. Downward revision is also provided for. The revision takes place after completion or termination of the contract and is wholly retroactive. The article is designed for and its use restricted to contracts for experimental or developmental

items or services for tests in (1) laboratories, (2) field operations, or (3) similar experiments. f. Form Vo

This article provides for upward or downward price revision negotiated upon happening of a specified contingent event and limited to that event and its direct effect. The contingency must come within one of the following categories (1) cases where the Government fixes the price or controls the supply of material to be used by the contractor in the performance of the contract; (2) cases where freight rates upon specified materials to be used by the contractor in the performance of the contract are charged under authority of the Interstate Commerce Commission; (3) cases where the contractor contemplates the purchase of a single specified important material or component, which is to be used in performing the contract, from a stated source or at a stated price and such source or price is necessarily changed without fault or any voluntary action on the part of the contractor; (4) cases where the contractor's compliance with the delivery schedule of the contract is prevented as a direct or proximate result of any act of the Government or any of its instrumentalities. The article sets forth the contingency; provides for submission of data by contractor; provides manner of redetermination, including percentage of increase to be allowed in event of happening of the contingency; and provides for termination of the contract and for settlement of disagreements on price revision under the "Disputes Article" of the contract. 

110. ESCALATION CLAUSES. In addition to price revision articles referred to in paragraph109,certain escalation clauses calling for upward and downward escalation of the unit prices under a contract are authorized for inclusion in certain Government contracts. These clauses are included in Government contracts to give protection to contractors against unforeseen contingencies and thus permit the contractor to offer lower over-all prices. On a rising market this clause affords protection to the contractor against rising costs, whereas on a falling market the Government receives the benefits of lower costs. These clauses afford the Government an opportunity to avail itself of price decreases on items being procured over a relatively long period of time. 

6. JAAFPR, 1 Nov 1947, par 4-308. 

155

Escalation clauses are normally used in the procurement of standard and semi-standard items where the contractor is familiar with the quantity of materials, the number of hours of labor, the administrative and overhead charges and the indirect cost of manufacture required in the production of the item. Inclusion of such a clause in the contract permits the contractor to secure an increased unit price in the event of an increase in the list price of the item, the price of materials, or the rates of labor before delivery of the item. It permits the Government to secure a lower unit price if the list price is lower at time of delivery or if the aforementioned costs are lower than estimated. Conversely, price revision clauses are used primarily in contracts for purchase of nonstandard items or in research and development contracts, where both direct and indirect costs are uncertain. Frice revision clauses rather than escalation clauses should be used where it is desired to redetermine an estimated price based upon change in quantity of material, difference in hours of labor, difference in general and administrative expenses, or other direct or indirect costs.

Several escalation clauses are now authorized for use in Government contracts. They are classified according to the item to be purchased: a. Escalation for standard steel items. The clause for inclusion in contracts for manufacture of articles, made in whole or in part of steel, by an "integrated steel producer" or an "operator of a steel foundry" may be used for either advertised or negotiated procurement. Invitations for bids must clearly state that bids containing the clause will be considered responsive but that such bids will be evaluated on the basis of applying the maximum escalation to the basic bid.7 The clause is based upon the dealers' "established price" and authorizes unlimited downward escalation as such established price decreases and upward escalation not to exceed 10 of the original applicable contract unit price. It provides for cancellation by the Government without liability in the event it does not desire to accept the increased price provided such notice of cancellation is sent to the contractor within 30 days after receipt of the contractor's request for price increase. No limitation is placed upon the number of such requests, but the number may be limited by the 10% limitation. b. Aluminum price escalation clause.8 This clause is substantially the same in form as the steel escalation clause. It is authorized 

7. CP Dir. 9-FY 1950, 26 Jul 1949. 

8.

As in the case of the steel clause, invitations for bids should state that bids including the clause will be considered responsible and that all bids will be evaluated after applying the maximum amount of escalation possible.
