CHAPTER 14 GOVERNMENT FURNISHED MATERIAL AND FACILITIES SECTION I. GENERAL 

111.

INTRODUCTION. It is often desirable for the Government to furnish property to a contractor for use in connection with procurement. 

Property so furnished is generally referred to as "Government-furnished property."

The Department's policy with respect to the furnishing of property, as well as the obligations imposed upon the contractor in connection therewith, is dependent upon the type of property involved. 

Section II of this chapter deals with the furnishing of material. 

Section III with Special Tooling and Section IV with Industrial Installations and Plant Equipment. 

"Government-furnished material" generally comprises property which may be incorporated in, or otherwise used in the production of, the "end-product" to be delivered to the Government. "Special Tooling" comprises property which is of such a specialized nature that the use thereof, without substantial modification, is limited to the present or future production of the particular supplies or services for which it was furnished. "Industrial Facilities" may be defined as industrial real property, including improvements and appurtenances thereto, together with all related maintenance and production machinery and equipment, primarily used or capable of use in the procurement of supplies or services. It follows that "Industrial Installations," and "Industrial Facilities" by usage and definition, do not include material furnished by the Government for incorporation in the end product to be procured, and that neither does it include jigs, dies, patterns, special hand and portable tools, and other items of a like nature which are usually referred to as special tooling. 

Some types of fixed-price contracts are entered into on the basis of the Government's furnishing the entire quantity of the "material" required. Normally, however, "material" is furnished to a contractor only where it is not readily available in the open market or cannot readily be manufactured by the contractor or its subcontractors. On the other hand, special tooling is generally furnished whenever it is available in a usable condition unless the administrative cost incident to such furnishing is disproportionately great. 159

The "Government-Furnished Material" clause, used whenever applicable, specified that title to all Government-furnished property shall remain in the Government. Where there is a likelihood that any Government-owned property will be attached to realty, which frequently is the case where plant equipment is involved, express provision is also made that no part or item of Government-furnished property shall be or become a fixture by reason of affixation to any realty now owned by the Government.

Where the Government undertakes to furnish material or special tooling and fails to do so, such failure may constitute a breach of the contract by the Government entitling the contractor to recover damages. Accordingly, provision is usually made for extending the time of performance to the extent the Government has delayed in furnishing the property, in which case the contractor is limited to an extension of time of performance and is not entitled to damages. 1 In order to compensate the contractor for additional costs incurred by it as a result of the Government's failure to meet its obligation to deliver, all fixed-price supply contracts involving Governmentfurnished property are required to provide that, in the event of such failure on the part of the Government, an equitable adjustment in the contract price, as well as in the time of performance, will be made in accordance with the terms and procedures provided in the "Changes" clause. Delay in furnishing Government property is not so serious a problem in the case of cost-reimbursement type contracts, inasmuch as any increased cost incurred by the contractor under such contract is reimbursable under the contract as an allowable cost. However, where such increased costs are likely to be substantial, thereby in effect requiring the contractor to expend greater effort than originally contemplated for the stipulated fixed fee, such contracts sometimes provide for an equitable adjustment to the fixed fee in the event of late delivery of Government-furnished property. 

Where Government-owned "special tooling" or plant equipment is furnished to a contractor, the contract generally provides that the contractor shall protect, preserve, maintain and repair such property in accordance with sound industrial practice. The cost of complying with such requirement is reimbursable as an allowable cost in the case of cost-reimbursement type contracts, and in fixed-price contracts may be included in the contract price in the discretion of the contracting officer and upon such terms and conditions as he may approve. Further, the contractor's right to use Government-owned property is limited to the particular contract under which such property is acquired or furnished. Such a contract may provide, however, in the case of special tooling or plant equipment, that the contractor may use such property in other designated Government contracts (usually cost- 

1. United States v. Howard P. Foley Co., 329 U.S. 64(1946). 

160

The reimbursement type contracts) and may under certain circumstances use such property in its commercial work, upon payment for such use. contractor's right to use property furnished by the Government is also made subject to the Government's right to terminate such use whenever the interests of national defense so require. 

With respect to special tooling furnished by a contractor, the cost of which is included in the price of the end item, the policy is that the Government should take title to special tooling in the case of fixed-price contracts (and the contract so provides) whenever, in the opinion of the contracting officer, the residual value of the special tooling will exceed the administrative burden and expense incident to acquiring title. Such burden and expense is usually substantial because, if title is taken to special tooling, the property must be identified, appropriate records with respect thereto must be maintained, and the property must be stored or disposed of at the completion of the contract. If the contractor is to be permitted to retain such property for other work, in that event rent and other terms of use must be agreed upon. Consequently, in most cases it is not desirable for the Government to acquire title to special tooling, especially if the dollar amount included in the contract price is reasonable and no real likelihood exists that any of the special tooling will have any residual value other than as scrap or junk. 

In many cases, however, where items of special tooling have substantial residual value, provision will be made in the contract (when it is in the interests of the Government to so provide) that the contractor shall undertake to retain the tooling for a considerable period of time, to maintain such property at its own expense and to use it in future Government procurement without charging the Government for storage maintenance costs or for its use in performing future contracts for the Government.

SECTION II. GOVERNMENT MATERIAL 112. SCOPE OF SECTION. 

In order to conserve basic material and labor and to attain the most advantageous position for the Government in the procurement of necessary supplies and equipment it is often not only desirable but necessary that the Government furnish to suppliers materials to be fabricated into the item desired and/or to furnish parts or assemblies to be incorporated into or attached to the item to be supplied.2 A procurement contract may provide for acquisition by the 

2. The Government may enter into a contract for the purchase of tents, The contract may provide that the Government will furnish the canvas which is to be fabricated into the tents, grommet which will be incorporated into the end item and ropes which will be attached. A contract for the purchase of tanks may be entered into whereby the Government will furnish the motors to be incorporated into the end item or furnish the guns which are to be attached or mounted on the tank. 161

contractor at Government expense of materials or parts to become a part of the finished item to be delivered to the Government. Where the contractor himself furnished the material and parts to be incorporated into the end item it is advantageous to the Government to provide in the supply contract that the Government acquire an interest in such material when the contract makes provision for advanced or progress payments or where the contract is terminated for the convenience of the Government prior to the completion thereof. 

113. AUTHORITY. The authority to furnish material to a contractor supplying the Government is inherent to the authority to purchase supplies and equipment.3 3. 

The authority of the Government to furnish materials to a contractor is neither expressly authorized by any act, nor expressly prohibited by any act. It can be reasonably said, however, that the power to so furnish is necessary for the due and efficient exercise of the powers granted to him to obtain supplies authorized by statute. In 

27 Op. Atty. Gen. 173 (1909), The Attorney General cited with approval the following from the case of Lewis v. Colgan, 115 Cal 529; 47 Pac 357 (1897): "In addition to the powers expressly given by statute to an officer or board of officers he or it has, by implication, such additional powers as are necessary for the due and efficient exercise of the powers expressly granted, or as may be fairly implied from the statutes granting the powers." 

In 40 Op. Atty. Gen 171 (1942), the Secretary of the Treasury requested an opinion of the Attorney General whether the Treasury "can lease or license the use of 'free silver' in the place of copper for war production in both Government and privately owned plants under an agreement to assure the return of the silver?"

It was not intended that the silver should become a part of the products or be used up, but rather should become a part of the plant equipment in such a way as to permit substantially all of it to be returned to the Government at the termination of the war. or before at the Government's request. The silver was not to be sold, and safeguards were to be adopted to insure the return of the silver and properly to indemnify the Government if any silver was lost from wear and tear or for other reason. Free silver was defined as uncoined silver owned by the Government which was not needed to be held as security for outstanding silver certificates.

The Attorney General stated: 

"Licensing or other similar permission to sue the 'free silver' is also authorized by Section 161 of the Revised

Statutes (U.S.C., Title 5, Sec. 22) 

162

114. DEFINITIONS. a. Government material. The term "Government 

Material" means such material as may be: (1) furnished by the Government to a contractor, (2) furnished by the contractor for incorporation into the item being procured by the Government, (3) acquired by the contractor at the cost of the Government. b. The term "Material" means such property as may be incorporated into the end product to be delivered to the Government under the contract or may be consumed in the performance of the contract. The term includes, but is not limited to, raw materials, parts, components, assemblies, sub-assemblies, and manufacturing, operating, maintenance, office, cafeteria, laboratory, and hospital supplies. In general this classification will include all property which will be consumed or will lose its identity by being incorporated in or attached to other property. 1 "Revocable licenses in the public interest for the use of Government property can be and have been granted many times under this statute. Illustrative is 34 Op. 320, in which 

Attorney General Stone held that the Secretary of the Navy had the power to grant revocable licenses to use Governmentowned patents without specific Congressional authority. "Aside from the question whether the war powers of the President under the Constitution may, under certain circumstances, authorize the President to dispose of property of the United States independently of the Constitutional provision authorizing the Congress to make and regulate such dispositions, it is clear that the Congress, in section 161 of the Revised Statutes, has given the head of a department power - short of complete alienation of title and control over 'the custody, use and preservation' of such property.

Under the statute, the Secretary of the Treasury is empowered to permit the use of free silver when he does not relinquish paramount control over it. But such permission to use must result in a benefit to the Government. The benefit need not be consideration in a technical sense. Under the circumstances stated, the Government would be arranging for the use of the silver to permit the release of copper - a strategic and critical material vitally necessary in the manufacture of articles of war. Such a benefit is of the highest order. 

For Government, as for individuals, self-preservation is one of the first rules of existence. "But such a benefit alone is not sufficient to justify a windfall to a private manufacturer. The lease or license should be so drafted that any exorbitant profit which might accrue to a private manufacturer by substitution of silver for copper would be compensated for by some payment or other 163

115. GENERAL POLICIES WITH RESPECT TO ACQUISITION BY CONTRACTOR, AND FURNISHING BY THE GOVERNMENT, OF MATERIALS REQUIRED FOR THE PROCURE- MENT OF SUPPLIES AND SERVICES. a. Cost or CPFF procurement (Government provided material). Where supplies or services are contracted for on a cost or cost-plus-fixed-fee basis, material may be furnished by the Government to the extent determined by the Contracting Officer to be in the best interest of the Government. Other material required for the performance of the contract shall be acquired by the contractor. b. Fixed price procurement (Government-furnished material). 

Where supplies or services are contracted for on a fixed price basis, all or part of the material may be furnished by the Government. Where such procurement is by formal advertising, material to be furnished by the Government shall be set forth in the Invitation for Bids. 

At any time after the award of a fixed price contract as a result of formal advertising or negotiation, the contract may be amended to provide for or to increase or decrease the Government-furnished material, provided the Government receives adequate consideration for such amendment. benefit flowing from the private manufacturer to the United States. The payment or other benefit need not be exactly equivalent; a broad range of negotiation, of course, exists in which the Secretary may use his administrative discretion."

Although the Attorney General was asked to rule upon the authority of the Secretary of the Treasury to either lease or license silver, the authority of a head of a department under Section 161, supra, over the "custody, use, and preservation" is not necessarily limited to leases or licenses. The Attorney General speaks of licenses "or other similar permission" to use such property, and such power exists in a broad sense, short of complete alienation of title and control of such property. 

The ruling of the Attorney General with respect to the power of the head of a department to authorize the use of property under his control is particularly applicable to the situation where Government-owned facilities are furnished either by provision in a supply contract or by a special facilities contract to contractors producing supplies for the Government. The use of such property results in a benefit to the Government, since the use of such property results in the reduction of the cost to the Government of the supplies furnished the Government. 164

C. Fixed price procurement (contractor-furnished material). 

Where supplies or services are procured on a fixed price basis, the Government shall acquire no interest in the material furnished by the contractor prior to the delivery of supplies and services to the Government except as otherwise provided in connection with advance payments, progress payments and termination.

SECTION III. SPECIAL TOOLING 116. SCOPE OF SECTION. In many instances supplies and equipment required by the Army are peculiar to its operation and functions and no public demand therefor exists. As there is no public demand for such items commercial manufacturers and suppliers are not able to supply the Government with the desired items without new or additional equipment and special tooling. Consequently, when suppliers bid on 

Government contracts for furnishing the desired supplies or services the costs of such equipment and special tooling is included in the costs to the Government of the supplies or services furnished.4 The cost of such special tooling having been paid for by the Government it is desirable that a supply contract that involves such tooling should give to Government certain rights in regard thereto. Where the Government owns the necessary special tooling it may be furnished to the contractor by contractual arrangements. 

117. AUTHORITY AND GENERAL POLICY. The authority to permit the use by a contractor, producing for the Government, of Government owned special tooling required for such productions or the providing by contract that the Government acquire certain rights in special tooling which the Government bears the cost of is not only inherent to the authority to purchase but is sanctioned by usage.5 The Department shall furnish special tooling under its control to contractors, if determined to be available, rather than authorize new special tooling at Government 4. 5.

The Government may desire to purchase ten motors of a special type, designed to do a specific job. With the necessary jigs, dies, patterns and test equipment the contractor could supply the motors for $50,000. However, in order to manufacture such a motor it may be necessary for the contractor to spend $70,000 for the necessary special tooling. Therefor the contractors bid for supplying the desired motors would be $120,000. As a result the Army not only pays for the motors but also for the special tooling necessary to produce the motors.

The Constitution (Art. IV, sec. 3, cl. 2) gives to Congress the power "to dispose of *** property belonging to the United States, " 165

expense, if the cost of such furnishing is less than the cost of new special tooling. In determining the cost to the Government, consideration shall be given to (a) the cost of the preparation of such special tooling and the shipment thereof to the plant of the contractor, (b) the cost of adapting such special tooling to meet the requirements of the contract, (c) any delays which may be occasioned, (d) the increase in the cost to the Government of the supplies or services being procured if such special tooling is not furnished by the Government, and (e) any other costs. If following the completion of the contract the Contracting Officer determines in accordance with the criteria set forth in connection with this subject that it is not in the best interest of the Government to require delivery of all or any part of the contractor acquired special tooling, such special tooling or part thereof may be retained by the contractor free and clear of any interest of the Government upon credits against any amounts due the contractor under the contract of an amount equal to the fair value of such tooling or part thereof as determined by the Contracting Officer, or upon payment of like amount. and Mr. Justice Thompson, sitting in Circuit, in United States v. 

Nicoll, 1 Paine 646, 27 Fed. Cas. 15879, pp. 149, 150 (1826), an early case concerned with the disposition of personal property said: "No public property can, therefor, be disposed of without the authority of law, either by an express Act of Congress for that purpose, or by giving the authority to some Department of the 

Government, or subordinate agent." 

See also Wisconsin R. Co. v. Price County, 133 U.S. 496, 504 (1889) in its usual connection as interpreted by the Courts "to dispose of" means "to alienate" or "to effectually transfer." United 

States v. Hacker, 73 Fed. 293, 294 (1896). The term includes a lease which results in a diminishing of the interest, control or right of the owners in the property. United States v. Nicoll, supra; United States v. Gratiot, 39 U.S. 526, 527 (1840); Platt v. 

Union Pacific, 99 U.S. 48, 49 (1878); Hill v. Summer, 132 U.S. 118 (1889). 

It follows, then, that property once acquired by the Government may not be sold, or title otherwise disposed of, except under the authority of Congress, and in the manner provided by law, and this prohibition extends to any attempt to alienate a part of the property, or in general, in any manner to limit or restrict the full and exclusive ownership of the United States therein. 

Op. Atty. Gen. 322 (1924). 34 166

118. DEFINITION. The term "Special Tooling" means property which is of such specialized nature that its use, without substantial modification or alteration, is limited to the production of the particular articles or the procurement of the particular services, for which acquired or furnished. Special tooling includes, but is not limited to, jigs, dies, fixtures, templates, patterns, special tops, special gauges, special hand tools, special portable tools and special test equipment. In general, this term covers property, other than material and special installation, the entire cost of which is chargeable to a particular contract in accordance with sound accounting practice. 

The question here is not of the right or power of the Army to dispose of the property, but the authority which must be invoked in order to grant the use of the property by a manufacturer who is to use it in producing for the Government.

While it is true that Congress is the only authority to be invoked where there is in fact an alienation or what amounts to a transfer or surrender of the Government property by which title or possession of the Government is lost, reduced, or abridged, the furnishing of Government property to a manufacturer in connection with procurement where the Government property is to be used exclusively by the manufacturer in producing for the Government is not a disposal of Government property within such meaning. 

In construing a statute (or a clause of the Constitution) the intent and object which is clearly apparent should be kept in mind, and "a construction which would go beyond the evil intended to be remedied and would produce apparently unforeseen and untoward 

results should, upon well-settled principles, be avoided. " 28 Op. 

Atty. Gen 78, 83.

It is plain that the intent of the Constitutional provision was to prevent alienation of the title, ownership, or control of Government property, whether real or personal, without Congressional sanction. That is the evil which was intended to be avoided, and no construction beyond that intended should be imposed on the prohibition unless clearly implied, especially when it would lead to unreasonable and unforeseen results.

The distinction between dispose, alienate, transfer and permitting the use of property in producing for the Government is easily seen. 

Section 161, Revised Statutes, (5 U.S.C. 21), provides that: "The head of each department is authorized to prescribe regulations, not inconsistent with law, for the government of his department, the conduct of its officers and clerks, the distribution and performance of its business, and the custody, use and preservation of the records, papers, and property appertaining to it." 167

119. a. 

POLICIES WITH RESPECT TO GOVERNMENT-PROVIDED AND GOVERNMENT- FURNISHED AND CONTRACTOR-FURNISHED SPECIAL TOOLING. 

Cost or CPFF 

procurement (Government-provided special tooling). Where supplies or services are contracted for on a cost or cost-plus-fixed-fee basis, special tooling may be furnished by the Government to the extent determined by the contracting officer to be in the best interest of the Government. Other special tooling required for the performance of the contract shall be acquired by the contractor. 

Judge Sawyer, in an opinion in re Neagle, 39 Fed. 833, 851 (1889), concurred in by Judge Sabin, said: "The statutes of Congress, in their express provisions, do not present all the law of the United States. Their incidents and implications are as much a part of the law as their express provisions. When they prescribe duties, provide of the accomplishment of certain designated objects, or confer authority in general terms, they carry with them all the powers essential to effect the ends designed." 

Under section 161 of the Revised Statutes, the head of each Department regulates the custody, use and preservation of property pertaining to it. So that it may be said that while the Constitution prohibits the alienation of the title, ownership or control of Government property without Congressional sanction, Congress has given to the head of a Department authority and control over the "use" and preservation of such property in his charge.

The inherent limitation upon furnishing Government property in connection with producing required supplies and equipment, namely, that the use of Government owned property by the manufacturer must subserve some purpose useful or beneficial to the Government itself is one that goes to the mode of exercise rather than the existence of the power.

Whether the furnishing is or is not a benefit to the Government is a question involving the exercise of judgment by the official vested with the power rather than a question of law. 

In United States v. Macdaniels, 7 Peters (33 U.S.) 1 (1833), it is made clear that the head of a Government Department does not have to show statutory authority for everything he does. Reasonable latitude in exercise of discretion is implied. Usages have been established in every Department of the Government, which have become a kind of common law and regulates the rights and duties of those who act within their respective limits ***. Usage cannot alter the law, but it is evidence of the construction given to it. 

See also 34 Op. Atty. Gen. 326 (1924); 25 Op. Atty. Gen. 470 (1905); United States v. Tingey, 5 Peters (30 U.S.) 115, 127 (1831). 168

b. Fixed-price procurement (Government-furnished special tooling). Where supplies or services are contracted for on a fixed price basis all or part of the special tooling required for performance of the contract may be furnished by the Government. Where such procurement is by formal advertising, special tooling to be furnished by the Government shall be set forth in the Invitation for Bids. At any time after the award of a fixed price contract, as a result of either formal advertising or negotiation, the contract may be amended to provide for or to increase or decrease the Government furnished special tooling, provided the Government receives adequate consideration for such amendment. 

C. Fixed-price procurement (Contractor-furnished special tooling). (1) Acquisition by the Government. If the cost to the

Government of supplies or services procured under a negotiated fixed price contract includes the cost of special tooling furnished by the contractor, or the cost thereof is specifically included in an advertised fixed price contract, the contract shall require the contractor to deliver such items of the special tooling to the Government when, and as directed by, the Contracting Officer. If delivery of any such items is directed prior to termination of the need for those items in the performance of the contract, an equitable adjustment will be made in the contract price or delivery schedule or both. In determining whether items of special tooling shall be delivered to the Government, consideration shall be given to (a) the need of the Government for such special tooling; (b) the amount offered by the contractor for such special tooling; (c) the residual value of such special tooling; (d) the administrative and other expense incident to delivery to, and preservation by, the Government of such special tooling; (e) the reduction in price, if any, which the Government would obtain by making such special tooling available for use by the contractor in future procurement; and (f) the cost incident to making such special tooling available for use by other contractors in the performance of contracts with the Government. (2) Retention by the contractor. If, following completion of performance under a contract, the Contracting Officer determines that it is not in the best interests of the Government to require delivery to the Government of all or any part of contractorfurnished or contractor-acquired special tooling, such special tooling or part thereof may be retained by the contractor, free and clear of any interest of the Government, upon payment to the Government of an amount equal to the fair value of such special tooling or by a credit of a like amount against the cost of the supplies or services procured. 

120. LEASE BY THE GOVERNMENT. The Department may, to the extent authorized by law, lease special tooling to the contractor or to any third person.

Each such lease shall (a) require the lessee to protect, preserve, maintain, and repair such special tooling in accordance with 169

sound industrial practice and as may be required by the Department concerned, (b) require the lessee to pay a fair and reasonable rental for the right to use such special tooling in or in connection with work other than that under negotiated Government contracts, which rental may reflect and take into consideration obligations assumed by the lessee to protect, preserve, maintain, and repair such special tooling, and (c) provide for termination thereof by the Department concerned whenever the interests of the national defense so require. 

The foregoing requirements shall not be waived except with the approval in each case by the Secretary of the Department. Each such lease shall also include a provision that title to such special tooling shall be and remain in the Government. 

SECTION IV. 

INDUSTRIAL FACILITIES AND PLANT EXPANSION 121. INDUSTRIAL FACILITIES. a. Scope of section. This section sets forth policies pertaining to providing contractors with industrial facilities required for procurement of supplies and services by the Army. b. General policy. It is the general policy of the Armed 

Services that contracts for supplies or services shall be awarded to contractors who have the industrial facilities necessary for the performance of such contracts or will themselves provide such facilities. 

However, when it is necessary or desirable, the Army may, if within its statutory authority, provide industrial facilities to a contractor, and to the extent authorized by law preference in such cases shall be given to contractors requiring the least amount of such facilities and to contractors employing 500 persons or less. Contracts for supplies or services based upon the use of industrial facilities to be provided by the Government shall not be awarded until the scope and magnitude of the facilities required have been determined and the facilities project has been authorized 

C. Facilities contracts. (1) Industrial facilities shall be provided only under a facilities contract or lease separate from any related contract for supplies or services, except that facilities for the performance of construction contracts and contracts for work within establishments operated by the Government may be provided under suitable clauses in the contracts involved. All facilities provided by a procuring activity under a facilities contract for use by a contractor at each plant or location shall be governed by a single facilities contract, amended from time to time as necessary, covering only the facilities at that plant or location, except that provision may be made for the temporary use of a portion of the facilities at another location when approved by the Contracting Officer. Except 170

for such temporary use, and except for facilities for the performance of subcontracts under construction contracts or under contracts for work within establishments operated by the Government, facilities provided to a subcontractor shall be pursuant to a separate facilities contract or lease between the Government and the subcontractor. (2) It is the policy of the Department of Defense that facilities at one location will be under the cognizance of a single procuring activity (normally the activity having the major procurement interest), which will administer the Facilities Contract, be accountable for the facilities, and control the ultimate disposition thereof. Such facilities will be made available to the extent possible for the performance at the location of contracts for supplies and services placed by other procuring activities. Additional facilities required at the location for performance of contracts for supplies and services for another procuring activity will be provided under the Facilities Contract of the cognizant Department, subject to transfer of funds and approval as to necessity for the facilities by the requiring procuring activity. At such times as the cognizant procuring activity ceases to have a substantial procurement interest in the location, administration of the Facilities Contract and accountability for the facilities for which a need continues will be transferred to the procuring activity with the remaining major procurement interest. 

d. Title. Each facilities contract shall provide that title to all industrial facilities fumished to the contractor shall remain in the Government, and that title to industrial facilities acquired by a contractor the cost of which is reimbursable to the contractor shall vest in the Government at the earliest possible time. Industrial facilities which are to be located outside the plant of the contractor shall be located on land owned by the Government or subject to the control of the Government for the estimated life of the facilities. 

With respect to industrial facilities which must be located within the plant of the contractor and which are not readily removable without substantial loss of value, including cost of installation, the facilities contract shall provide, to the extent authorized by law, either (1) that the contractor shall retain the industrial facilities upon the expiration or earlier termination of the contract, and shall pay to the Government therefor an amount equal to the installed cost of the facilities less depreciation at the rate or rates stated in the contract, or (2) that the Government shall retain ownership of the facilities and the contractor shall, at his expense, maintain such facilities in accordance with the maintenance and other provisions of the facilities contract for a period of five years from the date of expiration or earlier termination of the contract. Without prior approval of the head of the Department, no facilities contract shall require removal of the facilities or restoration of the contractor's property by the Government. 171

e. Contractor's right to use. Each facilities contract shall limit the contractor's right to use the industrial facilities to the performance of negotiated contracts specified in the facilities contract or added thereto by amendment. Such use may be without charge to the contractor. Provision may be made in the facilities contract, however, for the use of all or part of the facilities in connection with work other than that under negotiated contracts specified in the facilities contract whenever the contracting officer determines such use will not interfere with performance of the specified negotiated contracts. If the facilities contract provides for such use, the contract shall require the contractor to pay a fair and reasonable charge therefor based upon that proportion of the time or capacity that the facilities will be available for use by the contractor in connection with other than the specified negotiated contracts. Such fair and reasonable charge shall be established on the basis of sound commercial practice, including any prevailing commercial rates, and shall not permit the contractor to obtain an unfair competitive advantage by reason of such charge. As to personal property and equipment with respect to which there are no prevailing rates, the charge shall be at the rate of not less than 1% per month of the original acquisition cost of such property or equipment. f. Lease of industrial facilities. Industrial facilities may be leased to contractors under Public Law 364, 80th Congress, in lieu of a facilities contract. Each such lease shall require the lessee to pay a fair and reasonable rental for the right to use the industrial facilities but the obligations assumed by the lessee to protect and maintain the facilities in accordance with sound commercial standards and to repair or restore the facilities may be taken into consideration in determining the rental to be charged to the contractor. The requirement of a fair and reasonable rental shall not be waived in leases unless specifically authorized by the Secretary of the Department. g. Maintenance. Each facilities contract or lease under which industrial facilities are provided by the Government shall require the contractor or lessee, at its expense, to protect, preserve, maintain and repair such facilities in accordance with sound industrial practice until relieved of accountability therefor. However, when authorized by the Secretary of the Department, such contract or lease may provide for: (1) A specified degree of preservation, maintenance and repair that is less than sound industrial practice as to such part of a facility or the facilities as are determined to be non-essential to future utilization of a facility or the facilities as a whole. (2) Reimbursement by the Government for the cost of maintaining, preserving and repairing facilities not authorized for use by the contractor or lessee. 172

(3) Reimbursement by the Government for repairs, replace ment, and restoration performed by the contractor or lessee when such repairs, replacement and restoration are in excess of normal maintenance and are directed or approved by the Contracting Officer. h. Identification and records. Each facilities contract or lease under which industrial facilities are provided by the Government shall require the contractor or lessee at its expense to provide and maintain an adequate method or system of identification of and property accounting for Government property that is satisfactory to the Contracting Officer. The Contracting Officer shall require such method or system to be in accordance with the applicable regulations and instructions of the Department. The Government property, and the contractor's accounting records with respect thereto, shall be available for inspection by the Government at all reasonable times. i. Late delivery, diversion and substitution. Each facilities contract shall provide that: (1) The Government shall not be liable to the contractor for damages or loss of profit by reason of any delay in delivery of Government-furnished facilities. Provision will be made in the supply contracts affected thereby for equitable adjustment of the prices or delivery schedules or both. (2) The Government shall have the right, when it is determined to be in the best interest of the Government, to direct: (a) delivery of any facility or facilities acquired by contractor under the contract, to locations other than those specified in the contract; (b) assignment, to the Government or to others, of purchase orders or subcontracts of the contractor for any facility or facilities covered by the contract. 

Provision will be made in the facilities contract for change or modification thereof in such event and equitable adjustments will be made in supply contracts affected. (3) If the contract provides for acquisition by the contractor of any of the facilities covered thereby, the Government shall have the right to furnish any of the facilities in lieu of acquisition thereof by the contractor. Provision will be made for reimbursement to the contractor of proper costs incurred in the performance of its obligations with respect to such facilities including the costs of terminating purchase orders or subcontracts for such facilities. 173

Each j. Risk of loss or damage, liability and insurance. facilities contract or lease, under which industrial facilities are provided by the Government, shall require: (1) The contractor to indemnify the Government against all loss and damage to Government facilities, ordinary wear and tear excepted, unless the use of the Government facilities is limited to negotiated contracts, in which event the contractor shall indemnify the Government only to the extent that such loss and damage (a) is covered by insurance, (b) is due to willful misconduct of the contractor or its supervisory representatives, (c) is due to failure of the contractor or its supervisory representatives to exercise such degree of care as a reasonably careful owner or plant operator would exercise with respect to his own property, (d) is due to the failure of the contractor to comply with the terms of the contract. (2) The contractor to hold the Government harmless against claims for damage to persons or property of the contractor or others arising from use of the Government facilities, except for such liability as may result from operations required by Government negotiated contracts which are determined to be inherently dangerous. (3) The contractor to carry insurance, provide selfinsurance, post bonds or provide other security at his own expense in an amount and form satisfactory to the Contracting Officer for: (a) workmen's compensation and employer's liability; (b) liability to third persons for death, injury and property damage; (c) loss of or damage to Government property.

Except that, in the case of facilities used exclusively in the performance of negotiated Government contracts, insurance, bonds or other security may in whole or in part be waived or made a reimbursable item under the facilities contract or related supply contracts. k. Termination of facilities contracts and right to use. A facilities contract shall be terminated when the facilities covered thereby are no longer required for the performance of Government contracts. Each facilities contract shall provide that the Government shall have the right, upon notice, to terminate such contract with respect to all or any part of the facilities covered thereby, or to terminate or limit the contractor's right to use any or all of such facilities. In the event of any such termination for the convenience of the Government provision will be made for equitable adjustment in affected supply or service contracts which were based on the use of facilities to be provided by the Government under the facilities contract. 174

Each facilities contract shall provide that the contractor has the right to terminate the contract at any time with respect to all facilities covered thereby or, by agreement with the Contracting Officer with respect to a substantial portion of such facilities. Each facilities contract which gives the contractor the right to use all or any part of the facilities covered thereby on other than negotiated Government contracts, shall also provide that the contractor may terminate such right to use with respect to all the facilities as to which such right exists, or, by agreement with the Contracting Officer, with respect to a substantial portion thereof. No termination at the option of the contractor shall relieve the contractor of any of its obligations or liabilities under any supply or service contract affected thereby. 

1. Termination of leases. Every lease of industrial facilities shall reserve to the Government the right to revoke such lease at any time, unless the Secretary of the Department determines that the omission of such provision from the lease will promote the national defense or be in the public interest, in which event it is the policy that the lease provide for revocation by the Secretary when he determines such revocation to be in the interest of national defense. In any event all leases shall be revocable by the Secretary of the Department during a national emergency declared by the President.

In the event of termination of any such lease by the Government, provision will be made for equitable adjustment in affected supply or service contracts which were based on the use of facilities to be furnished by the Government under such lease. m. Unusable or unneeded facilities. Each facilities contract or lease shall require the contractor or lessee to notify the Contracting Officer within thirty days of any facility lost, damaged, or destroyed, or, in the case of facilities contract, no longer needed for performance of Government contracts. Settlement for lost, damaged or destroyed facilities and disposition of damaged and excess facilities shall be accomplished periodically as directed by the Contracting Officer. n. Disposition. Except as to those facilities not readily removable and located in the plant of the contractor for which specific provision is made, each Facilities Contract shall provide that within the period specified in the contract following the effective date of partial or complete termination, the contractor will account for all facilities with respect to which the contract is terminated. Within sixty days after the contractor accounts for the facilities, the Contracting Officer will notify the contractor as to disposition thereof. 

The contractor shall be required to prepare, protect, store, remove or ship the facilities as directed by the Contracting Officer. Provision shall be made for equitable reimbursement of the contractor for expenses so incurred. If the Contracting Officer fails to notify the contractor within the sixty day period, the contractor may remove and store elsewhere at Government expense those facilities located within the plant of the contractor. 175

122. PLANT EXPANSION. a. General. At the present time there is no general authority for plant expansion. However, it is well to consider the problems which arise when an imminent national emergency requires the construction, expansion, or conversion of industrial plants to meet the needs of the Armed Services. 

In order to conserve material and labor for use in direct war production, it has always been the policy to use existing production facilities to the maximum capacity. In placing contracts, therefore, preference will be given to contractors who have sufficient production facilities, or who will require the least amount of new facilities for performance.

The Government's policy, where new facilities are required, is to encourage private industry to provide them rather than to finance their construction itself. In awarding contracts preference is therefore given to contractors who are willing to finance construction of new facilities which may be necessary. 

Where it is necessary, however, for the Government to provide new facilities at its own expense, its interest must be protected as far as practicable. Adequate protection is deemed to exist if the facilities (1) form a separate unit and are situated on land covered by a longterm lease; or (2) are readily removable or separable from the contractor's existing plant without unreasonable expense or loss of value. 

b. Historical. Between World War I and World War II, the thinking of the Army was largely directed to the items which would be needed to wage a future war and not to the capacity of industry to produce such items in astronomical quantities. In addition, too little thought was given to the fact that, if the required items must be produced in much less time than had hitherto been thought possible, existing industrial capacity would have to be expanded beyond the point which a more leisurely rate of production would permit. 

It was soon apparent, both to the Army and to private industry, that the vast expansion of the Army could not be accomplished without a large expansion of private industrial capacity. Private industry took the position that it could not possibly undertake an expansion program of its own which would be of sufficient magnitude to meet increased requirements. At the same time, the expansion program of the Navy was demanding its share of available sources of supply. The result was that the Armed Services were faced with the grim alternatives of increasing existing sources of supply or doing without many of the items that each of them needed. 176

The need for new capacity, particularly for the expansion of airplane factories, was so great in 1939 and 1940, and the funds which were available for this purpose were so small, that methods other than those available under appropriations by Congress had to be utilized if the expansion program was to be successfully carried out. 

Three differing approaches were worked out and widely utilized: (1) financing through 

Defense Plant Corporation, (2) extended use of Emergency Plant Facilities contracts, and (3) direct financing by the Government.

(1) Defense Plant Corporation Contracts. Defense Plant 

Corporation was established on 22 August 1940 as a subsidiary of Reconstruction Finance Corporation and pursuant to the authorization contained in Public Law 664, 76th Congress.6 The implementing legislation authorized Reconstruction Finance Corporation to make loans to any corporation for plant construction or expansion, and further authorized RFC, either directly or through subsidiaries, "*** to purchase and lease land, to purchase and produce equipment, supplies, and machinery, for the manufacture of arms, ammunition, and implements of war ***." Such increased plant capacity was to be made available to private corporations for the manufacture of munitions of war. At the time the Defense Plant 

Corporation was established, neither the Army nor the Navy had the benefits of the sweeping War Powers Act (the First War Powers Act became law on 18 December 1941); nor, as pointed out heretofore, did they have sufficient funds available to undertake any substantial plant expansion. The machinery employed by the Army, Navy, RFC and DPC, aided by the Army-Navy Munitions Board, to make full use of the Act of 25 June 1940, enabled a contractor to construct or purchase industrial installations and machine tools as an agent of DPC, and provided that DPC would pay for such facilities either directly or through reimbursement of the contractor. With respect to DPC contracts sponsored by the Army, the Army obligated itself (by what was commonly termed a "take-out agreement") to reimburse DPC initially for 40 percent or 50 percent of the cost and to reimburse the balance if and when funds were appropriated by Congress for that purpose. In each case, interest was charged at the rate of 1 percent per annum. Rentals and proceeds of sales received by DPC were credited against the Army's obligation. 

In addition to the foregoing, the contract between DPC and the contractor vested title to the facilities in DPC and authorized the contractor to use them in connection with work for the Government or for suppliers of the Government. The contractor was required, at its expense, to maintain, insure, and pay taxes on the facilities. 

The contractor was charged rent by DPC for the right to use the facilities, although, in those cases where such right to use was 

6. 54 Stat. 572, as amended; 15 U.S.C. 606b. 177

confined to work directly for the Government, there was usually no obligation to pay more than a nominal rent. Where more than a nominal rental was paid, it was usually on a basis intended to reflect normal straight line depreciation.

The contract did not provide for a "standby period" (a period during which the contractor was obligated to maintain the facilities even though it had no right to use them, the Government bearing the cost of such maintenance), and the contractor's right to use expired on a specified date which was usually four to seven years from the date of the contract. Such right to use was subject to earlier termination by DPC with Army concurrence, or by the contractor when the use of the facilities for Government work was no longer required. 

Upon termination of the contract, the contractor had the option, for a period of ninety days, to purchase the facilities at cost plus interest at 4 percent per annum less rental payments with interest thereon at such rate, or at cost less depreciation, whichever resulted in the higher price. The contractor also had a right of first refusal. if DPC proposed to sell the facilities within ninety days from the expiration of the option.

If the option was not exercised and if the facilities were located in a plant of the contractor, DPC had a right to remove all of such facilities. However, DPC was not obligated to remove any building or other permanent structure or addition but was obligated, at its own expense, to remove all Government-owned movable machinery and equipment if the contractor so requested. Any such movable machinery and equipment not so removed within sixty days from the request of the contractor therefor, could be removed and stored by the contractor at the expense of DPC.

There were both advantages and disadvantages to DPC financing. 

Aside from not being charged initially against the Army's limited appropriations, the program saved the Army a considerable amount of administrative work and freed Army administrative officers for other duties. In addition, facilities financed by DPC could be disposed of by that organization once they had outlived their usefulness; and, unlike the situation involved in the Government-ownership contracts and, to a considerably lesser extent, in the Emergency Plant Facilities contracts, DPC contracts did not place disposal burdens on the Army. 

One disadvantage to DPC contracts was that the facilities, although constructed and utilized solely for war purposes, were not immune from local taxation. The act of 22 January 1932 which created the Reconstruction Finance Corporation, provided that "The corporation *** and its income shall be exempt from all taxation *** except that any real property of the corporation shall be subject to State, Territorial, County, municipality, or local taxation to the same extent according to its value as other real property is taxed." Since the provisions 

7. 47 Stat. 5; 15 U.S.C. 60 178

of the statute also applied to subsidiaries of RFC, all real property owned by DPC, whether or not used in or in connection with the war effort, was taxable; and such taxes, being paid by contractors using the facilities, were of course included by them in their overhead costs and were passed on to the Government, thereby increasing the cost of the war products being manufactured.

RFC reported to Congress that, as of 30 June 1946, its net unrecovered investment under all take-out agreements aggregated $2,108,699,682. RFC estimated that, in the event no further reimbursement to RFC was made under any of the take-out agreements, the probable realizable proceeds from disposition of such property would not exceed $960,000,000.

It is all too easy to review the record at this late date and conclude that the DPC-ARMY-NAVY take-out agreement method of financing plant expansion was an unsatisfactory but necessary means to a desired end. However, in the light of the then existing circumstances, including the all-important one of the failure of Congress to make appropriations to the Armed Services for direct financing during the vital period of preparation antedating the war, the method adopted did produce a substantial portion of the industrial installations, machine tools, and other facilities which, all too soon, were to be essential to the very existence of the nation. The extra time thus gained permitted facilities to go into production at a date earlier than would otherwise have been possible, and to this extent DPC financing of Army and Navy plant expansion made ultimate victory that much more certain and speedy. (2) 

Emergency Plant Facilities Contracts. In addition to the Defense Plant Corporation contracts used by the Armed Services during the early stages of the national emergency and prior to the declaration of war, the Army depended upon the Emergency Plant Facilities type of contract for constructing vitally needed plants and for expanding existing plants. Although the first EPF contracts were already in use when the Advisory Commission to the Council of National Defense was appointed in June 1940, radical revisions were made in the form during the latter part of 1940 and the early part of 1941. By the middle of 

1941, the use of the EPF type had practically been discontinued; and, wherever possible, such contracts were converted into contracts of the Government-ownership-type. 

8. For an interesting discussion involving this section of the RFC statute, see Baltimore National Bank v. State Tax Commission of 

Maryland, 297 U.S. 209 (1936). See also Penn Dairies, Inc. v.

Milk Control Commission of Pennsylvania, 318 U.S. 216, 270-271 

(1943). 179

The earliest EPF contract forms provided for what amounted to piecemeal acquisitions of facilities by the Government on a month-tomonth basis but failed to provide for an option in the Government to acquire privately-owned land in those cases in which buildings and other installations were erected on other than Government property. 

The Advisory Commission suggested numerous modifications to the form, which among other things eliminated piecemeal assumption of title by the Government and vested full ownership in the contractor until the Government had reimbursed the contractor in full for the cost of the facilities. Steps were also taken to amend the form to permit the 

Government to purchase the land on which the facilities were situated if such facilities formed a separate unit and were not "scrambled" with property of the contractor.

Among the more objectionable features of the EPF contracts were those which permitted the contractor (1) to require the Government to remove the facilities upon termination of the contract and to restore the property of the contractor to its condition immediately prior to the acquisition or construction of the facilities, all at the expense of the Government, and (2) to use the facilities on commercial work without the payment of rent therefor. In those few cases in which contractors refused to substitute a Government-Cwnership contract for an existing EPF contract, the contractor was in a position, following V-J Day, to use the facilities exclusively for commercial work, to pay no rent for such use, and to enjoy a substantial competitive advantage as a result. However, there were only four or five contractors who did not voluntarily agree to pay a fair rental under these circumstances when requested so to do.

The standard form of EPF contract required the contractor to construct or acquire the facilities, and to foot the bill therefor. 

Such payment was usually made with funds borrowed upon the security of the contract; and reimbursement was made by the Government, in sixty equal monthly installments, beginning with the date of completion of the facilities. A certificate of necessity was generally issued prior to completion of construction and, if the contractor was unable to secure such certificate, the contract promptly terminated. Reimbursement of costs was accelerated if termination occurred.

It follows from the foregoing that the basic feature of the EPF contract as a vehicle for industrial expansion was its inducement to private, rather than Government, financing during the construction period. The commitment of the Government to reimburse in full the costs of construction over a five-year period following completion of the facilities, together with other liberal rights and options conferred on the contractor, gave rise to a first-class security for hypothecation for private financing during the scarce-money period. 180

Under the EPF contract, title to the facilities remained in the contractor until reimbursement had been made and until the contractor transferred facilities not desired by it to the Government after the expiration of the contractor's option to purchase. As mentioned before, no rent was charged for the right to use the facilities, although the Government did reserve the right to terminate the contract at any time, and the contractor reserved the right to terminate whenever the facilities had not been used for any ninety-day period on Government work. Unlike the Government-ownership type of contract, there was no provision for a standby period.

Upon termination of the contract, the contractor had the right, for a period not in excess of 180 days, to retain the facilities upon payment to the Government of an amount equal to cost less normal straight line depreciation, or such lesser amount as might be negotiated with the Contracting Officer and approved by the Secretary of War. 

Although the contract expressly provided for the transfer to the Government of facilities not wanted by the contractor, the contractor had a right of first refusal with respect to such facilities and even after the transfer thereof to the Government, if the Government thereafter proposed to sell or lease such facilities. As a final commentary upon the questionable features of the EPF form, reference is made to the provision that, with respect to any facilities transferred to or removed by the Government, the Government agreed that "*** it will at no time use the same or any of them for business or commercial 

Without considering in greater detail the various provisions of the EPF form, attention is particularly invited to the manner in which the construction and acquisition of facilities was financed thereunder. 

The essence of the EPF form was that, although private capital was used in the first instance to construct new facilities or expand existing ones, the total bill was ultimately to be paid by the Government. The legal machinery used to achieve this end illustrates once again the diverse methods used by defense agencies in 1940 to finance essential emergency expansion by means other than request for direct appropriations, in recognition of the unwillingness of Congress at that time to appropriate the astronomical sums needed for adequate defense preparation.

Financing of EPF contracts required the removal of the prohibition contained in Sections 3477 and 3737 of the Revised Statutes9 against the assignment of claims against the United States. This was accomplished 9. 

31 U.S.C. 203; 41 U.S.c. 15. 181

by the Assignment of Claims Act of 194010 which permitted the assignment to a bank, trust company or other financing institution, including any Federal lending agency, of any moneys due or to become due from the United States under any contract. The statute was implemented by procedures set up by the Treasury Department (Procurement Division Circular 

Letter No. 447, dated 3 December 1940).11 In effect, the approved procedure permitted the contractor to assign payments due it under the contract to an accredited lending institution, and, with the contract as security, such lending institution would make money available for the construction project covered by the contract. The Comptroller 

General was careful to point out that the Act did not authorize assignment of the contract itself but only permitted assignment of claims arising thereunder. Consequently, the contractor was required to execute all certificates on vouchers and invoices, and it was the contractor's responsibility, rather than that of the assignee, to establish the correctness of any amounts due or payable. 

The provision in the EPF form to the effect that reimbursement of the costs under the contract was to be made in sixty equal monthly installments arose from the requirements of Section 124 of the Internal Revenue Code, 12 which became law on 8 October 1940, just one day before the effective date of the Assignment of Claims Act of 1940. This section authorized contractors, in their Federal income tax returns, to deduct the cost of emergency plant facilities over a period of sixty months, which deduction was in lieu of the usual deduction for depreciation. The sixty-month period could begin, at the option of the contractor, with the month following the month in which the facility was completed or with the beginning of the contractor's first taxable year after completion. As defined in the statute, an "emergency facility" was "*** any facility, land, building, machinery, or equipment, or part thereof, the construction, reconstruction, erection, installation or acquisition of which was completed after 31 December 1939, *** and which had been certified by the appropriate Government agency as necessary in the interest of national defense during the so-called 'emergency period'." This emergency period began 1 January 

1940 and ended on 29 September 1945, the date on which the President proclaimed that the utilization of a substantial portion of the emergency facilities, with respect to which prescribed certification had been made, was no longer required in the interest of national defense. 

10. 54 Stat. 1029; 31 U.S.C. 203. 11. 12.

Interpreted on 2 December 1940 by the Comptroller in 20 Comp. Gen. 

295 (1940). 

54 Stat. 999; 26 U.S.c. 124. 182

Since it was clearly the intent of Section 124 of the Internal Revenue Code that the tax deduction for amortization on a five-year basis of emergency plant facilities should not be allowed to the extent that any payments by the Government constituted reimbursement for capital expenditures made by the contractor in the acquisition or construction of such facilities, the contractor agreed, under Article 

XII (Tax Amortization) of the EPF form, to take the deduction for amortization only to the extent that such repayments by the Government were included in its gross income. In other words, the inclusion in the gross income of the contractor of the repayments by the Government was a condition precedent to the allowance of the amortization deduction and, since one offset the other, no income tax benefit resulted.

The use of certificates of necessity in connection with plant expansion under EPF contracts was but a small part of the entire picture. The major objective of the statute was to encourage private industry to expand existing facilities with its own funds, and the special tax privileges accorded industry by Section 124 provided a considerable portion of such encouragement. That industry was prompt to take advantage of the law is evident from the fact that in excess of $3,000,000,000 worth of certificates of necessity were issued by the War Department.

Although certificates of necessity were handled through the War and Navy Departments during 1941, 1942, and the greater part of 1943, the President transferred all functions with respect to the issuance of certificates of necessity to the Chairman of the War Production 

Board. During the period in which the War Production Board was in charge of the administration of this program, the Board limited the amortization deduction to 35% of the cost of any facilities which, in the opinion of the Board, had presumptive postwar utility. 

Consequently, as the war moved towards its end, the generous tax privileges of Section 124 were gradually withdrawn and were finally terminated on 29 September 1945. 7.13 (3) Direct financing by the Government. The first major expansion in the Army's financed war procurement program took place in June, 1940. After the fall of France and the Low Countries, Congress passed the emergency act of 2 July 194014 which authorized the Secretary of the Army to acquire, construct, operate, and maintain "plants, buildings, facilities, utilities, and appurtenances thereto" whenever he deemed such action essential for national defense purposes. 

13. U.S. Graphite Co. v. Harriman, 71 Supp. 944 (1947). 14. 

54 Stat. 712; 50 U.S.c. 1171 183

Consequently, and until termination of Title I of the First War Powers 

Act, said act of 2 July 1940 may be said to be the only substantive authority for facilities acquisition that the Army had aside from specific authorizations contained in annual appropriation acts. However a prior act had far reaching effect on the emergency procurement program15 and authorized the Secretary of War to place educational orders for munitions of war of special or technical design, noncommercial in character to familiarize commercial manufacturing establishments with the manufacture of such munitions. Bids were to be solicited only from establishments which in the determination of the Secretary of War were competent in time of war to manufacture the particular type of munitions. 

The award was to be made to such bidders as would best serve the interests of the United States and promote the national defense. No contract could be entered into without the approval of the President. The initial appropriation was $2,000,000. In 1939 Congress authorized an appropriation of $34,500,000 to be available during three fiscal years. Subsequently this amount was greatly increased. 

A number of Educational Order contracts were placed and these had a marked effect in speeding the procurement program. These contracts were usually on Standard Form No. 32 and ordinarily called for five items. Item No. 1 was the particular article of equipment such as shell forgings, gun mounts or recoil mechanisms. Items Nos. 2, 3, 4 were additional special machinery, machine tools, and gauges needed for manufacturing Item No. 1. The purpose was to provide at least one set of everything extra needed to turn out Item No. 1 on a mass production basis. They were purchased for the account of the United 

States. Item No. 5 was a production analysis. Its purpose was to show the steps necessary for the plant to turn to large scale production. 

Each contract had a number of special provisions covering the method of production and the disposition of the special machinery and tools acquired for the United States. These were usually left in the possession of the contractor under a storage agreement. 15. 

52 Stat. 707; 50 U.S.C. 91. 184

CHAPTER 15 CONTRACT BONDS 

123. GENERAL. Care in the administration of Government contracts recommends that Government contractors furnish security to the Government for the proper performance of various aspects of their contractual obligations. Congress frequently has enacted legislation in this regard, and has either authorized or required administrative officers of the Government to obtain security from contractors in connection with Government construction and supply contracts. Even where Congress has not spoken, administrative officers occasionally require security, and it has been held that in cases where security is not specifically authorized by law, it may be required by administrative action unless prohibited by law.1 Surety bonds are the usual type of security required, and take various forms, depending upon the purposes for which given. Some of the more important types of surety bonds used in connection with Government contracts are bid bonds, performance bonds and payment bonds.

A bid bond accompanying a bid on a Government contract is an instrument in which the bidder agrees to pay up to a stated amount (the penal sum) in damages if he prematurely withdraws his bid after the opening of the bids, or fails to enter into a written contract, giving such bonds as may be required, within 10 days after the prescribed forms are presented for signature.

A performance bond is an instrument to secure the performance of all the terms of the contract.

A payment bond is an instrument to secure the payment of all persons supplying labor and material in the prosecution of the work provided for in the contract.

On occasion, the terms of particular contracts may indicate the necessity for bonds other than or in addition to bid, performance and payment bonds. For example, an advance payment bond may be required to secure advance payments made under a contract, and a patent infringement bond may be required in lieu of a performance bond in connection with a contract providing for patent indemnity, In the event of default by the bidder or contractor, both he and his surety may be held to the extent of the penalty of the bond given, and if they refuse to pay, the appropriate United States Attorney may be notified to institute proceedings to recover or collect. 

1. Moses v. United States, 166 U.S. 571 (1897). 

185

124. SURETIES ON BONDS. Every bond given in support of a Government contract must be supported by good and sufficient surety or an approved substitute in lieu of surety. In most cases, sureties on bonds are surety companies, but the act of 13 August 18942 prohibits officers or other persons authorized to approve bonds from requiring that surety companies be used. On the other hand, individuals of approved financial stability may be accepted as sureties. In addition, the act of 24 February 

1919, as amended, 3 authorizes the furnishing and acceptance of certain types of security, such as United States notes, in lieu of sureties.4 

A contracting officer may, and should, insist that sureties offered on bonds shall submit to thorough examination as to their financial stability before acceptance of the bonds of contractors,5 and this is particularly appropriate in the cases of individual sureties. With respect to corporate sureties, the Secretary of the Treasury prepares and issues, for the information of Federal bond approving officers and persons required to give bonds to the United States, semi-annual lists which name the only surety companies acceptable to the Government as sureties. Use of the lists enables contracting officers to accept, as surety, without further investigation, companies named on the lists. 

125. BID BONDS. 

Bid bonds are authorized, although not required, by some statutes, and requirements for bid bonds are based upon administrative determinations. Bid bonds are not required by the Armed Services except in connection with contracts to be entered into as a result of formal advertising, and then only when the solicitation for bids specifies that the contract, when awarded, will be supported by a performance bond or by performance and payment bonds. Whenever a bid bond is required, the amount of the penal sum lies within discretion of the contracting officer, subject to the qualification that it shall be adequate to protect the interests of the Government.

126. PERFORMANCE AND PAYMENT BONDS. a. Construction contracts. 

On contracts exceeding $2,000 in amount for the "construction, alteration or repair of any public building or public work of the United States", the contractor, under the provisions of the Miller Act, 7 is required to furnish a performance bond for the protection of the United States, and a payment bond for the protection of all persons supplying labor and materials. The penal sum of the payment bond is required to be fixed in accordance with the schedule contained in the statute.9 The penal sum of the performance bond, however, is discretionary with the contracting officer.10

Both bonds may be waived for so much of the work under a contract as is to be performed in a foreign country, provided the contracting officer finds that it is impracticable for the contractor tɔ furnish them.11 The performance bond is for the exclusive protection of the United States, 12 and suit thereon may apparently be brought against the surety at any time after default on a supported contract, without regard to statutes of limitations. Generally, there are two classes of persons furnishing labor and materials who are protected by the payment bond: (1) those who have a direct contractual relationship with the contractor furnishing the bond, such as the first tier of subcontractors and (2) those having a contractual relationship with a subcontractor, but none with the contractor furnishing the bond. 

While any laborer or materialman who is not paid in full within 90 days after furnishing labor or material may sue on the payment bond in the name of the United States to collect money due him, where no contractual relationship exists between the laborer or materialman and the contractor furnishing the bond, the former must also give the contractor written notice of his claim within 90 days from the date he completed performance in order to acquire a right of action under the bond. 13 In any case, a suit must be brought within one year after the final settlement of the contract. The payment bond is actually a substitute for a lien which could attach in the case of nonpublic improvements.15 The Miller Act is remedial legislation and should be construed liberally.16

b. Supply contracts. Whether performance or payment bonds shall be required in connection with supply contracts is basically a matter of discretion of the contracting officer. When bonds are required, the amount of penalty is also within the discretion of the contracting officer, except that if the contract falls within the category of contracts for "public work of the United States" as described in the Miller Act, the requirements of the Miller Act for performance_and payment bonds apply as in the case of construction contracts.17 

The words "public work of the United States" used in the Miller Act have been broadly interpreted as applying to certain types of supply contracts for the manufacturing, producing, furnishing, construction, alteration, repair, processing or assembling of vessels, aircraft, materiel, munitions, supplies or other personal property when title to the items or parts of the items is originally in the United States, or the United States acquires title as work progresses. Since performance and payment bonds are not normally necessary for adequate protection of the Government in connection with such contracts, practical considerations have resulted in the enactment of the act of 29 April 194118 which, in effect, amends the Miller Act to authorize the waiver of performance and payment bonds in connection with armed forces "public work" supply contracts. The act of 29 April 1941 also provides, however, that although a waiver of bonds may be authorized in connection with such contracts, the provisions of the Miller Act will apply to any "public work" supply contracts concerning which bonds are not waived.
