CHAPTER 16 PERFORMANCE 127. GENERAL. a. Rules. Performance of Government contracts is governed by the same rules as are applicable between private parties. 

A contractor cannot recover for part performance of an entire contract unless full performance has been waived or prevented by the other party.2 

To justify abandonment, the acts of the Government must be such as will render impossible the performance of the remainder of the work, or in effect cause the work to be materially different than that contracted for.3 Failure of the Government to make available a site for construction work, or stoppage of the work by the Government to test the bearing capacity of the foundations and the like makes the Government responsible for the excess costs occasioned by such delays. 4 Failure on the part of the Government to make partial payments as the work progresses justifies abandonment of the work."

Delays. 128. INSPECTIONS. 

Purpose. The purpose of inspection is to assure the Government that the contractor is performing the contract so as to produce supplies, works or services which will conform with contract specifications. Although the determination of whether the workmanship and material furnished in a given case are in accordance with the contract requirements is a question of fact for the Government Inspectors, the contractor may avail himself of the procedure provided in the "Iisputes" article of the contract to appeal such finding to the 

Armed Services Board of Contract Appeals or the head of the department, depending upon the provisions of said article. The parties to a contract may provide that decisions by a designated officer shall be final on questions of fact arising from the performance of the contract. 

Such decisions are final in the absence of fraud or such gross mistake as would imply bad faith or dishonesty.11 b. Responsibilities of inspectors. Since the power of the Government over the contract is complete and the decision of its agent final, a corresponding duty exists that the agent exercise his, judgment reason- 12 ably and with due regard to the rights of both parties. Where the 

Government officer is authorized to inspect and accept work or supplies, his acceptance is equally binding on the Government as on the contractor with respect to defects which a reasonable inspection should have revealed.13 An officer cannot bind the Government by an inspection and approval of work and materials that deviate from the plans and specifications where he is without authority to authorize such deviations. 

An inspection and approval by a duly authorized officer will not bind the Government where it was made in ignorance of structural defects, not discoverable by reasonable tests. 14 

9. Griffiths v. United States, 77 Ct. Cl. 542 (1933). 10. 

Sun Shipbuilding Co. v. United States, 76 Ct. Cl. 154 (1932);

Standard Steel Products Car Co. v. United States, 67 Ct. Cl. 475 (1929). 

11. Sweeny v. United States, 109 U.S. 618 (1883). 12. 

Mueller & Huber v. United States, 90 ct. Cl. 401 (1940). 

13. Electric Fireproofing Co. v. United States, 39 Ct. Cl. 307 (1904). 

14. 

United States v. Walsh, 115 Fed. 697 (1902). 190

C. Supply contracts. The clause required for inclusion in fixedprice supply contracts gives the Government the right to inspect and test the supplies at all times and places, including the period of manufacture and, in any event, prior to final acceptance. In cases of defective material the Government has the right either to require correction of fault or substitution of material, or to accept the defective material at a price equitable under the circumstances. If the contractor refuses to replace or correct, the Government may contract or otherwise replace or correct such supplies and charge the contractor with cost occasioned thereby, or terminate the contract for default. The clause also provides that failure to inspect and accept or reject supplies shall neither relieve the contractor from responsibility for such supplies as are not in accordance with contract requirements nor impose liability on the Government therefor. 

Final acceptance is made conclusive except as regards latent defects, fraud, or such gross mistakes as amount to fraud. d. Construction contracts. Materials that are to be incorporated into the work at the site are subject to inspection and such inspection and acceptance are final, unless otherwise stated in the specifications, except as to latent defects, departures from specific requirements of the contract and specifications, damage or loss in transit, fraud or such gross mistake as amounts to fraud. The contract clause permits the Government at any time before the final acceptance of the entire work to make an examination of the work already completed by removing or tearing out the same. The contractor on request is required to furrish necessary facilities, labor and material. If the work is defective in any material respect due to the fault of the contractor or his subcontractor, the contractor bears the cost of the examination and reconstruction. If such examination reveals that the work meets the contract requirements, an adequate adjustment is provided for in the contract. This paragraph is necessary for the protection of the Government when it is considered that the inspecting officer may not be continually present or if the work is of an extensive character, some parts of it would be completed without his having seen the process or observed the nature of the hidden parts. 129.

RESPONSIBILITY FOR SUPPLIES-CONTRACT CLAUSE. A mandatory clause to be included in all fixed-price supply contracts is the "Responsibility for Supplies" clause16 which provides that the contractor shall be responsible for supplies covered by the contract until they are delivered at a designated delivery point, regardless of point of inspection, and that the contractor shall bear all risks 

15. ASPR 7-103.5. 16. 

ASPR 7-103.6. 191

as to rejected supplies after notice of rejection. Even though final inspection is made at the contractor's plant, his responsibility continues until the articles or materials are delivered to the Government at the designated place of delivery. Frequently Government contracts specify final inspection at, and delivery f.o.b. contractor's plant. In these cases the contractor's responsibility terminates, except for latent defects, fraud, or such gross mistakes as amount to fraud, as soon as the final inspection has been made and the goods are accepted for shipment by the carrier. In other cases Government contracts specify final inspection at contractor's plant but require delivery at some other point. In such cases the contractor's responsibility continues until delivery is effected. Where contracts specify delivery f.o.b. contractor's plant but final inspection at point of destination, the contractor's responsibility for delivery ceases at the time the goods are accepted for shipment by the carrier, but the whole shipment or part of it may be rejected at point of destination because of failure to comply with specifications or for other valid reasons. 

130. DISPUTES. a. "Disputes" clause. Department of the Army contracts contain the following clause: "Except as otherwise provided in this contract, any dispute concerning a question of fact arising under this contract which is not disposed of by agreement shall be decided by the Contracting Officer, who shall reduce his decision to writing and mail or otherwise furnish a copy thereof to the contractor. Within 30 days from the date of receipt of such copy, the contractor may appeal by mailing or otherwise furnishing to the Contracting Officer a written appeal addressed to the Secretary, and the decision of the Secretary or his duly authorized representative for hearing such appeals shall be final and conclusive; provided that, if no such appeal is taken, the decision of the Contracting Officer shall be final and conclusive. In connection with any appeal proceeding under this clause, the contractor shall be afforded an opportunity to be heard and to offer evidence in support of its appeal. Pending final decision of a dispute hereunder, the contractor shall proceed diligently with performance of the contract and in accordance with the 

Contracting Officer's decision."

A contract provision such as the "Disputes" clause is valid and binding on the Government and the contractor. A mere mistake in judgment on the part of the designated officer, not tainted with fraud, gross mistake or dishonesty will not void his decision. 

The burden is on the 17. 

Kihlberg v. United States, 97 U.S. 398 (1878). 

18. Kennedy v. United States, 24 Ct. Cl. 695 (1889). 192

party attacking the decision of the officer to show it is improper because of fraud. Where a Government officer is designated as the person to determine disputes, the contractor has a contractual right to the decision of that officer and not some other officer though the latter be superior in rank. A decision by one other than the designated officer would not be binding on the contractor.20 If the contracting officer fails or refuses to perform the duties imposed on him by the contract by resolving disputes of fact, then the court may pass on such matter. The term "Contracting Officer" includes "successor of the Contracting Officer or his duly authorized representative." 21 b. Questions of fact and law. The "Disputes" clause limits the decision by a contracting officer to questions of fact. His decisions on questions of law are not conclusive or binding on the parties. While there seems to be complete agreement on this rule, the difficult problem arises in determining whether a particular question is one of fact or law.23 

Any decision in a dispute arising under a building contract requires in some degree a construction of the contract. A review of some of the cases decided by the courts will indicate that they have approved, where the contract was broad enough to encompass the dispute, the finality of decisions by officers which resolved mixed questions of law and fact. The determination of unliquidated damages resulting from breach of contract is a question of law and not within the authority of the contracting officer under the "Disputes" clause.25 However, the contracting officer has the authority to determine the extent of delay caused by the contractor pursuant to a liquidated damage clause in the contract as well as the "Disputes" clause. Such determination is one of fact and is conclusive.

C. Appeals. The jurisdiction of the Secretary is appellate only and his orders are valid when confined to appeals from determinations of the contracting officer. A failure on the part of the contractor to perfect his appeal within the time fixed, bars the contractor from questioning the contracting officer's decision even though it be erroneous. The authority to hear and determine appeals with authority to pass finally there on presently is in the Armed Services Board of 

Contract Appeals. 

d. Action during pendency of dispute. The contractor, during the pendency of the dispute, is required by the "Disputes" article to proceed diligently with the work as directed. A contractor who delays performance of a contract because of a disputed question of fact, does so at his own risk and expense. There may be cases where, pending a decision on a dispute, delay would be excusable as, for example, where there is a material conflict between specifications or plans and the contract. 194

CHAPTER 17 TERMINATION FOR CONVENIENCE OF THE GOVERNMENT 131. GENERAL RULES. A contract may contain a clause permitting it to be terminated by one of the parties thereto. In the absence of a provision in the contract for adjusting the rights of the parties, a termination by the Government pursuant to a reservation of such right will entitle the contractor to recover on a quantum meruit.

Illustrative Case: 

Contract between X and the Government to furnish 500 Army ranges at $89 each with a provision that "any part of the order may be cancelled at any time without any obligation to the United States Government. The Government cancelled the contract after X had expended money in carrying out the contract for labor and essential materials in the sum of $19,000 but before I had delivered any ranges. 

I salvaged materials of a value of $7,000. Held I may recover $12,000. X is entitled to recover the cost of the labor and material expended less the proceeds of the material salvaged. 

However, the contractor may not recover damages for anticipated profits under a contract in which the Government reserves a right to terminate.2 

The power to terminate a contract may be reserved by statute which permits contracts to be "modified, suspended, cancelled, or requisitioned" on the payment of just compensation. Such a statute applies to the termination of the Government's own contracts as well as that of private persons. 

Although the contract does not reserve the right of termination, a contracting officer duly authorized may terminate it when the public interest requires." 4 In such case, if the contractor resorts to the courts he may recover for breach of contract. 

1. Peninsular Stove Co. v. United States, 58 Ct. Cl. 36 (1922). 

2. Duesenberg Motors Corp. v. United States, 260 U. S. 115 (1922). 

3. Russell Motor Car Co. v. United States, 261 U. S. 514 (1922). 

4. 5. 

United States v. Corliss Steam Engine Co., 91 U. S. 321 (1875), 18 Comp. Gen. 826, 828 (1939). 

Cf. American Propeller Co. v. United States, 83 Ct. a. 100 (1936). 195

Government contracts frequently contain a provision that if a contractor is unable, or fails to proceed with or make satisfactory progress with the work, the contract may be terminated at the option of the Government. Where the Government terminates the contract for the cause named, there is no breach of contract and consequently, no recovery of damages for anticipated profits may be had."

Settlement of a terminated contract may be made by the contracting officer by means of a supplemental contract with the contractor for the work performed provided the amount agreed upon is proper and just and the contractor will accept such amount in full and final settlement. a. General.

The Presidential Proclamation of 31 December 1946, relative to the cessation of hostilities, raised considerable question as to the continued wartime contracting authority of the Services. However, the previous experience of the Services, both in peacetime and wartime, indicated a strong need for the adoption of termination clauses and procedures

133. SCOPE OF SECTION VIII OF THE ARMED SERVICES PROCUREMENT REGU- 

LATION. This section establishes for the Departments of the Army, Navy and Air Force uniform policies relating to the termination of contracts of any Department entered into under the Armed Services Procurement Act of 1947. It sets forth: (a) the policies and methods to be followed in connection with the termination of contracts for the convenience of the Government; (b) provisions as to the settlement of contracts so terminated, including disposition of property incident to termination; and (c) approved forms for use in terminating contracts for the convenience of the Government and in the settlement of such contracts. 

134. APPLICABILITY OF UNIFORM TERMINATION CLAUSE. The clause applies to contracts which by their terms provide for termination thereof for the convenience of the Government. Contracts which do not contain such a clause or which contain a termination clause different from that provided for in 

Part 6 of Section VIII of the Armed Services Procurement Regulation, may be amended to include their substitute standard clause. It does not apply to any modification of a contract pursuant to the "changes" clause except that the head of a procuring activity may authorize the use of methods and procedures provided for under Section VIII in determining an equitable adjustment. Cancellations of fixed price contracts for the default of the contractor are governed by the "Default" clause of the contract rather than by the Standard Termination Clause.10 135. ANALYSIS OF THE UNIFORM TERMINATION CLAUSE. 

a. Introductory. 

The article provides for the termination of the contract by the Government in accordance with the termination clause, in whole, or from time to time in part, whenever the Contracting Officer determines that such termination is in the best interest of the Government. It provides a procedure for determination of the payment of fair compensation to the contractor in such event. The compensation may be an amount agreed upon between the parties or an amount determined by the Contracting Officer in accordance with a formula contained in the article. 

9. ASPR 8-100. 

10. ASPR 7-103.11. 197

b. Notice of termination. Upon receipt of notice of such termination, the contractor is required to comply with a number of provisions intended to minimize all possible losses on the part of the contractor and the Government and to facilitate the settlement of the terminated contract. These requirements in general provide for the discontinuance of all work, unless otherwise directed, the placing of all orders as may be necessary for the completion of such portion of the work as is not terminated and the termination of all orders and subcontracts to the extent that they are related to the performance of work terminated. The contractor is also required to transfer to the Government, by delivery or otherwise, title to all completed supplies and take such action as may be necessary, or as the Contracting Officer may direct, for the protection and preservation of the property which would have been required to be furnished to the Government. 

C. Negotiated settlement. The negotiated settlement is reached by agreement between the parties concerned (with the Contracting Officer and contractor under a prime contract, or the contractor and subcontractor under a subcontract) on the basis of the facts and in the exercise of sound business judgment. The settlement may be upon the whole or any part of the amount to be paid to the contractor by reason of the termination, which amount may include a reasonable allowance for profit, but only on work done in connection with the terminated portion of the contract. The settlement is effected by amending the contract accordingly and the contractor being paid the agreed amount. d. The formula settlement. A settlement arrived at by unilateral determination is known as a formula settlement and is used in the event of the failure of the contractor and the Contracting Officer to negotiate a settlement. The unilateral determination may cover the entire termination claim or only the disputed portions of the claim. The formula settlement takes into account a number of factors which may be summarized 

(1) Adjustment for supplies accepted by the Government and not previously paid for. (2) Reasonable costs of settling with subcontractors. (3) Settlement expenses in cost of protection and preservation of property. a. (4) An allowance for profit reasonable under the circumstances. 

136. THE STATEMENT OF COST PRINCIPLES. 

General principles. The standard termination article provides that the determination of costs under a negotiated or formula settlement shall be governed by the Statement of 

Principles for Consideration of Costs as set forth in Part 4 of Section VIII 

198

of the Armed Services Procurement Regulation. The Statement of Cost Prin ciples covers in à general manner, costs and types of costs to be allowed and those to be excluded in termination settlements and provides that the following factors will govern: 

(1) Reasonableness. 

(2) Generally accepted accounting principles and practices. (3) Any limitations or special provisions as to types or amounts of cost items set forth or otherwise included in the contract. 

b. Direct and indirect costs distinguished. The Statement of Prin ciples for Consideration of Costs differentiates between the direct and indirect costs which may be allowed as & basis for arriving at a determination of costs. Direct costs have been defined as those costs which, in the first instance of their distribution as costs, are charged directly and finally to the contract without any intermediate distribution to an overhead unit. Indirect costs have been defined as those costs which are not charged directly and finally to the contract in the first instance, but are first charged to departments, burden centers, or other overhead units, and through those units subsequently apportioned to the work which they have benefitted. In a simple cost accounting system, direct costs usually consist of the cost of material and labor entering directly into production of the end product. In complex producti on, and particularly under Government contracts, many contractors also classify other expenses as direct costs of contracts. This practice may be acceptable when the expenses are in fact directly related to the Government contract, but in no event should the contractor be permitted to charge similar expenses as indirect costs, since duplication of charges would result. 

The distinction between direct and indirect costs is important in termination primarily because of the different methods employed in calculating the amount of the costs applicable to the entire terminated contract or to the terminated portion of the contract, as the case may be. 

Direct costs, such as material and labor, relate to specific physical quantities of inventory. Indirect costs are distributed among different contracts on the basis of some common element used as a base. 

137. SETTLEMENT OF TERMINATED CONTRACTS. a. Methods of settlement. 

The termination clauses set forth in Part 6, Section VIII of the Armed 

Services Procurement Regulation provide for settlement (1) by negotiated agreement, (2) by determination based on a formula, or (3) by combination of these two methods. b. Settlement agreements. The settlement by agreement is evidenced by an amendment to the contract substantially as set forth in Part 6, Section VIII. Such settlement is final and conclusive upon the parties thereto except (1) to the extent otherwise agreed in the settlement, and (2) for fraud. 199

If no costs have been incurred by the contractor in respect to the terminated portion of the contract, or if the contractor is willing to waive the costs incurred by it, and no amounts are due to the Government under the contract, a no-cost settlement agreement, substantially in the form set forth in Part 6 of Section VIII is executed with the contractor.

Partial settlements. When a Contracting Officer cannot promptly effect a complete settlement of rights and liabilities under the terminated contract, a partial settlement may be entered into, provided (1) the issues on which agreement has been reached are clearly severable from other issues, and (2) such agreement will not prejudice the interests of the Government in disposing of the unsettled part of the claim. d. Settlement by determination. To the extent that the contractor and the Contracting Officer are unable to agree upon the settlement of a terminated contract, or if a termination claim is not submitted within the period required by the termination clause contained in the contract, the Contracting Officer determines the amount due and advises the contractor thereof in accordance with the termination clause and the principles set forth in Part 4 of this Section.12 

Whenever the Contracting e. Fraud or other criminal conduct. 

Officer has reason to suspect fraud or other criminal conduct in connection with the settlement of a terminated contract under this Section, he is required to discontinue all further negotiations with the contractor and report the facts of the case to higher authority, in accordance with procedures presented by each respective Department. 

138. APPEALS. The contractor has a right of appeal, under the clause of the contract entitled "Disputes", from any determination of the amount due to the contractor made by the Contracting Officer under paragraph 

8-406 or 8-412 of the Armed Services Procurement Regulation, except that if the contractor fails to submit his settlement proposal within the time provided in the contract and fails to request an extension of such time, he has no such right of appeal. The pendency of any such appeal does not affect the authority of the Contracting Officer to settle the termination claim or any part thereof by a negotiated agreement with the contractor зете at any time before the appeal is decided.14

CHAPTER 18 BREACH BY CONTRACTOR 

139. IN GENERAL. The law relating to breach of Government contracts by the contractor is the same as that applicable to private contracts. The usual principles of contract law may be varied, as in private contracts, by express provisions of the contract. The breach of a contract by a Government contractor may be affected by two special provisions, viz, the Disputes Clause, which is required in all contracts, and the Liquidated Damage Clause, which may be used when its need is indicated. 

140. THE DEFAULT CLAUSE. The inclusion of a Default Clause is required by regulation in all fixed price supply contracts. A Default Clause for use in fixed price construction contracts has not been written to date for inclusion in the Armed Services Procurement Regulation and although the two may differ to some extent, fundamentally their effect will be the same. The clause as prescribed is reproduced in the appendix.

The Government has the right to terminate the contractor's right to proceed with the contract or such part of it as has not been performed if the contractor fails to make deliveries of supplies within the agreed time, or fails to prosecute construction work with proper diligence or to complete it according to schedule, unless the delay is excusable. 

None of the causes of delay enumerated in the Default Clause renders a delay excusable per se. They represent some of the most common instances in which absence of fault, unforeseeability and lack of control may exist. The contracting officer determines in the first instance whether a delay is an excusable one under the Default Clause. The Contractor may appeal from this decision to the Secretary of the Army or his duly authorized representative within thirty days. The chief of the technical service concerned or some person named by him may be designated the Secretary's representative for the purpose of hearing appeals under this article. An unappealed decision of the contracting officer is final. A decision on appeal is final unless it is arbitrary, capricious, or without substantial evidence to support it. 1. 2. 

United States v. Utah, Nevada and California Stage Co., 199 U.S. 414 (1905);

International Arms & Fuze Co. v. United States, 73 Ct. Cl. 231 (1931). 

ASPR 7-103.11. 

3. "Not every fire or quarantine or strike or freight embargo should be an excuse for delay under the proviso * * * A quarantine, or freight embargo, may have been in effect for many years as a permanent policy of the controlling government. A strike may be an old and chronic one whose settlement within an early period is not expected. In any of these situations there would be no possible reason why the contractor who of course anticipated these obstacles in his estimate of time and cost, should have his time extended because of them. same is true of high water or floods. The normally expected high water in a stream over the course of a year, being foreseeable, is not an 'unforeseeable' cause of delay." United States v. Brooks-Callaway Co., 318 U.S. 120, 123 (1943).

141. EFFECT OF STANDARD TERMINATION CLAUSE. Although the Standard 

Termination Clause has not been prepared for publication in the Armed Services Procurement Regulation as of this date, a study of the Termination Clause previously prescribed in the Procurement Regulations would indicate that the right of the Government to terminate a contract under the provisions of the Default Clause is limited in some circumstances if the contract contains the Standard 

Termination Clause. The Termination Article for fixed price supply contracts pro vided that if the contracting officer found that the contractor's default was not gross or willful, termination of the contractor's right to proceed was required under the Termination Article rather than the Delays-Damages Article, now known as the Default Clause, if such termination was simultaneous with or a part of a general termination (a) of substantially all of a group of War Department contracts for the same or a closely related product, or (b) of war contracts about the time of the cessation of hostilities in a major theater. The chief of any technical service could terminate a war contract for the convenience of the Government rather than for the contractor's default whenever he believed that (a) the contractor's default had not been gross or willful and had not caused the Government substantial damage, and (b) the use of the Termination Article rather than the Delays Damages Article would be equitable under all the circumstances. 

142. PROVISIONS FOR LIQUIDATED DAMAGES. 

8.

General. War Department policy opposed the general use in war contracts of provisions for liquidated damages for delays in performance since enforcement of the provisions would have seriously impeded the prosecution of the war.

It is contemplated that the Army Procurement Procedures will provide for the use of such an article only with the approval of the chief of the technical service concerned. b. Penalties. A provision which purports to be one for liquidated damages can not be enforced if it is in fact a penalty. penalty. Whether a provision is one for liquidated damages or a penalty depends on whether it represents an effort by the parties in good faith to estimate in advance the actual damage resulting from a breach. If the parties have thus attempted to arrive at a bona fide preestimate of damage, the provision will be enforced as one for liquidated damages. 

If the amount agreed upon as damages in case of a breach has been fixed without reference to the probable actual damage but as a deterrent against breach of the contract, then the provision is a penalty and will not be enforced. The true 40 

See Ch. 17 "Termination for Convenience of the Government". Other articles providing for termination at the option or convenience of the Government contain similar provisions. 

5. Kennedy v. United States, 24 Ct. Cl. 122 (1889). 

6. Nise v. United States, 249 U.S. 361 (1919). 

7. Kennedy v. United States, 24 Ct. Cl. 122 (1889); 17 Comp. Gen. 827 (1938). 202

intent of the parties will be determined primarily by the reasonableness of the amount fixed as damages, viewed as of the time the contract was executed. 8 The greater the difficulty in accurately calculating probable actual damages, the more readily will a provision for liquidated damages be upheld." 

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract for the delivery of 25,000 work suits at a unit price of $2.12 or a total of $53,000. 

The contract contained a liquidated damages clause and provided for termination for default. Delays in performance occurred and liquidated damages were assessed from time to time. When all but about 6,000 suits had been delivered, the Government terminated the contract. Its excess costs in securing the balance of the suits amounted to only $3.56 plus one or two small items of damage totaling $18.00. The liquidated damages assessed at the rate of 

1/2 of 1 per cent per day amounted to $20,886.95. The question before the court was whether the amount assessed was so disproportionate to actual damages as to constitute a penalty. Held: 

Liquidated damages in the amount assessed were proper. The dangers and possible losses that might ensue if the contractor defaulted in the deliveries were things against which the Government had a right to protect itself by a clause for liquidated damages, "The damages that might arise from delay in obtaining equipment were incalculable and might well be very great. 

Delay might disrupt preparations for war in all sorts of ways". 10 

Co Liquidated damages where delay is caused by both parties. 

The contractor is entitled to an extension of time coextensive with the period of any delay caused by the Government, and is also entitled to relief from any assessment of liquidated damages for such period. Liquidated damages will not be assessed when the work has been delayed by both parties, and the actual delays caused by each can not be definitely ascertained.

lustrative Case: 

The Government contracted for the construction of an embassy building in Tokyo, Japan, to be completed by a specified date. 

Due to delays caused in part by the contractor, but principally by the Government's failure to provide certain drawings, the building was not completed on time. The Government deducted liquidated damages from the contract price, and the contractor brought suit to recover them. Held: Recovery allowed. 

It was impossible, said the court, "to determine from the evidence just how much of the delay was caused by the plaintiff and how much was caused by the defendant. 

Under such circumstances, 

no deduction for liquidated damages can be allowed. The pro12 visions of the contract fixing a time limit were abrogated". 

Where the contract contains no provision requiring apportionment of delays, the courts will not attempt, in a case where both parties are in default, to accomplish any division or apportionment, even though it might be feasible to do so. 

Illustrative Case: 

The Government contracted to purchase 1500 duck-feather pillows for a total price of $2,025, delivery to be made within ten days of receipt of a Government order. An order was given 

1 July 1935 and the goods were shipped 29 July 1935; notification was given by the Government on 25 October 1935, 118 days later, that the pillows did not meet specifications. Twenty-six days later the Government notified the contractor that the goods were rejected, and that a proper product might be substituted by 

21 December 1935. On 14 January 1936, the Government bought pillows elsewhere at no extra cost. It then brought suit for liquidated damages for delay in the sum of $1,460. Held:

Recovery denied. The Government was responsible for a large part of the delay. One party to a contract may not recover for a breach where its acts have contributed to prevent or delay performance by the other party. The Government waived delivery (1938), 12. Wharton Green & Co., Inc. v. United States, 86 Ct. Cl. 100, 109 in which the court further stated: "Under such circumstances, all the authorities hold that liquidated damages should not be imposed. 

In some of the decisions it is said that the provision with reference to time was waived; in some that it has been annulled; and in others it has simply been held that it could not be enforced. Also, in some of the cases it was said that when the defendant had delayed the plaintiff beyond the date prescribed by the contract, the court has no fixed date from which the contract could be enforced or liquidated damages computed." See also United States v. United Engineering Co., 234 U.S. 236 (1914); Sweig

Co. v. United States, 92 Ct. Cl. 472 (1941); Standard Steel Car Co. v. 

United States, 67 Ct. Cl. 445 (1929). 204

शुरु as of the time provided and the parties had never agreed on another date. In the absence of a provision requiring such apportionment, where both parties are in default, the courts will not attempt to apportion the delay caused by each party. 

Liquidated damages will be allowed in accordance with the contract where the contract expressly provides for an apportionment of the delays attributable to the respective parties, and the extent of the delay caused by each party is in fact ascertainable.

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract for installation of interior finish in the New York Custom House, the work to be completed by a certain date. The contract provided for liquidated damages for delay and stipulated that the contractor was to be given an additional day of time for each day of delay caused by the Government. The project ran 121 days beyond the time fixed, 60 days being due to delays by the Government and 

61 days to delays by the contractor. The Government deducted liquidated damages for 61 days and the contractor brought suit for recovery of the money withheld on the ground that the liquidated damage provisions had been waived by the mutal delays. Held: No recovery. The fact that the Government's action caused some of the delay presents no legal ground for denying it compensation for loss suffered wholly through the fault of the contractor." d. Liquidated damages recoverable in supply and construction contracts. Liquidated damage provisions in supply contracts usually bind the contractor to pay damages for each day of delay until the supplies are delivered, or, if the Government terminates the contract because of the delay, until the Government reasonably procures the supplies elsewhere. The 

Government may recover three distinct items of damage if the contract is terminated: (1) The excess cost which it was compelled to pay; (2) liquidated

The form of Delays-Damages Article formerly used in War Department construction contracts provided that the Government might terminate the contract for the contractor's delay and recover any excess costs incurred in completing the work, or permit the contractor to complete the work and deduct liquidated damages for the delay. Liquidated damages were recoverable under this type of article only if the contractor was permitted to complete the work.

The Government entered into a contract for the construction of certain public buildings by a specified date. The project being uncompleted 13 months after the extended completion date, the Government terminated the contract, relet it at an excess cost of some $2,000, and assessed liquidated damages under the contract at $25 per day for 13 months. Held: Recovery allowed. Under the terms of the contract, liquidated damages can be assessed only where the Government does not terminate the contractor's right to proceed. e. Administrative determination of extent of delay. The number of days of delay chargeable to the contractor for purposes of assessing liquidated damages is a question of fact. It may therefore be decided by the contracting officer under the Disputes Article, subject to the contractor's right of appeal to the duly authorized representative of the Secretary of the Army, whose decision on appeal is, in the absence of bad faith or gross error, final and conclusive. 15.

CHAPTER 19 BREACH BY THE GOVERNMENT 

143.

SOVEREIGN ACTS OF THE GOVERNMENT-DISTINCTIONS. A fundamental distinction must be made between acts of the Government as a contractor and acts of the Government as a sovereign. The Government, as a contractor, is bound by its contracts properly made. Rights under Government contracts as well as private contracts are property rights within the meaning of the Fifth Amendment to the Constitution and are protected against governmental appropriation without compensati on. The Government may not constitutionally repudiate its contract obligations.1

The Government, in its sovereign capacity, may enact laws or cause the promulgation of regulations which interfere with or prevent performance of both public and private contracts. The Government is not liable for damages resulting from the effect of such laws or regulations on either private contracts or Government contracts. 

Illustrative Cases: 

a.

The Government issued a war risk renewable term insurance policy during World War I. In 1933, Congress passed the Economy Act, by which it purported to abolish all rights under such Government policies. The insured died in 

1934, and plaintiff sued to recover the death benefit provided for in the policy. Held: Recovery allowed. The policy was a contract of the United States. Congress may not abrogate or repudiate the Government's contractual obligations.2 b. The Government, in December, 1919, sold a quantity of silk for immediate shipment on receipt of shipping instructions. The buyer, on 16 February 1920, ordered the silk shipped at once by freight. The Government did not make shipment until 12 March because of an embargo placed by the Railroad Administration on all shipments of silk by freight. In the meantime, silk prices declined about 25 per cent, resulting in a loss to the buyer of $11,000. The buyer sued to recover this loss as damages for breach of contract. 

1. While the Government may not abrogate vested rights acquired under 

Government contracts, it may abolish all remedies for the enforcement of such rights. See Ch. 20, "Remedies." 2. 

Lynch v. United States, 292 U. S. 571 (1934). See also Seatrain 

Lines, Inc. v. United States, 99 Ct. al. 272 (1943). 207

Held: Recovery denied. The Government is not liable as contractor for its acts as a sovereign. 

C. The Government entered into a contract to install certain facilities at Fort Howard, Maryland. The contractor was delayed in obtaining priorities from the War Production Board for necessary materials, and alleged that the delay constituted a breach of contract. Held: No recovery. The 

Second War Powers Act, empowering the President to allocate materials and give priorities for obtaining them, "authorized the exercise of sovereign powers in the defense of the nation. 

That the Government is not liable for such acts needs no argument."4

While a sovereign act which prevents or delays performance by the contractor under a Government contract does not constitute a breach by the Government, neither can it be the basis of recovery by the Government against the contractor. Just as in private contracts, where such an act by the Government prevents or delays performance of a contract, the delay or non-performance is excused." 144. 

BREACH OF CONTRACT BY THE GOVERNMENT. a. In general. The 

Government as contractor is subject to the same principles of contract law relating to breach of contract as apply to, private contracts. A breach of contract may take place "by failure to perform acts promised, by prevention or hindrance, or by repudiation."7 

3. Horowitz v. United States, 267 U.S. 458 (1925). The court quoted with approval a statement by the Court of Claims in Jones v. United States, 1 Ct. Cl. 383 (1865): Whatever acts the Government may do, be they legislative or executive, so long as they be public and general, cannot be deemed specially to alter, modify, obstruct or violate the particular contracts into which it enters with private persons ***." 

But cf. Perry v. United States, 294 U.S. 330 (1935) (Gold clause case affecting Government bonds). 

4. Gothwaite v. United States, 102 Ct. Cl. 400 (1944). 

5. See 6 Williston on Contracts (Rev. Ed. 1936), sec. 1938 and 1939. 6. 

Delay or non-performance will be excused, however, only if the sovereign act renders performance, or performance within the time specified, impossible rather than merely more difficult or expensive. See Maxwell v. 

United States, 3 F. 2d 906 (C.C.A. 4th 1925), aff'd., 271 U.S. 647 (1926).

Congress may of course vary the applicable legal rules by enacting statutes for the purpose of relieving contractors in hardship cases. See McCloskey & Co. v. United States, 98 Ct. al. 90 (1942). 

7. Restatement of the Law of Contracts (1932) sec. 312. See United States v. 

Purcell Envelope Co., 249 U.S. 313 (1919); Garfielde v. United States, 93

208

Illustrative Cases: 

(1) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to design and construct two experimental night bombardment planes for $170,000. The contract provided that progress payments corresponding to the percentage of completion were to be made by the Government. The Government failed to make such payments, and the contractor discontinued work because of lack of funds. The Government terminated the contract. Held: Discontinu ance of work because of the Government's failure to make progress payments was not a breach by the contractor. On the contrary, failure to make the progress payments was a breach by the Government which entitled the contractor to abandon the contract and recover damages for the breach. (2) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to construct foundations for certain buildings. 

The contractor was required to pay certain minimum hourly wages to designated classes of laborers. Before the work was completed, the Government advertised for bids on the construction of the buildings above the foundations. The advertisement stated that bidders would be required to pay specified minimum hourly wages higher than those specified in plaintiff's contract. The contractor's workers went on strike for the higher wage scale, and the contractor was compelled to pay it. Held: The fact that the 

Government's action resulted indirectly in increasing the contractor's costs did not render the Government liable for breach of contract.9 

8. Whitbeck v. United States, 77 Ct.Cl. 309 (1933). In Brooklyn & Queens 

Screen Mfg. Co. v. United States, 97 Ct.Cl. 532, 544 (1942), involving somewhat similar facts, the court stated: "The law does not allow the defendant by its refusal to observe an obligation of a contract to place a contractor in a position where he cannot escape forfeiture of his rights ***." See also Guerini Stone Co. v. Carlin Construction 

Co., 248 U.S. 334 (1919); Overstreet v. United States, 55 ct.cl. 154 (1920). 

9. United States v. Beuttas, 324 U.S. 768 (1945), reversing 101 Ct.Cl. 748 (1944). The court stated: "The Government did not stipulate that the two groups, those employed on the foundations and those employed on the superstructure, should work at the same time at different wages. On the contrary it had every reason to suppose that the former would finish their work at the then agreed rate before the latter came on the work. *** It follows that there is no basis for a holding that the Government knowingly hindered respondents in the performance of the contract or culpably increased their costs." See also Le Veque v. United States, 96 Ct. 1. 250 (1942).

While the Government is under an implied obligation to refrain from "culpably" increasing the contractor's costs,it is under no duty to help the contractor reduce costs by making special effort to enable it to finish the work in less than the period of time contemplated by the contract. See 

United States v. Blair, 321 U.S. 730 (1944). 209

b. 

Misrepresentation of conditions. The liability of the Govern ment for misrepresentations of conditions which result in damage is the same as that of a private contractor. Where the Government merely describes in good faith conditions which it believes a contractor will encounter, without warranting or representing that such conditions in fact exist, it is not liable if the conditions prove in fact to be different from those anticipated. 10. 11.

Illustrative Cases: 

(1) The Government sold a quantity of surplus Army blankets. The advertisement described the wool content and other features of the blankets offered, but stated that the sale was to be "as is". The buyer claimed that the blankets purchased were inferior in quality to the advertised description. Held: By stipulating that the sale was to be "as is", the Government gave express notice that it did not warrant the quality of the blankets. Recovery denied.10 (2) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to complete a partly constructed canal and locks. The Government had already completed a bulkhead deemed to be of sufficient height to protect the project against river floods. The contract contained no guarantee, however, that it was adequate. Because of an unanticipated flood of extraordinary proportions, the bulkhead proved to be in fact inadequate, and the contractor expended labor and materials in constructing other structures to protect the project. Held: Since the contract contained no warranty by the Government as to the adequacy of the bulkhead, the 11 contractor cannot recover. (3) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to construct a pumping plant. Before entering into the contract, the contractor had been shown a chart which disclosed that certain probings had been made by the Government at the site. These probings indicated that the subsurface consisted of solid rock. The chart portrayed fully and accurately the results of probings made by the

Government. In fact, part of the subsurface consisted of large boulders. The contractor claimed that this condition caused the collapse of a cofferdam which had to be rebuilt at considerable expense, and that this added cost was due to the 

Government's misrepresentation of conditions. Held: No General Textile Corp. v. United States, 76 Ct. Cl. 442 (1932). 

Day v. United States, 245 U.S. 159 (1917). See also Simpson v.

United States, 172 U.S. 372 (1899). 

210

recovery. The Government made no representation of conditions, but merely stated what its preliminary investigation indicated such conditions to be. Since this statement was accurate and complete, the Government was guilty of no misrepresentation or breach of contract.12

The Government must make a full and complete disclosure where it undertakes or purports to set forth information it possesses concerning conditions likely to be encountered. Withholding of material knowledge under such circumstances may constitute misrepresentation. 

Illustrative Case: 

The specifications under a dredging contract contained the statement: "A number of test borings have been made in all of the areas where dredging is to be done under these specifications, and the results thereof may be seen by intending bidders on the maps on file in this office. 

No guaranty is given as to the correctness of these borings in representing the character of the bottom over the entire vicinity in which they were taken, although the general information given thereby is believed to be trustworthy." In fact, other borings had been made which indicated the existence of material more difficult to remove than the fine sand and mud referred to in the specifications. No disclosure of such borings was made. The specifications stated that bidders were expected to examine the work themselves. The contractor abandoned the work and sued for the cost of removing the material. Held: Recovery allowed. The 

Government officials misleadingly withheld information and asserted a belief as to the truth of representations which they knew to be untrue.13 

The Government is liable for making false representations under the same principles as apply in private contract law. 12. 13. 

Illustrative Case: 

The specifications under a contract for the repair of a dam stated that "the dam is now backed for about 50 feet with broken stone, sawdust and sediment to a height of within 2 or 3 feet of the crest." The specifications further provided that each bidder was expected to visit the site of the work and obtain information necessary to bid intelligently. In fact the dam was not backed as represented, and as a result the contractor was put to

Atlantic Dredging Co. v. United States, 253 U.S. 1 (1920). See also

Held: The contractor was entitled to recover the additional expense occasioned by the false representation.14

The Government and not the contractor is responsible for defects in specifications which the Government prepares. The Government impliedly warrants the adequacy of such specifications for the work to be performed.

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract for reconditioning airplane engines in accordance with prescribed specifications. The specifications were defective, and satisfactory conditioning of the engines in accordance with them impossible. 

A change order was later issued, prescribing new specifications. Held: Where the Government provides that work shall be performed in accordance with prescribed specifications, it warrants that such specifications are adequate; if they are not, the contractor may recover for damages sustained in attempting to perform under them.- 15

C. Necessity for tender. The contractor, in order to recover for breach of contract by the Government, must ordinarily have tendered performance by attempting or offering to perform, in addition to having been ready, willing and able to perform. 

14. Hollerbach v. United States, 233 U.S. 165, 172 (1914). The court said: "If the Government wished to leave the matter open to the independent investigation of the claimants it might easily have omitted the specification as to the character of the filling back of the dam. In its positive assertion of the nature of this much of the work it made a representation upon which the claimants had a right to rely without an investigation to prove its falsity." 

15. Steel Products Engineering Co. v. United States, 71 Ct.cl. 457 (1931). 

In United States v. Spearin, 248 U.S. 132 (1918), where a similar result was reached in connection with a construction contract, the court said: "Where one agrees to do, for a fixed sum, a thing possible to be performed, he will not be excused or become entitled to additional compensation because unforeseen difficulties are encountered.*** But if the contractor is bound to build according to plans and specifications prepared by the owner, the contractor will not be responsible for the consequences of defects in the plans and specifications. * * * This responsibility of the owner is not overcome by the usual clauses requiring builders to visit the site, to check the plans, and to inform themselves of the requirements of the work * * *." 212

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to deliver ice at New Orleans and other localities. The Government refused to accept the deliveries to be made at New Orleans. The contractor sued for breach of contract, but failed to show that it offered or was ready and able to make delivery at New Orleans. Held: No recovery. 16 Tender of performance, but not ability, willingness, and readiness to perform, is excused, however, where: (1) under the terms of the contract, the contractor's performance is not due until after performance by the Government of some act which the Government in fact fails to perform; (2) the Government repudiates its obligations under the contract before the time set for the contractor's performance; or (3) the Govern ment makes it impossible for the contractor to perform, or fails to provide an agreed means of making performance by the contractor possible. 

Illustrative Cases: 

(a) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to furnish 14,190 sacks of cement, to be delivered "as requested" within 90 days. The Government called for only 2,400 sacks, and the contractor sued for breach of contract. Held: No tender was required. Tender is necessary only to place the other party in default. Since the contractor was required to make deliveries only as requested and the Government failed to request delivery of the total quantity agreed upon, the Government was in default and no necessity for tender of performance existed.17 (b) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to furnish 600,000 bricks.

The super- intendent in charge of the job was authorized, under the contract, to determine whether the bricks met specifications. 

Twelve thousand five hundred bricks were delivered, approved by the superintendent and used by the Government. Thereafter, the Government cancelled the contract on the ground that the 

16. Parish v. United States, 8 Wall. (75 U.S.) 489 (1869). 

17. Steiner v. United States, 36 F. Supp. 496 (D.C., 1941). 213

bricks failed to meet specifications. Held: Tender of performance by offer of delivery of the balance of the bricks was not necessary as a prerequisite to suit by the contractor.18 (c) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to furnish hay to a military post. 

The hay was to be cut from certain Government land, which was the only practicable place for the contractor to secure it. The Government, fearing that the contractor would not carry out his contract, permitted others to cut the hay on the land in question. Held: Attempted performance or tender of performance by the contractor was unnecessary, since the Government had made performance impossible.19 d. Measure of damages. The measure of damages upon breach of contract by the Government is similar to that applicable in the case of a private contract. The contractor may (1) treat the contract as rescinded and recover in quantum meruit the value of the services rendered, or (2) treat the contract as breached and recover damages.

The contractor, if it elects to treat the contract as breached, may recover two distinct elements of damage: (1) The cost of the work done under the contract less the market value of materials on hand, and (2) the profit which would have been realized had the contract gone to completion.

The profit is computed by deducting from the contract price 18. 19. 20. 

Moore v. United States, 17 ct. c. 17 (1881). See also Hughes v. 

United States, 4 Ct. Cl. 64 (1868); Saylor v. United States, 14 

Ct. Cl. 453 (1878). A contract may be repudiated by acts as well as words, as where one party places itself in a position in which performance of its part of the contract is made impossible. Cf. H. Muehlstein & Co. v. Hickman, 26 F. 2d 40 (C.C.A. 8th 1928). 

United States v. Peck, 102 U. S. 64 (1880). 

United States v. Behan, 110 U.S. 338 (1884); United States Potash 

Co. v. McNutt, 70 F. 2d: 126 (C.C.A. 10th 1934). 214

the estimated cost of performing the entire contract. This rule of damages is subject to the usual limitation that recovery of profit will be allowed only if it is capable of reasonable proof. 

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to make certain improvements at New Orleans 

Harbor. The Government cancelled the contract without cause.

The plan of improvement was a novel one, and it was impossible to estimate the cost of completion or the profit which would have been realized. The contractor treated the contract as breached, and, while making no claim for profits, sought recovery of expenditures to the date of cancellation, Held: 22 

Recovery of expenditures allowed. 

21. If part performance of the contract has resulted in the completion of some of the items called for under the contract, a different rule prevails as to such items. If delivered prior to the breach, they must be paid for at the contract price. If completed but not delivered, they will be subject to the rule which applies in the case of supply contracts generally, that the contractor is entitled to the difference between the contract price and the market price at the time delivery was to be made. 

22. United States v. Behan, 110 U.S. 338, 344 (1884). "The prima facie measure of damages for the breach of a contract is the amount of the loss which the injured party has sustained thereby. If the breach consists in preventing the performance of the contract, without the fault of the other party, who is willing to perform it, the loss of the latter will consist of two distinct items or grounds of damage, namely: first, what he has already expended towards performance (less the value of materials on hand); secondly, the profits that he would realize by performing the whole contract. The second item, profits, cannot always be recovered. 

They may be too remote and speculative in their character, and therefore incapable of that clear and direct proof which the law requires. * * * But failure to prove profits will not prevent the party from recovering his losses for actual outlay and expenditure." 1 215

CHAPTER 20 REMEDIES SECTION I. GENERAL 

145. INTRODUCTION. The Government may be sued only if and to the extent it consents. Consent may be withdrawn at any time.1 Even where final judgment has been entered against the Government, no execution will issue to enforce it, and payment is dependent on an adequate appropriation by Congress for the purpose.2 These basic limitations upon the contractor's judicial remedies against the Government, together with other limitations hereafter considered, enhance the importance of the administrative remedies available to the contractor.

SECTION II. REMEDIES WITHIN THE CONTRACTING AGENCY DETERMINATION OF DISPUTES. 

146.

Government contracts commonly provide that disputed questions of fact arising during performance shall be referred for decision to some designated officer of the Government, usually the head of the department or the contracting officer. That decision is final and binding on the contractor and the Government in the absence of fraud or such gross mistake as to imply bad faith, except to the extent that the contract provides for an appeal from the officer's decision. 

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to transport Army stores between certain points. Compensation was to be paid in accordance with the distances between points of departure and delivery. The contract provided that such distances were to be finally determined by the Chief Quartermaster for the District of 

New Mexico. No provision was made for appeal from such determination. The Quartermaster's determinations of 1. 

In Lynch v. United States, 292 U.S. 571 (1934), where a statute which purported to abolish rights under Government war risk insurance policies was held unconstitutional, the court pointed out that had the statute merely abolished the remedies by withdrawing from the courts jurisdiction to entertain suits on the policies, it would have been constitutional. 

2. Cloutier v. Morgenthau, 88 F. 2d 846 (App. D.C. 1937); Hetfield v. 

United States, 78 Ct. Cl. 419 (1933). 216

147. distances were slightly less than those by the roads usually travelled. Held: Since his determinations were not shown to be the result of fraud or bad faith, they were conclusive on the parties. 3

THE DISPUTES ARTICLE. Every fixed-price contract of the Armed 

Services must contain the standard Disputes Article. This article provides: "Except as otherwise provided in this contract, any dispute concerning a question of fact arising under this contract which is not disposed of by agreement shall be decided by the Contracting Officer, who shall reduce his decision to writing and mail or otherwise furnish a copy thereof to the Contractor. Within 30 days from the date of receipt of such copy, the Contractor may appeal by mailing or otherwise furnishing to the Contracting Officer a written appeal addressed to the 

Secretary, and the decision of the Secretary or his duly. authorized representative for the hearing of such appeals shall be final and conclusive; provided that, if no such appeal is taken, the decision of the Contracting Officer shall be final and conclusive. In connection with any appeal proceeding under this clause, the Contractor shall be afforded an opportunity to be heard and to offer evidence in support of its appeal. Pending final decision of a dispute hereunder, the 

Contractor shall proceed diligently with the performance of the contract and in accordance with the Contracting Officer's decision."

The decision of the contracting officer is final on a matter within the scope of the Disputes Article, even though it may have been arbitrary or so erroneous as to imply bad faith, if the contractor fails to appeal within the time prescribed.

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to construct certain buildings. The contract contained a clause similar to the standard Disputes 3. 

Kihlberg v. United States, 97 U.S. 398 (1878). In S. J. Groves & 

Sons Co. v. Warren, 135 F. 2d 264, 266 (App. D.C. 1943), the court stated: "Where parties to a *** contract, including the United

States, designate a person to determine disputes arising under the contract and stipulate that the determination shall be final and conclusive, they are bound by such determination in the absence of fraud or such gross mistake as to imply bad faith." See Penn Bridge 

Co. v. United States, 59 Ct. Cl. 892' (1924), for collection of authorities. 

4. ASPR 7-103.12. The term "article" is utilized for uniformity herein since all cases cited used this term in contrast to "clause", adopted by the recently published Armed Services Procurement Regulations. 217

Article. A number of disputed questions arose. As a result of arbitrary, capricious and grossly erroneous rulings on these questions, made by the Government superintendent on the job and approved by the contracting officer, the contractor expended about $80,000 in extra labor and materials and other miscellaneous items. The contractor failed to appeal from these rulings by the contracting officer to the head of the department, because, as found by the Court of Claims, to do so would have subjected the contractor to punishments and reprisals by the superintendent on the job. Held: "The ap- peal provisions of the contract must be exhausted before relief is sought in the courts." There is nothing to indicate that an appeal to the department head would have been futile, or that he could not have effectively controlled the actions of his subordinates. Having failed to appeal in accordance with the terms of the contract, the contractor was bound by the contracting officer's decision.

The 148. DECISION OF THE SECRETARY OF THE ARMY APPEALABLE, WHEN. 

decision of the Secretary of the Army or his duly authorized representative under the Disputes Article is not appealable to the courts unless it is so arbitrary or grossly erroneous as to imply bad faith.

Illustrative Cases: 

a. The Government entered into a contraet under which the contractor agreed to furnish certain naval equipment and machinery. The contract contained a Disputes Article and a provision for liquidated damages. The Government deducted liquidated damages for a delay of more than 100 days allegedly due to the fault of the contractor. The contractor claimed that the delay was caused by the Government, and appealed to the Secretary of the Navy under the Disputes Article. The 

Secretary decided that the contractor was responsible for the delay. Held: The decision of the Secretary, in the absence of fraud or bad faith, is binding on the courts.6 

5. United States v. Blair, 321 U.S. 730, 736 (1944). The Court stated: 

"If it were shown that the appeal procedure provided in the contract were in fact inadequate for the correction of the alleged unreasonable attitude of the subordinate Government officials, we would have quite a different case." 

6. American Engineering Co. v. United States, 24 F. Supp. 449 (E.D. Pa. 

1938) 218

149. b. The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to excavate earth at a designated location. "Earth" was defined as "material which it is practicable to remove and handle with pick and shovel or by hand or to loosen and remove with a power shovel." The material to be excavated was found to consist of a conglomerate mass frozen together, which could be removed only by blasting. The contractor claimed extra compensation on the ground that such material was not "earth" as defined by the contract. The contracting officer determined, under the Disputes Article, that the contractor was not entitled to extra compensation, and this decision was affirmed on appeal to the head of the department involved. 

Held: The decision was so arbitrary as to indicate bias, prejudice or bad faith. The contractor was therefore not precluded from suing to recover additional compensation.7 

RECOURSE TO THE COURTS, WHEN. 

A contractor may seek relief directly in the courts where the contracting officer fails or refuses to make a decision under the Disputes Article. 

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to construct certain buildings. The contract contained the standard Disputes Article. A dispute arose concerning payment for extra work. The contracting officer referred the dispute to the Comptroller General for decision 

Held: The contractor had a contractual right to a decision by the contracting officer and an appeal to the Secretary of War or his designated representative. Denial of such right constituted a breach of contract entitling the contractor to seek relief from the courts.8 7.

Baruch Corporation Inc., Ltd. v. United States, 92 Ct. Cl. 571 (1941). 

8. James McHugh Sons, Inc. v. United States, 99 Ct. Cl. 414 (1943). See also Zweig Co. v. United States, 92 Ct. Cl. 472 (1941); Karno-Smith Co. v. United States, 84 Ct. Cl. 110 (1936); Sun Shipbuilding and Dry Dock 

Co. v. United States, 76 Ct. Cl. 154 (1932). Similarly, if the Secreretary of the Army or his authorized representative refers an appeal to the Comptroller General instead of deciding it himself, or if he merely affirms the contracting officer's decision without independently reviewing or weighing the evidence, the contractor is entitled to a decision on the merits by the courts. 150. APPEALS UNDER THE DISPUTES ARTICLE. The Armed Services Board of Contract Appeals is the authorized representative of the Secretaries of the Army, Navy, and Air Force in hearing, considering and determining as fully and finally as might each of the Secretaries (1) Appeals by contractors from decisions on disputed questions by contracting officers or their authorized representatives or by other authorities pursuant to the provisions of Armed Services contracts requiring the determination of appeals by the head of a Department of the Armed Services or by his duly authorized representative or board, or pursuant to the provisions of any directive whereby the Secretary of a Department of the Armed Services has granted a right of appeal not contained in the contract; 

(2) Appeals by Armed Services contractors pursuant to section 13 (c)(1)(i) and Section 17 (c) of the Contract Settlement Act of 1944. 

When an appeal is taken pursuant to a disputes clause in a contract which provides only for appeals from decisions on questions of fact, the Board may in its discretion hear, consider and decide all questions of law necessary for the complete adjudication of the issue. Unless the contract provides otherwise, when in the consideration of an appeal it appears that a claim for unliquidated damages is involved therein, the Board, insofar as the evidence permits, makes findings of fact with respect to such claims without expressing opinion on questions of liability. 

When a contract requires the Secretary of a Department of the Armed Services, personally, to render a decision on the matter in dispute, the Board submits its findings and recommendations to the Secretary of the Department.

There are three panels of the Board--the Army, Navy, and Air Force panels. In general, appeals are assigned for decision to the panel of the Department whose contract or procurement is directly involved. Each of the panels acts in divisions, which normally consist of three or more members of the panel. Hearings may be held by a division, by a designated member, or by a duly authorized examiner. The decision of a majority of a division constitutes the decision of the panel and of the Board, provided that all three panel chairmen signify that in their opinion a review by the full Board is not required. If a majority of the members of a division do not agree upon a decision, or if one or more panel chairmen do not waive review by the full Board, determination of the appeal is made by a majority of the members of the full Board. 9.

Pursuant to Joint directive of the Secretaries of the Army, Navy, and 

Air Force effective 1 May 1949, establishing the Armed Services Board of Contract Appeals. 

220

151. SCOPE OF DISPUTES ARTICLE. The standard Disputes Article ap plies only to questions of fact.10 Special contract provisions may provide for decisions on disputed issues other than those within the Disputes 

Article.11 

It is necessary to distinguish between two separate problems: (a) the scope of the term "questions of fact" as used in the Disputes Article; and (b) the extent to which officers or agents of the Government may properly be empowered, under special provisions, to decide disputes involving questions of law. (1) Questions of fact. The tendency, in general, is toward a broad construction of the term "questions of fact." 

Illustrative Cases: 

(a) The Government entered into a contract for the construction of a levee on the Mississippi River providing for payment at the rate of 14.43¢ per cubic yard of earthwork.

The contract contained the standard Changes Article and a Dis putes Article. When the work was partly finished, it was found necessary to make a change, which involved additional work, to correct a tendency towards subsidence'. The contractor requested an increased unit price for the additional work ordered. The contracting officer denied the request. Without appealing to the head of the department, the contractor sued for additional compensation. Held: Recovery denied. 

Determination of whether an equitable adjustment should be made in the price of the extra work involved merely the ascertainment of the cost of digging, moving and placing the earth, and the addition to that cost of a reasonable and customary allowance for profit. These were questions of fact, as to which the decision of the contracting officer was final in the absence of an appeal to the head of the department.12 10. "*** any dispute concerning a question of fact *** shall be decided by the contracting officer ***." ASPR 7-103.12 

11. Such as a provision authorizing the contracting officer to interpret drawings and specifications. See Merrill-Ruckgaber Co. v. United States 

241 U.S. 387 (1916); United States v. Madsen Construction Co., 139 F. 

2d 613 (C.C.A. 6th 1943). For other provisions, see United States v. 

Gleason, 175 U.S. 588 (1900); United States v. Mason & Hanger Co., 260 U.S. 323 (1922); Dayton Airplane Co. v. United States, 21 F. 2d 673 (C.C.A. 6th 1927). 12.

United States v. Callahan Walker Construction Co., 317 U.S. 56 (1942). 

221

(b) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to construct a building. The specifications as originally written specifically required the installation of drip pans and canopies. The drawings, however, included drip pans and canopies within the designation "kitchen equipment", and an addendum to the contract excluded kitchen equipment from the contract. The contract contained a Disputes 

Article. The contracting officer directed the contractor to furnish drip pans and canopies, on the ground that such items are not ordinarily considered "kitchen equipment" and that the express inclusion of these items in the specifications governed over their indirect exclusion by the addendum and the drawings. 

The contractor did not appeal the decision of the contracting officer, but furnished drip pans and canopies and sued for ad ditional compensation. Held: Recovery allowed. The determin ation of what the parties intended by the specifications, drawings, and addendum involves interpretation of the contract and therefore presents a question of law rather than of fact. The 

Disputes Article is inapplicable, and the matter is properly one for decision by the (c) Under a construction contract with the Government, the contractor, without first submitting its claim to the contracting officer, sued to recover damages caused by the Government's delay. Held: Recovery allowed. A claim for unliquidated damages involves a question of law outside the scope of the Disputes Article.14

(2) Questions of law. It has been held that officers may not law, since the result be empowered to determine disputed questions of officers of the would be to oust the courts of their jurisdiction."

Government may be empowered to determine "questions of fact or questions of law arising in connection with questions of fact."16 The courts have given effect in cases involving mixed questions of law and fact to the specific agreement of the parties that decisions of the contracting officer shall be final.

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract which provided that the decision of the contracting officer or his authorized representative as to the proper interpretation of drawings and specifications should be final. Held: In the absence of fraud or such arbitrary action as to indicate bad faith, the contracting officer's decision is binding on the contractor, the Government, and the courts.17 

152. ARBITRATION. The standard Disputes Article is not an arbitra tion agreement, since it provides for determinations by the agents of one of the contracting parties rather than by a disinterested person or body. 

A Government contract may not, in the absence of specific, gtatutory authority, provide for submission of disputes to arbitration. Under the 

Contract Settlement Act of 1944 authority has been granted by Congress for the submission to arbitration of claims arising out of the Government's termination of war contracts.19 

15. The Court of Claims has so held in a number of cases. See Davis v. 

United States, 82 Ct. Cl. 334 (1936); Lyons v. United States, 30 Ct.

153. CORRECTION OF ERRORS AND MISTAKES IN BIDS AND CONTRACTS. Under the formal bid procedure, a bidder is free, up to the time fixed for the opening of bids, to withdraw or correct its bid in any way it chooses.20 

ASPR 2-404 provides "The Contracting Officer shall give to the bidder an opportunity to cure any deficiency resulting from a minor informality or irregularity in a bid, or in the alternative, when it is not to the disadvantage of the Government, may waive any such deficiency when time does not permit the curing thereof. Illustrative examples of minor informalities or irregularities are the following: inadvertent failure to furnish bid bond with bid; failure to affix corporate seal; failure to furnish require catalogs, cuts or descriptive data." This general right of withdrawal or modification terminates when the bids are opened.21 Purely formal or clerical errors which are apparent on the face of the instrument, such as the misplacement of a decimal point or an error in multiplication, may be corrected, even after the opening of bids, by agreement between the bidder and the Government 22 Material errors caused by a unilateral mistake on the part of the bidder may not be corrected administråtively by the department concerned or by the Comptroller General or the courts, unless the mistake was apparent on the face of the bid, putting the Government's representatives on notice of its existence." 18. 23 (Cont'd) See 7 Comp.
