Material errors may be corrected if they result from a mutual mistake of the contractor and the Government, or from unilateral mistake of the contracting officer apparent on the face of the bid.24 Differences of opinion exist as to where the authority for correcting such mistakes is vested. One view holds that where the mistake is discovered after the opening of the bid but before an award is made, the departmental executive has the power to permit withdrawal or correction of the bid.25 The Comptroller General holds that such relief can be granted only by him.26 Where the mistake is discovered after the award has been made, it is generally held that the matter is one for the Comptroller General or the courts. 

23. (Cont'd) 652 (C.C.A. 1st 1941); Saligman v. United States, 56 F. 

Supp. 505 (E.D.Pa. 1944). Where the mistake is brought to the

Government's attention prior to the making of an award, some cases have granted relief if it clearly appeared that the mistake was bona fide and not the result of the bidder's negligence. In other cases, relief has been denied. Compare Moffett H. & Co. v. Rochester, 178 U.S. 373 (1900) with American Water Softener Co. v. United States, 

50 Ct. Cl. 209 (1915). See 5 Williston on Contracts (Rev. Ed., 1936), sec. 1578; annotations, 80 A.L.R. 586, 107 A.L.R. 1451. In most of the cases granting relief, there were actually facts which should have apprised the Government of the mistake before its attention was directed thereto by the bidder. It is believed that the better view denies relief whether the claim of mistake was advanced before or after an award, unless the mistake was or should have been apparent to the Government.

The type of relief available to the contractor depends upon the nature of the mistake and when it is discovered. Where a unilateral mistake of the contractor is apparent on the face of the bid, the contractor may, if correction is denied, refuse to perform. If the contractor does perform, he may recover its costs, even though in excess of the contract price, but not in excess of the next lowest bid. 28 Where a mistake is mutual and relates to a fact basic to the contract, the contractor may refuse to perform. If a mutual mistake relates to the inclusion or exclusion of contract provisions contrary to the intention of the parties, the contractor may perform and then recover compensation on the basis of the provisions which the contract was intended to contain.29 

154. MODIFICATION OF CONTRACTS. The Government may enter into sup plemental agreements modifying existing contracts even in the absence of special wartime legislation.31 Such agreements must contain all the elements of a valid contract? A number of limitations on the use of supplemental agreements modifying Government contracts must, in the absence of special legislation, 32be observed. a. The Government must derive a benefit. No Government agent nas authority to waive a vested contractual right of the Government. A supplemental agreement is therefore valid only if based upon a new consideration or benefit moving to the Government. 28. 29. 30. 

8 Comp. Gen 397 (1929). 

United States v. Milliken Imprinting Co., 202 U.S. 168 (1906); Cramp v. United States, 239 U.S. 221 (1915); Ackerlind v. United States, 240 U.S. 531 (1916). For full discussion see 5 Williston on Contracts (Rev. Ed., 1936), ch. XLVI. 

2 Comp. Dec. 182 (1895). The Attorney General, in 21 Op. Atty. Gen. 

207 (1895), said, "It would be remarkable, indeed, in view of the diversity of subjects-matter and conditions under which public contracts of the Government are made, that modifications to meet the infinite contingencies to which the limits of the human forecast and sagacity subject us, could not, even by consent of the contracting parties, be made without rescinding and abrogating the entire contract. Certainly no such rule is known in the general law of contracts. The minds which agreed to form may in like manner agree to modify." See Kling Bros. & Co. v. United States, 89 Ct. Cl. 329 (1939). 

31. The modifications here discussed are not limited to those authorized by the Changes Article or other provision of the contract. 

32.

Such legislation existed during the war. See discussion infra, this chapter. 226

Illustrative Case: 

The Government advertised for bids on the sale of certain metal scrap, and the contract was awarded to the high bidder. 

Between the date the bids were opened and the date of award, the Government issued an order fixing maximum prices for certain of the metals involved at less than the amount bid. The order did not apply to sales by the Government, but reduced the market value of the metals. The purchaser requested cancellation of the contract or a modification thereof by eliminating the item in question. Held: The Government having acquired vested contract rights, no authority existed for waiving these rights without some benefit to the Government. Hence, the contract could not be modified nor cancelled.33

A contract may not be modified after having been fully performed, since in such case there can be no new consideration to the Government.34 b. An existing contract may not, by supplemental agreement, be transformed into a wholly new or different contract. A supplemental agreement may not be used to defeat the statutory requirement of advertising and competitive bidding. A new contract, based on readvertising and new bids, is required where it is desired to alter materially the nature of the work called for under the original agreement.35 functions.

Extent of authority to settle claims. Some difficulty arises in determining the extent to which administrative officials are authorized, in the absence of special legislation, to settle contract claims, in the sense of determing the amount due thereunder. Where the amount is liquidated and can therefore be determined by arithmetical calculation, executive officers are authorized to make such determinations, subject to review by the accounting office. If a disbursing officer, in reliance upon a settlement so made, makes a payment which is later found by the accounting officers to be erroneous, the amount involved may be recovered from the payee or charged against the account of the disbursing officer.37 No executive officer is empowered, in the absence of specific statutory authority, to settle claims for unliquidated damages.38 

Illustrative Cases: 

(1) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor was to construct the battleship "Alabama." The vessel was completed, the contract price was paid, and the contractor executed a release of all claims except those which the 

36. See 15 Comp. Gen. 238 (1935); 25 Comp. Dec. 764 (1919); 27 Comp.

In Globe Indemnity Co. v. United States, 291 U.S. 476 (1934) at p. 480, the court said: "Before, as after, the Budget and Accounting 

Act, claims against the United States might be paid from the proper appropriation upon approval of the authorized officer of the department concerned, without previous settlement or audit by the accounting office. Before, as after, department heads or disbursing officers might ask the accounting office to render a decision upon a question involving payment to be made by them, in order to protect the disbursing officers and their bondsmen from liability for a payment unauthorized by law.

Secretary of the Navy had no jurisdiction to entertain. Thereafter, the contractor sued to recover the value of extra work perfomed at the instance of authorized Government officers, and the Government pleaded the release as a bar to the action. 

Held: Since the amount involved was not liquidated, the Secretary of the Navy had no jurisdiction to entertain the claim. 

Accordingly, the release was not a bar to the action and the contractor was entitled to recover. 39 (2) The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to transport 66,000 pounds of provisions. 

The contractor was ready, able and willing to perform, but the Government tendered only 11,235 pounds for carriage. The contractor claimed damages for breach of contract. Held: Since the claim was for unliquidated damages, it could not be settled by the head of the department concerned.40 

Executive officers are authorized, however, to enter into supplemental agreements modifying existing contracts. Such agreements may provide for termination of work under existing contracts and fix the amount to be paid the contractor for its part performance. A contractor's claim for unliquidated damages can thus, in effect, be settled by a supplemental agreement entered into while the original contract is still in full force and effect. 

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to furnish certain machinery for the Navy 

Department. Before the contract was completed, the Secretary of the Navy suspended further work under it, and entered into an agreement with the contractor under which the machinery was to be delivered in its incomplete condition, and the contractor was to receive an agreed price for partial performance.

The contractor delivered the machinery, the Government failed to make payment, and the contractor sued to recover the amount agreed upon. Held: Recovery allowed. In the absence of fraud, misrepresentation or concealment, the agreement was binding on both parties.41

155. SETTLEMENT OF CLAIMS UNDER WARTINE LEGILSATION. SECTION III. SETTLEMENT AND ADJUSTMENT OF CONTRACT CLAIMS BY THE GENERAL ACCOUNTING OFFICE. 

156. BASIC STATUTE. The Budget and Accounting Act provides that all claims and demands by or against the Government, and all accounts in which the Government is concerned, either as debtor or creditor, must be "settled and adjusted in the General Accounting Office."47 This statutory provision is based upon a much earlier law which lodged similar powers in the Treasury Department.48 The Act created no new powers with respect to the settlement of Government claims, but transferred existing duties from the Treasury Department to the newly created General Accounting Office." 

157. APPEAL TO GENERAL ACCOUNTING OFFICE. Contractors may submit their claims to the General Accounting Office if they are dissatisfied with the decisions of the contracting agency. The claim may be made in the form of a letter or a brief or both but the evidence should be clear and sufficient enough to permit the Comptroller General to render an opinion thereon. The General Accounting Office is not bound by any previous legal determinations of the contracting agency but is bound by such determinations of fact as may have been made under the Disputes Clause of the contract.50 As 

45. M.S. Comp. Gen. B-24215, 2 Sept. 1942. 

46. Contract Settlement Act of 1944, 58 Stat. 358; 41 U.S.C. 106. 

47. 42 Stat. 24; 31 U.S.C. 71. 48. 

The Act of 3 Mar. 1817, (R.S. 236) provided, in part: "..... all claims and demands whatever, by the United States or against them, and all accounts whatever, in which the United States are concerned, either as debtors or creditors, shall be settled and adjusted in the Treasury Department." 

49. Globe Indemnity Co. v. United States, 291 U.S. 476 (1934). 

50. 17 Comp. Gen. 150 (1937); id. 503 (1937). 231

a general rule, the General Accounting Office requests an administrative report and recommendation on the claim from the department concerned to assist that office in rendering its decision. 

158. RELIEF FROM LIQUIDATED DAMAGES. Additional authority has been granted to the Comptroller General by Congress to afford relief to contractors in connection with liquidated damages for delay assessed under a contract. Section 6, Public Law 413, 80th Congress, provides: 

"Whenever any contract made on behalf of the Government by the agency head or by officers authorized by him so to do includes a provision for liquidated damages for delay, the Comptroller General on the recommendation of the agency head is authorized and empowered to remit the whole or any part of such damages as in his discretion may be just and equitable." 

159. EQUITABLE CLAIMS. The Comptroller General also has authority to consider equitable claims against Government agencies and to recommend payment thereof to Congress.51 No general rule may be stated as to what constitutes an equitable claim since each claim must be considered upon its own merits. Such a claim may be said to be one which the Government in equity and good conscience should pay even though there is no remedy at law providing for payment thereof.

Recent legislation provides that the General Accounting Office may in its discretion use any lapsed appropriation for the payment of claims within the limits of and chargeable to the respective balances of any lapsed appropriations which may be certified by the Comptroller General to be lawfully due.52

SECTION IV. REMEDIES IN THE COURTS 160. SUITS BY THE GOVERNMENT. The Government has inherent capacity to bring suits to enforce its contract and other property rights.53 The 

United States District Courts have jurisdiction of "all suits of a civil nature, at common law or in equity, brought by, the United States, or by any officer thereof authorized by law to sue 4 This jurisdiction may be 

51. 45 Stat. 413; 31 U.S.C. 236. 

52. Surplus Fund 

53. 

Certified Claims Act of 1949, Pub. Law 159, 81st Congress.

54. 36 Stat. 1091 as amended; 28 U.S.C. 41 (1). 232

exercised without regard to the amount in controversy55 or the citizenship of the defendant.56 The Government may also sue in a state court if it desires.57 The suit, whether brought in a Federal or a state court, may be either in contract or in tort, and in general any appropriate legal or equitable remedy available to a private litigant is available to the 

Government.58 

The Government is subject to the same rules of court procedure as 59 any other litigant, except to the extent otherwise provided by Congress Certain defenses which would be available against a private party are not available against the Government. Neither laches nor statutes of limitations may ordinarily be pleaded against the Government. A counterclaim may be asserted against the Government only to the extent that it has consented to subject itself to such claim.61 Consequently, a claim

161. SUITS AGAINST THE GOVERNMENT. 

The Government is immune from suit without its consent, and a plaintiff must, in order to invoke the jurisdiction of the court, show that its case falls within some Federal statute giving such consent. Most suits against the Government are brought under the portion of the Tucker Act which confers upon the Court of Claims in all cases, and upon the District Courts in cases involving not more than $10,000, jurisdiction to hear and determine suits based "upon any contract, express or implied, with the Government of the United States, or for damages, liquidated or unliquidated, in cases not sounding in tort."64 

62. For example, where the Government seeks to enjoin collection of state taxes levied on property owned by it, a court has no jurisdiction to entertain a counterclaim seeking foreclosure of a prior tax lien which came into existence before title passed to the Government. United 

States v. Davidson, 139 F. 2d 908 (C.C.A. 5th 1943). 

The Tucker Act was held, in United States v. Nipissing Mines Co., 206 Fed. 431 (C.C.A. 2d 1913), not to confer jurisdiction on the Court of Claims or the District Courts to render affirmative judgments against the United States on counterclaims, as distinguished from original suits. Similarly, where the United States sues in a state court, it does not thereby submit itself to the jurisdiction of such court for the entry of an affirmative judgment against it. United 

States v. Shaw, 309 U.S. 495 (1940). Admiralty suits represent an apparent exception to the rule stated. See United States v. The Thekla, 266 U.S. 328 (1924). 

64. 36 Stat. 1093 as amended; 28 U.S.C. 41 (20) and 250. From time to time, Congress has passed other statutes in which it has consented to suits not maintainable under the Tucker Act. See 1 Cyclopedia of 

Federal Procedure (2d. Ed.), pp. 652-655. A party having a claim against the United States in excess of $10,000 may waive the excess and bring suit in a District Court for $10,000. W. E. Hedger Co. v. 

United States 42 F. 2d 553 (S.D.N.V. 1930). Where the suit is for an amount exceeding $10,000, the District Court is wholly without jurisdiction unless and until the complaint is amended by reduction of the amount claimed to $10,000 or less. Hammond-Knowlton v. United 

States, 121 F. 2d 192 (C.C.A. 2d 1941). Where a judgment may properly be rendered for interest, the total amount of the judgment, including interest, may not exceed the $10,000 limitation on the District 

Court's jurisdiction. United States v. Willis, 141 F. 2d 314 (C.C.A. 

4th 1944). 234

Simi- The Court of Claims may render only money judgments. It is without power to issue injunctions, decree specific performance, determing the ownership of property, or grant other similar equitable remedies. larly, in cases under the Tucker Act involving $10,000 or less, a District Court has only such jurisdiction as the Court of Claims would have had if the suit had been brought in that tribunal. 

Illustrative Case: 

Plaintiff recovered a judgment in a New York court against I. 

X had a claim against the United States for breach of contract on which suit had not been instituted. The New York court entered an order authorizing plaintiff to sue the Government on X's claim. 

Under the state statute and the order, X was a necessary party to such an action. Plaintiff brought suit in a United States District 

Court, naming the Government and X as defendants. Held: The 

District Court was without jurisdiction to entertain the suit. The 

Court of Claims has jurisdiction only to render money judgments against the Government, and may not hear a suit in which a private individual is a necessary party. In a suit brought under the Tucker Act, the jurisdiction of the District Court is subject to the same limitations as that of the Court of Claims. 

65. United States v. Jones, 131 U.S. 1 (1889). It may, however, in determining a claim under an express contract, reform the contract to accord with the actual intent of the parties and render judgment on the claim under the reformed contract. United States v. Milliken Imprinting Co., 202 U.S. 168 (1906); Cramp v. United States, 239 U.S. 221 (1915); Ackerlind v. United States, 240 U.S. 531 (1916). 66. 

United States v. Sherwood, 312 U.S. 584 (1941): "Except as Congress has consented there is no jurisdiction in the Court of Claims more than in any other court to entertain suits against the United States ***. For that reason it has been uniformly held * * * that its jurisdiction is confined to the rendition of money judgments in suits brought for that relief against the United States *** and if the relief sought is against others than the United States the suit as to them must be ignored as beyond the jurisdiction of the court *** or if its maintenance against private parties is prerequisite to prosecution of the suit against the United States the suit must be dismissed. *** Construing the statutory language with that conservatism which is appropriate in the case of a waiver of sovereign immunity, and in the light of the history of the Court of Claims' jurisdiction to which we have referred, we think that the Tucker Act did no more than authorize the District Court to sit as a court of claims and that the authority thus given to adjudicate claims against the United States does not extend to any suit which could not be maintained in the Court of Claims." 235

Suits under the Tucker Act must be filed within six years from the date the cause of action accrues.67 This requirement is jurisdictional and neither the Court of Claims nor a United States District Court has jurisdiction to hear cases after the statute of limitations expires. No officer of the Government has authority to waive the statute, and the court is bound on its own motion to dismiss the suit even if the statute is not pleaded.68 It is interesting to note that the General Accounting 

Office may take cognizance of certain claims if reviewed in said office within ten years after the date the claims first accrue.69 The running of the statute of limitations does not affect the validity of the claim but deprives the claimant of the remedy. Congress may, if it desires, grant appropriate relief.

Certain normal incidents of litigation between private parties are not present in suits against the Government. Interest does not run on a contract claim against the Government unless the contract or a statute specifically so provides, costs may not be included in a judgment against the Government, in the absence of a specific statute;' 71 and a judgment against the Government cannot be enforced by execution or other similar process.72 

162. SUITS AGAINST OFFICERS OF THE GOVERNMENT. The limited character of the remedies available against the Government has led to numerous attempts to secure relief by suits against Government officers. The general

rule is that if the officer is acting within the authority conferred upon him by a valid statute, any suit seeking to prevent such action is in effect a suit against the Government and may not be maintained. If, on the other hand, an officer is exceeding his authority or is acting under an unconstitutional statute, and in so doing physically invades property rights, he may be enjoined or ejected.

Illustrative Cases: 

a. 

The Government, by illegal methods, acquired color of title to property in Virginia belonging to the Lee family and converted it into the Arlington National Cemetery. A member of the Lee family brought ejectment against the officers in possession of the premises. 

The Attorney General appeared specially and urged that the suit involved property to which the Government claimed title and was in effect a suit against the Government. Held: Since the action was merely one to recover possession of property from individual officers who allegedly held it without valid statutory authority, it could be maintained.73 b. The Government leased a fleet of barges and towboats to plaintiff for use on the Mississippi River and its tributaries. 

Thereafter, the Secretary of War annulled the lease and began seizing the vessels covered by the lease. Held: Assuming that the Secretary was acting outside of any authority conferred upon him by statute, or by the lease it self, a suit against him for an injunction could be maintained.74 73. 74.

United States v. Lee, 106 U.S. 196 (1882). The court pointed out that any judgment obtained in the action against the officers would not be binding upon the Government, if, in a subsequent suit, it chose to litigate its title to the property against the plaintiffs in the ejectment action. Any suit involving the title to property of the Government is a suit against the Government. United States v. Alabama, 313 

U.S. 274 (1941). Hence, a suit against an officer which seeks to quiet plaintiff's title to property, rather than to eject the officer, will not lie. Stanley v. Schwalby, 162 U.S. 255 (1896); Oregon v. Hitchcock, 202 U.S. 60 (1906); New Mexico v. Lane, 243 U.S. 52 (1917). 

Goltra v. Weeks, 271 U.S. 536 (1926). In Philadelphia Co. v. Stimson, 223 U.S. 605, 619 (1912), the court stated: "If the conduct of the defendant constitutes an unwarrantable interference with property of the complainant, its resort to equity for protection is not to be defeated upon the ground that the suit is one against the United States. 

The exemption of the United States from suit does not protect its officers from personal liability to persons whose rights of property they have wrongfully invaded." 237

A different rule prevails where the action of the Government officer consists of breaching or annulling plaintiff's contract with the Government, as distinguished from a physical invasion of property. Whether the 

Government shall perform or breach one of its contracts is a matter for determination by the appropriate members of the executive branch of the Government, and their judgment is not subject to judicial review. 

Illustrative Case: 

The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to carry governmental air mail until 5 April 1936. 

On 9 February 1934, the Postmaster General cancelled the contract effective 19 February. Under applicable laws, the effect of such an order was to disqualify the contractor from bidding on Government air mail contracts for a period of five years. The contractor sought to enjoin the Postmaster General from proceeding with the order of annulment, on the ground that he was without statutory authority to issue it without first giving the contractor an opportunity to be heard on any charges against it. Held: Whether or not there was statutory authority for the order, a suit for an injunction to control the exercise of judgment by a department head as to the cancellation of a contract is in effect a suit against the Government and cannot be entertained. The contractor's sole remedy is to sue the. Covernment for money damages for breach of contract. 75

Nor does suit lie against an officer who invades private property under authority conferred by a valid statute. The property owner may sue the Government if the invasion amounts to an appropriation or taking within the Fifth Amendment. Relief may be had only by act of Congress in the absence of such a taking. This rule has been applied to protect not only federal officers, but also Government contractors who invade the property of third persons in strict accordance with the terms of their contracts. 

Illustrative Cases: 

a. Plaintiff owned land which he claimed would be permanently overflowed by the construction of a projected river improvement. 

He sought an injunction to prevent the Secretary of War from proceeding with the improvement without first arranging to compensate him for the land. Held: Relief denied. If the improvement did 

75. Transcontinental & Western Air, Inc. v. Farley, 71 F. 2d 288 (C.C.A. 

2d, 1934). See also Wells v. Roper, 246 U.S. 335 (1918); Boeing Air 

Transport v. Farley, 75 F. 2d 765 (App. D.C. 1935). Granting injunctive relief in such a case would be tantamount to decreeing specific performance of the contract by the Government. 238

rule is that if the officer is acting within the authority conferred upon him by a valid statute, any suit seeking to prevent such action is in effect a suit against the Government and may not be maintained. If, on the other hand, an officer is exceeding his authority or is acting under an unconstitutional statute, and in so doing physically invades property rights, he may be enjoined or ejected.

a. 

The Government, by illegal methods, acquired color of title to property in Virginia belonging to the Lee family and converted it into the Arlington National Cemetery. A member of the Lee family brought ejectment against the officers in possession of the premises.

Illustrative Cases: 

a.

Plaintiff owned land which he claimed would be permanently overflowed by the construction of a projected river improvement. 

He sought an injunction to prevent the Secretary of War from proceeding with the improvement without first arranging to compensate him for the land. Held: Relief denied. If the improvement did 75. 

Transcontinental & Western Air, Inc. v. Farley, 71 F. 2d 288 (C.C.A. 

2d, 1934). See also Wells v. Roper, 246 U.S. 335 (1918); Boeing Air 

Transport v. Farley, 75 F. 2d 765 (App. D.C. 1935). Granting injunctive relief in such a case would be tantamount to decreeing specific performance of the contract by the Government. 238

result in permanent inundation of plaintiff's property, there would be an appropriation of it for the public use and he would be entitled to recover on an implied promise by the Government to pay its fair value. He was not entitled to be compensated in advance of the tak- 76 ing, but only after it actually occurred. b. The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to construct a system of dikes in the Missouri River. 

The contractor constructed the dikes in strict accordance with the terms of the contract. Their effect was to divert the river from its natural channel, and cause it to wash away a large part of plaintiff's land. Plaintiff sued the contractor. Held: Since the contractor merely carried out the terms of its contract with the Government, recovery can be had only against the latter.77 

163. SUITS AGAINST GOVERNMENT CORPORATIONS. A considerable amount of wartime procurement is done by or with the assistance of Government corporations. Government corporations are legal entities separate and distinct from the Government.78 Congress has power to exempt them from suit to whatever extent it wishes.79 Most Government corporations are specifically authorized by their charters to sue and be sued.80 Even where no such provision has been included, the very fact that a governmental agency is created in the form of a corporation impliedly gives it the power to sue and be sued in the absence of a clearly expressed Congressional intention to the contrary. Further, the corporation, if suable at all, can ordinarily be sued in tort as well as on its contracts, 81 and, unlike the Government, is subject to most of the usual incidents of suit. 

76. Hurley v. Kincaid, 285 U.S. 95 (1932). 

77. Yearsley v. Ross Construction Co., 309 U.S. 18 (1940). See also

Salliotte v. King Bridge Co., 122 Fed. 378 (C.C.A. 6th 1903); 

Burnett v. Alabama Power Co., 199 Ala. 337 (1917); Benner v. Atlantic 

Dredging Co., 134 N.Y. 156 (1892). 

78. United States v. Strang, 254 U.S. 491 (1921); United States ex rel. 

Skinner & Eddy Corp. v. McCarl, 275 U.S. 1 (1927). 79.

Federal Land Bank v. Priddy, 295 U.S. 229 (1935); Brady v. Roosevelt 

Steamship Co., 317 U.S. 575 (1943). 

80. A list of such corporations (no longer complete) is set out at note 3 of the opinion in Keifer & Keifer v. Reconstruction Finance Corporation, 306 U.S. 381 (1939). 

81. Sloan Shipyards Corporation v. United States Shipping Board Emergency Fleet Corp., 258 U.S. 549 (1922). 

239

Illustrative Cases: 

a. The Reconstruction Finance Corporation was authorized to create regional agricultural credit corporations to make loans to farmers and stockmen. The RFC had been specifically empowered to sue and be sued, but neither the statute nor the charters issued to the regional corporations contained any provision as to their right to sue and be sued. Defendant, one of such regional corporations, entered into a contract under which it undertook to provide sufficient feed and water for certain cattle delivered to it by plaintiff. Plaintiff sued to recover for alleged injury to the cattle resulting from defendant's failure properly to care for them. Held: The suit will lie. Even though defendant was not made expressly subject to suit, the fact that it was created as a corporation renders it suable, in the absence of a clearly expressed Congressional intention to the contrary. This applies to suits in tort as well as in contract.82 b. The RFC brought an unsuccessful suit to enjoin alleged infringements of trade marks, which it claimed as part of certain property assigned to it as security for a loan. Held: It is liable for costs and such additional allowances as are customarily made by courts of equity.83 82.

Keifer & Keifer v. Reconstruction Finance Corporation, 306 U.S. 381, 

389 (1939): "Congress may, of course, endow a governmental corporation with the Government's immunity. But always the question is: has it done so? *** Because of the advantages enjoyed by the corporate device compared with conventional executive agencies, the exigencies of war and the enlarged scope of Government in economic affairs have greatly extended the use of independent corporate facilities for governmental ends. ***Congress has provided for not less than forty of such corporations discharging governmental functions, and without exception the authority to-sue-and-be-sued was included. Such a firm practice is partly an indication of the present climate of opinion which has brought governmental immunity from suit into disfavor, partly it reveals a definite attitude on the part of Congress which should be given hospitable scope." 

When empowered to sue and be sued, Government corporations are ordinarily suable in state as well as Federal District courts. They are not suable in the Court of Claims. Suits begun against them in state courts are removable to the federal courts without regard to the citizenship of the parties, and usually without regard to the amount in controversy. See D'Oench, Duhme & Co. v. Federal Deposit 

Insurance Corporation, 315 U.S. 447 (1942); Grant v. Tennessee

Valley Authority, 44 F. Supp. 589 (E.D. Tenn. 1941).

240

The fact that a Government corporation may be sued does not mean that it is liable to the same extent and in the same circumstances as private individuals. Particular provisions of its charter, or the fact that it is a federal instrumentality performing federal functions, may operate to prevent liability which would attach in the case of a private person. 

Illustrative Cases: 

a.

The Home Owners' Loan Act authorized the Federal Home Loan Bank Board to create the Home Owners' Loan Corporation. The Act imposed criminal penalties for the making of any false statement or the over-valuing of any security for the purpose of influencing the corporation to grant a loan. Plaintiff sued the corporation for malicious prosecution, alleging that certain of its agents had, maliciously and without probable cause, caused him to be indicted by a grand jury for violating the act. Held: Assuming that these agents acted with actual malice, the fact that their acts were done within the scope of their official duties in the enforcement of a statute which the corporation was charged with administering bars any action against the corporation.84 b. The Tennessee Valley Authority was authorized to construct and operate a system of dams primarily to aid navigation and control floods, and incidentally to produce electric power. Plaintiff alleged that one of these dams had been negligently operated during a period of flood, resulting in damage to his land and crops. Held: 

The Tennessee Valley Authority is suable for ordinary torts, but it is not suable for alleged negligence in the performance of a governmental function directed by statute. A contrary holding would result in judicial review of a discretionary executive duty. 

83. Reconstruction Finance Corporation v. Menihan Corporation, 312 U.S. 

81 (1941). See also Federal Housing Administration v. Burr, 309

U.S. 242 (1940) (garnishment for salary due an employee); In re Clerk's 

Fees and Cost Bonds, 49 F. Supp. 1011 (M.D. Tenn 1943). 

84. Adams v. Home Owners' Loan Corporation, 107 F. 2d 139 (C.C.A. 8th, 1939). 

85. Grant v. Tennessee Valley Authority, 49 F. Supp. 564 (E.D. Tenn 1942) 

The court stated that "the conventional to-sue-and-to-be-sued clause"! in the statute creating the Authority "is not a shibboleth to engender liability generally, but is to be taken into consideration with the Congressional purposes in creating this governmental corporation." 

See also Lynn v. United States, 110 F. 2d 586 (C.C.A. 5th 1940). 241

Government corporations are also in a somewhat different position than the Government itself as respects the settlement of claims. extent of control exercised over the accounts of such corporations by the General Accounting Office depends upon the terms of the particular statutes and charters involved.86 In general, Government corporations have always possessed the power to settle contract claims by or against third persons, whether liquidated or unliquidated, without reference to the Comptroller General.87

PROBLEMS IN CONFLICT OF LAWS. The construction of contracts between the Government and private parties and the determination of the rights of the parties thereunder are governed by federal law. "The validity and construction of contracts through which the United States is exercising its constitutional functions, their consequences on the rights and obligations of the parties, the titles or liens which they create or permit, all present questions of federal law not controlled by the law of any state."88 This rule applies to both express contracts and contracts implied in fact.89 

86. See McDiarmid, Government Corporations and Federal Funds (1938); 

Mansfield, The Comptroller General (1939), p. 216n; Lilienthal and

Marquis, The Conduct of Business Enterprises by the Federal Govern ment, 54 Harv. L. Rev. 545, 577-586 (1941). 87. 88.

In United States ex rel. Skinner & Eddy Corp. v. McCarl, 275 U.S. 

1, 8 (1927), the court, in so holding with regard to governmental corporations created during World War I, stated: "Indeed, an important if not the chief reason for employing these incorporated agencies was to enable them to employ commercial methods and to çonduct their operations with a freedom supposed to be inconsistent with accountability to the Treasury under its established procedure of audit and control over the financial transactions of the United States." Cf. 

United States v. Allegheny County, 322 U.S. 174, 183 (1944). 

Clearfield Trust Company v. United States, 318 U.S. 363 (1943).

The extent to which the transactions of Government corporations are governed by state law, in the absence of an applicable federal statute, has not been clearly defined by the courts. Where state law conflicts with a federal statute applicable to the particular transaction, the federal law prevails. See D'Oench, Duhme & Co. V. 

Federal Deposit Insurance Corp., 315 U.S. 447 (1942). 

89. Thus, the question of whether property has been "taken" within the meaning of the Fifth Amendment, and the measure of damages to be awarded, are governed by federal law. See Kohl v. United States, 242

It is well settled that a state may not tax the property or activities of the Federal Government without the latter's consent. 90 Similarly, state regulatory statutes do not control the Government, or its officers and agents in the performance of their federal duties. 

Illustrative Case: 

An Ohio statute made it a criminal offense to sell or serve oleomargerine under certain conditions. Defendant was the governor of a soldiers' home located in Ohio on land over which the Government did not have exclusive jurisdiction. Oleomargerine was served at the home under conditions prohibited by the Ohio statute, and criminal proceedings were begun against defendant. Held: Federal officers may not be controlled in the exercise of their federal functions by state regulatory statutes. The Ohio courts had no jurisdiction to entertain the proceedings.91 

89. (Cont'd) 91 U.S. 367 (1875); Nahant v. United States, 136 Fed. 273 (C.C.A. 1st 1905); Kanakanui v. United States, 244 Fed. 923 (C.C.A. 

9th 1917); United States v. Alcorn, 80 F. 2d 487 (C.C.A. 9th 1935). 

See also Lynn v. United States, 110 F. 2d 586 (C.C.A. 5th 1940), applying the same rule in the case of an invasion of property by a Government corporation in exercising a statutory function. 

90. McCulloch v. Maryland, 4 Wheat. (17 U.S.) 316 (1819); Van Brocklin v. 

Tennessee, 117 U.S. 151 (1886). As governments of reserved powers, the states may engage in both governmental and proprietary activities, and the latter are taxable by the United States. South Carolina v. 

United States, 199 U.S. 437 (1905); Allen v. Regents, 304 U.S. 439 (1938). Since the United States is a government of limited powers, exercising only the functions given it by the Constitution, all of its functions, even when performed by Government corporations, are governmental. Graves v. New York ex rel. O'Keefe, 306 U.S. 466 (1939);

Mayo v. United States, 319 U.S. 441 (1943), a Florida law requiring state inspection of fertilizer was held unconstitutional in so far as it applied to fertilizer shipped into Florida by the Secretary of Agriculture pursuant to a federal statute. See also Tennessee

The immunity of the Government from state taxation does not extend to its contractors. This principle applies even though the economic burden of the tax, under the contract, ultimately falls upon the Government. 

A state cannot indirectly tax property of the Government by a tax purportedly on the contractor.

Illustrative Cases: 

a. 

Respondents sold lumber to a contractor who held a Government cost-plus-a-fixed-fee construction contract. The construction was in the state of Alabama. A state statute levied a 2% tax on the gross retail sales price of tangible personal property. Th legal incidence of the tax was on the purchaser, the seller being required to add the amount of the tax to his sales price. Respondents, who were the sellers of the lumber, contended that such a tax could not validly be levied against the contractor, since under the contractor's CPFF contract with the Government the economic burden of the tax would fall on the Government. Held: An independent contractor is not an agent of the Government and does not share its immunity from state taxation. A state tax on the contractor is valid, even though the burden of it will be passed on to the Government under the terms of the contract.92 b.

The Government entered into a contract with the Mesta Machine Company under which the latter agreed to produce certain ordnance materiel at its plant in Allegheny County, Pennsylvania. The contract provided that machinery needed for its performance should be purchased at Government cost and installed in Mesta's plant, title to the machinery to remain in the Government. The county assessing authorities included the value of the machinery in Mesta's assessment for state ad valorem taxes. This assessment was sustained by the Pennsylvania Supreme Court on the ground that under Pennsylvania law, real estate assessable for taxation included machinery placed upon it with the owner's consent, even though it was a bailee rather than the owner of the machinery. Held: In so far as it taxed property of the Government, even though the tax was levied upon the, contractor as bailee, the Pennsylvania law was unconstitutional.93 

91. (Cont'd) Valley Authority v. Kinzer, 142 F. 2d 833, 837 (C.C.A. 6th 

1944), where the court stated: "In view of certain contentions of appellant, it is to be said that the Tennessee Valley Authority, as a governmental instrumentality, is free from a state's regulation or 

92. Alabama v. King & Boozer, 314 U.S. 1 (1941). See also James v.

Dravo Contracting Co., 302 U.S. 134 (1937). 

93. United States v. Allegheny County, 322 U.S. 174 (1944). 244

A Government contractor does not share the immunity of the Government from state regulatory legislation, except as such legislation may actually handicap efforts to carry out Government plans embodied in the contract or as the Government may have declared the state law to be inapplicable. 

Illustrative Cases: 

a. 

The Government entered into a contract under which the contractor agreed to construct a building in New York. The Government had acquired exclusive jurisdiction over the land on which it was to be erected, but state laws remained in effect. A section of the New York Labor Law required the planking-over of floor beams on which iron or steel work was erected in building construction. Plaintiff sued the contractor for the wrongful death of her intestate which was allegedly caused by the contractor's failure to comply with this statutory provision. The contractor contended that the provision did not apply to the performance of a Government contract. Held: Recovery allowed. Government contractors must obey state regulatory enactments which do not "handicap efforts to carry out the plans of the United States. 1194 b. The Government invited bids to supply milk to an Army installation located at Indiantown Gap, Pennsylvania, on land over which the Government did not have exclusive jurisdiction. 

The contract was awarded to the low bidder at a price which was less than the minimum fixed by the Pernsylvania Milk Control 

Commission under authority of the state Milk Control Law. The 

Commission refused to renew the contractor's license as a milk dealer on the ground that it had violated the state regulations in selling milk to the Government at prices below the minimum. 

The contractor contended that such a regulation did not apply in connection with sales to the Government. Held: In the absence of a federal statute or regulation making the state statute inapplicable, it may be constitutionally applied to a Government contractor.95 

94. Stewart & Co. v. Sadrakula, 309 U.S. 94 (1940). 

95. Penn Dairies, Inc. v. Milk Control Commission, 318 U.S. 261 (1943). 

The court did not specifically decide whether a regulation by the Secretary of War could exclude the operation of the state statute. 

It would seem that such a regulation should be given that effect. 

See Standard Oil Co. v. Johnson, 316 U.S. 481 (1942); Tennessee

SECTION V. MISCELLANEOUS REMEDIES 165. CONGRESSIONAL RELIEF. Further relief may be afforded con tractors through the enactment of congressional legislation authorizing the Court of Claims to hear and determine claims, either de novo or where the claim had been denied either by the Comptroller General or the court due to the statute of limitations or otherwise. Such acts may specifically direct the court or the General Accounting Office to disregard certain formalities which do not go into the substance of the claim.96 for private relief are usually referred to the department or agency concerned for report and recommendations much in the same manner as claims considered by the General Accounting Office. 

Bills 166. REMEDIES ON IMPLIED CONTRACTS. "The power to bind the govern ment by contract can only be exercised by those officers whom Congress has constituted its agents, either by the express words of the statute or by necessary legal implication; yet where a person has parted with his property for a lawful purpose, which has been received and used by the proper agents of the government, there the owner may recover for its actual value."97

The foregoing principle has been followed in numerous instances of a parol contract wholly or partially performed, recovery thereon having been based upon a quantum meruit.98 Recovery may be similarly had for articles sold and delivered without stipulation as to price on the theory of quantum valebat. It would appear that the remedy is available to the contractor either in the General Accounting Office or the courts, since the damages under implied contract are unliquidated and such damages may not be setggated tled by a departmental executive. 95. 96. (Cont'd) of the Penn Dairies Case, supra, where the Army installation to which milk deliveries were made was located on land over which the Government had exclusive jurisdiction, and the state law was enacted after such jurisdiction had been acquired. 

Cook & Co., Inc. v. United States 89 Ct. Cl. 553 (1939); Steedman, et al. v. United States 35 Fed. Supp. 533 (1940); Marx, Inc. v. United 

States, 93 Ct. Cl. 628 (1941), and Pacamino Co. v. Marshall, 309 U. S. 370 (1939).

CHAPTER 21 PUBLIC RELATIONSHIPS - CONTRACTS AND CLAIMS 167. RELEASE OF PROCUREMENT INFORMATION-RESPONSIBILITY OF PER- 

SONNEL. It is the individual responsibility of all personnel of the 

Department of the Army, both military and civilian, to refrain from releasing to individual business concerns or their representatives any pre-knowledge such personnel may possess or have acquired in any way concerning procurements or purchase of supplies by any procuring service of the Army. Such information will be released to all potential contractors as nearly simultaneously as possible and only through duly designated agencies, so that one potential contractor may not be given an unfair advantage over another. All dissemination of such information will be in accordance with existing authorized procedures and only in connection with the necessary and proper discharge of official duties. 168. the Army.

CONDUCT OF PROCUREMENT PERSONNEL. a. Policy of Department of The Secretary of the Army has announced the following policy: 

"Every member of the service, military or civilian, is bound to refrain from all business and professional activities and interests not directly connected with his duties which would tend to interfere with or hamper in any degree his full and proper discharge of such duties or would normally give rise to a reasonable suspicion that such participation would have that effect. 

If such person finds that his duties require him to act as an agent of the United States in a manner from which he may derive financial profit or other benefits he will report the facts immediately to higher authority and he will be immediately relieved of such duties. Agents of of the Government must not accept gratuities intended to influence the strict impartiality that must prevail in all business contracts." b. Contracts with Government Officers and Employees. While not necessarily unlawful, contracts between the Government and its employees present an undesirable situation, suggesting favoritism, and are not to be entered into except for the most convincing reasons, such as nonavailability of other sources for the service to be rendered, and even the nonavailability of other sources may not always justify such contracts.3 

Especially is the practice objectionable when the contracting is between an employee and the particular service in which he is employed. 1. 2. 3. 

Sec. II, J.A.A.F. Proc. Cir. 11, 6 May 1948. 

Par. 4, D.A. Memo. No. 600-104, 1 Jul 1948,

169, RETIRED OFFICERS. a. Status. A retired officer retains his status as an officer of the United States and may not engage in any activity incompatible with such status. Retired officers, not on active duty, are subject to the following prohibitions: 18 U.S.C. 283 as amended by the act of 28 June 1949 (Public Law 137 - 81st Congress) which, among other things, prohibits retired officers from aiding or assisting in the prosecution of claims against the Government, involving the Department from which he retired and involving any subject matter with which he was directly connected while on active status, for a period of two years after such retirement; 18 U.S. C. 216 which, among other things, prohibits retired officers from procuring or aiding to procure Government contracts for another; and 18 U.S.C. 281 which prohibits retired officers from representing another in the sale of anything to the Government through the Department in whose service he holds retired status. In addition, language contained in the recent appropriation acts provides that no payment shall be made from money appropriated to any officer on the retired list of the Army who, for himself or for others, is engaged in the selling of, contracting for the sale of or negotiating for the sale of, to the Department of the Army, any war materials or supplies. It b. Statutory plan. The plan is, generally, to prohibit retired officers from directly participating in Government contract activities of the type described above and to prevent such officers from prosecuting claims against the Department from which they retired on matters they were connected with, for a period of two years after such retirement. is considered that the spirit of the statutes prohibiting participation in Government contract activities extends beyond any mere bargaining which may precede the execution of a contract or modification thereof, and includes, as well, any activity by a retired officer on behalf of a prospective contractor which reasonably and directly is aimed toward forming the basis for a contract or agreement with the Government. On the other hand, it is not the intent of these statutes to preclude a retired officer from accepting employment in private industry solely because his employer is a contractor with the Government. Therefore, these statutes should not be construed as applicable to activities which are only remotely connected with contractual matters as distinguished from direct participation in obtaining a contract with the Government on behalf of a prospective contractor. 

3.70 RESERVE AND NATIONAL GUARD PERSONNEL-STATUS. Reserve officers and members of the National Guard, while not on active duty, are not by reason solely of their status as Reserve or National Guard officers, deemed to be officers or employees of the United States, or persons holding any office of trust or profit or discharging any official function under or in connection with any department of the Government of the United States.4 

Therefore, they are not subject to the restrictions applicable to retired officers. They are subject, however, to other laws referred to in the following paragraph. 

4. Sec. 37, National Defense Act as amended (10 U.S.C. 372).

171. 

REPRESENTATIVE ACTIVITIES. a. Activities before Department of the Army. No person is allowed to appear in a representative capacity before the Department of the Army or any of its agencies in a matter involving a claim against the United States before said Department on matters which are preliminary to the formal presentation of a claim, or concerning surplus property unless and until said person has filed a notice of appearance in duplicate in the form set forth in paragraph 4, Department of the Army Special Regulations No. 600-785-1, dated 17 May 

1949. b. Applicable statutes. The following statutes apply to representative activities of former officers, clerks or employees of the

Government: 

Act5 

(1) The Act of 1 June 1872. This Act makes it unlawful for any "officer, clerk, or employee in any of the departments to act as counsel, attorney, or agent for prosecuting any claim against the United States which was pending in either of said departments while he was such officer, clerk or employee, nor in any manner, nor by any means, to aid in the prosecution of any such claim, within two years next after he shall have ceased to be such officer, clerk, or employee." Act6 (2) Act of 3 October 1944. This Act provides that such officer shall not for a period of two years after the time such employment has ceased act as counsel, attorney or agent or be employed as representative in connection with any matter involving the disposition of surplus property by the agency in which such person was employed if such person during his employment in such agency ratified, approved or authorized the disposition of any surplus property pursuant to the provisions of the Surplus Property Law or recommended any such approval, authorization, or ratification as part of his official duties. (3) Act of 25 June 1948. This Act provides that such officer shall not within two years after the time when such employment or service has ceased prosecute or act as counsel, attorney, or agent for prosecuting any claim against the United States involving any subject matter directly connected with which such person was so employed or performed duties.

APPENDIX Armed Services Procurement Regulation 7-103.11 Default 

"DEFAULT "(a) The Government may, subject to the provisions of paragraph (b) below, by written Notice of Default to the Contractor, terminate the whole or any part of this contract in any one of the following circumstances: "(1) if the Contractor fails to make delivery of the supplies or to perform the services within the time specified herein or any extension thereof; or "(ii) if the Contractor fails to perform any of the other provisions of this contract, or so fails to make progress as to endanger performance of this contract in accordance with its terms, and in either of these two circumstances does not cure such failure within a period of 10 days (or such longer period as the Contracting Officer may authorize in writing) after receipt of notice from the 

Contracting Officer specifying such failure. "(b) The Contractor shall not be liable for any excess costs, if any failure to perform the contract arises out of causes beyond the control and without the fault or negligence of the Contractor. Such causes include, but are not restricted to, acts of God or of the public enemy, acts of the Government, fires, floods, epidemics, quarantine restrictions, strikes, freight embargoes, unusually severe weather, and defaults of subcontractors due to any of such causes unless the Contracting Officer shall determine that the supplies or services to be furnished by the subcontractor were obtainable from other sources in sufficient time to permit the Contractor to meet the required delivery schedule. "(c) In the event the Government terminates this contract in whole in part as provided in paragraph (a) of this clause, the Government may procure, upon such terms and in such manner as the Contracting Officer may deem appropriate, supplies or services similar to those so terminated, and the Contractor shall be liable to the Government for any excess costs for such similar supplies or services, provided that the Contractor shall continue the performance of this contract to the extent not terminated under the provisions of this clause. " (a) If this contract is terminated as provided in paragraph (a) of this clause, the Government, in addition to any other rights provided in this clause, may require the Contractor to transfer title and delivery 251

to the Government, in the manner and to the extent directed by the Contracting Officer, (i) any completed supplies, and (ii) such partially completed supplies and materials, parts, tools, dies, jigs, fixtures, plans, drawings, information and contract rights (hereinafter called 'manufacturing materials') as the Contractor has specifically produced or specifically acquired for the performance of such part of this contract as has been terminated; and the Contractor shall, upon direction of the Contracting Officer, protect and preserve property in possession of the Contractor in which the Government has an interest. The Government shall pay to the Contractor the contract price for completed supplies delivered to and accepted by the Government, and the amount agreed upon by the Contractor and the Contracting Officer for manufacturing materials delivered to and accepted by the Government and for the protection and preservation of property. 

Failure to agree shall be a dispute concerning a question of fact within the meaning of the clause of this contract entitled 'Disputes'. " (e) If, after notice of termination of this contract under the provisions of paragraph (a) of this clause, it is determined that the failure to perform this contract is due to causes beyond the control and without the fault or negligence of the Contractor pursuant to the provisions of paragraph (b) of this clause, such Notice of Default shall be deemed to have been issued pursuant to the clause of this contract entitled 'Termination for Convenience of the Government', and the rights and obligations of the parties hereto shall in such event be governed by such clause. "(f) The rights and remedies of the Government provided in this clause shall not be exclusive and are in addition to any other rights and remedies provided by law or under this contract." 252 GPO DSO 4-118-AA
